Self-paced course · 16 modules + 2 projects · every line of code runs in Colab

How to build agents that actually work

Everything useful from Stanford's CS329Z "Engineering AI Agents" (Fall 2026), rewritten in plain language. Each module gives you the idea, key terms from the papers, the evidence, the exact code from its Colab notebook with explanations, the traps, a self-check, and worked solutions.

Built from 11 lecture decks, about 50 papers and posts, and the course codeThe code on this page is generated from the Colab notebooks, cell for cell
The one idea to remember: a great agent is mostly not the model. It is an eval you trust, a simple workflow, a few good tools, a clean context, hard rules in code, and a loop that turns every mistake into a test. The weaker the model, the more this matters, which is why it is the core of what Local AI Lab sells.
Run every module in Google Colab.

Each module has an Open in Colab button. The notebook installs a free open model (Meta Llama 3.1 8B via Ollama) on Colab's free T4 GPU and runs everything locally: no API keys, no data leaving the machine. In Colab, pick Runtime → Change runtime type → T4 GPU, then run the first cell (2–3 minutes).

All notebooks and the two projects: github.com/shivamkalakaar/local-ai-lab-agents

For one-click Colab, use the team site: claude.ai blocks pages from opening new tabs, so the buttons here may not open. The same course with working buttons and solutions: https://shivamkalakaar.github.io/local-ai-lab-agents/

Module 00

Start here

Open in Colab

How to use this page

Work through the modules in order. The course itself goes basics → building blocks → making it good, and later modules assume earlier ones. Each module has:

  • The big idea, in one or two sentences.
  • Evidence: the numbers from the lectures and papers that back it up.
  • Code: short, readable sketches. They are meant to be adapted, not pasted into production unchanged.
  • Traps: the mistakes the course saw people make.
  • Check yourself: two or three questions. Click to see the answer.
  • Try it: a small exercise, usually on our own contract-review or document-check work.

The setup cell (first cell of every notebook)

On Colab it clones the course repo, installs the packages, installs Ollama, starts it, and downloads the model. On a laptop with Ollama it just connects. Notebooks that need extra packages add them to EXTRA.

every notebook · cell 1

if "google.colab" in sys.modules:
    if not os.path.exists("/content/local-ai-lab-agents"):
        subprocess.run(["git", "clone", "-q", "https://github.com/shivamkalakaar/local-ai-lab-agents", "/content/local-ai-lab-agents"], check=True)
    else:
        subprocess.run(["git", "-C", "/content/local-ai-lab-agents", "pull", "-q"])   # get the latest fixes
    os.chdir("/content/local-ai-lab-agents")

    os.chdir("..")
sys.path.insert(0, os.getcwd())
from lal.runtime import setup
MODEL = setup()

The helper every notebook uses: lal/llm.py

A thin wrapper around the OpenAI-compatible API that Ollama and vLLM expose. Point LAL_BASE_URL somewhere else and the same notebooks run on another server.

lal/llm.py
"""One small wrapper around the OpenAI-compatible API that Ollama (and vLLM) expose.

Change LAL_BASE_URL to point the same code at vLLM or any other server.
"""
import json
import os
import time

from openai import OpenAI

client = OpenAI(base_url=os.getenv("LAL_BASE_URL", "http://localhost:11434/v1"),
                api_key=os.getenv("LAL_API_KEY", "local"))


def model() -> str:
    return os.getenv("LAL_MODEL", "llama3.1:8b")


def chat(messages, model_name=None, **kw):
    """One model call. Returns the assistant message object."""
    if isinstance(messages, str):
        messages = [{"role": "user", "content": messages}]
    kw.setdefault("temperature", 0)
    resp = client.chat.completions.create(model=model_name or model(), messages=messages, **kw)
    return resp.choices[0].message


def ask(prompt, system=None, **kw) -> str:
    """Shortcut: prompt in, text out."""
    msgs = ([{"role": "system", "content": system}] if system else []) + [{"role": "user", "content": prompt}]
    return chat(msgs, **kw).content or ""


def structured(messages, schema_model, model_name=None, retries=2, **kw):
    """Force the reply to match a Pydantic model (the server enforces the JSON schema), then validate.

    Small models occasionally return a cut-off or empty reply, so we retry a couple of times
    before giving up, and cap the length so a runaway reply can't hang the loop.
    """
    if isinstance(messages, str):
        messages = [{"role": "user", "content": messages}]
    kw.setdefault("temperature", 0)
    kw.setdefault("max_tokens", 1024)
    fmt = {"type": "json_schema", "json_schema": {"name": schema_model.__name__, "schema": schema_model.model_json_schema()}}
    last = None
    for _ in range(retries + 1):
        resp = client.chat.completions.create(model=model_name or model(), messages=messages, response_format=fmt, **kw)
        try:
            return schema_model.model_validate_json(resp.choices[0].message.content or "")
        except Exception as e:      # invalid or empty JSON: try again
            last = e
    raise last


def timed(fn, *a, **kw):
    """Run fn and print how long it took."""
    t0 = time.time()
    out = fn(*a, **kw)
    print(f"[{time.time() - t0:.1f}s]")
    return out

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 00_setup_first_call.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

1. Talk to the model on this machine

Ollama runs the model on this computer and serves it at http://localhost:11434. It speaks the same API as OpenAI, so we use the normal openai Python library and only change two things: - base_url points at localhost, not at OpenAI's servers; - the API key is a dummy value, because nothing leaves this machine.

The setup cell already loaded the model into GPU memory (that first load takes about a minute), so this call should take a second or two.

00_setup_first_call.ipynb · cell 2
import time
from openai import OpenAI

local = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:11434/v1", api_key="not-needed")   # the model server on THIS machine
t0 = time.time()
resp = local.chat.completions.create(
    model=MODEL,                                           # llama3.1:8b, downloaded by the setup cell
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "In one sentence: why would a law firm want AI that runs on its own computers?"}],
    temperature=0,
)
reply = resp.choices[0].message.content
print(reply)
print(f"\n{time.time() - t0:.1f} seconds, model = {MODEL}, server = {local.base_url}")

Every later notebook uses two short helpers from lal/llm.py, so the code stays readable. They wrap exactly the call above, pointed at the same local server: - ask(prompt): prompt in, text out; - structured(prompt, Schema): forced JSON that matches a Pydantic schema.

00_setup_first_call.ipynb · cell 3
from lal.llm import ask, chat, client
print("lal.llm talks to:", client.base_url)            # the same localhost address
print(ask("Reply with exactly: ready"))

2. Measure speed and token use

On your own hardware there is no per-token bill, but speed still matters.

00_setup_first_call.ipynb · cell 4
t0 = time.time()
resp = local.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, temperature=0,
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "List five common clauses in a services contract, one line each."}])
secs = time.time() - t0
u = resp.usage
print(resp.choices[0].message.content)
print(f"\nprompt tokens: {u.prompt_tokens}, output tokens: {u.completion_tokens}, "
      f"speed: {u.completion_tokens / secs:.0f} tokens/second")

3. Temperature: same question, different settings

Low temperature gives the same answer every time (good for extraction). Higher temperature gives variety (good for brainstorming, or for sampling several candidates to check).

00_setup_first_call.ipynb · cell 5
for temp in (0, 1.0):
    answers = {ask("Name one risky clause in a vendor contract. Answer in 3 words or fewer.", temperature=temp).strip()
               for _ in range(3)}
    print(f"temperature {temp}: {answers}")

4. Our practice data

We use 20 made-up service agreements. Some have problems planted on purpose: an uncapped or oversized liability cap, the wrong governing law, a long auto-renewal, or a short notice period. Because we planted them, we know the right answers, so we can grade our agents. (See data/gen.py.)

print("\nTrue answer for msa_07:", labels()["msa_07"])
print("\nPlaybook rules:")
for k, v in playbook()["rules"].items():
    print(f"  - {k}: {v}")

check("model answers", len(reply) > 10)

# Solution 2: the same summary at three temperatures
for temp in (0, 0.7, 1.3):
    print(f"--- temperature {temp} ---")
    print(ask("Summarize this contract in exactly three short bullet points:\n\n" + contract("msa_07"), temperature=temp))
# What to notice: at 0 the summary is the same every run. At higher temperatures the wording changes,
# and at very high values facts can drift (for example, the cap period). Use 0 for anything you will check.
Module 01

What an agent is, and when you don't need one

Open in Colab

Lecture 1 · Zaharia et al., "Compound AI Systems" · Weng, "LLM Powered Autonomous Agents" · Kapoor et al., "AI Agents That Matter"

An agent is an LLM in a loop that picks its own next step, uses tools, and sees the results. Most real business tasks should not be agents. They should be workflows, where your code fixes the steps and the LLM fills in the fuzzy parts.

Key terms

Agent
An LLM running in a loop that chooses its own next action, calls tools, observes the results, and stops when it decides the task is done.Source: Weng, "LLM Powered Autonomous Agents" (2023); CS329Z Lecture 1
Compound AI system
A system that gets its results from several interacting parts (model calls, retrievers, tools, code) rather than one model call. Most production AI is built this way.Source: Zaharia et al., "The Shift from Models to Compound AI Systems", BAIR (2024)
Workflow
A compound system whose steps are fixed in code. The LLM fills in fuzzy parts but does not decide what happens next.Source: Schluntz & Zhang, "Building Effective Agents", Anthropic (2024)
ReAct
Reason + Act: the model alternates a short thought, an action (tool call) and an observation. Grounding reasoning in actions cut made-up facts compared with reasoning alone.Source: Yao et al., "ReAct", ICLR (2023)
Cost-controlled evaluation
Reporting accuracy together with cost, because retries can buy accuracy. Simple baselines (retry, escalate to a stronger model) often match complex agents.Source: Kapoor et al., "AI Agents That Matter", TMLR (2025)

The anatomy

  • LLM core: reads the situation and decides or writes.
  • Reasoning: thinking before acting (chain of thought, planning).
  • Tools: functions it can call, such as search, a database, or code.
  • Memory: what it keeps between steps and between sessions.
  • Environment: the world it acts on, which sends back results.

The spectrum

LevelWho decides the stepsUse it whenExample
Single callNobody, one shotThe task fits in one promptSummarize one clause
Workflow (compound AI system)Your codeYou know the stepsParse contract → extract terms → check against playbook → draft redlines
AgentThe LLMThe steps depend on what it finds"Research this counterparty and tell me what to worry about"

Evidence

50×

LATS, a complex agent, cost over 50 times more than simple baselines and scored lower (Kapoor).

85% / $0.27

"Cheap model first, escalate on failure" vs. 89.6% at $1.93 for GPT-4 alone (Kapoor).

56% → 0%

Share of failures that were made-up facts: reasoning alone vs. reasoning plus actions (ReAct, L1).

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 01_agent_loop.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

1. Tools the agent can use

01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 2
import json, re, time
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import client, ask
from lal.data import contract, clauses, sections, labels

def list_sections(contract_id: str) -> str:
    """Section headings with numbers."""
    return "\n".join(f"{s['id']}. {s['title']}" for s in sections(contract(contract_id)))

def get_clause(contract_id: str, clause: str) -> str:
    """One numbered clause, e.g. '7.1'."""
    cl = clauses(contract(contract_id))
    return cl.get(clause, f"NOT FOUND. Valid clauses: {', '.join(cl)}")

TOOLS = [
    {"type": "function", "function": {"name": "list_sections",
        "description": "List the section headings of a contract with their numbers. Call this first.",
        "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"contract_id": {"type": "string"}}, "required": ["contract_id"]}}},
    {"type": "function", "function": {"name": "get_clause",
        "description": "Read one clause by number, for example '7.1' or '10.1'.",
        "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"contract_id": {"type": "string"}, "clause": {"type": "string"}},
                       "required": ["contract_id", "clause"]}}},
]
IMPL = {"get_clause": get_clause, "list_sections": list_sections}
print(list_sections("msa_07"))

2. The agent loop

Four safety limits matter most: a turn cap, a stuck detector (the same call twice), errors returned as results so the model can fix its own mistakes, and a format check: small local models sometimes write a tool call as text instead of making it, so we send that back as an error.

01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 3
def run_agent(task, max_turns=8, verbose=True):
    messages = [{"role": "system", "content": "You review contracts. Use the tools to read clauses before answering. Cite clause numbers."},
                {"role": "user", "content": task}]
    seen, calls = set(), 0
    for turn in range(max_turns):                                   # limit 1: turn cap
        msg = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=messages, tools=TOOLS, temperature=0).choices[0].message
        messages.append(msg)
        if not msg.tool_calls:
            if '"name"' in (msg.content or "") and turn < max_turns - 1:     # limit 4: a tool call written as text
                messages.append({"role": "user", "content": "FORMAT ERROR: you wrote a tool call as text. "
                                 "Use the tool-calling interface, or give your final answer in plain words."})
                continue
            return {"answer": msg.content, "turns": turn + 1, "tool_calls": calls}
        for call in msg.tool_calls:
            calls += 1
            key = (call.function.name, call.function.arguments)
            if key in seen:                                         # limit 2: stuck detector
                result = "ERROR: you already made this exact call. Use the result you have, or answer."
            else:
                seen.add(key)
                try:
                    result = IMPL[call.function.name](**json.loads(call.function.arguments))
                except Exception as e:                              # limit 3: errors become results
                    result = f"ERROR: {type(e).__name__}: {e}. Check the tool name and arguments."
            if verbose:
                print(f"  turn {turn+1}: {call.function.name}({call.function.arguments}) -> {str(result)[:90]}")
            messages.append({"role": "tool", "tool_call_id": call.id, "content": str(result)})
    return {"answer": "STOPPED: turn limit reached.", "turns": max_turns, "tool_calls": calls}

t0 = time.time()
out = run_agent("In contract msa_07, what is the liability cap? Quote the clause.")
print("\nANSWER:", out["answer"])
print(f"{out['turns']} turns, {out['tool_calls']} tool calls, {time.time()-t0:.1f}s")

3. The same task as a workflow

We already know where the liability clause is, so code fetches it and the model only reads one clause. Fewer decisions for the model means fewer ways to fail.

01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 4
def cap_workflow(cid):
    sec = next(s for s in sections(contract(cid)) if "Liability" in s["title"])     # code finds the clause
    return ask(f"{sec['text']}\n\nWhat is the liability cap, in months of fees? "
               f"Answer with just a number, or 'none' if liability is unlimited.")   # model reads it

t0 = time.time()
print(cap_workflow("msa_07"), f"({time.time()-t0:.1f}s)")
print("Truth:", labels()["msa_07"]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"])

4. Compare on five contracts

This is the course's main lesson in miniature: try the simple workflow first.

01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 5
def to_months(text):
    if not text: return "?"
    if re.search(r"\b(none|unlimited|no cap|not limited)\b", text.lower()): return None
    for word, n in {"twelve": 12, "twenty-four": 24, "thirty-six": 36}.items():
        if word in text.lower(): return n
    m = re.search(r"\b(12|24|36)\b", text)
    return int(m.group(1)) if m else "?"

rows = []
for cid in ["msa_01", "msa_07", "msa_08", "msa_15", "msa_18"]:
    truth = labels()[cid]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"]
    t0 = time.time(); a = run_agent(f"In contract {cid}, what is the liability cap in months of fees? Answer with a number or 'none'.", verbose=False); ta = time.time()-t0
    t0 = time.time(); w = cap_workflow(cid); tw = time.time()-t0
    rows.append((cid, truth, to_months(a["answer"]) == truth, round(ta, 1), to_months(w) == truth, round(tw, 1)))
print(f"{'contract':9} {'truth':>5} {'agent ok':>9} {'secs':>5} {'workflow ok':>12} {'secs':>5}")
for r in rows:
    print(f"{r[0]:9} {str(r[1]):>5} {str(r[2]):>9} {r[3]:>5} {str(r[4]):>12} {r[5]:>5}")
01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 6
from lal.checks import check
check("agent loop returns an answer", bool(out["answer"]))
check("workflow correct on at least 4 of 5", sum(r[4] for r in rows) >= 4)

Your turn

  1. Remove the stuck detector and ask something the tools can't answer ("What is the vendor's tax ID?"). What happens?
  2. Add a search_text(contract_id, phrase) tool. Does the agent use it?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 7
# Solution 1: the same loop WITHOUT the stuck detector, on a question the tools can't answer
def run_agent_no_stuck(task, max_turns=8):
    messages = [{"role": "system", "content": "You review contracts. Use the tools to read clauses before answering. Cite clause numbers."},
                {"role": "user", "content": task}]
    for turn in range(max_turns):
        msg = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=messages, tools=TOOLS, temperature=0).choices[0].message
        messages.append(msg)
        if not msg.tool_calls:
            return {"answer": msg.content, "turns": turn + 1}
        for call in msg.tool_calls:
            try:
                result = IMPL[call.function.name](**json.loads(call.function.arguments))
            except Exception as e:
                result = f"ERROR: {e}"
            print(f"  turn {turn+1}: {call.function.name}({call.function.arguments})")
            messages.append({"role": "tool", "tool_call_id": call.id, "content": str(result)})
    return {"answer": "STOPPED: turn limit reached.", "turns": max_turns}

q = "What is the vendor's tax ID in contract msa_07?"
print("WITHOUT stuck detector:", run_agent_no_stuck(q))
print("\nWITH stuck detector:   ", run_agent(q, verbose=False))
# What to notice: without the detector, small models often re-read the same clauses until the turn cap.
# With it, the model is told to stop repeating and usually answers "not in the contract". The turn cap is the last safety net.
01_agent_loop.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 2: add a search_text tool
STOP = {"of", "the", "a", "an", "to", "in", "for", "and", "or", "is", "how", "many", "what"}
def search_text(contract_id: str, phrase: str) -> str:
    """Return every clause containing ALL the meaningful words of the phrase ("days of notice" finds "days' prior written notice")."""
    words = [w for w in re.findall(r"[a-z0-9]+", phrase.lower()) if w not in STOP]
    hits = [f"{num}: {text}" for num, text in clauses(contract(contract_id)).items() if all(w in text.lower() for w in words)]
    return "\n".join(hits) or "No clause contains those words. Try one keyword."

TOOLS.append({"type": "function", "function": {"name": "search_text",
    "description": "Find clauses in one contract that contain all the given keywords, e.g. 'notice renewal' or 'governing law'. Faster than reading every clause.",
    "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"contract_id": {"type": "string"}, "phrase": {"type": "string"}},
                   "required": ["contract_id", "phrase"]}}})
IMPL["search_text"] = search_text

out = run_agent("In contract msa_13, how many days of notice are needed to stop the renewal?")
print("\nANSWER:", out["answer"], "| truth:", labels()["msa_13"]["terms"]["notice_days"])
Traps
  • Building an agent because it sounds impressive when a workflow would do.
  • Reporting accuracy without cost. Retries can buy accuracy, so always show both.
  • No turn limit. Loops are the most common agent failure (47% of ReAct's failures involved repeated or wrong reasoning steps).

Check yourself

A law firm wants every NDA checked against its 12-point playbook. Agent or workflow?

Workflow. The steps are known and the same every time. Use the LLM only to read clauses and spot unusual wording, and do the 12 checks in code wherever possible.

What should you try before building a complex agent?

Simple baselines: retrying, and running a cheap model first and escalating to a stronger one only when a check fails. They often match complex agents at a fraction of the cost.

Try it

Take one task from the Release Desk or the contract-review pilot. Write it as a list of steps, then mark each step C (code can do it) or L (needs an LLM). Most teams find 60–80% of steps are C.

Module 02

LLMs for builders

Open in Colab

Lecture 2 · DSPy · SGLang and vLLM structured output

Treat the model as an unreliable component with a typed interface. Force its output into a schema, and when you can check answers, sample several and keep the one that passes.

Key terms

Temperature
A setting that controls randomness when the model picks each next token. 0 is (nearly) the same answer every time; higher values give more varied answers.Source: CS329Z Lecture 2
Structured output
Asking the model to return data in a fixed schema (fields, types, allowed values) instead of free text, so code can use it.Source: CS329Z Lecture 2; DSPy signatures (Khattab et al., ICLR 2024)
Constrained decoding
The server only allows tokens that keep the output valid for a grammar or JSON schema, so the format can't break. The values can still be wrong.Source: CS329Z Lecture 2 (xgrammar / SGLang / vLLM structured outputs)
Test-time scaling
Spending more compute when answering (more samples, longer reasoning) instead of using a bigger model. With a checker, sampling many answers and verifying can beat a much larger model.Source: CS329Z Lecture 2
Self-consistency
Sample several reasoning paths and take the majority answer. Useful when no checker exists.Source: Wang et al., "Self-Consistency" (2023), cited in Lecture 2

Four things every builder should know

  1. Temperature. Use low temperature (0–0.3) for extraction, code and structured output. Use higher temperature only when you want variety, such as generating several candidates to check.
  2. Structured output. Give every component a typed contract: fields, types and fixed choice lists. Then the next step can rely on it.
  3. Constrained decoding. The server can force the model to produce valid JSON matching your schema, token by token (vLLM uses xgrammar for this). Parsing never fails. The content can still be wrong, so you still validate it.
  4. Test-time scaling. Spending more compute at answer time often beats a bigger model. With a checker, sample many answers and verify. Without a checker, take a majority vote.
8B > GPT-4o

With enough samples and a checker, small Llama models beat GPT-4o's single answer on math (L2 s.145).

43% → 53%

Majority vote over 40 reasoning paths on ARC, with no checker needed (L2 s.148).

Note the explicit "null if absent" option. Without it, models invent values to fill required fields.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 02_llms_for_builders.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

1. A typed contract with Pydantic

Note the "null if absent" option. Without it, models invent values to fill required fields.

02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 2
from typing import Literal
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import structured, ask
from lal.data import contract, labels, ids
from lal.checks import quote_is_in_doc, check

class Terms(BaseModel):
    governing_law: str | None = Field(description="US state name, e.g. 'Delaware', or null")
    liability_cap_months: int | None = Field(description="Cap as months of fees, e.g. 12. null if liability is unlimited")
    renewal_years: int | None = Field(description="Length of each automatic renewal period in years")
    notice_days: int | None = Field(description="Days of notice needed to stop renewal")
    cap_quote: str | None = Field(description="Exact sentence from the contract stating the liability cap")

SYSTEM = "Extract the requested terms from the contract. Copy quotes exactly. If a term is absent, use null. Never guess."

def extract(cid):
    return structured([{"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM}, {"role": "user", "content": contract(cid)}], Terms)

t = extract("msa_07")
print(t.model_dump_json(indent=2))
print("Truth:", labels()["msa_07"]["terms"])

2. Valid JSON is not correct content

The server guarantees the format. We still check the content: is the quote really in the document?

02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 3
print("cap quote really in the contract?", quote_is_in_doc(t.cap_quote, contract("msa_07")))
print("made-up quote in the contract?   ", quote_is_in_doc("Liability is capped at $1 million.", contract("msa_07")))

3. Field accuracy on 8 contracts

02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 4
FIELDS = ["governing_law", "liability_cap_months", "renewal_years", "notice_days"]
sample = ids()[:4] + ids()[-4:]
right = {f: 0 for f in FIELDS}
quotes_ok = 0
for cid in sample:
    t = extract(cid)
    truth = labels()[cid]["terms"]
    for f in FIELDS:
        right[f] += getattr(t, f) == truth[f]
    quotes_ok += quote_is_in_doc(t.cap_quote, contract(cid))
for f in FIELDS:
    print(f"{f:22} {right[f]}/{len(sample)}")
print(f"{'cap quote verified':22} {quotes_ok}/{len(sample)}")

4. Majority vote: when you have no checker

Ask several times at a higher temperature and take the most common answer. The agreement rate is a useful confidence signal; low agreement means "send to a human".

02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 5
from collections import Counter
def vote(question, n=5):
    answers = [ask(question, temperature=0.8).strip().lower().rstrip(".") for _ in range(n)]
    label, count = Counter(answers).most_common(1)[0]
    return label, count / n, answers

q = ("Is this clause favorable to the customer? Answer only 'yes' or 'no'.\n\n"
     + contract("msa_08").split("7.1 ")[1].split("\n")[0])
label, agree, answers = vote(q)
print(f"vote: {label} (agreement {agree:.0%}) from {answers}")

5. Best-of-n: when you do have a checker

Sample until an answer passes a check. Here the check is code: the quote must be in the document.

02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 6
def best_of_n(cid, n=4):
    for i in range(n):
        t = structured([{"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM}, {"role": "user", "content": contract(cid)}], Terms, temperature=0.7)
        if quote_is_in_doc(t.cap_quote, contract(cid)):
            return t, i + 1
    return None, n   # nothing passed: escalate to a person instead of guessing

t, tries = best_of_n("msa_12")
print(f"passed after {tries} tries:" if t else "no sample passed", t.cap_quote if t else "")
02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 7
check("extraction returns a valid Terms object", isinstance(extract("msa_01"), Terms))
check("governing law right on at least 6 of 8", right["governing_law"] >= 6)

  1. Remove "use null" from the system prompt and run extract on a contract with unlimited liability (msa_16). Does the model invent a cap?
  2. Change governing_law to a Literal[...] of US states. Does accuracy change?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 1: remove the "use null" escape hatch and force an integer cap on an UNLIMITED contract
class CapForced(BaseModel):
    liability_cap_months: int          # no None allowed
    cap_quote: str

forced = structured([{"role": "system", "content": "Extract the requested terms from the contract. Copy quotes exactly."},
                     {"role": "user", "content": contract("msa_16")}], CapForced)
print(forced)
print("truth:", labels()["msa_16"]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"], "(None = unlimited)")
# What to notice: when the schema demands a number, the model has to invent one. Always give an explicit "absent" option.
02_llms_for_builders.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: governing law as a fixed list of choices
STATES = Literal["California", "Delaware", "New York", "Texas", "Florida", "Nevada", "Other"]
class LawOnly(BaseModel):
    governing_law: STATES

hits = sum(structured([{"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM}, {"role": "user", "content": contract(c)}], LawOnly).governing_law
           == labels()[c]["terms"]["governing_law"] for c in sample)
print(f"governing law with a fixed list: {hits}/{len(sample)}  (free text was {right['governing_law']}/{len(sample)})")
# A fixed list makes off-menu answers impossible ("State of Delaware", "DE", "Delaware law") and makes grading exact.
Traps
  • Asking for JSON in the prompt and hoping. Use the server's schema mode.
  • Free-text answers for fixed choices. Use an enum (Literal) or a small classifier, so the model can't answer off the menu.
  • Trusting the model's own confidence score. Use agreement across samples or an outside check instead.

Check yourself

Constrained decoding guarantees valid JSON. Why validate anyway?

Valid format is not correct content. The value can still be wrong, made up, or in the wrong unit. Check values against the source (for example, the quote must really appear in the document).

When does majority vote help and when does best-of-n help?

Best-of-n needs a checker (tests, rules, a schema plus source match). Majority vote works without a checker, but only for short answers you can compare, like labels or numbers.

Module 03

Context engineering

Open in Colab

Lectures 2 and 6 · Anthropic, "Effective Context Engineering" · LangChain, "Context Engineering" · MemGPT

The context window is a small, expensive desk. Put only what the next step needs on it. Quality starts to drop well before the window is full.

Key terms

Context window
The maximum text (in tokens) a model can read at once. Everything the model knows about the current task must fit here.Source: CS329Z Lecture 2
Context rot
Answer quality drops as the context grows, often well before the window is full, and facts in the middle are used least.Source: Anthropic, "Effective Context Engineering for AI Agents" (2025); Lecture 2
Context engineering
Choosing the smallest set of high-value tokens to put in front of the model at each step: what to write down, select, compress or isolate.Source: Rajasekaran et al., Anthropic (2025); LangChain, "Context Engineering" (2025)
Observation masking
Replacing old tool outputs with a short placeholder. Cheap, needs no model call, and in coding agents roughly halved cost with equal or better results.Source: CS329Z Lecture 6
Compaction
Summarizing older history when nearing the window limit while keeping the system prompt, goal and recent turns word for word.Source: Lecture 6; MemGPT (Packer et al., 2023)
KV cache / prefix caching
The server keeps computed state for a prompt prefix it has seen. A stable prefix means the next call skips that work, which speeds up local inference.Source: Lecture 2
~84%

Share of a coding agent's tokens that are tool outputs. Hiding old ones roughly halved cost with equal or better results (L6).

51% vs 34%

A small model searching a 263K-token input with code beat a big model reading it all (L2 s.173).

8K

Context length where measurable quality decline began on the OOLONG test (L2).

The six techniques

  1. Append-only event log. Store every step forever. Rebuild what the model sees from the log each turn. You can then hide or summarize parts without losing the truth.
  2. Stable prefix. Keep the system prompt and tool list identical across calls and only add to the end. The server can then reuse its cache (vLLM --enable-prefix-caching), which cuts time to first token on your own GPU.
  3. Mask old observations. Replace old tool outputs with a one-line placeholder. It is cheap and needs no extra LLM call. Try this before summarizing.
  4. Compact near the limit. Summarize the middle of the history, but keep the system prompt, the user's goal and the last few turns word for word.
  5. Pin critical rules. Never let a rule like "confirm before acting" live only in the chat history. One agent deleted a user's inbox after that rule was summarized away (L2 s.171).
  6. Just-in-time loading and notes. Keep references (file paths, IDs) and load content when needed. Write progress to a notes file so it survives resets.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 03_context_engineering.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

1. Measure it: longer prompts are slower

We ask the same question with 1, 5 and 10 contracts in the prompt.

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 2
import time
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import client, ask
from lal.data import contract, ids, labels

question = "What governing law does contract msa_09 use? Answer with the state name only."
for n in (1, 5, 10):
    docs = [cid for cid in ids() if cid != "msa_09"][: n - 1] + ["msa_09"]
    prompt = "\n\n".join(f"=== {cid} ===\n{contract(cid)}" for cid in docs) + "\n\n" + question
    t0 = time.time()
    r = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": prompt}], temperature=0)
    print(f"{n:2} contracts: {r.usage.prompt_tokens:6} prompt tokens, {time.time()-t0:5.1f}s, answer: {r.choices[0].message.content.strip()[:30]}")
print("Truth:", labels()["msa_09"]["terms"]["governing_law"])

2. An append-only event log

Store every step forever. Rebuild what the model sees from the log each turn. Then you can trim what the model sees without losing the record (which you need for audit and replay).

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 3
events = []   # the full truth, never edited
def log(role, content, kind="chat"):
    events.append({"role": role, "content": content, "kind": kind})

log("user", "Review contract msa_15 against the playbook.")
for sec in ["3", "4", "7", "9", "10"]:
    log("assistant", f"Reading section {sec}.")
    log("user", f"[tool result] section {sec}: " + contract("msa_15").split(f"\n{sec}. ")[1][:400], kind="tool")
print(len(events), "events,", sum(len(e["content"]) for e in events), "characters")

3. Observation masking and pinned rules

Old tool outputs are most of the tokens. Replace them with a one-line placeholder; keep the goal, the hard rules and the last few turns word for word. This needs no extra model call.

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 4
PINNED = """You review contracts for a law firm.
HARD RULES (never dropped by trimming):
- You never send, file or delete anything. You only write a review for a lawyer.
- Every issue must cite a clause number."""

def build_context(events, keep_recent=4):
    msgs = [{"role": "system", "content": PINNED}, events[0]]             # pinned rules + the user's goal
    for e in events[1:-keep_recent]:
        if e["kind"] == "tool":
            msgs.append({"role": e["role"], "content": f"[old tool output hidden, {len(e['content'])} chars]"})
        else:
            msgs.append(e)
    return msgs + events[-keep_recent:]

full = sum(len(e["content"]) for e in events)
trimmed = sum(len(m["content"]) for m in build_context(events))
print(f"full: {full} chars -> trimmed: {trimmed} chars ({1 - trimmed/full:.0%} smaller)")

4. Compaction: summarize the middle when you must

When masking isn't enough, summarize the middle of the history. Keep the pinned rules out of the summary so they can never be lost. (One real agent deleted a user's inbox after "confirm before acting" was summarized away.)

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 5
def compact(events, keep_recent=2):
    middle = "\n".join(e["content"] for e in events[1:-keep_recent])
    summary = ask("Summarize these contract-review notes in at most 5 short bullets. Keep clause numbers and numbers exact.\n\n" + middle)
    return [{"role": "system", "content": PINNED}, events[0],
            {"role": "assistant", "content": "Summary of earlier work:\n" + summary}] + events[-keep_recent:]

ctx = compact(events)
print(ctx[2]["content"])
print("\nPinned rules still present:", "never send" in ctx[0]["content"])

5. Just-in-time loading

Instead of pasting all 10 contracts, keep a list of IDs and load the one you need. Compare with step 1.

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 6
def answer_with_lookup(cid, q):
    return ask(f"{contract(cid)}\n\n{q}")
t0 = time.time()
print(answer_with_lookup("msa_09", question).strip(), f"({time.time()-t0:.1f}s)")
03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 7
from lal.checks import check
check("masking makes the context smaller", trimmed < full)
check("pinned rules survive compaction", "never send" in ctx[0]["content"])

03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 1: question at the START of a 10-contract prompt vs at the END
docs = [cid for cid in ids() if cid != "msa_09"][:9] + ["msa_09"]
body = "\n\n".join(f"=== {cid} ===\n{contract(cid)}" for cid in docs)
for where, prompt in [("question first", question + "\n\n" + body), ("question last", body + "\n\n" + question)]:
    answers = [ask(prompt).strip()[:25] for _ in range(2)]
    print(f"{where:15} -> {answers}")
print("truth:", labels()["msa_09"]["terms"]["governing_law"])
# Models often do better when the question is near the end (closest to where they start writing),
# and they can lose facts buried in the middle. Measure it on your own model rather than assuming.
03_context_engineering.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: a notes file the agent appends to, and a context rebuilt from it
from pathlib import Path
from lal.data import sections
from lal.llm import chat
notes = Path("notes.md")
notes.write_text("# Review notes: msa_15\n")
for s in sections(contract("msa_15")):
    if s["title"] in ("Renewal", "Limitation Of Liability", "Governing Law"):
        line = ask(f"In one line, with exact numbers, what does this clause say?\n{s['text']}").strip()
        with open(notes, "a") as f:
            f.write(f"- {s['id']}. {s['title']}: {line}\n")
print(notes.read_text())
ctx = [{"role": "system", "content": PINNED},
       {"role": "user", "content": "Using only these notes, list any concerns against a playbook that requires CA or DE law "
                                   "and a cap of at most 12 months of fees:\n" + notes.read_text()}]
print(chat(ctx).content)
# The notes survive any context reset: a new session can start from notes.md instead of re-reading the whole contract.
Traps
  • Stuffing every document into the prompt "just in case". It makes answers worse, not better.
  • Editing earlier messages. It breaks the cache, and you pay to reprocess everything.
  • Keeping safety rules only in conversation history, where compaction can delete them.

Check yourself

Your local agent slows down and gets worse after 20 turns. What do you try first?

Mask old tool outputs. It is free (no extra LLM call) and tool outputs are most of the tokens. Summarize only if that isn't enough.

Why rebuild the context from a log instead of keeping one growing message list?

The log stays the complete truth for audit and replay, while what the model sees can be trimmed freely. You get both a small context and a full record.

Module 04

Retrieval (RAG)

Open in Colab

Lecture 3 · Lewis et al. (RAG) · DPR · ColBERT · Anthropic, "Contextual Retrieval"

Retrieval finds the few passages the model needs. The default that works: search two ways (keywords and meaning), merge, rerank, and check every citation in code.

Key terms

RAG
Retrieval-augmented generation: first find relevant passages, then have the model answer from them.Source: Lewis et al., "Retrieval-Augmented Generation", NeurIPS (2020)
BM25
A classic keyword-matching score. Strong for exact strings such as clause numbers, IDs and names.Source: Lecture 3
Dense retrieval / embeddings
Turning text into vectors so passages with similar meaning are close together, even with different words.Source: Karpukhin et al., "Dense Passage Retrieval", EMNLP (2020)
Reciprocal rank fusion (RRF)
Merging ranked lists by adding 1/(k + rank) for each list (k ≈ 60). Simple and robust for hybrid search.Source: Lecture 3
Reranker / late interaction
A slower model that scores (query, passage) pairs more carefully to reorder the top candidates. ColBERT does this efficiently by comparing token-level vectors.Source: Khattab & Zaharia, "ColBERT", SIGIR (2020)
Contextual retrieval
Prepending a short note to each chunk saying where it comes from before indexing. Failed retrievals dropped from 5.7% to 1.9% with reranking.Source: Anthropic, "Contextual Retrieval" (2024), cited in Lecture 3
Recall@k
Share of questions where the right passage is in the top k results. Measures retrieval on its own.Source: Lecture 3

The decision

  • Under about 200K tokens of documents? Put it all in the prompt and skip retrieval.
  • More than that? Build the pipeline below.

The pipeline

  1. Chunk on structure. Split by headings or clauses first. Otherwise use 100–500 token chunks with 10–20% overlap.
  2. Add context to each chunk. Prepend a one-line note: "From the MSA with Acme, section 12, Limitation of Liability."
  3. Search two ways. Keyword search (BM25) catches exact strings like "12.3" or a lot number. Embedding search catches rewording like "cap on damages" vs. "limitation of liability."
  4. Merge with reciprocal rank fusion (RRF): score = Σ 1/(60 + rank).
  5. Rerank. Fetch about 150 candidates, rerank with a slower, more accurate model, and keep about 20.
  6. Verify citations. Every quote the model gives must appear in the source text, checked in code.
5.7% → 1.9%

Failed retrievals: plain chunks vs. contextual chunks with hybrid search and reranking (Anthropic).

10–20

Documents in the prompt beyond which accuracy stops improving; models also ignore the middle of long contexts (L4).

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 04_retrieval_rag.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 2
import re
import numpy as np
from rank_bm25 import BM25Okapi
from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
from lal.data import contract, sections, ids, labels
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import structured
from lal.checks import quote_is_in_doc, check

1. Chunk on structure, with context

Each chunk is one section of one contract. We prepend a short note saying where it came from (Anthropic's "contextual retrieval"), which helps both search methods.

04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 3
chunks = []
for cid in ids():
    for s in sections(contract(cid)):
        chunks.append({"id": f"{cid}#{s['id']}", "contract": cid, "section": s["id"],
                       "context": f"From {cid}, section {s['id']} ({s['title']}).", "text": s["text"]})
print(len(chunks), "chunks. Example:", chunks[6])

2. Two searches, merged with RRF

04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 4
embedder = SentenceTransformer("BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5")      # small, local embedding model
QUERY_PREFIX = "Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages: "   # what bge models expect on queries
tok = lambda s: re.findall(r"\w+", s.lower())                 # word tokens, so "msa_15," matches "msa_15"

class HybridIndex:
    def __init__(self, chunks):
        self.chunks = chunks
        docs = [f"{c['context']}\n{c['text']}" for c in chunks]
        self.bm25 = BM25Okapi([tok(d) for d in docs])
        self.vecs = embedder.encode(docs, normalize_embeddings=True, show_progress_bar=False)

    def keyword(self, q, n):
        return list(np.argsort(-self.bm25.get_scores(tok(q)))[:n])

    def semantic(self, q, n):
        qv = embedder.encode([QUERY_PREFIX + q], normalize_embeddings=True)[0]
        return list(np.argsort(-(self.vecs @ qv))[:n])

    def search(self, q, k=5, pool=50, mode="hybrid", rrf_k=60):
        if mode == "keyword": return [self.chunks[i] for i in self.keyword(q, k)]
        if mode == "semantic": return [self.chunks[i] for i in self.semantic(q, k)]
        scores = {}
        for ranking in (self.keyword(q, pool), self.semantic(q, pool)):
            for r, i in enumerate(ranking):
                scores[i] = scores.get(i, 0) + 1 / (rrf_k + r + 1)
        return [self.chunks[i] for i in sorted(scores, key=scores.get, reverse=True)[:k]]

index = HybridIndex(chunks)
for c in index.search("msa_15 cap on damages", k=3):
    print(c["id"], "|", c["text"][:80])

3. Measure retrieval on its own: recall@k

Did the right chunk come back? We test with questions that use different words from the contract ("cap on damages" vs. "limitation of liability"), and questions with exact IDs.

04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 5
QUERIES = []
for cid in ids()[:10]:
    QUERIES += [(f"{cid} cap on damages", f"{cid}#7"), (f"which state's law applies in {cid}", f"{cid}#10"),
                (f"{cid} how do we stop it renewing", f"{cid}#4")]

def recall_at_k(mode, k=3):
    return sum(any(c["id"] == gold for c in index.search(q, k=k, mode=mode)) for q, gold in QUERIES) / len(QUERIES)

for mode in ("keyword", "semantic", "hybrid"):
    print(f"{mode:9} recall@3 = {recall_at_k(mode):.0%}")

4. Answer from retrieved chunks, with citations checked in code

04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 6
from pydantic import BaseModel
class Answer(BaseModel):
    answer: str
    chunk_id: str
    quote: str

def rag(question, k=4):
    hits = index.search(question, k=k)
    ctx = "\n\n".join(f"[{c['id']}] {c['text']}" for c in hits)
    a = structured([{"role": "system", "content": "Answer only from the sources. Give the source id and an exact quote."},
                    {"role": "user", "content": f"Sources:\n{ctx}\n\nQuestion: {question}"}], Answer)
    cid = a.chunk_id.strip().strip("[]")                     # models often copy the brackets
    src = next((c for c in hits if c["id"] == cid), None)
    a_ok = src is not None and quote_is_in_doc(a.quote, src["text"])
    return a, a_ok

a, ok = rag("What is the liability cap in msa_20?")
print(a.model_dump(), "\ncitation verified:", ok)
04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 7
check("hybrid recall@3 is at least as good as keyword-only", recall_at_k("hybrid") >= recall_at_k("keyword"))
check("RAG answer cites a real quote", ok)

Your turn

  1. Remove the context note from each chunk and rerun recall@k. What drops?
  2. Add a reranker: from sentence_transformers import CrossEncoder; CrossEncoder("cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2"), score the top 20, keep 4.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 1: the same index WITHOUT the context note on each chunk
plain_index = HybridIndex([{**c, "context": ""} for c in chunks])
def recall_with(idx, mode, k=3):
    return sum(any(c["id"] == gold for c in idx.search(q, k=k, mode=mode)) for q, gold in QUERIES) / len(QUERIES)
print(f"{'mode':9} {'with context':>13} {'without':>8}")
for mode in ("keyword", "semantic", "hybrid"):
    print(f"{mode:9} {recall_with(index, mode):>13.0%} {recall_with(plain_index, mode):>8.0%}")
# What to notice: our queries name the contract ("msa_07 cap on damages"), but the chunk text alone never says which
# contract it is from. The context note carries that, which is exactly why contextual retrieval helps.
04_retrieval_rag.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: add a reranker: fetch 20, rerank with a cross-encoder, keep the best
from sentence_transformers import CrossEncoder
reranker = CrossEncoder("cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2")
def search_rerank(q, k=3, first=20):
    cands = index.search(q, k=first)
    scores = reranker.predict([(q, f"{c['context']} {c['text']}") for c in cands])
    return [c for _, c in sorted(zip(scores, cands), key=lambda x: -x[0])][:k]
rr = sum(any(c["id"] == gold for c in search_rerank(q)) for q, gold in QUERIES) / len(QUERIES)
print(f"hybrid recall@3 {recall_at_k('hybrid'):.0%} -> hybrid + rerank recall@3 {rr:.0%}")
Traps
  • Embeddings only. They miss exact identifiers like clause numbers, IDs and lot numbers.
  • Judging retrieval by the final answer. Measure recall@k separately, or you can't tell which part failed.
  • Treating retrieved text as instructions. It is data. A contract can contain "ignore previous instructions".
  • Ignoring access rights. Filter by client or matter at search time, not after.

Check yourself

A lawyer asks "what does 7.2(b) say?" and the embedding search returns the wrong clause. Why?

Embeddings capture meaning, not exact strings like "7.2(b)". Keyword search (BM25) finds it. That is why hybrid search is the default.

Why fetch 150 and keep 20, instead of fetching 20?

Fast first-stage search is noisy. A wide pool makes sure the right passage is in there, and the slower reranker puts it at the top.

Module 05

Tools and function calling

Open in Colab

Lecture 4 · Toolformer · Gorilla · MCP spec · BFCL · τ-bench · SWE-agent

Spend more design effort on tools than on the prompt. A good tool is narrow, clearly described, hard to misuse, returns short output, and fails with a helpful message.

Key terms

Function (tool) calling
The model returns a structured request to run a named function with JSON arguments; your code runs it and returns the result.Source: Lecture 4
JSON Schema
A standard way to describe the allowed shape of JSON (types, required fields, patterns, enums). Used to validate tool arguments before running anything.Source: MCP specification (2025-06-18)
Model Context Protocol (MCP)
An open standard for exposing tools, resources and prompts to models through servers, with errors returned as results (isError).Source: modelcontextprotocol.io, spec revision 2025-06-18
Agent-computer interface (ACI)
The design of the tools and outputs an agent sees. Small changes (paged file views, edits that reject syntax errors) changed coding-agent success a lot.Source: Yang et al., "SWE-agent", NeurIPS (2024)
Toolformer / Gorilla
Research showing models can learn when and how to call APIs, and that retrieval of up-to-date API docs reduces made-up calls.Source: Schick et al., NeurIPS (2023); Patil et al., NeurIPS (2024)
τ-bench
A benchmark of tool-using agents talking to simulated users under policies; it popularized pass^k because agents were inconsistent across repeats.Source: Yao et al., τ-bench (2024), cited in Lecture 4

Tool design rules

  • Few and distinct. Overlapping tools confuse the model. The Pi harness used 4 tools and a prompt under 1,000 tokens and was among the most cost-effective.
  • Write descriptions like docs for a new hire. Say what it does, what it returns, and when not to use it.
  • Make misuse impossible. Use enums, required fields, and absolute paths. One fix like that removed a whole class of errors in SWE-agent.
  • Short, paged output. Showing whole files cut SWE-agent's success from 18.0% to 12.7%. Return 100 lines at a time.
  • Validate before running. Check the call against its schema first. Bad calls never execute.
  • Errors are results. Return "ERROR: clause '99' not found; valid clauses are 1–24" so the model can recover. MCP marks these with isError.
  • Allow "no tool fits". BFCL tests this. A model forced to pick a tool will pick a wrong one.
  • Prefer native function calling. On τ-bench it beat ReAct-style text calls for every model. On weak local servers, a strict text format plus a parser can be more reliable.

Example: a good vs. a bad tool description

BadGood
search(q): "Searches documents."search_contract(contract_id, query, max_results=5): "Find passages in ONE contract. Use clause numbers like '7.2(b)' for exact lookups. Returns up to 5 passages with clause numbers. Use get_clause to read a full clause."

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 05_tools.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

05_tools.ipynb · cell 2
import json
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any, Callable, Literal
import jsonschema
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import client
from lal.data import contract, clauses, sections
from lal.checks import check

1. The registry

Order matters: validate → permission → run → truncate. Bad calls never run. Actions that change the world need approval.

05_tools.ipynb · cell 3
@dataclass
class ToolSpec:
    name: str
    description: str
    parameters: dict
    fn: Callable[..., Any]
    read_only: bool = True

@dataclass
class ToolResult:
    status: Literal["ok", "unknown_tool", "invalid_args", "tool_error", "denied"]
    content: str

class Registry:
    def __init__(self, specs, approve=lambda name, args: False, max_chars=1500):
        self.specs = {s.name: s for s in specs}
        self.approve, self.max_chars = approve, max_chars

    def schemas(self):
        return [{"type": "function", "function": {"name": s.name, "description": s.description,
                 "parameters": s.parameters}} for s in self.specs.values()]

    def execute(self, name, args) -> ToolResult:
        spec = self.specs.get(name)
        if spec is None:
            return ToolResult("unknown_tool", f"ERROR: no tool '{name}'. Available: {', '.join(self.specs)}")
        try:
            jsonschema.validate(args, spec.parameters)
        except jsonschema.ValidationError as e:
            return ToolResult("invalid_args", f"ERROR: bad arguments: {e.message}")
        if not spec.read_only and not self.approve(name, args):
            return ToolResult("denied", "DENIED: a person declined this action. Do not retry it.")
        try:
            out = str(spec.fn(**args))
        except Exception as e:
            return ToolResult("tool_error", f"ERROR: {type(e).__name__}: {e}")
        if len(out) > self.max_chars:
            out = out[: self.max_chars] + f"\n[truncated; {len(out)} chars total. Ask for a narrower range.]"
        return ToolResult("ok", out)

2. Three tools: two read-only, one that sends email

Notice the descriptions: what it does, what it returns, when to use it. clause must match a pattern, so wrong formats are rejected before running.

05_tools.ipynb · cell 4
def read_pages(contract_id, start_line=1, n_lines=15):
    lines = contract(contract_id).splitlines()
    return "\n".join(f"{i}: {l}" for i, l in enumerate(lines[start_line-1:start_line-1+n_lines], start_line))

def get_clause(contract_id, clause):
    return clauses(contract(contract_id)).get(clause, "NOT FOUND")

SENT = []
def send_email(to, body):
    SENT.append((to, body)); return f"sent to {to}"

CID = {"type": "string", "pattern": "^msa_[0-9]{2}$", "description": "Contract id like 'msa_07'"}
registry = Registry([
    ToolSpec("read_pages", "Read a contract 15 lines at a time, with line numbers. Use start_line to page.",
             {"type": "object", "properties": {"contract_id": CID, "start_line": {"type": "integer", "minimum": 1},
              "n_lines": {"type": "integer", "minimum": 1, "maximum": 30}}, "required": ["contract_id"]}, read_pages),
    ToolSpec("get_clause", "Read one clause by number like '7.1'. Returns NOT FOUND if it doesn't exist.",
             {"type": "object", "properties": {"contract_id": CID, "clause": {"type": "string", "pattern": r"^\d+\.\d+$"}},
              "required": ["contract_id", "clause"]}, get_clause),
    ToolSpec("send_email", "Email someone. Requires human approval.",
             {"type": "object", "properties": {"to": {"type": "string"}, "body": {"type": "string"}}, "required": ["to", "body"]},
             send_email, read_only=False),
])

3. Watch the registry catch mistakes (no model needed)

05_tools.ipynb · cell 5
for name, args in [("get_clause", {"contract_id": "msa_07", "clause": "7.1"}),
                   ("get_clause", {"contract_id": "msa_7", "clause": "seven"}),
                   ("delete_contract", {"contract_id": "msa_07"}),
                   ("send_email", {"to": "x@example.com", "body": "contract attached"}),
                   ("read_pages", {"contract_id": "msa_07", "n_lines": 30})]:
    r = registry.execute(name, args)
    print(f"{r.status:13} {name}: {r.content[:80]!r}")
print("emails actually sent:", SENT)

4. Now let the model use it

The model asks to email the contract to someone. The registry denies it, the model is told, and nothing is sent.

05_tools.ipynb · cell 6
def run(task, max_turns=6):
    msgs = [{"role": "system", "content": "You help lawyers. Use tools."}, {"role": "user", "content": task}]
    for _ in range(max_turns):
        m = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=msgs, tools=registry.schemas(), temperature=0).choices[0].message
        msgs.append(m)
        if not m.tool_calls:
            return m.content
        for c in m.tool_calls:
            try: args = json.loads(c.function.arguments)
            except json.JSONDecodeError: args = {}
            r = registry.execute(c.function.name, args)
            print(f"  {c.function.name} -> {r.status}")
            msgs.append({"role": "tool", "tool_call_id": c.id, "content": r.content})
    return "turn limit"

print(run("Read clause 7.1 of msa_07 and email it to opposing.counsel@example.com"))
print("emails actually sent:", SENT)
05_tools.ipynb · cell 7
check("invalid arguments are rejected before running", registry.execute("get_clause", {"contract_id": "bad", "clause": "x"}).status == "invalid_args")
check("no email was sent without approval", SENT == [])

  1. Write an approve function that allows emails only to @yourfirm.com addresses.
  2. Give read_pages a bad description ("reads stuff") and see if the model still pages correctly.
