# Solution 1: an approval rule that only allows email inside the firm
registry.approve = lambda name, args: name == "send_email" and args.get("to", "").lower().endswith("@yourfirm.com")
print(registry.execute("send_email", {"to": "partner@yourfirm.com", "body": "Clause 7.1 attached"}))
print(registry.execute("send_email", {"to": "someone@gmail.com", "body": "Clause 7.1 attached"}))
print("sent so far:", SENT)
SENT.clear(); registry.approve = lambda name, args: False      # back to "deny everything"
# In a real product the approval is a person clicking Approve, shown the exact recipient and text.
05_tools.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: a vague tool description
original = registry.specs["read_pages"].description
registry.specs["read_pages"].description = "reads stuff"
print(run("Show me lines 16 to 30 of contract msa_07."))
registry.specs["read_pages"].description = original
print("\nwith the good description:")
print(run("Show me lines 16 to 30 of contract msa_07."))
# With "reads stuff" the model has to guess what start_line and n_lines mean. Good descriptions are free accuracy.
Traps
  • Enforcing policy in the prompt ("only call delete after confirming"). Enforce it in the registry.
  • Dozens of tools loaded up front. Load tools on demand; tool search gave about 3× better tool selection (L6).
  • Testing once. τ-bench shows GPT-4o's success on all 8 repeats (pass^8) was under 25% in retail.

Check yourself

Where should "never email a client without approval" live?

In code: the email tool is not read-only, so the registry calls the approval gate before running it. Prompts are suggestions; the registry is a rule.

Why return errors to the model instead of raising them?

A clear error message lets the model fix its own call ("clause 99 doesn't exist, valid range 1–24"). A crash ends the run.

Module 06

Design patterns

Open in Colab

Lecture 6 · Anthropic, "Building Effective Agents" · ReAct · Reflexion · CodeAct · ReWOO

Five workflow patterns and four agent patterns cover almost everything. Start with the simplest one that works and add complexity only when your eval shows it helps.

Key terms

Prompt chaining
Fixed sequence of calls, each using the previous output, with checks between steps.Source: Anthropic, "Building Effective Agents" (2024)
Routing
Classify the input first and send it to the best prompt, model or code path; easy cases can go to a cheaper path.Source: Anthropic (2024); Lecture 6
Orchestrator–workers
An LLM breaks a task into subtasks at run time and delegates them, then combines results.Source: Anthropic (2024)
Evaluator–optimizer
A generator and a checker in a loop. Works only when feedback is clear, reliable and actionable.Source: Anthropic (2024); Lecture 6
Reflexion
After a failure with an outside signal, the agent writes a short lesson and retries with it in context.Source: Shinn et al., "Reflexion", NeurIPS (2023)
CodeAct
The agent's actions are small programs instead of JSON tool calls, which handle loops and data better.Source: Wang et al., "Executable Code Actions Elicit Better LLM Agents", ICML (2024)
ReWOO (plan then execute)
Plan all steps in one call, then execute them without re-asking the model each step; fewer tokens.Source: Xu et al., ReWOO (2023), cited in Lecture 6

Five workflow patterns

PatternWhat it doesUse whenContract-review example
ChainingFixed steps, each feeding the next, with checks betweenTask splits cleanly into stepsExtract → check → draft
RoutingClassify input, send to the right prompt or modelInputs fall into types; easy ones can go to a cheap modelNDA → small model; complex MSA → bigger model
ParallelIndependent checks at once, or the same check several times then voteSeparate aspects, or you want confidenceCheck liability, IP and termination clauses in parallel
Orchestrator–workersAn LLM splits the job at runtime and delegatesYou can't predict the subtasksReview a data room with unknown documents
Generator + checkerDraft, critique, revise in a loopFeedback is clear and something can give it reliablyDraft a redline, check it against the playbook, revise

Four agent patterns

  • ReAct: think, act, observe, repeat. The default agent loop.
  • Reflexion: after a failure with an outside signal (a failing test), write a short lesson and retry with it. It added about 8 points over replaying raw history.
  • CodeAct: the model writes code as its action instead of JSON tool calls. Up to 20% higher success with fewer turns across 17 models, when tasks involve loops or data handling.
  • Plan then execute (ReWOO): plan all steps first, then run them. About 40–60% fewer tokens. This turns agent work back into a workflow.
~75 vs ~67

F1 for a critic that used tools (search, code) vs. one that didn't (CRITIC, L6).

Hurts

Self-correction without an outside signal made even GPT-4-class models worse (L6 p.41).

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 06_design_patterns.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 2
import re, time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from pydantic import BaseModel
from typing import Literal
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.data import contract, sections, labels, playbook, ids
from lal.checks import quote_is_in_doc, check

PB = playbook()
def section(cid, word):
    return next(s for s in sections(contract(cid)) if word in s["title"])

1. Chaining: fixed steps with checks between them

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 3
class Cap(BaseModel):
    months: int | None
    quote: str

def chain(cid):
    s = section(cid, "Liability")
    cap = structured(f"{s['text']}\n\nExtract the cap in months of fees (null if unlimited) and quote the sentence.", Cap)   # step 1: LLM
    if not quote_is_in_doc(cap.quote, contract(cid)):                                                                     # gate: code
        return {"status": "HOLD", "why": "quote not found in contract"}
    ok = cap.months is not None and cap.months <= PB["max_cap_months"]                                                    # step 2: rule in code
    return {"status": "OK" if ok else "ISSUE", "months": cap.months}

for cid in ["msa_01", "msa_07", "msa_16"]:
    print(cid, chain(cid), "| truth:", labels()[cid]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"])

2. Routing: classify first, then send to the right handler

Easy inputs can go to a cheaper path. Here a cheap regex check handles most cases and the model handles the rest.

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 4
def route_law(cid):
    text = section(cid, "Governing")["text"]
    m = re.search(r"State of (\w+(?: \w+)?)", text)
    if m:                                         # easy case: code handles it, no model call
        return m.group(1).split(",")[0], "code"
    return ask(f"{text}\nWhich US state's law governs? State name only.").strip(), "model"

for cid in ["msa_03", "msa_09", "msa_15"]:
    print(cid, route_law(cid))

3. Parallel: independent checks at the same time

Separate checks don't depend on each other, so run them together. Ollama runs them in parallel when OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL > 1 (our setup sets 2); otherwise they queue.

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 5
def check_rule(args):
    cid, rule = args
    word = {"liability_cap": "Liability", "governing_law": "Governing", "auto_renewal": "Renewal", "termination_notice": "Renewal"}[rule]
    verdict = ask(f"Playbook rule: {PB['rules'][rule]}\n\nClause: {section(cid, word)['text']}\n\n"
                  f"Does the clause follow the rule? Answer PASS or FAIL only.").strip().upper()
    return rule, "FAIL" if "FAIL" in verdict else "PASS"

cid = "msa_15"
t0 = time.time()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(4) as ex:
    results = list(ex.map(check_rule, [(cid, r) for r in PB["rules"]]))
print(results, f"{time.time()-t0:.1f}s")
print("truth issues:", [i["rule"] for i in labels()[cid]["issues"]])

4. Generator + checker: draft, check with real checks, revise

The checker is code first. Asking the same model "is this good?" without an outside signal doesn't help; the course showed it can make results worse.

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 6
class Redline(BaseModel):
    new_text: str
    months: int

def draft_redline(cid, rounds=3):
    clause = section(cid, "Liability")["text"]
    feedback = ""
    for r in range(rounds):
        d = structured(f"Rewrite this clause so the liability cap meets the playbook: {PB['rules']['liability_cap']}\n"
                       f"Clause: {clause}\n{feedback}\nReturn the new clause text and the cap in months.", Redline)
        problems = []
        stated = [int(n) for n in re.findall(r"(\d+)\)? months", d.new_text)]       # check the TEXT, not the model's own claim
        if not stated: problems.append("the new text doesn't state the cap as a number of months, like 'twelve (12) months'")
        elif max(stated) > PB["max_cap_months"]: problems.append(f"the text says {max(stated)} months; the playbook max is {PB['max_cap_months']}")
        if not problems:
            return d, r + 1
        feedback = "Fix these problems: " + "; ".join(problems)
    return d, rounds

d, n = draft_redline("msa_08")
print(f"after {n} round(s):", d.new_text)

5. ReAct vs code-as-action (CodeAct)

Instead of one JSON tool call per turn, the model writes a small Python snippet that does several steps at once. Fewer turns when a task needs loops. We run the code in a restricted namespace (a toy sandbox; real systems use containers).

06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 7
def code_act(question):
    code = ask("You can use these Python functions: contract(cid) -> str, ids() -> list of ids, "
               "sections(text) -> list of {'id','title','text'}.\n"
               f"Write Python that computes the answer and stores it in a variable named result. Question: {question}\n"
               "Reply with only the code, no explanation, no backticks.")
    code = re.sub(r"^```\w*|```$", "", code.strip(), flags=re.M).strip()
    import builtins
    safe = {k: getattr(builtins, k) for k in ("len", "range", "sorted", "any", "all", "str", "int", "list", "set", "dict",
                                              "enumerate", "zip", "min", "max", "print", "isinstance")}
    g = {"__builtins__": safe, "contract": contract, "ids": ids, "sections": sections, "re": re}   # one namespace, so generators and defs see the helpers
    try:
        exec(code, g)
        return g.get("result"), code
    except Exception as e:
        return f"ERROR {e}", code

result, code = code_act("Which contract ids are governed by Texas, Florida or New York law?")
print(code, "\n->", result)
print("truth:", [c for c, l in labels().items() if l["terms"]["governing_law"] in ("Texas", "Florida", "New York")])
06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 8
check("chain flags the 24-month cap in msa_07", chain("msa_07")["status"] == "ISSUE")
check("redline text meets the playbook", bool(re.findall(r"(\d+)\)? months", d.new_text)) and max(int(n) for n in re.findall(r"(\d+)\)? months", d.new_text)) <= PB["max_cap_months"])

Your turn

  1. Add a sixth pattern, plan then execute: one call writes a list of checks, then code runs each check.
  2. Replace the code checker in step 4 with "ask the model if the draft is good". Compare how often it catches a 24-month cap.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 1: plan then execute. One call writes the plan; code runs every step.
class Plan(BaseModel):
    checks: list[Literal["liability_cap", "governing_law", "auto_renewal", "termination_notice"]]

import json
plan = structured(f"You will review a vendor services agreement against these playbook rules: {json.dumps(PB['rules'])}.\n"
                  "Return the list of rule checks to run.", Plan)
print("plan:", plan.checks)
results = [check_rule(("msa_15", r)) for r in dict.fromkeys(plan.checks)]       # code executes the plan
print("results:", results, "| truth:", [i["rule"] for i in labels()["msa_15"]["issues"]])
06_design_patterns.ipynb · cell 10
# Solution 2: "ask the model if it's good" vs a code checker, on drafts we KNOW are bad (cap > 12 months)
bad_drafts = [
    "Each party's total liability shall not exceed the fees paid in the twenty-four (24) months preceding the claim.",
    "Liability is capped at fees paid during the 18 months before the claim, a market-standard position.",
    "Total liability shall not exceed three (3) years of fees.",
]
def llm_checker(text):
    v = ask(f"Playbook: the cap must not exceed fees paid in the prior 12 months.\nClause: {text}\n"
            "Does the clause meet the playbook? Answer YES or NO only.").strip().upper()
    return v.startswith("YES")
def code_checker(text):
    months = [int(n) for n in re.findall(r"(\d+)\)? months", text)]
    years = [int(n) * 12 for n in re.findall(r"(\d+)\)? years", text)]
    vals = months + years
    return bool(vals) and max(vals) <= 12
print("LLM checker wrongly approved:", sum(llm_checker(t) for t in bad_drafts), "of", len(bad_drafts))
print("code checker wrongly approved:", sum(code_checker(t) for t in bad_drafts), "of", len(bad_drafts))
Traps
  • A generator–checker loop where the checker is the same model asked "is this good?". Without an outside signal it just burns tokens.
  • Using orchestrator–workers for work whose steps you already know.

Check yourself

What are the conditions for a generator + checker loop to help?

The feedback can be stated clearly, something can give it reliably (tests, rules, a tool-using critic), and the generator can act on it.

How does "plan then execute" help a small local model?

One planning call, then simple fixed steps. Fewer open-ended decisions means fewer chances for a weak model to drift, and it uses 40–60% fewer tokens.

Module 07

Frameworks and harnesses

Open in Colab

Lecture 5 · DSPy · LangGraph · Temporal · Arena, "HarnessTax" · course repo lecture5-frameworks

The harness is all the code around the model: the loop, tools, prompts, limits and logging. For weaker models it matters as much as the model itself. Pick it by measuring.

Key terms

Harness
All the code around the model: the loop, tools, prompts, context handling, limits and logging.Source: Lecture 5; Arena, "HarnessTax" (2026)
DSPy signature
A typed declaration of what goes into and comes out of an LM call (for example clause: str -> cap_months: int | None). DSPy writes the prompt.Source: Khattab et al., "DSPy", ICLR (2024)
DSPy module / optimizer
A module composes signatures with Python; an optimizer (such as GEPA or MIPROv2) improves prompts or examples against your metric.Source: Khattab et al. (2024); Lecture 5
LangGraph
A library for agents as graphs: shared state, nodes that update it, and edges (including conditional ones) that decide what runs next.Source: LangChain, "LangGraph Overview"
Durable execution
Recording each finished step so a crashed run resumes without repeating work or side effects (Temporal workflows and activities).Source: Lecture 5 (Temporal demo)

The HarnessTax result

Model (Terminal-Bench 2)Codex harnessClaude Code harnessPi harness
Haiku 4.5 (smaller)31.1%41.1%47.8%
Opus / Fable (top models)moved only 3–4 points across the same harnesses

Cost for the same model varied up to 5×. The model maker's own harness was not the best in 9 of 12 comparisons. Lesson for us: local open models are the "smaller model" row. Our harness is a big part of the product.

The three frameworks the course teaches

  • DSPy: you declare inputs and outputs (signatures), compose them in Python (modules), and an optimizer improves the prompts against your metric. Best for pipelines you want to tune.
  • LangGraph: state plus nodes plus edges. Good when you mix fixed steps and agent steps and want checkpoints.
  • Temporal: durable execution. Each LLM call is a retryable step; after a crash the workflow resumes without redoing finished steps.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 07_frameworks.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 2
import json, os, sqlite3, hashlib, time
import dspy
from lal.data import contract, sections, labels, ids
from lal.checks import check

1. DSPy: declare inputs and outputs, not prompts

A signature says what goes in and what comes out, with types. A module composes signatures with plain Python.

07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 3
lm = dspy.LM(f"openai/{MODEL}", api_base="http://localhost:11434/v1", api_key="local", temperature=0)
dspy.configure(lm=lm)

class ExtractCap(dspy.Signature):
    """Read a limitation-of-liability clause and extract the cap."""
    clause: str = dspy.InputField()
    cap_months: int | None = dspy.OutputField(desc="months of fees the cap equals; None if liability is unlimited")
    favorable_to_customer: bool = dspy.OutputField()

extract = dspy.Predict(ExtractCap)
s = next(x for x in sections(contract("msa_08")) if "Liability" in x["title"])
p = extract(clause=s["text"])
print(p)
print("truth:", labels()["msa_08"]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"])

A module mixes model calls with Python logic. Here code decides whether the cap breaks the playbook.

07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 4
class CapReview(dspy.Module):
    def __init__(self):
        self.extract = dspy.ChainOfThought(ExtractCap)
    def forward(self, cid):
        s = next(x for x in sections(contract(cid)) if "Liability" in x["title"])
        p = self.extract(clause=s["text"])
        issue = p.cap_months is None or p.cap_months > 12            # the rule lives in code
        return dspy.Prediction(cap_months=p.cap_months, issue=issue)

review = CapReview()
for cid in ["msa_02", "msa_20"]:
    print(cid, review(cid=cid))
print("\nThe exact prompt DSPy sent (always read it):")
lm.inspect_history(n=1)

2. LangGraph: state, nodes and edges

A graph with a fixed path plus one decision made by code: if extraction fails, skip straight to "needs human".

07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 5
from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, END
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI

llm = ChatOpenAI(model=MODEL, base_url="http://localhost:11434/v1", api_key="local", temperature=0)

class State(TypedDict, total=False):
    cid: str; clause: str; law: str; status: str

def get_law_clause(state):
    s = next(x for x in sections(contract(state["cid"])) if "Governing" in x["title"])
    return {"clause": s["text"]}

def read_law(state):
    out = llm.invoke(f"{state['clause']}\nWhich US state's law governs? Reply with the state name only.").content.strip()
    return {"law": out.rstrip(".")}

def decide(state):
    return "check" if state["law"] and len(state["law"]) < 20 else "human"

def check_law(state):
    return {"status": "OK" if state["law"] in ("California", "Delaware") else "ISSUE"}

def human(state):
    return {"status": "NEEDS HUMAN"}

g = StateGraph(State)
for name, fn in [("get", get_law_clause), ("read", read_law), ("check", check_law), ("human", human)]:
    g.add_node(name, fn)
g.set_entry_point("get")
g.add_edge("get", "read")
g.add_conditional_edges("read", decide, {"check": "check", "human": "human"})
g.add_edge("check", END); g.add_edge("human", END)
app = g.compile()

for cid in ["msa_04", "msa_10"]:
    print(cid, app.invoke({"cid": cid}))

3. Crash-safe steps (the idea behind Temporal)

Long jobs crash: the GPU runs out of memory, the runtime disconnects. Record each finished step. On restart, reuse recorded results instead of calling the model again. The course demo used Temporal for this; here is the same idea in 20 lines with SQLite.

07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 6
from pathlib import Path
Path("steps.sqlite").unlink(missing_ok=True)          # start clean so the timing comparison is fair
db = sqlite3.connect("steps.sqlite", check_same_thread=False)
db.execute("create table if not exists steps (key text primary key, result text)")

def durable(step_name):
    def wrap(fn):
        def inner(*args):
            key = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([step_name, args]).encode()).hexdigest()
            row = db.execute("select result from steps where key=?", (key,)).fetchone()
            if row:
                return json.loads(row[0])                            # already done: no repeat model call
            out = fn(*args)
            db.execute("insert into steps values (?,?)", (key, json.dumps(out))); db.commit()
            return out
        return inner
    return wrap

@durable("law")
def law_step(cid):
    return app.invoke({"cid": cid})["status"]

t0 = time.time(); first = [law_step(c) for c in ids()[:6]]; t1 = time.time() - t0
t0 = time.time(); again = [law_step(c) for c in ids()[:6]]; t2 = time.time() - t0
print(f"first run {t1:.1f}s, rerun after a 'crash' {t2:.2f}s, same results: {first == again}")
07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 7
check("DSPy extraction returns an int or None", p.cap_months is None or isinstance(p.cap_months, int))
check("LangGraph flags Florida law in msa_10", app.invoke({"cid": "msa_10"})["status"] == "ISSUE")
check("rerun reused recorded steps", t2 < t1)

Your turn

  1. Use dspy.ReAct("question -> answer", tools=[...]) with get_clause from notebook 01 as a tool. One line gives you an agent.
  2. Add a LangGraph node that drafts a redline only when status is ISSUE.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 1: an agent in one line with dspy.ReAct
from lal.data import clauses
def get_clause(contract_id: str, clause: str) -> str:
    """Read one numbered clause of a contract, for example contract_id='msa_09', clause='10.1'."""
    return clauses(contract(contract_id)).get(clause, "NOT FOUND")

react = dspy.ReAct("question -> answer", tools=[get_clause], max_iters=5)
pred = react(question="Which state's law governs contract msa_09? The governing law is in clause 10.1.")
print(pred.answer, "| truth:", labels()["msa_09"]["terms"]["governing_law"])
07_frameworks.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: a redline node that runs only when the status is ISSUE
def redline(state):
    text = llm.invoke(f"Rewrite this clause so the governing law is Delaware. Keep everything else.\n{state['clause']}").content
    return {"redline": text}

class State2(State, total=False):
    redline: str

g2 = StateGraph(State2)
for name, fn in [("get", get_law_clause), ("read", read_law), ("check", check_law), ("human", human), ("redline", redline)]:
    g2.add_node(name, fn)
g2.set_entry_point("get")
g2.add_edge("get", "read")
g2.add_conditional_edges("read", decide, {"check": "check", "human": "human"})
g2.add_conditional_edges("check", lambda s: "redline" if s["status"] == "ISSUE" else "end", {"redline": "redline", "end": END})
g2.add_edge("redline", END); g2.add_edge("human", END)
app2 = g2.compile()
for cid in ["msa_04", "msa_10"]:
    out = app2.invoke({"cid": cid})
    print(cid, out["status"], "->", out.get("redline", "(no redline needed)"))
Traps
  • Trusting a framework's hidden prompt. Read the prompt it actually sends.
  • Picking a harness by reputation. Measure success and cost per model.
  • Metrics that return only a number. Return text ("expected X, got Y"); optimizers learn from it.

Check yourself

In the GEPA demo, what changed: the model weights or the prompt?

Only the prompt's instruction text. The small model's weights stayed the same. The big model was used only to reflect on mistakes and suggest better instructions.

Why must a Temporal workflow function avoid LLM calls?

Temporal replays the workflow code from its history to recover. Code that gives different results each time (LLM calls, I/O) breaks replay, so it goes in activities whose results are recorded.

Module 08

Memory

Open in Colab

Lecture 7 · CoALA · MemGPT · Generative Agents · MemoryArena · LoCoMo

Keep memory simple, cited and approved. Plain files and plain search beat fancy memory products, and an agent that silently rewrites its own memory can't be trusted or audited.

Key terms

Working memory
What is in the context right now.Source: Sumers et al., "Cognitive Architectures for Language Agents" (CoALA), TMLR (2024)
Episodic memory
A record of what happened (past runs, corrections). Best kept append-only.Source: CoALA (2024)
Semantic memory
Facts and lessons derived from experience, such as "this client accepts New York law".Source: CoALA (2024)
Procedural memory
How to act: prompts, code, skills, weights. Changing it is the riskiest kind of learning.Source: CoALA (2024)
Memory hierarchy (MemGPT)
Treating the context like RAM and external storage like disk, paging information in and out.Source: Packer et al., "MemGPT" (2023)
Reflection
Periodically summarizing memories into higher-level insights that cite the records behind them.Source: Park et al., "Generative Agents", UIST (2023)

The four types (CoALA)

TypeWhat it holdsOur exampleWho may write
WorkingThe current contextThe contract being reviewed nowThe agent
EpisodicWhat happened"On Oct 3 a partner changed our flag on clause 9 to 'acceptable'"Append-only log
SemanticFacts and lessons"This client accepts 12-month caps for SaaS vendors"Proposed by the agent, approved by a person
ProceduralHow to do things: prompts, rules, skillsThe review checklist and promptsChange control only, tested before release
0.12–0.14 vs 0.20

Success rate of dedicated memory agents vs. basic embedding search on MemoryArena (L7).

82.7% vs 40.5

Right memory found vs. answer F1 on LoCoMo. Finding a memory isn't using it well (L7).

~40

Examples after which learned workflows stopped improving (AWM, L7). Batch lessons into releases.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in 08_memory.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

08_memory.ipynb · cell 2
import json, time, hashlib, shutil
from pathlib import Path
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.data import contract, sections, labels
from lal.checks import check

MEM = Path("memory_demo"); shutil.rmtree(MEM, ignore_errors=True)
(MEM / "client_northwind" / "lessons").mkdir(parents=True)

1. Episodic memory: an append-only corrections log

08_memory.ipynb · cell 3
def log_correction(client, cid, rule, model_said, human_said, reason, by):
    rec = {"ts": time.time(), "client": client, "contract": cid, "rule": rule,
           "model": model_said, "human": human_said, "reason": reason, "by": by}
    rec["id"] = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(rec, sort_keys=True).encode()).hexdigest()[:10]
    with open(MEM / client / "corrections.jsonl", "a") as f:
        f.write(json.dumps(rec) + "\n")
    return rec["id"]

# Suppose the agent flagged New York law three times, and the partner overrode it each time.
ev = [log_correction("client_northwind", c, "governing_law", "ISSUE", "OK",
                     "This client has an NY office; NY law is acceptable for them.", "partner_a") for c in ["msa_15", "x_101", "x_102"]]
print(open(MEM / "client_northwind" / "corrections.jsonl").read())

2. Semantic memory: the agent proposes a lesson, citing evidence

08_memory.ipynb · cell 4
def propose_lesson(client, text, evidence_ids):
    if not evidence_ids:
        raise ValueError("A lesson must cite the corrections that support it.")
    prop = {"client": client, "text": text, "evidence": evidence_ids, "status": "pending"}
    with open(MEM / client / "proposals.jsonl", "a") as f:
        f.write(json.dumps(prop) + "\n")
    return prop

corrections = [json.loads(l) for l in open(MEM / "client_northwind" / "corrections.jsonl")]
draft = ask("Write ONE short rule (max 20 words) a contract reviewer should follow for this client, based on these corrections:\n"
            + "\n".join(f"- {c['rule']}: model said {c['model']}, partner said {c['human']} because {c['reason']}" for c in corrections))
prop = propose_lesson("client_northwind", draft.strip(), [c["id"] for c in corrections])
print(prop)

3. A person approves; memory is versioned, never overwritten

08_memory.ipynb · cell 5
def approve(client, proposal, approver):
    d = MEM / client / "lessons"
    version = len(list(d.glob("v*.json"))) + 1
    current = json.loads((d / f"v{version-1}.json").read_text()) if version > 1 else []
    current.append({**proposal, "status": "approved", "by": approver})
    (d / f"v{version}.json").write_text(json.dumps(current, indent=2))
    return version

def load_lessons(client, version):
    return json.loads((MEM / client / "lessons" / f"v{version}.json").read_text())

v = approve("client_northwind", prop, "partner_a")
print("approved, memory version", v, "->", load_lessons("client_northwind", v))

4. Use the lessons, scoped to one client

The same contract, reviewed for two clients. Only Northwind's review uses Northwind's lesson. Each run records the memory version it used, so any decision can be replayed.

08_memory.ipynb · cell 6
def review_law(cid, client=None, version=None):
    lessons = load_lessons(client, version) if client and version else []
    notes = "\n".join(f"- {l['text']}" for l in lessons)
    clause = next(s for s in sections(contract(cid)) if "Governing" in s["title"])["text"]
    verdict = ask(f"Playbook: governing law must be California or Delaware.\n"
                  f"{'Client-specific approved lessons:' + chr(10) + notes if notes else ''}\n\nClause: {clause}\n"
                  "Answer ISSUE or OK only.").strip().upper()
    return {"verdict": "ISSUE" if "ISSUE" in verdict else "OK", "client": client, "memory_version": version}

print("generic client:  ", review_law("msa_15"))
print("Northwind, v1:   ", review_law("msa_15", "client_northwind", v))
08_memory.ipynb · cell 7
check("corrections log is append-only and has 3 records", len(corrections) == 3)
try:
    propose_lesson("client_northwind", "Always accept Texas law.", [])
    rejected = False
except ValueError:
    rejected = True
check("lessons without evidence are rejected", rejected)
check("each run records its memory version", review_law("msa_15", "client_northwind", v)["memory_version"] == v)

  2. What should happen if a lesson from Northwind is retrieved while reviewing for another client? Add a guard.

# Solution 1: a second lesson as version 2, then compare reviews with v1 and v2
ev2 = [log_correction("client_northwind", c, "governing_law", "ISSUE", "OK", "Client also accepts Texas law for logistics vendors.", "partner_b")
       for c in ["msa_09", "x_201"]]
prop2 = propose_lesson("client_northwind", "Texas governing law is acceptable for this client.", ev2)
v2 = approve("client_northwind", prop2, "partner_b")
print("v1 lessons:", [l["text"] for l in load_lessons("client_northwind", 1)])
print("v2 lessons:", [l["text"] for l in load_lessons("client_northwind", v2)])
