check("no email was sent despite the injection", SENT == [])

Stretch goals

  1. Add compaction by summary: when masking isn't enough, summarize the middle with a second, cheaper model.
  2. Add a disk cache keyed by a hash of the messages, so reruns cost nothing.
  3. Add steering: a pending_message() hook checked before every model call.
  4. Run the live part 5 times. How often does it end with done? That's pass^5 for your harness.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
P1_build_your_own_harness.ipynb · cell 11
# Stretch 1: compaction by summary. When masking isn't enough, summarize the middle with a second model.
from lal.llm import ask
class SummarizingAgent(S.Agent):
    def context(self):
        ctx = super().context()                                   # already masks old tool results
        if sum(len(m["content"]) for m in ctx) > self.cfg.compact_at_chars:
            head, mid, tail = ctx[:2], ctx[2:-self.cfg.keep_recent], ctx[-self.cfg.keep_recent:]
            summary = ask("Summarize this agent history in at most 6 bullets. Keep document names, clause numbers and numbers exact:\n\n"
                          + "\n".join(f"{m['role']}: {m['content'][:400]}" for m in mid))
            self.log(type="compaction", content=summary)
            ctx = head + [{"role": "user", "content": "Summary of earlier steps:\n" + summary}] + tail
        return ctx                                                 # the system prompt (head[0]) is never summarized

a = SummarizingAgent(local_lm, registry, Config(compact_at_chars=3000))
print(a.send("Read msa_15 page by page and list every number you find in it.").text[:400])
print("compactions:", sum(e["type"] == "compaction" for e in a.transcript))
P1_build_your_own_harness.ipynb · cell 12
# Stretch 2: a disk cache keyed by a hash of the messages, so reruns cost nothing
import hashlib, os
os.makedirs(".lm_cache", exist_ok=True)
def cached(lm):
    def inner(messages):
        key = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(messages, sort_keys=True).encode()).hexdigest()
        path = f".lm_cache/{key}.txt"
        if os.path.exists(path):
            return open(path).read()
        out = lm(messages)
        open(path, "w").write(out)
        return out
    return inner

import time
cached_lm = cached(local_lm)
for run in (1, 2):
    t0 = time.time(); S.Agent(cached_lm, registry).send("Which contract has the governing law of Texas?")
    print(f"run {run}: {time.time()-t0:.1f}s")
P1_build_your_own_harness.ipynb · cell 13
# Stretch 3: steering. A message queued by a person is added before the next model call.
class SteerableAgent(S.Agent):
    def __init__(self, *a, **kw):
        super().__init__(*a, **kw); self.queue = []
    def pending_message(self):
        return self.queue.pop(0) if self.queue else None
    def context(self):
        msg = self.pending_message()                              # polled once before every model call
        if msg:
            self.messages.append({"role": "user", "content": f"[steering from the user] {msg}"})
            self.log(type="steer", content=msg)
        return super().context()

s = SteerableAgent(local_lm, registry)
s.queue.append("Only look at msa_16.")
print(s.send("Find a contract with unlimited liability.").text)
P1_build_your_own_harness.ipynb · cell 14
# Stretch 4: pass^5 for the harness on one task
from lal.checks import pass_hat_k, pass_at_k
done = 0
for i in range(5):
    r = S.Agent(lambda m: chat(m, temperature=0.5).content or "", registry).send("Which contracts are governed by Florida law? Answer with ids.")
    ok = r.stop_reason == "done" and "msa_10" in r.text
    done += ok
    print(f"try {i+1}: {r.stop_reason}, correct={ok}")
print(f"pass@5 {pass_at_k(5, done, 5):.0%}  pass^5 {pass_hat_k(5, done, 5):.0%}")
Project 2

Build an eval task, then test agents on it

Open in Colab

Homework-style project built on the skills of CS329Z Homework 2 and the Terminal-Bench lecture · not the course's assignment

A benchmark task has four parts: an instruction, a starting environment, a hidden verifier that writes a reward, and a reference solution. You prove the task itself is sound, then run an agent on it several times and report pass@k and pass^k.

Key terms

Verifier
Hidden code that runs after the agent and writes a reward from 0 to 1. Checks the final state, not the steps.Source: Merrill et al., "Terminal-Bench", ICLR (2026)
Oracle solution
A reference solution that must score 1.0 through the same verifier.Source: Terminal-Bench / Harbor task format
Shortcut / reward hacking
A way to score well without doing the task (flag everything, edit the tests). A sound task makes shortcuts fail.Source: Zhu et al. (2025); Lecture 10
Task soundness checks
Oracle passes, do-nothing fails, an alternative correct solution passes, and known shortcuts fail.Source: Zhu et al. (2025); CS329Z Homework 2 design

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 2
import json, shutil, subprocess, sys, tempfile
from pathlib import Path
from lal.checks import pass_at_k, pass_hat_k, check

TASK = Path("projects/p2_tasks/contract-flags")
print(TASK.joinpath("instruction.md").read_text())
print("environment:", sorted(p.name for p in (TASK / "environment").iterdir()))

1. The runner: fresh workspace → attempt → hidden verifier → reward

P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 3
def run_attempt(task: Path, attempt_fn) -> float:
    ws = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="attempt_"))
    shutil.copytree(task / "environment", ws, dirs_exist_ok=True)      # fresh copy every time; tests are NOT copied
    try:
        attempt_fn(ws, (task / "instruction.md").read_text())
    except Exception as e:
        print("attempt crashed:", type(e).__name__, e)
    # The reward file lives OUTSIDE the agent's workspace, so the agent can't write its own score.
    reward_file = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="reward_")) / "reward"
    out = subprocess.run([sys.executable, str(task / "tests" / "test.py"), str(ws), str(reward_file)],
                         capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=120)
    print("  verifier:", out.stdout.strip() or out.stderr.strip()[-200:])
    reward = float(reward_file.read_text()) if out.returncode == 0 and reward_file.exists() else 0.0   # a crashed verifier scores 0
    shutil.rmtree(ws, ignore_errors=True)
    return reward

2. Test the task before testing any agent

A broken task gives meaningless scores. These four checks come straight from the course's benchmark checklist.

P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 4
def oracle(ws, instruction):
    subprocess.run([sys.executable, str(TASK / "solution" / "solve.py"), str(ws)], check=True)

def do_nothing(ws, instruction):
    pass

def alternative(ws, instruction):
    """A different correct approach: parse numbers with a different method. Must also pass."""
    import re
    pb = json.loads((ws / "playbook.json").read_text())
    rep = {}
    for f in ws.glob("msa_*.txt"):
        t = f.read_text().lower(); out = []
        nums = lambda pat: [int(x) for x in re.findall(pat, t)]
        cap = nums(r"\((\d+)\) months preceding") or nums(r"in the (\d+) months preceding")
        if not cap or cap[0] > 12: out.append("liability_cap")
        if not any(f"state of {s.lower()}" in t for s in pb["allowed_law"]): out.append("governing_law")
        if nums(r"successive \w+ \((\d+)\) year")[0] > 1: out.append("auto_renewal")
        d = nums(r"at least \w+ \((\d+)\) days") or nums(r"at least (\d+) days")
        if d[0] < 30: out.append("termination_notice")
        rep[f.stem] = sorted(out)
    (ws / "report.json").write_text(json.dumps(rep))

def shortcut(ws, instruction):
    """Gaming attempt: flag every rule for every contract."""
    pb = json.loads((ws / "playbook.json").read_text())
    (ws / "report.json").write_text(json.dumps({f.stem: sorted(pb["rules"]) for f in ws.glob("msa_*.txt")}))

scores = {name: run_attempt(TASK, fn) for name, fn in
          [("oracle", oracle), ("do nothing", do_nothing), ("alternative correct", alternative), ("flag everything", shortcut)]}
print(scores)
P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 5
check("oracle scores 1.0", scores["oracle"] == 1.0)
check("doing nothing scores 0", scores["do nothing"] == 0.0)
check("a different correct solution also scores 1.0", scores["alternative correct"] == 1.0)
check("the flag-everything shortcut scores low", scores["flag everything"] < 0.5)

3. Run our local agent on the task, 5 times

The agent uses the harness from Project 1 with three tools limited to its workspace: list files, read a file, write a file. It never sees the tests.

P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 6
from lal import harness_solution as S
from lal.harness_solution import ToolSpec, Config
# lal.llm = the OpenAI client pointed at the local Ollama server, http://localhost:11434/v1 (see notebook 00)
from lal.llm import chat

def agent_attempt(ws: Path, instruction: str, temperature=0.3):
    def safe(name):                                   # keep the agent inside its workspace
        p = (ws / name).resolve()
        if ws.resolve() not in p.parents and p != ws.resolve():
            raise PermissionError("outside workspace")
        return p
    tools = S.Registry([
        ToolSpec("list_files", "List files in the workspace.", {"type": "object", "properties": {}}, lambda: "\n".join(sorted(x.name for x in ws.iterdir()))),
        ToolSpec("read_file", "Read a text file from the workspace.", {"type": "object", "properties": {"name": {"type": "string"}}, "required": ["name"]},
                 lambda name: safe(name).read_text()),
        ToolSpec("write_file", "Write a text file in the workspace.", {"type": "object", "properties": {"name": {"type": "string"}, "content": {"type": "string"}},
                 "required": ["name", "content"]}, lambda name, content: (safe(name).write_text(content), "written")[1], read_only=False),
    ], approve=lambda n, a: True, max_chars=4000)
    lm = lambda messages: chat(messages, temperature=temperature).content or ""
    agent = S.Agent(lm, tools, Config(max_turns=16, token_budget=40_000, compact_at_chars=30_000))
    r = agent.send(instruction)
    print(f"  agent stopped: {r.stop_reason} after {r.turns} turns")

K = 5
rewards = []
for i in range(K):
    print(f"attempt {i+1}")
    rewards.append(run_attempt(TASK, agent_attempt))

4. Report it like a benchmark

  • Mean reward: partial credit across contracts.
  • pass@k: at least one of k attempts fully solved it.
  • pass^k: every attempt fully solved it. For repeated business work, this is the number that matters.
P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 7
solved = sum(r == 1.0 for r in rewards)
print(f"rewards: {rewards}")
print(f"mean reward {sum(rewards)/K:.2f} | pass@1 {pass_at_k(K, solved, 1):.0%} | pass@{K} {pass_at_k(K, solved, K):.0%} | "
      f"pass^2 {pass_hat_k(K, solved, 2):.0%} | pass^{K} {pass_hat_k(K, solved, K):.0%}")
P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 8
check("agent ran 5 attempts through the hidden verifier", len(rewards) == K)

5. Your turn: build your own task

Make a folder projects/p2_tasks/my-task/ with the same four parts. Ideas: - NDA check: is the confidentiality period at least 3 years? Is there a non-solicit clause? - Invoice vs. contract: do the monthly fees on 3 invoices match clause 2.1? - Redline: rewrite clause 7.1 so the cap is 12 months; the verifier checks the new text with code.

Then prove your task is sound with the same four checks (oracle 1.0, do-nothing 0, alternative correct solution 1.0, shortcut low), and run the agent 5 times.

Writing tips from the Terminal-Bench lecture: keep the instruction short and complete; each test should match one requirement in the instruction; test the final result, not the steps; keep the tests where the agent can't read or edit them.

Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)

The agent gets one contract and three invoices. It must list the invoices whose monthly amount doesn't match the fee in clause 2.1. We build all four parts, prove the task is sound, then run the agent.

P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 9
from lal.data import contract, labels
NEW = Path("projects/p2_tasks/fee-check")
shutil.rmtree(NEW, ignore_errors=True)
for sub in ("environment", "tests", "solution"):
    (NEW / sub).mkdir(parents=True)

fee = labels()["msa_07"]["terms"]["monthly_fee"]                       # $4,500 in our data
(NEW / "environment" / "contract.txt").write_text(contract("msa_07"))
invoices = {"invoice_jan.txt": fee, "invoice_feb.txt": fee + 500, "invoice_mar.txt": fee}   # February is wrong
for name, amount in invoices.items():
    (NEW / "environment" / name).write_text(f"INVOICE\nVendor: Orbit Software LLC\nPeriod: {name[8:11].title()} 2027\n"
                                            f"Services per Master Services Agreement\nAmount due: ${amount:,}\n")
(NEW / "instruction.md").write_text(
    "Compare each invoice_*.txt in this folder with the monthly fee in clause 2.1 of contract.txt.\n"
    "Write mismatches.json: a JSON list of the invoice file names whose 'Amount due' differs from the monthly fee. "
    "Use an empty list if all match.\n")
(NEW / "tests" / "expected.json").write_text(json.dumps(sorted(n for n, a in invoices.items() if a != fee)))
(NEW / "tests" / "test.py").write_text('''import json, sys
from pathlib import Path
ws, out = Path(sys.argv[1]), Path(sys.argv[2])
want = json.loads((Path(__file__).parent / "expected.json").read_text())
p = ws / "mismatches.json"
try:
    got = json.loads(p.read_text()) if p.exists() else None
except json.JSONDecodeError:
    got = None
ok = isinstance(got, list) and sorted(got) == want
out.write_text("1.0" if ok else "0.0")
print("reward=" + ("1.0" if ok else "0.0"), "got", got)
''')
(NEW / "solution" / "solve.py").write_text('''import json, re, sys
from pathlib import Path
ws = Path(sys.argv[1])
fee = int(re.search(r"\\$([\\d,]+) per month", (ws / "contract.txt").read_text()).group(1).replace(",", ""))
bad = [f.name for f in sorted(ws.glob("invoice_*.txt"))
       if int(re.search(r"Amount due: \\$([\\d,]+)", f.read_text()).group(1).replace(",", "")) != fee]
(ws / "mismatches.json").write_text(json.dumps(bad))
''')
print(NEW.joinpath("instruction.md").read_text())
P2_build_an_eval_task.ipynb · cell 10
def oracle2(ws, instruction):
    subprocess.run([sys.executable, str(NEW / "solution" / "solve.py"), str(ws)], check=True)
def nothing2(ws, instruction):
    pass
def alternative2(ws, instruction):
    import re
    fee_line = next(l for l in (ws / "contract.txt").read_text().splitlines() if l.startswith("2.1"))
    fee_val = int("".join(ch for ch in fee_line.split("$")[1].split(" ")[0] if ch.isdigit()))
    bad = [f.name for f in ws.glob("invoice_*.txt") if int("".join(ch for ch in f.read_text().split("$")[-1] if ch.isdigit())) != fee_val]
    (ws / "mismatches.json").write_text(json.dumps(bad))
def shortcut2(ws, instruction):                          # gaming attempt: list every invoice
    (ws / "mismatches.json").write_text(json.dumps([f.name for f in ws.glob("invoice_*.txt")]))

s2 = {n: run_attempt(NEW, f) for n, f in [("oracle", oracle2), ("do nothing", nothing2), ("alternative", alternative2), ("list all", shortcut2)]}
print(s2)
check("fee-check: oracle 1.0", s2["oracle"] == 1.0)
check("fee-check: do nothing 0", s2["do nothing"] == 0.0)
check("fee-check: alternative correct solution 1.0", s2["alternative"] == 1.0)
check("fee-check: listing everything fails", s2["list all"] == 0.0)

rewards2 = [run_attempt(NEW, agent_attempt) for _ in range(3)]
solved2 = sum(r == 1.0 for r in rewards2)
print(f"rewards {rewards2} | pass@3 {pass_at_k(3, solved2, 3):.0%} | pass^3 {pass_hat_k(3, solved2, 3):.0%}")

Part 2

Self-improving AI agents

Everything from Stanford CS329A "Self-Improving AI Agents" (Autumn 2025, Azalia Mirhoseini and Aakanksha Chowdhery): every assigned paper summarized, a runnable Colab notebook per lecture, and small rebuilds of the past student projects. Course site: cs329a.stanford.edu.

Part 2 · Start here

What this means for building agents

The big idea of CS329A: an agent gets better only as fast as you can check its work automatically. Almost every paper below is a way to use a checker: to pick the best answer, to give feedback, to search, or to train.

  1. More tries plus a good checker can beat a bigger model. The chance that at least one of many samples is right keeps rising as you sample more, roughly following a power law. The hard part is picking the right answer: with a checker you capture the gain, while majority vote flattens early (Brown et al., Large Language Monkeys; module A01).
  2. Weak checkers, combined, make a strong one. Several imperfect checks, such as a model judge, answer agreement and simple rules, can be weighted into one reliable verifier (Weaver; module A02). Contract review needs exactly this: quote checks + rule checks + a judge.
  3. Feedback from running code is the best teacher. Train or loop on real test results, and pick among many attempts by running tests (RLEF, CodeMonkeys; modules A03 and A08).
  4. Searching for better designs beats hand-designing them, but only with an automatic score. AlphaEvolve, ADAS and the AI Scientist all propose, score automatically, keep the best and repeat. If the score has a loophole, the search will find it (module A06 shows a cheat winning once a validity check is removed).
  5. Reinforcement learning on checkable rewards is now cheap enough to use. GRPO and DAPO's fixes make it practical on small models; module A05 trains a 360M model on a free Colab GPU.
  6. Measure how long a task an agent can finish, not just whether it's right. METR's "time horizon" is the length of task an AI can complete half the time, and it has been roughly doubling every seven months. GDPval grades AI work on real paid tasks against expert work (module A10).
For Local AI Lab: the moat isn't the model. It's the checkers and tests we build for each customer's repeated task. Once a task has an automatic score, every technique in this part (sample and select, combined verifiers, search, RL on small models) can keep improving a local model on that task, cheaply. The pitch: we turn your repeated work into a scored task, and the model on your own computers keeps getting better at it.
Part 2 · A01

Test-time compute scaling

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 2 (Sep 26)

You can buy accuracy at answer time instead of training time: let the model try many times, or revise its own work, and keep a good answer. The gain is only real if something can tell which try is right, and the best way to spend the compute depends on how hard the question is.

Key terms

Test-time compute
Extra computation spent when answering (more samples, revisions, search) instead of when training.Source: Snell et al. 2024
Coverage (pass@k)
Share of problems where at least one of k tries is right. An upper bound on what any picker can reach.Source: Brown et al. 2024
Repeated sampling
Asking the same question many times at a temperature above 0 so the tries differ.Source: Brown et al. 2024
Majority vote (self-consistency)
Keep the answer that the most tries agree on. Needs no checker, but plateaus.Source: Brown et al. 2024
Fuser / Ranker
A model call that merges several candidates into one answer / that orders candidates and keeps the best.Source: Saad-Falcon et al. 2024 (Archon)
Sequential vs parallel
Sequential: revise one answer step after step. Parallel: many independent tries, then pick.Source: Snell et al. 2024
Compute-optimal strategy
Choosing how to spend test-time compute per question, based on its predicted difficulty.Source: Snell et al. 2024

The papers

Large Language Monkeys: Scaling Inference Compute with Repeated Sampling

Brown, Juravsky, Ehrlich, Clark, Le, Ré, Mirhoseini · 2024

What
Sampled the same model up to 10,000 times per problem on GSM8K, MATH, MiniF2F, CodeContests and SWE-bench Lite. Measured coverage (share of problems where any sample is right) and fit it with an exponentiated power law in the number of samples.
Result
Coverage grew with samples over four orders of magnitude. SWE-bench Lite with DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Instruct: 15.9% with one sample to 56% with 250 (single-sample state of the art was 43%). 5 DeepSeek tries solved 29.62% for $10.8 vs 24.00% for $39 with one GPT-4o try. On MATH with Llama-3-8B-Instruct, coverage went from 82.9% (100 samples) to 98.44% (10,000), but majority vote and reward models plateaued around 100 samples (best gain only 40.50% to 41.41%).
Takeaway
If your task has a cheap automatic checker (tests, a solver, a schema plus business rules), many cheap tries from a small local model can beat one expensive call. Without a checker, invest in verification before you invest in more samples.

Archon: An Architecture Search Framework for Inference-Time Techniques

Saad-Falcon, Gamarra Lafuente, Natarajan, Maru, Todorov, Guha, Buchanan, Chen, Guha, Ré, Mirhoseini · 2024

What
Treats inference-time techniques as modules (generator/ensemble, fuser, ranker, critic, verifier, unit-test generator and evaluator) stacked in layers, and uses Bayesian optimization to search a space of 9,576 configurations for the best stack under a compute budget.
Result
Searched stacks beat o1, GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Sonnet by 15.1% on average across instruction-following, reasoning and coding benchmarks; open-source-only stacks were 11.2% above prior open-source approaches. Bayesian optimization found the best stack in 96.0% of searches with 88.5% fewer evaluations than greedy search. Unit tests plus more samples raised CodeContests Pass@1 from 17.9% to 29.3%. The fuser helped on every benchmark (8.9% on average); verifiers helped most on reasoning tasks.
Takeaway
Don't hand-pick one trick. Build sampling, ranking, fusing and checking as swappable steps, then let a small eval set choose the stack per task.

Scaling LLM Test-Time Compute Optimally can be More Effective than Scaling Model Parameters

Snell, Lee, Xu, Kumar · 2024

What
On MATH with PaLM 2-S*, compared two ways to spend test-time compute: searching against a process reward model (best-of-N, beam search, lookahead) and a fine-tuned model that revises its own answer (sequential vs parallel). Questions were binned into 5 difficulty levels by the base model's own success rate over 2048 samples.
Result
The best method depends on difficulty: sequential revision did best on easy questions, a mix of sequential and parallel on harder ones; beam search beat best-of-N on medium-hard questions but over-optimized the verifier on easy ones. Choosing per difficulty matched or beat best-of-N with up to 4x less compute (abstract: more than 4x). FLOPs-matched, a small model with test-time compute beat a 14x larger model on questions where it had some success, but pretraining was better on the hardest questions or with high inference load.
Takeaway
Estimate difficulty first (for example, by how much a few samples agree), then route: easy questions get one try plus a revision, hard ones get more parallel tries, and the hardest may need a bigger model.

How Do Large Language Monkeys Get Their Power (Laws)?

Schaeffer, Kazdan, Hughes, Juravsky, Price, Lynch, Jones, Kirk, Mirhoseini, Koyejo · 2025

What
Explains why aggregate success with k attempts follows a power law even though each single problem's failure rate falls exponentially in k: the power law comes from a heavy left tail of very hard problems in the distribution of single-attempt success rates. Tested on Pythia models on MATH and frontier models on HarmBench jailbreaking.
Result
Proved the tail near zero (density like p^(b-1)) is necessary and sufficient for a k^(-b) power law. Cases without the heavy tail (e.g. Llama 3 8B IT, jailbroken on every prompt within budget) did not show a power law. A distributional estimator of the exponent had about an order of magnitude lower relative error than curve fitting, which the authors equate to roughly 2-4 orders of magnitude less inference compute.
Takeaway
Before buying a big sampling budget, sample a little, look at the per-problem success rates, and forecast. A task with many near-zero problems will be expensive to brute-force.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a01_test_time_compute.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 2
import math, time
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from lal.llm import client
from lal.tasks import math_problems, extract_int
from lal.checks import pass_at_k, check

SOLVE = ("Solve this problem step by step. Keep it short.\n"
         "On the last line write exactly: ANSWER: <whole number>\n\nProblem: {q}")

def gen(prompt, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=400, model_name=None):
    """One model call. Returns (text, completion tokens). Never raises, so one bad call can't stop a loop."""
    try:
        r = client.chat.completions.create(model=model_name or MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": prompt}],
                                           temperature=temperature, max_tokens=max_tokens)
        text = r.choices[0].message.content or ""
        toks = getattr(r.usage, "completion_tokens", None) or max(1, len(text) // 4)
    except Exception as e:
        print("call failed:", e)
        text, toks = "", 0
    return text, toks

def majority(answers):
    """Most common answer, ignoring replies we could not parse. None if there are none."""
    votes = Counter(a for a in answers if a is not None)
    return votes.most_common(1)[0][0] if votes else None

1. A task set we can grade with code

Test-time scaling only pays off when you can tell right from wrong. So we use word problems with exact integer answers, made by lal.tasks.math_problems with a fixed seed. "Easy" takes 2 steps. "Hard" takes 3 to 5 steps (percentages, rounding up, a little algebra).

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 3
EASY = math_problems(4, seed=0, difficulty="easy")
HARD = math_problems(4, seed=0, difficulty="hard")
PROBS = EASY + HARD
BY_ID = {p["id"]: p for p in PROBS}
for p in PROBS[:1] + PROBS[-1:]:
    print(p["id"], "|", p["question"], "| answer:", p["answer"])
print("extract_int test:", extract_int("...so 12 x 5 = 60 apples.\nANSWER: 60"))

2. Repeated sampling: K tries per problem

We ask the same question K = 6 times at temperature 0.8, so the tries differ. That is 8 problems x 6 = 48 calls, two at a time.

Brown et al. 2024 (Large Language Monkeys) did this with up to 10,000 samples per problem. Their key word is coverage: the share of problems where at least one sample is right.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 4
K = 6
jobs = [p for p in PROBS for _ in range(K)]
t0 = time.time()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    outs = list(ex.map(lambda p: gen(SOLVE.format(q=p["question"])), jobs))
print(f"{len(jobs)} calls in {time.time() - t0:.0f}s")

SAMPLES = {p["id"]: [] for p in PROBS}
for p, (text, toks) in zip(jobs, outs):
    a = extract_int(text)
    SAMPLES[p["id"]].append({"text": text, "answer": a, "ok": a == p["answer"], "tokens": toks})

for pid, ss in SAMPLES.items():
    print(f"{pid}: right {sum(s['ok'] for s in ss)}/{K}   answers {[s['answer'] for s in ss]}   truth {BY_ID[pid]['answer']}")

3. Coverage (pass@k) grows with k

pass_at_k(n, c, k) is the unbiased estimate of "at least one of k tries is right", given c right out of n. We plot it with a log scale on k. Brown et al. found coverage often rises in a straight line against log(k), over four orders of magnitude.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 5
def coverage(probs, k):
    return sum(pass_at_k(K, sum(s["ok"] for s in SAMPLES[p["id"]]), k) for p in probs) / len(probs)

ks = list(range(1, K + 1))
cov = {name: [coverage(ps, k) for k in ks] for name, ps in [("all", PROBS), ("easy", EASY), ("hard", HARD)]}
for name, ys in cov.items():
    print(f"{name:5}", "  ".join(f"k={k}:{y:.0%}" for k, y in zip(ks, ys)))

plt.figure(figsize=(5, 3.2))
for name, ys in cov.items():
    plt.plot(ks, ys, marker="o", label=name)
plt.xscale("log"); plt.xticks(ks, [str(k) for k in ks]); plt.ylim(0, 1.05)
plt.xlabel("k (tries per problem, log scale)"); plt.ylabel("coverage (pass@k)"); plt.title("More tries, more problems solved")
plt.legend(); plt.grid(alpha=0.3); plt.show()

4. Coverage is an upper bound. Picking is the hard part.

Coverage assumes an oracle verifier: a checker that always knows which try is right. Here we have one (the exact answer), but real work often doesn't.

Without it, the simplest picker is majority vote (also called self-consistency): keep the most common answer. Brown et al. report that on MATH with Llama-3-8B-Instruct, coverage passed 95% while majority vote and reward models stalled near 40-41% after about 100 samples.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 6
def majority_acc(probs, k):
    return sum(majority([s["answer"] for s in SAMPLES[p["id"]][:k]]) == p["answer"] for p in probs) / len(probs)

maj = [majority_acc(PROBS, k) for k in ks]
print(" k   oracle (coverage)   majority vote   gap")
for k, c, m in zip(ks, cov["all"], maj):
    print(f"{k:2}   {c:>14.0%}   {m:>13.0%}   {c - m:+.0%}")

How to read this: the gap between the two columns is the generation-verification gap. The model could give the right answer, but our picker can't find it. Module a02 is about closing that gap.

5. What it costs: tokens vs accuracy

Every try costs tokens. Plot accuracy against total tokens spent, so you can compare one big call with many small ones on the same axis.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 7
avg_toks = sum(s["tokens"] for ss in SAMPLES.values() for s in ss) / (len(PROBS) * K)
spent = [k * avg_toks * len(PROBS) for k in ks]
print(f"average tokens per try: {avg_toks:.0f}")
for k, t, c, m in zip(ks, spent, cov["all"], maj):
    print(f"k={k}: {t:6.0f} tokens for the set   oracle {c:.0%}   majority {m:.0%}")

plt.figure(figsize=(5, 3.2))
plt.plot(spent, cov["all"], marker="o", label="oracle verifier (coverage)")
plt.plot(spent, maj, marker="s", label="majority vote")
plt.xlabel("output tokens spent on the 8 problems"); plt.ylabel("share solved"); plt.ylim(0, 1.05)
plt.legend(); plt.grid(alpha=0.3); plt.title("Accuracy per token"); plt.show()

Brown et al. made the same point with money: on SWE-bench Lite, 5 tries of DeepSeek-Coder-V2-Instruct solved 29.62% for $10.8, while 1 try of GPT-4o solved 24.00% for $39. Cheap model plus many tries plus a checker can beat an expensive model.

6. Why the curve looks like a power law (no model calls)

Schaeffer et al. 2025 (How Do Large Language Monkeys Get Their Power (Laws)?) explain the shape. - For one problem with single-try success p, the chance that all k tries fail is (1 - p)^k. That falls exponentially in k. - Averaged over many problems, the failure curve looks like a power law in k (a straight line on a log-log plot). That happens when a few problems are very hard (p close to 0). This is called a heavy left tail.

We check this with exact math. Say single-try success rates follow a Beta(alpha, beta) distribution, which has a tail near 0 shaped like p^(alpha-1). Then the theory says -log(coverage) should fall like k^(-alpha).

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 8
ALPHA, BETA = 0.3, 3.0

def agg_fail(k, a=ALPHA, b=BETA):
    """Mean over problems of (1-p)^k when p ~ Beta(a, b). Exact: B(a, b+k) / B(a, b)."""
    lb = lambda x, y: math.lgamma(x) + math.lgamma(y) - math.lgamma(x + y)
    return math.exp(lb(a, b + k) - lb(a, b))

kk = np.unique(np.logspace(0, 4, 40).astype(int))
neglog_cov = np.array([-math.log(1 - agg_fail(k)) for k in kk])
big = kk >= 100
slope = np.polyfit(np.log(kk[big]), np.log(neglog_cov[big]), 1)[0]
print(f"fitted power-law exponent for k >= 100: {slope:.3f}   (theory: -alpha = {-ALPHA})")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(9, 3.2))
for p in (0.3, 0.05, 0.01):
    fail = [(1 - p) ** k for k in kk]
    ax[0].plot(kk, [-math.log1p(-f) if 0 < f < 1 else np.nan for f in fail], label=f"one problem, p={p}")
ax[0].set_xscale("log"); ax[0].set_yscale("log"); ax[0].set_ylim(1e-6, 10)
ax[0].set_title("Each problem: falls off a cliff (exponential)"); ax[0].set_xlabel("k"); ax[0].set_ylabel("-log(pass@k)"); ax[0].legend(fontsize=7)
ax[1].plot(kk, neglog_cov, marker=".", label=f"many problems, p ~ Beta({ALPHA}, {BETA})")
ax[1].set_xscale("log"); ax[1].set_yscale("log")
ax[1].set_title("Average: straight line (power law)"); ax[1].set_xlabel("k"); ax[1].legend(fontsize=7)
plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

Why a builder cares: the exponent tells you how fast more samples pay off, so you can forecast before you spend. Schaeffer et al. estimate it from the distribution of single-try success rates and report about an order of magnitude lower error than fitting the curve directly, which they describe as roughly 2-4 orders of magnitude less inference compute.

7. An Archon-style pipeline: generate, rank, fuse, verify

Saad-Falcon et al. 2024 (Archon) treat inference-time tricks as building blocks and search for the best stack. The blocks include a Generator (makes candidates), a Ranker (picks the best ones), a Fuser (merges candidates into one better answer), and a Verifier (keeps only answers whose reasoning checks out).

We build one fixed stack by hand on the 4 hard problems: reuse the 6 samples, then rank (1 call), fuse (1 call), verify the fused answer (1 call). If the verifier says NO, we fall back to majority vote. That is 12 calls.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 9
def numbered(cands, limit=600):
    return "\n\n".join(f"Candidate {i + 1}:\n{c['text'][:limit]}" for i, c in enumerate(cands))

RANK = ("Problem: {q}\n\n{c}\n\nWhich candidate has the correct final answer? Check the arithmetic. "
        "Reply with the candidate number only.")
FUSE = ("Problem: {q}\n\nHere are several attempts. Some may be wrong.\n\n{c}\n\n"
        "Use the attempts to write one careful, correct solution. On the last line write exactly: ANSWER: <whole number>")
VERIFY = ("Problem: {q}\n\nProposed solution:\n{s}\n\nCheck every step and the arithmetic. "
          "Is the final answer correct? Reply YES or NO only.")

def archon(p):
    cands = SAMPLES[p["id"]]
    r, _ = gen(RANK.format(q=p["question"], c=numbered(cands)), temperature=0, max_tokens=10)
    digits = [int(x) for x in "".join(ch if ch.isdigit() else " " for ch in r).split()]
    idx = digits[0] - 1 if digits and 1 <= digits[0] <= len(cands) else 0
    fused, _ = gen(FUSE.format(q=p["question"], c=numbered(cands)), temperature=0, max_tokens=500)
    v, _ = gen(VERIFY.format(q=p["question"], s=fused), temperature=0, max_tokens=5)
    fused_ans = extract_int(fused)
    final = fused_ans if "YES" in v.upper() else majority([c["answer"] for c in cands])
    return {"first": cands[0]["answer"], "majority": majority([c["answer"] for c in cands]),
            "ranked": cands[idx]["answer"], "fused": fused_ans, "fused+verify": final,
            "oracle": p["answer"] if any(c["ok"] for c in cands) else None}

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    ARCH = dict(zip([p["id"] for p in HARD], ex.map(archon, HARD)))
for method in ["first", "majority", "ranked", "fused", "fused+verify", "oracle"]:
    right = sum(ARCH[p["id"]][method] == p["answer"] for p in HARD)
    print(f"{method:13} {right}/{len(HARD)}")

With 4 problems this is noisy, so don't read too much into one run. The paper's lesson is about combining blocks: Archon reports that across its benchmarks the Fuser helped on every one (8.9% on average), the Ranker came within 2.7% of oracle selection on MT-Bench and Arena-Hard-Auto, and the Verifier helped most on reasoning tasks. Their searched stacks beat o1, GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Sonnet by 15.1% on average.

8. Spend compute by difficulty (Snell et al. 2024)

Snell et al. 2024 (Scaling LLM Test-Time Compute Optimally) ask: with a fixed budget, should you sample in parallel (many independent tries, then vote) or sequentially (one try, then revise it, then revise again)? Their answer: it depends on difficulty. On easy questions, sequential revision worked best. On harder ones, a mix of both did. Picking the strategy per question beat best-of-N with up to 4x less compute.

First, how hard is each problem for this model? Snell et al. measure difficulty as the model's own success rate over many samples, not a human label. We do the same with our 6 samples.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 10
for p in PROBS:
    rate = sum(s["ok"] for s in SAMPLES[p["id"]]) / K
    print(f"{p['id']}  label={p['difficulty']:4}  model success rate={rate:.0%}")

Now a budget of 3 generations per problem, two ways: - parallel: majority vote over 3 of our existing samples (0 new calls); - sequential: take sample 1, then ask the model twice to check and fix its own work (2 new calls per problem, 16 in total).

Note: Snell et al. fine-tuned a special revision model. We only prompt, so our revisions are weaker than theirs.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 11
REVISE = ("Problem: {q}\n\nYour previous attempt:\n{prev}\n\nCheck each step of the attempt. If you find a mistake, fix it. "
          "Write the full corrected solution. On the last line write exactly: ANSWER: <whole number>")

def sequential(p, rounds=2):
    text = SAMPLES[p["id"]][0]["text"]
    for _ in range(rounds):
        new, _ = gen(REVISE.format(q=p["question"], prev=text[:1500]), temperature=0.3)
        text = new or text
    return extract_int(text)

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    SEQ = dict(zip([p["id"] for p in PROBS], ex.map(sequential, PROBS)))
PAR = {p["id"]: majority([s["answer"] for s in SAMPLES[p["id"]][:3]]) for p in PROBS}

for name, ps in [("easy", EASY), ("hard", HARD)]:
    par = sum(PAR[p["id"]] == p["answer"] for p in ps)
    seq = sum(SEQ[p["id"]] == p["answer"] for p in ps)
    print(f"{name}: parallel (vote of 3) {par}/{len(ps)}   sequential (1 + 2 revisions) {seq}/{len(ps)}")

How to read this: a "compute-optimal" policy picks, for each difficulty level, whichever strategy wins there. To do that in production you need a cheap difficulty predictor (Snell et al. used a verifier's scores; you could use agreement between a few samples). Snell et al. also found the trade has limits: on the hardest questions, or when you serve a lot of traffic, a bigger pretrained model was the better use of compute.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 12
check("task generator is seeded (same seed, same problems)", math_problems(5, 3, "hard") == math_problems(5, 3, "hard"))
check("coverage never goes down as k grows", all(b >= a - 1e-9 for a, b in zip(cov["all"], cov["all"][1:])))
check("repeated sampling helps: coverage@6 > pass@1 (or pass@1 is already 100%)", cov["all"][-1] > cov["all"][0] or cov["all"][0] == 1.0)
check("the model solves at least half the easy problems within 6 tries", cov["easy"][-1] >= 0.5)
check("power-law exponent matches the Beta tail (within 0.1)", abs(slope + ALPHA) < 0.1)

Your turn

  1. Temperature and diversity. Rerun repeated sampling on the 4 hard problems with 4 tries each at temperature 0.2 and at 1.0. Which gives higher coverage? Which gives better majority vote?
  2. Small model, more tries. Pull llama3.2:3b and give it 6 tries on the hard problems. Does the 3B model's coverage@6 beat the 8B model's pass@1? Compare tokens spent too. (This is a tiny version of Snell et al.'s "smaller model plus test-time compute vs bigger model".)
  3. Fit your own power law. Change ALPHA to 0.1 and 0.8 and refit the slope. Which tail gives slower gains from more samples, and why?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)

Each solution says how many model calls it makes. Run only the ones you want; together they add about 40-55 calls.

a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 13
# Solution 1: temperature 0.2 vs 1.0 (4 problems x 4 tries x 2 settings = 32 calls)
for temp in (0.2, 1.0):
    jobs = [p for p in HARD for _ in range(4)]
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        outs = list(ex.map(lambda p: gen(SOLVE.format(q=p["question"]), temperature=temp), jobs))
    ans = {p["id"]: [] for p in HARD}
    for p, (text, _) in zip(jobs, outs):
        ans[p["id"]].append(extract_int(text))
    cov4 = sum(any(a == p["answer"] for a in ans[p["id"]]) for p in HARD) / len(HARD)
    maj4 = sum(majority(ans[p["id"]]) == p["answer"] for p in HARD) / len(HARD)
    print(f"temperature {temp}: coverage@4 {cov4:.0%}   majority@4 {maj4:.0%}   distinct answers {[len(set(v)) for v in ans.values()]}")
# Low temperature gives near-identical tries, so extra tries add little coverage.
# High temperature adds diversity (more coverage) but more noise, which can hurt majority vote.
a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 14
# Solution 2: llama3.2:3b with 6 tries vs llama3.1:8b with 1 try (24 calls plus a ~2 GB download)
from lal.runtime import pull
SMALL = "llama3.2:3b"
pull(SMALL)
jobs = [p for p in HARD for _ in range(K)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    outs = list(ex.map(lambda p: gen(SOLVE.format(q=p["question"]), model_name=SMALL), jobs))
right = {p["id"]: 0 for p in HARD}
small_toks = 0
for p, (text, toks) in zip(jobs, outs):
    right[p["id"]] += extract_int(text) == p["answer"]
    small_toks += toks
small_cov = sum(pass_at_k(K, c, K) for c in right.values()) / len(HARD)
big_p1 = cov["hard"][0]
big_toks = sum(s["tokens"] for p in HARD for s in SAMPLES[p["id"]]) / K
print(f"3B coverage@{K}: {small_cov:.0%} using {small_toks} tokens")
print(f"8B pass@1:      {big_p1:.0%} using about {big_toks:.0f} tokens")
# A 3B token is much cheaper than an 8B token. If the small model's coverage is higher AND you have
# a checker to pick the right try, many small tries can be the better deal. Without a checker, it often isn't.
a01_test_time_compute.ipynb · cell 15
# Solution 3: the tail shape sets the exponent
for a in (0.1, 0.3, 0.8):
    y = np.array([-math.log(1 - agg_fail(k, a=a)) for k in kk])
    s = np.polyfit(np.log(kk[big]), np.log(y[big]), 1)[0]
    print(f"alpha={a}: fitted exponent {s:.2f}")
# Small alpha = many problems with success rate near 0 = a flatter line: each 10x more samples
# removes less of the failure. That is the "heavy tail" that makes some tasks expensive to brute-force.
Traps
  • Reporting coverage (pass@k) as if it were accuracy. Coverage assumes you can always spot the right try; in production you usually can't.
  • Adding samples without a verifier. Brown et al. saw majority vote and reward models stall near 100 samples while coverage kept rising.
  • Sampling at temperature 0. Every try is the same, so more tries add nothing.
  • Counting calls instead of tokens. A fuser or revision call reads all candidates, so its cost grows with the number of candidates.
  • Assuming test-time compute always replaces a bigger model. Snell et al. found pretraining wins on the hardest questions and at high inference volume.
  • Choosing the strategy per difficulty on the same small set you then report on. That overfits; hold out data.

Check yourself

Coverage at k=100 is 90% but majority vote at k=100 is 45%. What is missing?

A good verifier. The model can produce the right answer, but the picker can't find it: that 45-point difference is the generation-verification gap.

Why can a per-problem failure rate fall exponentially while the average follows a power law?

Because the average mixes problems of different difficulty. A heavy tail of problems with near-zero single-try success makes the average fall slowly, like a power law (Schaeffer et al. 2025).

For an easy question, should you spend 4 calls on 4 parallel samples or on 1 sample plus 3 revisions?

Snell et al. found sequential revision best on easy questions, and a mix of both on harder ones. Test on your own task, since they used a fine-tuned revision model.

Part 2 · A02

Robust verification

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 3 (Sep 29)

More tries only help if something can tell right from wrong. Verifiers score candidate answers, either the whole answer (outcome) or each step (process), and several weak verifiers combined can come close to a perfect checker.

Key terms

Verifier
Anything that scores a candidate answer: an exact checker, unit tests, a trained model, an LLM judge, or a rule.Source: Cobbe et al. 2021
Generation-verification gap
The difference between coverage (some try is right) and what your verifier actually picks.Source: Saad-Falcon et al. 2025 (Weaver)
Outcome reward model (ORM)
A verifier that judges only the final answer of a solution.Source: Lightman et al. 2023
Process reward model (PRM)
A verifier that scores each reasoning step; the solution score multiplies the step scores.Source: Lightman et al. 2023
Soft / hard step label
From a step, finish the solution N times. Soft: share that reach the right answer. Hard: 1 if any do.Source: Wang et al. 2023 (Math-Shepherd)
Weak supervision
Estimating how accurate each noisy verifier is from how they agree with each other, without true labels.Source: Saad-Falcon et al. 2025 (Weaver)
Reward hacking (verifier exploitation)
Search finds answers that score high but are wrong. More samples make it more likely.Source: Cobbe et al. 2021

The papers

Shrinking the Generation-Verification Gap with Weak Verifiers (Weaver)

Saad-Falcon, Buchanan, Chen, et al. · 2025

What
Combines many weak verifiers (33 open reward models and LLM judges, 7B-72B) into one score to pick among 100 samples from Llama 3.3 70B Instruct. Normalizes and binarizes scores, filters weak verifiers, and learns verifier weights with weak supervision (no ground-truth labels). Then distills the ensemble into a small cross-encoder.
Result
Average accuracy over MATH500, GPQA Diamond, MMLU College and MMLU Pro: 87.7% for Weaver vs 72.2% majority vote, 69.0% GPT-4o and 86.7% o3-mini; within 4.2% of the pass@100 oracle. Weighted ensembles with oracle labels beat naive averaging by up to 11.2 points. A 400M ModernBERT distillation kept 98.7% of the accuracy (98.2% in another section) while cutting verification compute by up to 99.97%. Struggles where correct answers are very rare.
Takeaway
Don't hunt for one perfect judge. Stack cheap checks that fail in different ways (rules, agreement, a judge model, reward models), weight them by estimated accuracy, and distill the result into a small model for production.

Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems

Cobbe, Kosaraju, Bavarian, Chen, Jun, Kaiser, Plappert, Tworek, Hilton, Nakano, Hesse, Schulman · 2021

What
Released GSM8K (8.5K grade-school word problems: 7.5K train, 1K test). Trained a verifier to judge whether a full solution is correct, then at test time sampled 100 solutions per problem and returned the one the verifier scored highest.
Result
Verification gave about the same boost as a 30x larger model over a fine-tuning baseline, and scaled better with more data (but did not help with small training sets). A large generator with a small verifier beat a small generator with a large verifier. For the 6B verifier, accuracy rose up to 400 completions per problem, then fell as search found wrong answers that fooled the verifier. A token-level verifier eventually beat a solution-level one.
Takeaway
A trained outcome verifier plus sampling is a cheap way to lift a small model, but cap the number of samples and watch for adversarial answers that game the verifier.

Let's Verify Step by Step

Lightman, Kosaraju, Burda, Edwards, Baker, Lee, Leike, Schulman, Sutskever, Cobbe · 2023

What
Compared an outcome reward model (judges the final answer) with a process reward model (judges each step) for picking among GPT-4 solutions on MATH. Humans labeled steps positive, negative or neutral; a solution's PRM score is the probability that every step is correct.
Result
Best-of-1,860 on a 500-problem MATH test subset: PRM 78.2%, ORM 72.4%, majority vote 69.6%. Released PRM800K: 800K step labels across 75K solutions to 12K problems. Active learning was about 2.6x more data-efficient than uniform labeling. On out-of-distribution AP and AMC problems (best-of-100) the PRM also led: aggregate 72.9% vs 63.8% ORM and 61.3% majority vote.
Takeaway
When you can, check steps, not just the result. Step scores also tell a reviewer where a solution broke. Spend labeling budget on convincing wrong answers (active learning).

Math-Shepherd: Verify and Reinforce LLMs Step-by-step without Human Annotations

Wang, Li, Shao, Xu, Dai, Li, Chen, Wu, Sui · 2023

What
Labels steps automatically: from each step, a completer model finishes the solution N times (N=8 in their data) and the step is scored by how often it reaches the right answer (soft label) or whether it ever does (hard label). Trains a process reward model on these labels and uses it for reranking and for step-by-step PPO.
Result
Step-by-step PPO lifted Mistral-7B from 77.9% to 84.1% on GSM8K and 28.6% to 33.0% on MATH; adding verification reached 89.1% and 43.5%. DeepSeek-67B with best-of-256 went from 88.2% (self-consistency) to 93.3% on GSM8K, and 48.1% on MATH500 when combined with self-consistency.
Takeaway
If you have final-answer checks, you can turn them into step labels with rollouts, offline. That gives a process verifier and an RL signal without paying people to label steps.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a02_verification.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 2
import ast, math, re, operator as op
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from pydantic import BaseModel
from lal.llm import client, structured
from lal.tasks import math_problems, extract_int
from lal.checks import prf, check

SOLVE = ("Solve this problem step by step, one step per line. Keep it short.\n"
         "On the last line write exactly: ANSWER: <whole number>\n\nProblem: {q}")

def gen(prompt, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=400):
    """One model call, returns the text. Never raises."""
    try:
        r = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": prompt}],
                                           temperature=temperature, max_tokens=max_tokens)
        return r.choices[0].message.content or ""
    except Exception as e:
        print("call failed:", e)
        return ""

def pmap(fn, items):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        return list(ex.map(fn, items))

1. Candidates to verify

6 problems (2 easy, 4 hard), 4 tries each at temperature 0.8: 24 calls. We want a mix of right and wrong tries, so most problems are hard.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 3
PROBS = math_problems(2, seed=1, difficulty="easy") + math_problems(4, seed=1, difficulty="hard")
BY_ID = {p["id"]: p for p in PROBS}
N = 4
jobs = [p for p in PROBS for _ in range(N)]
texts = pmap(lambda p: gen(SOLVE.format(q=p["question"])), jobs)

CANDS = []          # one dict per candidate answer
for i, (p, t) in enumerate(zip(jobs, texts)):
    a = extract_int(t)
    CANDS.append({"key": f"{p['id']}#{i % N}", "pid": p["id"], "text": t, "answer": a, "ok": a == p["answer"]})
for p in PROBS:
    cs = [c for c in CANDS if c["pid"] == p["id"]]
    print(f"{p['id']}: answers {[c['answer'] for c in cs]}  truth {p['answer']}")
print(f"\n{sum(c['ok'] for c in CANDS)}/{len(CANDS)} candidates are right")

2. The oracle verifier, and how we score any verifier

A verifier accepts or rejects each candidate. We score it like a classifier: - precision: of the candidates it accepted, how many were really right; - recall: of the right candidates, how many it accepted.

The oracle compares with the true answer. It is perfect by definition, and it is what Brown et al. had for code (unit tests) and proofs (Lean). For most business work you don't have one.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 4
GOLD = {c["key"] for c in CANDS if c["ok"]}

def score_accepts(accepted: set, name: str):
    s = prf(accepted, GOLD)
    print(f"{name:26} accepts {len(accepted):2}   precision {s['precision']:.0%}   recall {s['recall']:.0%}")
    return s

oracle = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if c["answer"] == BY_ID[c["pid"]]["answer"]}, "oracle (exact answer)")

3. An outcome verifier: an LLM judges the final answer

Cobbe et al. 2021 (Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems) trained a model to judge whole solutions, then picked the best of 100 samples. On their GSM8K dataset this gave about the same boost as a 30x bigger model. They also warn that with too many samples (beyond about 400 for their 6B verifier) accuracy dropped, because search starts finding wrong answers that fool the verifier.

We can't train here, so we prompt the model to act as the judge. One call per candidate: 24 calls.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 5
JUDGE = ("Problem: {q}\n\nProposed solution:\n{s}\n\n"
         "Check the reasoning and arithmetic. How likely is the final answer correct, from 0 (surely wrong) to 10 (surely right)? "
         "Reply with one number only.")

def judge(c):
    r = gen(JUDGE.format(q=BY_ID[c["pid"]]["question"], s=c["text"][:1500]), temperature=0, max_tokens=8)
    m = re.search(r"\d+", r)
    return min(10, int(m.group())) / 10 if m else 0.5     # unparsable -> "don't know"

for c, s in zip(CANDS, pmap(judge, CANDS)):
    c["judge"] = s
judge_acc = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if c["judge"] >= 0.7}, "LLM judge (score >= 7)")
right_mean = sum(c["judge"] for c in CANDS if c["ok"]) / max(1, sum(c["ok"] for c in CANDS))
wrong_mean = sum(c["judge"] for c in CANDS if not c["ok"]) / max(1, sum(not c["ok"] for c in CANDS))
print(f"average judge score: right candidates {right_mean:.2f}, wrong candidates {wrong_mean:.2f}")

How to read this: small models tend to say "yes" too often, which gives high recall and poor precision. A judge that gives every candidate 9/10 can't help you pick.

4. Two cheap weak verifiers that need no model call

  • Agreement (self-consistency): what share of the other tries reached the same answer. This is majority vote turned into a score.
  • Arithmetic check: find every "a op b = c" in the text and recompute it with Python. A plain rule, but it never gets tired.
a02_verification.ipynb · cell 6
OPS = {ast.Add: op.add, ast.Sub: op.sub, ast.Mult: op.mul, ast.Div: op.truediv}

def safe_eval(expr):
    """Evaluate + - * / and brackets only. Anything else raises ValueError. No eval() on model text."""
    def ev(n):
        if isinstance(n, ast.Expression):
            return ev(n.body)
        if isinstance(n, ast.Constant) and isinstance(n.value, (int, float)):
            return n.value
        if isinstance(n, ast.BinOp) and type(n.op) in OPS:
            return OPS[type(n.op)](ev(n.left), ev(n.right))
        if isinstance(n, ast.UnaryOp) and isinstance(n.op, ast.USub):
            return -ev(n.operand)
        raise ValueError("not plain arithmetic")
    return ev(ast.parse(expr, mode="eval"))

EQ = re.compile(r"([\d.+\-*/×÷x() ]+)=\s*(-?\d+(?:\.\d+)?)")

def arithmetic_score(text):
    """Share of 'a op b = c' equations that are right. 0.5 if there are none to check."""
    ok = total = 0
    for line in (text or "").splitlines():
        line = re.sub(r"^\s*(?:step\s*)?\d+[.):]\s+", "", line, flags=re.I)         # drop "1." / "Step 2:" markers
        line = re.sub(r"(?<=\d),(?=\d{3})", "", line).replace("$", "")
        for left, right in EQ.findall(line):
            expr = left.strip().replace("×", "*").replace("÷", "/").replace("x", "*")
            if not re.search(r"\d\s*[+\-*/]\s*[\d(]", expr):
                continue
            try:
                val, want = safe_eval(expr), float(right)
            except Exception:
                continue
            total += 1
            close = abs(val - want) <= 1e-6 * max(1.0, abs(want))
            rounded = "/" in expr and want in (math.floor(val), math.ceil(val))   # "96 / 9 = 10 full bags" is fine
            ok += close or rounded
    return ok / total if total else 0.5

print(arithmetic_score("Step 1: 12 * 5 = 60\nStep 2: 60 - 7 = 53"), arithmetic_score("12 x 5 = 70"), arithmetic_score("no maths here"))

for c in CANDS:
    others = [o["answer"] for o in CANDS if o["pid"] == c["pid"] and o["key"] != c["key"]]
    c["agree"] = sum(a == c["answer"] for a in others) / len(others) if c["answer"] is not None and others else 0.0
    c["arith"] = arithmetic_score(c["text"])
agree_acc = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if c["agree"] >= 0.5}, "agreement (>= half agree)")
arith_acc = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if c["arith"] >= 0.75}, "arithmetic check (>= 75%)")

5. A process verifier: score each step (PRM-style)

Lightman et al. 2023 (Let's Verify Step by Step) compared an outcome reward model (ORM: judges only the end result) with a process reward model (PRM: judges every step). People labeled 800K steps (the PRM800K dataset). Picking the best of 1,860 GPT-4 solutions on a 500-problem MATH test set, the PRM got 78.2%, the ORM 72.4%, and majority vote 69.6%. A solution's PRM score is the chance that every step is right: multiply the step scores.

We prompt our model to score each numbered step, on 6 candidates (right and wrong mixed). That is 6 calls.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 7
class StepScores(BaseModel):
    scores: list[int]

def steps_of(text, max_steps=8):
    lines = [l.strip() for l in (text or "").splitlines() if l.strip()]
    if len(lines) <= max_steps:
        return lines
    size = math.ceil(len(lines) / max_steps)
    return [" ".join(lines[i:i + size]) for i in range(0, len(lines), size)]

PRM_PROMPT = ("Problem: {q}\n\nA solution, split into numbered steps:\n{steps}\n\n"
              "For EACH step, give a score from 0 (this step is wrong) to 10 (this step is surely correct). "
              "Judge the step itself: its logic and arithmetic. Return one score per step, in order.")

def prm(c):
    st = steps_of(c["text"])
    body = "\n".join(f"{i + 1}. {s}" for i, s in enumerate(st))
    try:
        r = structured(PRM_PROMPT.format(q=BY_ID[c["pid"]]["question"], steps=body), StepScores, max_tokens=200)
        sc = [min(10, max(0, int(x))) / 10 for x in r.scores]
    except Exception as e:
        print("PRM call failed:", e)
        sc = []
    sc = (sc + [0.5] * len(st))[:len(st)]          # pad or cut to one score per step
    return st, sc

right = [c for c in CANDS if c["ok"]][:3]
wrong = [c for c in CANDS if not c["ok"]][:6 - len(right)]
PRM_SET = right + wrong
for c, (st, sc) in zip(PRM_SET, pmap(prm, PRM_SET)):
    c["steps"], c["step_scores"] = st, sc
    c["prm_prod"] = math.prod(sc) if sc else 0.0
    c["prm_min"] = min(sc) if sc else 0.0
    first_bad = next((i + 1 for i, s in enumerate(sc) if s < 0.5), None)
    print(f"{c['key']:16} right={c['ok']!s:5}  outcome judge {c['judge']:.1f}  PRM product {c['prm_prod']:.2f}  "
          f"min {c['prm_min']:.1f}  first flagged step: {first_bad}")

Why steps help: a wrong final answer usually comes from one bad step. A step score tells you where it broke, which is useful for a human reviewer, for retrying from that step, and as a training signal.

6. Step labels without humans (Math-Shepherd)

People labeling 800K steps is expensive. Wang et al. 2023 (Math-Shepherd) label steps automatically: cut the solution after step i, let a model finish it N times, and check the final answers. - Soft label: share of finishes that reach the right answer. - Hard label: 1 if any finish reaches the right answer.

They built their dataset with N = 8 finishes per step, and used it to train a PRM and to guide RL. We use N = 3 on 3 cut points of one right and one wrong candidate: 18 calls.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 8
CONTINUE = ("Problem: {q}\n\nHere is the start of a solution:\n{prefix}\n\n"
            "Continue from exactly where it stops. Do not start over. "
            "On the last line write exactly: ANSWER: <whole number>")
ROLLOUTS = 3

def cut_points(n_steps):
    return sorted({1, max(1, n_steps // 2), max(1, n_steps - 1)})

def shepherd(c):
    p = BY_ID[c["pid"]]
    rows = []
    for i in cut_points(len(c["steps"])):
        prefix = "\n".join(c["steps"][:i])
        outs = pmap(lambda _: gen(CONTINUE.format(q=p["question"], prefix=prefix)), range(ROLLOUTS))
        hits = sum(extract_int(o) == p["answer"] for o in outs)
        rows.append({"after_step": i, "soft": hits / ROLLOUTS, "hard": int(hits > 0),
                     "prm": c["step_scores"][i - 1] if i - 1 < len(c["step_scores"]) else None})
    return rows

SHEP = {}
for c in [x for x in PRM_SET if x["ok"]][:1] + [x for x in PRM_SET if not x["ok"]][:1]:
    SHEP[c["key"]] = (c["ok"], shepherd(c))
    print(f"\n{c['key']} (candidate is {'right' if c['ok'] else 'wrong'})")
    for r in SHEP[c["key"]][1]:
        print(f"  after step {r['after_step']}: soft {r['soft']:.2f}  hard {r['hard']}  | prompted PRM said {r['prm']}")

How to read this: on a wrong solution, the soft label should drop after the step where it went wrong. Compare that with the step the prompted PRM flagged. Math-Shepherd reports that training on these labels lifted Mistral-7B from 77.9% to 84.1% on GSM8K with step-by-step RL, and to 89.1% when also used as a verifier. The catch: rollouts cost many calls per step, so this is an offline, data-making tool.

7. Combine weak verifiers (Weaver)

Saad-Falcon et al. 2025 (Shrinking the Generation-Verification Gap with Weak Verifiers, "Weaver") start from many imperfect verifiers: reward models and LLM judges. Each one alone is weak. Their recipe: 1. turn each verifier's score into a yes/no vote (they normalize and binarize); 2. estimate how accurate each verifier is without labels, from how its votes agree with the others (weak supervision); 3. drop verifiers that look no better than chance, and weight the rest by accuracy.

With Llama 3.3 70B as the generator and 100 samples per question, Weaver averaged 87.7% across MATH500, GPQA Diamond, MMLU College and MMLU Pro, against 72.2% for majority vote and 86.7% for o3-mini.

Our tiny version uses 3 verifiers we already have: judge, agreement, arithmetic. No new calls.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 9
VERIFIERS = {"judge": lambda c: c["judge"] >= 0.7, "agree": lambda c: c["agree"] >= 0.5, "arith": lambda c: c["arith"] >= 0.75}
VOTES = {c["key"]: {v: int(f(c)) for v, f in VERIFIERS.items()} for c in CANDS}

def label_free_accuracy(name):
    """How well this verifier agrees with the average vote of the OTHER verifiers. No labels used.

    Per candidate: 1 if it matches all the others, 0 if it matches none, 0.5 on a split. Then average.
    A verifier that says "yes" to everything only scores as well as the others say "yes".
    """
    scores = []
    for votes in VOTES.values():
        others = [x for v, x in votes.items() if v != name]
        if others:
            scores.append(1 - abs(votes[name] - sum(others) / len(others)))
    return sum(scores) / len(scores) if scores else 0.5

def true_accuracy(name):
    return sum(VOTES[c["key"]][name] == int(c["ok"]) for c in CANDS) / len(CANDS)

def weights(acc_fn, min_acc=0.55):
    out = {}
    for v in VERIFIERS:
        a = min(0.95, max(0.05, acc_fn(v)))
        out[v] = math.log(a / (1 - a)) if a >= min_acc else 0.0     # filter: weak-looking verifiers get no say
    if not any(out.values()):                                         # everything filtered: fall back to equal votes
        print("note: no verifier passed the filter, using equal weights")
        out = {v: 1.0 for v in VERIFIERS}
    return out

W_FREE, W_LABELS = weights(label_free_accuracy), weights(true_accuracy)
for v in VERIFIERS:
    print(f"{v:6} accuracy estimated without labels {label_free_accuracy(v):.0%}  true {true_accuracy(v):.0%}  "
          f"-> weight {W_FREE[v]:+.2f} (with labels {W_LABELS[v]:+.2f})")

def combined(c, w):
    return sum(w[v] * (2 * VOTES[c["key"]][v] - 1) for v in VERIFIERS)

# "with labels" uses the true answers of these same candidates, so it is a cheating upper bound,
# shown only to see how close the label-free weights get.
weaver_acc = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if combined(c, W_FREE) > 0}, "Weaver-lite (no labels)")
naive_acc = score_accepts({c["key"] for c in CANDS if sum(VOTES[c["key"]].values()) >= 2}, "naive vote (2 of 3)")

8. What matters in the end: did we pick the right answer?

For each problem, keep the candidate with the highest score (ties go to the earlier try). Compare pickers. "Oracle" is the share of problems where some try was right: the ceiling.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 10
def pick_accuracy(score_fn):
    right = 0
    for p in PROBS:
        cs = [c for c in CANDS if c["pid"] == p["id"]]
        best = max(cs, key=score_fn)            # max keeps the first of equal scores
        right += best["ok"]
    return right / len(PROBS)

PICKERS = {
    "first try (no verifier)": lambda c: 0,
    "majority vote": lambda c: c["agree"],
    "LLM judge": lambda c: c["judge"],
    "arithmetic check": lambda c: c["arith"],
    "Weaver-lite (no labels)": lambda c: (combined(c, W_FREE), c["agree"]),
    "weighted with labels": lambda c: (combined(c, W_LABELS), c["agree"]),
    "oracle": lambda c: c["ok"],
}
RESULTS = {name: pick_accuracy(fn) for name, fn in PICKERS.items()}
for name, acc in RESULTS.items():
    print(f"{name:26} {acc:.0%}")

How to read this: with 6 problems one flip is 17 points, so treat this as a demo of the method, not a result. The lessons from the papers are: (1) a verifier's precision matters more than its recall when you pick one answer, (2) several weak, different verifiers beat one, and (3) Weaver distilled its ensemble into a 400M model that kept about 98% of the accuracy at a tiny fraction of the verification compute, so the combination can be cheap in production.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 11
check("oracle verifier has 100% precision and recall", oracle["precision"] == 1.0 and oracle["recall"] == 1.0)
check("arithmetic check catches a wrong equation and passes a right one",
      arithmetic_score("12 * 5 = 70") == 0.0 and arithmetic_score("12 * 5 = 60\n96 / 9 = 10") == 1.0)
mixed = any(c["ok"] for c in CANDS) and any(not c["ok"] for c in CANDS)
check("LLM judge scores right candidates higher than wrong ones on average", (right_mean > wrong_mean) if mixed else True)
check("Weaver-lite picks at least as well as the first try", RESULTS["Weaver-lite (no labels)"] >= RESULTS["first try (no verifier)"])
shep_right = [r["soft"] for ok, rows in SHEP.values() if ok for r in rows]
shep_wrong = [r["soft"] for ok, rows in SHEP.values() if not ok for r in rows]
check("Math-Shepherd: prefixes of the right solution succeed at least as often as the wrong one's",
      (sum(shep_right) / len(shep_right) >= sum(shep_wrong) / len(shep_wrong)) if shep_right and shep_wrong else True)

Your turn

  1. Add a fourth weak verifier. Ask the model a different question: "Find the first wrong step, or say NONE." Count NONE as a yes vote. Add it to VERIFIERS and rerun the Weaver-lite steps. Does its label-free weight look sensible?
  2. Try to fool the judge. Take a right candidate, change its final answer to a wrong number, and add "I double-checked every step; this is definitely correct." Does the LLM judge still accept it? Does the arithmetic check?
  3. Soft labels need enough rollouts. On one cut point, run 6 rollouts instead of 3, twice. How much does the soft label move between the two runs?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)

Each solution says how many model calls it makes. Run only the ones you want; together they add about 40-55 calls.

a02_verification.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 1: a "find the first error" verifier (24 calls)
FIRST_ERR = ("Problem: {q}\n\nSolution:\n{s}\n\nFind the first step that is wrong. "
             "Reply with the step text, or reply NONE if every step is correct.")
replies = pmap(lambda c: gen(FIRST_ERR.format(q=BY_ID[c["pid"]]["question"], s=c["text"][:1500]), temperature=0, max_tokens=60), CANDS)
for c, r in zip(CANDS, replies):
    c["no_error"] = "NONE" in (r or "").upper()
VERIFIERS["first_error"] = lambda c: c["no_error"]
VOTES = {c["key"]: {v: int(f(c)) for v, f in VERIFIERS.items()} for c in CANDS}
W_FREE = weights(label_free_accuracy)
print({v: f"{w:+.2f}" for v, w in W_FREE.items()})
print("Weaver-lite with 4 verifiers picks:", f"{pick_accuracy(lambda c: (combined(c, W_FREE), c['agree'])):.0%}")
# A verifier that copies another one (e.g. two judges with the same blind spot) looks "accurate" to the
# label-free estimate because it agrees with its twin. Weaver's real method models this; ours does not.
a02_verification.ipynb · cell 13
# Solution 2: a confident wrong answer (2 calls)
base = next((c for c in CANDS if c["ok"]), CANDS[0])
truth = BY_ID[base["pid"]]["answer"]
fake_text = re.sub(r"ANSWER:\s*-?\d+", f"ANSWER: {truth + 13}", base["text"]) + \
    "\nI double-checked every step; this is definitely correct."
fake = dict(base, text=fake_text, key="fake")
honest = judge(base)
print("judge on the honest right answer:", honest, "| judge on the confident wrong answer:", judge(fake))
print("arithmetic check on the fake:", arithmetic_score(fake_text), "(it only sees the equations, so the changed answer line slips past it)")
# The fix is not one better judge: it is several verifiers that fail in DIFFERENT ways. Here, the
# agreement verifier would catch the fake, because no other try reached that answer.
a02_verification.ipynb · cell 14
# Solution 3: rollout noise (12 calls)
c = next((x for x in PRM_SET if not x["ok"]), PRM_SET[0])
p = BY_ID[c["pid"]]
prefix = "\n".join(c["steps"][:1])
for run in range(2):
    outs = pmap(lambda _: gen(CONTINUE.format(q=p["question"], prefix=prefix)), range(6))
    print(f"run {run + 1}: soft label {sum(extract_int(o) == p['answer'] for o in outs) / 6:.2f}")
# With few rollouts the soft label is noisy (each rollout moves it by 1/6). Math-Shepherd used 8 per step;
# more rollouts give steadier labels but cost more calls. Hard labels (any success) are steadier but cruder.
Traps
  • Trusting an LLM judge that says yes to almost everything. High recall with low precision can't help you pick one answer.
  • Searching too hard against one verifier. Cobbe et al. saw accuracy fall past 400 samples as wrong answers started fooling the verifier.
  • Combining verifiers that share the same blind spot (two prompts to the same model). They agree with each other, so label-free weighting overrates them.
  • Reading step scores from a prompted 8B model as if they were a trained PRM. They are a weak signal; measure them against an oracle first.
  • Using rollout-based step labels with too few rollouts. Each rollout moves a soft label by 1/N, so small N gives noisy labels.
  • Fitting verifier weights on your test set and reporting the result. Use a separate set, or a label-free method.

Check yourself

Why can majority vote fail even when the right answer was sampled?

If the right answer is rare (sampled once in many tries) and wrong answers cluster, the vote picks the cluster. Brown et al. note correct answers can have 1% or lower probability.

What does a process verifier give you that an outcome verifier doesn't?

A score per step, so you see where a solution broke. Lightman et al. found it also picked better: 78.2% vs 72.4% for outcome supervision on their MATH subset.

How does Math-Shepherd label a step without people?

It finishes the solution from that step several times and checks the final answers: the share that are right is the soft label; whether any is right is the hard label.

Part 2 · A03

Learning from feedback with tools and code

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 4 (Oct 3)

A model improves when the world answers back: tool results, test results, or a written set of rules. Use outside signals you can check, not the model's own opinion of itself, and log every loop so it can become training data later.

Key terms

ReAct
A loop of Thought, Action (tool call) and Observation (tool result), repeated until the agent answers.Source: Yao et al. 2022
Execution feedback
The results of running code (which tests failed, errors, timeouts) shown back to the model so it can fix the code.Source: Gehring et al. 2024
Public vs private tests
Public tests are visible and give feedback during the loop; private tests are hidden and decide the final grade, so the model cannot overfit to them.Source: Gehring et al. 2024
Constitution
A short list of written principles that an AI uses to critique and revise answers, replacing most human labels.Source: Bai et al. 2022
Critique-and-revise
The model reviews its own answer against one principle, writes a critique, then rewrites the answer to fix it.Source: Bai et al. 2022
RLAIF
Reinforcement learning from AI feedback: an AI, not a person, picks the better of two answers, and those picks train the reward model.Source: Bai et al. 2022
Preference pair
A training example with a prompt, a chosen answer and a rejected answer; the input format for reward models and DPO.Source: Inferred: standard format used with RLAIF/RLHF data

The papers

ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models

Yao et al. · 2022

What
The model writes a Thought, then an Action (a tool call such as a Wikipedia search), then reads the Observation, and repeats. They prompt PaLM-540B with a few example trajectories and compare against reasoning-only (chain-of-thought) and acting-only prompts on HotpotQA, FEVER, ALFWorld and WebShop.
Result
FEVER accuracy 60.9 vs 56.3 for chain-of-thought; on HotpotQA ReAct alone was slightly behind (27.4 vs 29.4 EM), but combining ReAct with self-consistent CoT gave the best prompting results (35.1 EM HotpotQA, 64.6 FEVER). In a manual study of HotpotQA failures, hallucination caused 56% of CoT failures and 0% of ReAct failures, while 47% of ReAct failures were reasoning errors such as repeating steps. ALFWorld success rose by an absolute 34% and WebShop by 10% over imitation and RL baselines, with only one or two in-context examples.
Takeaway
Give agents tools for facts and let them write a short reason before each action. Watch for loops (the same action again and again) and add a fallback to plain reasoning when the agent runs out of steps.

RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning

Gehring et al. · 2024

What
Code generation becomes a multi-turn episode: the model writes code, sees results on the public tests, and tries again (up to 3 attempts). The final reward is +1 if hidden private tests pass and -1 otherwise, and the model is trained with PPO. Starting models are Llama 3.1 8B and 70B Instruct.
Result
On CodeContests (test set, 3 attempts), 8B went from 10.5% to 16.0% and 70B from 27.5% to 40.1% solve rate. Before training the models barely used feedback: 8B scored 11.8% single-turn vs 10.5% multi-turn. With random feedback from an unrelated problem, the trained models got much worse, showing they really read the feedback. The trained 70B beat AlphaCodium (GPT-4) with far fewer samples.
Takeaway
A repair loop with test feedback is not free accuracy: measure it against simply sampling again with the same budget. If the loop doesn't win, log the episodes; they are exactly the data needed to train a model that does use feedback.

Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback

Bai et al. · 2022

What
Humans write only a short list of principles (the constitution). Phase 1: the model answers red-team prompts, critiques its answer against a randomly sampled principle, and revises; a model is fine-tuned on the revisions (SL-CAI). Phase 2 (RLAIF): a model compares pairs of answers using a principle, those AI preferences train a preference model, and RL optimizes against it.
Result
They wrote 16 principles and sampled 4 critique-revision rounds per red-team prompt (182,831 red-team prompts in total, plus helpfulness data). RL-CAI models were rated by crowdworkers as significantly more harmless than RLHF and SL-CAI models, while engaging with harmful requests by explaining objections instead of being evasive. Turning the AI judge's probabilities into soft labels clamped to 40-60% worked better than hard labels when the judge used chain-of-thought. Over-training led to Goodharting: preachy boilerplate in many answers.
Takeaway
Write your quality bar down as short, checkable rules. Use them to critique and revise outputs, and keep (draft, revision) pairs as preference data. Where a rule can be checked in code (citations exist, verdict present), check it in code instead of trusting the AI judge.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 2
import json, random, re, time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.data import contract, clauses, sections, labels, playbook
from lal.codetasks import tasks, task, run_tests, extract_code, run_python
from lal.checks import check

CALLS = [0]                                   # count model calls; this notebook aims for about 60-80
def llm(prompt, **kw):
    CALLS[0] += 1
    try:
        return ask(prompt, **kw)
    except Exception as e:                    # a server hiccup should not kill the whole notebook
        return f"ERROR: {e}"

TASKS = tasks()
print(len(TASKS), "coding tasks:", [t["id"] for t in TASKS])
print("\nExample prompt:\n" + TASKS[4]["prompt"])

1. A grader the model cannot argue with

Each task has a reference solution and hidden unit tests (some generated with a fixed seed). run_tests runs code in a separate Python process with a timeout, so a bad or endless program cannot hurt the notebook. It returns (passed, total, feedback), and the feedback is short text we can show the model.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 3
t = task("second_largest")
print("reference:", run_tests(t["reference"], t)[:2])
buggy = "def second_largest(nums):\n    return sorted(nums)[-2]\n"
p, n, fb = run_tests(buggy, t)
print("buggy:", p, "/", n)
print(fb)
print("endless loop:", run_tests("def second_largest(nums):\n    while True: pass\n", t, timeout=2))

2. Execution feedback, at inference time (the idea behind RLEF)

RLEF (Gehring et al. 2024, RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning) sets code writing up as a conversation: the model writes code, sees the results on the public tests, and tries again, up to 3 attempts. Grading uses private tests it never sees. Their key finding: off-the-shelf Llama 3.1 Instruct models barely use the feedback (8B went from 11.8% single-turn to 10.5% multi-turn on CodeContests). After RL training on this loop, the 8B model reached 16.0% and the 70B reached 40.1%.

We can't run RL here, but we can run the same loop and measure it. Our prompt examples are too few to catch bugs, so we give the loop a dev set: the prompt examples plus half of the hidden tests. We grade on all tests and also report the held-out half separately. If a fix only passes the tests it was shown, the held-out number tells us.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 4
def split(t):
    hidden = t["tests"][len(t["public_tests"]):]
    dev = dict(t, public_tests=t["public_tests"] + hidden[::2])          # what the loop may see
    held = dict(t, tests=t["public_tests"] + hidden[1::2])               # never shown to the model
    return dev, held

SYSTEM = "You write correct, simple Python. Reply with one ```python code block containing the function, and nothing else."

def solve_with_feedback(t, max_turns=3, temperature=0):
    """Turn 1 is a normal one-shot attempt. Later turns see the dev-test failures."""
    dev, held = split(t)
    history, code, log = t["prompt"], "", []
