    for turn in range(1, max_turns + 1):
        reply = llm(history, system=SYSTEM, temperature=temperature, max_tokens=700)
        code = extract_code(reply)
        dp, dn, fb = run_tests(code, dev, public=True)                    # feedback signal
        ap, an, _ = run_tests(code, t)                                    # grade: all tests
        log.append({"turn": turn, "dev_ok": dp == dn, "all_ok": ap == an, "held_ok": run_tests(code, held)[0] == len(held["tests"])})
        if dp == dn:                                                      # stop when the visible tests pass (as in RLEF)
            break
        history = (f"{t['prompt']}\n\nYour previous attempt:\n```python\n{code}```\n"
                   f"Running it on tests gave:\n{fb}\nFix the code. Give it another try.")
    return code, log

t0 = time.time()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    fb_runs = dict(zip([t["id"] for t in TASKS], ex.map(solve_with_feedback, TASKS)))
print(f"{time.time()-t0:.0f}s, {CALLS[0]} calls so far")
for tid, (_, log) in fb_runs.items():
    print(f"{tid:18s}", " -> ".join(("PASS" if r["all_ok"] else "fail") for r in log))

Pass rate after each turn: a task counts as solved at turn k if its last attempt up to turn k passes all tests.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 5
def pass_by_turn(runs, max_turns=3):
    out = []
    for k in range(1, max_turns + 1):
        solved = sum((log[min(k, len(log)) - 1]["all_ok"]) for _, log in runs.values())
        out.append(solved / len(runs))
    return out

fb_curve = pass_by_turn(fb_runs)
for k, r in enumerate(fb_curve, 1):
    print(f"after turn {k}: {r:.0%} of tasks pass all tests")
fixed = [tid for tid, (_, log) in fb_runs.items() if len(log) > 1]
print("tasks that needed feedback:", fixed)
print("of the final answers, how many pass the held-out half:",
      sum(log[-1]["held_ok"] for _, log in fb_runs.values()), "/", len(fb_runs))

The fair baseline: same budget, no feedback

RLEF compares against independent sampling: just try again from scratch, at a higher temperature, without showing the errors. If feedback doesn't beat this, the model is not really using the feedback. We give each task that failed turn 1 the same extra attempts, with the dev tests picking which attempt to keep.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 6
def resample(t, extra=2):
    dev, _ = split(t)
    for i in range(extra):
        code = extract_code(llm(t["prompt"], system=SYSTEM, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=700))
        if run_tests(code, dev, public=True)[0] == len(dev["public_tests"]):
            break
    return run_tests(code, t)[0] == len(t["tests"])

failed_first = [t for t in TASKS if not fb_runs[t["id"]][1][0]["all_ok"]]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    rs = list(ex.map(resample, failed_first))
first_turn = sum(log[0]["all_ok"] for _, log in fb_runs.values())
print(f"turn 1 (shared):            {first_turn}/{len(TASKS)}")
print(f"+ 2 turns with feedback:     {sum(log[-1]['all_ok'] for _, log in fb_runs.values())}/{len(TASKS)}")
print(f"+ 2 fresh samples, no feedback: {first_turn + sum(rs)}/{len(TASKS)}")
print(CALLS[0], "calls so far")

On 10 small tasks the gap is noisy, so read it as a sign, not proof. RLEF's point is that the gap is small for untrained models and grows after RL on exactly this loop. To train, you would log these episodes: (prompt, attempts, feedback, final reward = all private tests pass).

3. ReAct: think, act, observe

ReAct (Yao et al. 2022, ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models) has the model write a Thought, then an Action (a tool call), then read the Observation (the tool result), and repeat. On HotpotQA, chain-of-thought answers were wrong because of made-up facts in 56% of the failures the authors studied, against 0% for ReAct, which looks facts up.

Our task needs two lookups and one multiplication: the dollar liability cap = cap months × monthly fee. Tools: read the section list, read a clause, and a calculator that runs in a subprocess.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 7
def tool_sections(cid):
    try:
        return "; ".join(f"{s['id']}. {s['title']}" for s in sections(contract(cid)))
    except Exception:
        return f"ERROR: unknown contract id {cid!r}"

def tool_clause(cid, num):
    try:
        cl = clauses(contract(cid))
    except Exception:
        return f"ERROR: unknown contract id {cid!r}"
    return cl.get(num.strip(), f"NOT FOUND. Valid clauses: {', '.join(cl)}")

def tool_calc(expr):
    if not re.fullmatch(r"[\d\s+\-*/().,]+", expr):                         # numbers and operators only
        return "ERROR: calculator accepts only numbers and + - * / ( )"
    out, err = run_python(f"print({expr.replace(',', '')})", timeout=3)       # still in a subprocess
    return out.strip() or f"ERROR: {err.strip()[-200:]}"

REACT = """Answer the question by thinking step by step and using tools. Use EXACTLY this format, one step per reply:
Thought: <your reasoning>
Action: <one of: sections[contract_id] | clause[contract_id, clause_number] | calculate[expression] | finish[answer]>

Tools:
- sections[msa_01] lists the section headings.
- clause[msa_01, 2.1] returns the text of clause 2.1.
- calculate[12 * 4500] does arithmetic.
- finish[54000] gives the final answer (a plain number).
"""

def react(question, max_steps=5, verbose=True):
    transcript = f"{REACT}\nQuestion: {question}\n"
    for step in range(max_steps):
        reply = llm(transcript, temperature=0, max_tokens=250, stop=["Observation:"])
        m = re.search(r"Action:\s*(\w+)\[(.*?)\]", reply, flags=re.S)
        thought = (re.search(r"Thought:\s*(.*)", reply) or re.search(r"(.*)", reply)).group(1)[:120]
        if not m:
            obs = "FORMAT ERROR: end with a line 'Action: tool[arguments]'."
        else:
            name, arg = m.group(1).lower(), m.group(2)
            if name == "finish":
                if verbose: print(f"  {step+1}. finish[{arg}]")
                return arg, step + 1
            parts = [a.strip(" '\"") for a in arg.split(",", 1)]
            obs = {"sections": lambda: tool_sections(parts[0]),
                   "clause": lambda: tool_clause(parts[0], parts[1]) if len(parts) == 2 else "ERROR: use clause[contract_id, number]",
                   "calculate": lambda: tool_calc(arg)}.get(name, lambda: f"ERROR: unknown tool {name}")()
        if verbose: print(f"  {step+1}. {thought} | {m.group(0) if m else '??'} -> {str(obs)[:80]}")
        transcript += reply.strip() + f"\nObservation: {obs}\n"
    return None, max_steps

def to_number(s):
    try:
        return float(re.sub(r"[^\d.]", "", str(s).split()[0] if s else "") or "nan")
    except Exception:
        return float("nan")

L = labels()
react_ids = ["msa_07", "msa_08", "msa_20"]
react_results = {}
for cid in react_ids:
    truth = L[cid]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"] * L[cid]["terms"]["monthly_fee"]
    print(f"{cid} (truth ${truth:,})")
    ans, steps = react(f"In contract {cid}, what is the liability cap in dollars? "
                       "(The cap is a number of months of fees; multiply by the monthly fee.)")
    react_results[cid] = to_number(ans) == truth
    print("  ->", ans, "| correct:", react_results[cid])
print(CALLS[0], "calls so far")

Notice where each fact came from: the clause text, not the model's memory. When a step fails (wrong clause number, bad format), the error comes back as an observation and the model can recover. That is "learning from feedback" inside one episode.

4. Constitutional AI: critique and revise against written rules

Constitutional AI (Bai et al. 2022, Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback) replaces most human labels with a short list of principles. Phase 1 (supervised): the model answers, critiques its answer against a randomly picked principle, and revises. The paper used 16 principles and 4 critique-revision rounds per prompt, then fine-tuned on the revisions. Phase 2 (RLAIF): a model compares two answers using a principle; those AI preferences train a reward model.

Our constitution is for contract-review answers. Each rule is also something code can partly check.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 8
PB = playbook()
CONSTITUTION = [
    "Cite the clause number (like 7.1) for every claim about the contract.",
    "Judge the contract ONLY against the firm playbook rules given. Do not give legal advice beyond the playbook; "
    "for anything else, say the team should ask counsel.",
    "Name every playbook rule the contract breaks, and say clearly whether the contract can be signed as written.",
    "Be short and plain: at most 6 sentences, no legal jargon.",
]

def contract_question(cid):
    return (f"Firm playbook rules: {json.dumps(PB['rules'])}\n\nContract {cid}:\n{contract(cid)}\n\n"
            "Question from the business team: can we sign this contract as written? What needs to change?")

def critique_revise(cid, rng):
    q = contract_question(cid)
    draft = llm(q + "\nAnswer briefly.", temperature=0.7, max_tokens=300)           # a typical first answer
    principle = rng.choice(CONSTITUTION)                                              # CAI samples one principle per step
    critique = llm(f"{q}\n\nAnswer to review:\n{draft}\n\nPrinciple: {principle}\n"
                   "Critique the answer ONLY against this principle. List concrete problems, or say 'No problems'.",
                   max_tokens=250)
    revision = llm(f"{q}\n\nOriginal answer:\n{draft}\n\nCritique:\n{critique}\n\n"
                   f"Rewrite the answer so it follows every rule below and fixes the critique.\nRules:\n- "
                   + "\n- ".join(CONSTITUTION) + "\nReturn only the new answer.", max_tokens=300)
    return {"cid": cid, "principle": principle, "draft": draft, "critique": critique, "revision": revision}

def score(answer, cid):
    """Code checks for the constitution: real citations, issue clauses cited, a clear verdict."""
    cited = set(re.findall(r"\b(\d{1,2}\.\d)\b", answer))
    valid = set(clauses(contract(cid)))
    issue_clauses = {i["clause"] for i in L[cid]["issues"]}
    return {"cites": len(cited & valid),
            "bad_cites": len(cited - valid),
            "issue_recall": len(cited & issue_clauses) / max(1, len(issue_clauses)),
            "verdict": bool(re.search(r"\b(cannot|can't|should not|not be signed|do not sign|don't sign|not sign)\b", answer, re.I))}

cai_ids = ["msa_15", "msa_17", "msa_20"]            # each breaks two playbook rules
rng = random.Random(0)
cai = [critique_revise(cid, rng) for cid in cai_ids]
for r in cai:
    r["s_draft"], r["s_rev"] = score(r["draft"], r["cid"]), score(r["revision"], r["cid"])
    print(f"=== {r['cid']} | principle: {r['principle'][:60]}...")
    print("DRAFT:", r["draft"][:400].replace("\n", " "), "\n  ", r["s_draft"])
    print("REVISED:", r["revision"][:400].replace("\n", " "), "\n  ", r["s_rev"], "\n")
print(CALLS[0], "calls so far")

5. From revisions to preference data (RLAIF)

Each (draft, revision) pair is a training example: chosen = revision, rejected = draft. In CAI's phase 2, a model is shown two answers and a principle and picks the better one; the paper turned the model's probabilities into soft labels and found clamping them to 40-60% worked best when the judge reasoned step by step first. We ask our model to judge, shuffle the order so position bias can't fake agreement, and compare its pick with our code score.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 9
def code_total(s):
    return s["cites"] - 2 * s["bad_cites"] + 3 * s["issue_recall"] + s["verdict"]

def ai_judge(r, rng):
    a, b, flipped = r["draft"], r["revision"], False
    if rng.random() < 0.5:
        a, b, flipped = b, a, True
    principle = "Which answer better follows these rules? " + " ".join(CONSTITUTION)
    v = llm(f"{contract_question(r['cid'])}\n\nAnswer (A):\n{a}\n\nAnswer (B):\n{b}\n\n{principle}\n"
            "Think in one or two sentences, then end with exactly 'Choice: A' or 'Choice: B'.", max_tokens=200)
    m = re.findall(r"Choice:\s*\(?([AB])", v)
    pick = m[-1] if m else None
    if pick is None:
        return None
    return (pick == "A") == flipped          # True if the judge prefers the revision

pairs = []
for r in cai:
    prefers_rev = ai_judge(r, rng)
    code_prefers_rev = code_total(r["s_rev"]) >= code_total(r["s_draft"])
    pairs.append({"prompt": contract_question(r["cid"]), "chosen": r["revision"], "rejected": r["draft"],
                  "principle": r["principle"], "ai_prefers_revision": prefers_rev, "code_prefers_revision": code_prefers_rev})
    print(r["cid"], "| AI judge prefers revision:", prefers_rev, "| code score prefers revision:", code_prefers_rev)
print(f"\n{len(pairs)} preference pairs built. Total model calls: {CALLS[0]}")

A real pipeline would keep only pairs where the AI judge and the code checks agree, then train with DPO or a reward model plus RL. The constitution is the only human input, which makes it cheap to change: edit a rule, regenerate the data.

a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 10
n_turn1, n_final = fb_curve[0], fb_curve[-1]
check("code grader: reference passes, buggy fails", run_tests(task("second_largest")["reference"], task("second_largest"))[0] == 11 and run_tests(buggy, task("second_largest"))[0] < 11)
check("feedback loop solves at least half the tasks", n_final >= 0.5)
n_all = sum(log[-1]["all_ok"] for _, log in fb_runs.values())
n_dev = sum(log[-1]["dev_ok"] for _, log in fb_runs.values())
check("passing the dev tests mostly means passing all tests (fixes generalize)", n_all >= 0.7 * n_dev)
check("ReAct gets at least one dollar cap right with tools", sum(react_results.values()) >= 1)
check("revisions cite real clauses at least as often as drafts",
      sum(r["s_rev"]["cites"] > 0 for r in cai) >= sum(r["s_draft"]["cites"] > 0 for r in cai))

Your turn

  1. Is the model reading the feedback? RLEF tested this by sending random feedback. Re-run the feedback loop on the tasks that failed turn 1, but replace the failure details with only "Some tests failed." Does the pass rate drop?
  2. Act without thinking. Change the ReAct prompt so the model must skip the Thought: line (the paper's "Act" baseline) and run it on one contract. Does it still find both clauses?
  3. Data, not just demos. Write the preference pairs to cai_pairs.jsonl in the {"prompt","chosen","rejected"} format that TRL's DPO trainer reads, keeping only pairs where the AI judge and the code score agree.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 11
# Solution 1: same loop, but the feedback hides WHICH tests failed and why.
def solve_blind(t, max_turns=3):
    dev, _ = split(t)
    history = t["prompt"]
    for turn in range(1, max_turns + 1):
        code = extract_code(llm(history, system=SYSTEM, temperature=0, max_tokens=700))
        if run_tests(code, dev, public=True)[0] == len(dev["public_tests"]):
            break
        history = f"{t['prompt']}\n\nYour previous attempt:\n```python\n{code}```\nSome tests failed. Give it another try."
    return run_tests(code, t)[0] == len(t["tests"])

retry = failed_first[:3]                       # small budget: at most 3 tasks x 3 calls
blind = [solve_blind(t) for t in retry]
full = [fb_runs[t["id"]][1][-1]["all_ok"] for t in retry]
print("solved with detailed feedback:", sum(full), "| with 'some tests failed':", sum(blind), "| of", len(retry))
# Notice: at temperature 0 with a vague message, the model often repeats its old code (RLEF saw this too in untrained models).
a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 2: Act-only, no thoughts.
ACT = REACT.replace("Thought: <your reasoning>\n", "").replace("thinking step by step and using tools", "using tools. Do not explain")
REACT_SAVED, REACT = REACT, ACT
ans, steps = react("In contract msa_08, what is the liability cap in dollars? (Cap months times the monthly fee.)")
REACT = REACT_SAVED
print("Act-only answer:", ans, "| truth:", L["msa_08"]["terms"]["liability_cap_months"] * L["msa_08"]["terms"]["monthly_fee"])
# Notice: without a thought step, small models more often finish after one lookup, or forget to multiply.
a03_feedback_tools_code.ipynb · cell 13
# Solution 3: keep only pairs where the AI judge and the code checks agree, then write DPO-format JSONL.
keep = [p for p in pairs if p["ai_prefers_revision"] is not None and p["ai_prefers_revision"] == p["code_prefers_revision"]]
with open("cai_pairs.jsonl", "w") as f:
    for p in keep:
        chosen, rejected = (p["chosen"], p["rejected"]) if p["ai_prefers_revision"] else (p["rejected"], p["chosen"])
        f.write(json.dumps({"prompt": p["prompt"], "chosen": chosen, "rejected": rejected}) + "\n")
print(f"kept {len(keep)} of {len(pairs)} pairs -> cai_pairs.jsonl")
# Notice: when both judges prefer the DRAFT, we flip chosen/rejected. A revision is not automatically better.
print("total model calls in this notebook:", CALLS[0])
Traps
  • Calling a repair loop a win without a baseline. Compare against fresh samples with the same number of calls; RLEF found untrained models barely beat it.
  • Showing the model the same tests you grade on. Fixes can pass the visible tests and still fail hidden ones; keep a held-out set.
  • Trusting the model's own 'looks good to me'. Use outside signals: tests, tool results, code checks.
  • Assuming every revision is better. Score drafts and revisions with the same checks, and flip or drop pairs where the revision lost.
  • Ignoring position bias in AI judges. Shuffle which answer is A and which is B.
  • Over-optimizing against an AI reward. CAI saw Goodharting: preachy boilerplate added to most answers.

Check yourself

In RLEF, why are the private tests kept hidden from the model?

So the final reward measures real correctness. If the model saw the grading tests, it could pass them without solving the task (overfit to the feedback).

On HotpotQA, ReAct alone scored slightly below chain-of-thought. Why does the paper still favor it?

Its answers are grounded: hallucination caused 0% of its failures vs 56% for CoT. Combining ReAct with CoT-SC gave the best scores (35.1 EM).

How does a Constitutional AI critique-and-revise step become training data?

The original answer is the rejected example and the revision is the chosen one. These pairs (or AI-judged comparisons) train a preference model or a DPO update.

Part 2 · A04

Multi-step reasoning and planning

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 5 (Oct 6)

Hard tasks need more than one straight pass. Search a tree of steps and back up from dead ends, split a task only when a direct try fails, spend budget on new ideas or on refining old ones depending on feedback, and run independent steps at the same time. Every step you log can become step-level training data.

Key terms

Tree search
Exploring several possible next steps, scoring them, and going back to a better branch when one fails, instead of committing to one chain.Source: Zhou et al. 2023
Value function
A score for how promising a partial solution is. In LATS it mixes the LM's own rating with self-consistency (how often a state is proposed).Source: Zhou et al. 2023
As-needed decomposition
Split a task into sub-tasks only after a direct attempt fails, and recurse on the failing parts.Source: Prasad et al. 2024
Wider vs deeper
Spending the next call on a new independent answer (wider) or on refining an existing one with feedback (deeper).Source: Inoue et al. 2025
Thompson sampling
Pick the option whose random draw from its score distribution is highest; it balances trying new options with using proven ones.Source: Inoue et al. 2025
Sequential tokens / critical path
The tokens or steps that must happen one after another; parallel steps only count once per round.Source: Biju et al. 2025
Sub-trajectory and process filtering
Cutting a multi-step run into one example per action, and keeping steps a judge calls reasonable regardless of the final outcome.Source: Goldie et al. 2025

The papers

Synthetic Data Generation & Multi-Step RL for Reasoning & Tool Use (SWiRL)

Goldie et al. · 2025

What
Gemma 2 generates multi-step trajectories with tools (a search engine for QA, a calculator for math). Each trajectory with k actions is cut into k sub-trajectories (context so far, then the next action). These are filtered, and the model is trained with step-wise RL where a generative reward model (Gemini 1.5 Pro) judges each action.
Result
Relative accuracy gains over baselines: 21.5% GSM8K, 12.3% HotPotQA, 14.8% CofCA, 11.1% MuSiQue, 15.3% BeerQA. Training only on HotPotQA improved zero-shot GSM8K by 16.9% relative. Process-only filtering (keep steps judged reasonable, even if the final answer was wrong) gave the best results for RL; outcome filtering usually hurt compared with no filter. Smaller 2B and 9B models generalized less consistently than 27B.
Takeaway
Log agent runs step by step and label steps, not only final outcomes. A failed run still contains good steps worth training on.

Language Agent Tree Search Unifies Reasoning Acting and Planning in Language Models (LATS)

Zhou et al. · 2023

What
Monte Carlo Tree Search over agent actions. Each round: select a node by UCT, expand n sampled actions (n = 5 by default) and run them in the environment, evaluate with V = λ·(LM score) + (1-λ)·(self-consistency), simulate to the end, backpropagate, and write a verbal reflection after a failure.
Result
HumanEval pass@1 92.7% with GPT-4 (83.8% with GPT-3.5, vs 68.1% for Reflexion). HotPotQA EM 0.63 vs 0.32 for ReAct (GPT-3.5, 100-question subset). WebShop score 75.9 without gradient training. Game of 24 success 0.44 vs 0.20 for Tree-of-Thoughts. It costs more than ReAct or Reflexion and assumes you can return to earlier states.
Takeaway
When you have a checkable reward (tests, a verifier), search beats a single chain. Only use it where states can be undone, and cap the expansions.

SPRINT: Enabling Interleaved Planning and Parallelized Execution in Reasoning Models

Biju et al. · 2025

What
A data pipeline rewrites long sequential reasoning traces (from DeepSeek-R1 on MATH) into rounds: a plan listing independent subtasks, then parallel execution of those subtasks. GPT-4o and GPT-4o-mini split traces into steps and build a dependency graph; about 1,700 examples fine-tune a 7B distilled reasoning model to plan and run steps in parallel.
Result
Matched reasoning baselines on math (MATH-500 92.5% vs 91.0% for the same model fine-tuned on sequential traces) while cutting sequential tokens by up to 39% on problems needing more than 8,000 tokens. The gains carried over to out-of-domain tasks: up to 45% fewer sequential tokens on GPQA and 65% on Countdown.
Takeaway
Latency is set by the longest chain of dependent steps, not the total work. Have the planner mark which steps are independent and run those in parallel.

ADaPT: As-Needed Decomposition and Planning with Language Models

Prasad et al. · 2024

What
An executor LLM first tries the whole task. Only if it reports failure does a planner split the task into a few sub-tasks joined by AND/OR, and ADaPT recurses on each, up to a max depth (3 for ALFWorld and WebShop, 4 for TextCraft). Success is the executor's own judgment.
Result
With GPT-3.5: ALFWorld 71.6% vs 43.3% ReAct and 57.5% Reflexion; WebShop 44.0% vs 32.0% ReAct; TextCraft (a new compositional dataset) 52.0% vs 19.0% ReAct. The abstract summarizes this as up to 28.3%, 27% and 33% higher success rates than strong baselines. The executor's self-reported success overestimated real success, by more than 30 points on WebShop.
Takeaway
Don't plan everything up front. Try directly, and decompose only the parts that fail. Use a real checker for 'did it work' when you can; self-reports are optimistic.

Wider or Deeper? Scaling LLM Inference-Time Compute with Adaptive Branching Tree Search (AB-MCTS)

Inoue et al. · 2025

What
A tree search where each node can generate a brand-new answer (wider, via a GEN node) or refine an existing child (deeper), chosen by Thompson sampling on feedback scores. Two variants: AB-MCTS-M (a mixed model) and AB-MCTS-A (node aggregation). Tested with GPT-4o and DeepSeek-V3 on LiveCodeBench, CodeContests, ARC-AGI and MLE-Bench.
Result
At 128 calls on CodeContests with GPT-4o: AB-MCTS-M 40.6%, repeated sampling 37.9%, standard MCTS 37.5%, pure sequential refinement 30.1%. AB-MCTS-M had the best average rank across benchmarks. On ARC-AGI at 128 calls repeated sampling was still best, but with up to 512 calls AB-MCTS kept improving while repeated sampling plateaued. Many LiveCodeBench gaps look within the reported standard deviations (our reading of the tables).
Takeaway
Neither 'sample more' nor 'refine more' always wins. Let feedback scores decide where the next call goes, and expect the gains to appear at larger budgets.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a04_planning_search.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 2
import ast, json, math, random, re, time
from fractions import Fraction
from functools import lru_cache
from typing import Literal
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from pydantic import BaseModel
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.data import contract, sections, labels, playbook
from lal.checks import check

CALLS = [0]                                   # this notebook aims for about 70-80 model calls in total
def llm(prompt, **kw):
    CALLS[0] += 1
    try:
        return ask(prompt, **kw)
    except Exception as e:
        return f"ERROR: {e}"

def llm_struct(prompt, schema, **kw):
    CALLS[0] += 1
    try:
        return structured(prompt, schema, **kw)
    except Exception:                         # bad JSON after retries: treat as "no answer"
        return None

1. The puzzle and its checker

Rules: use each number exactly once; + - * /; every intermediate result must be a whole number that is not negative. The puzzles are generated with a fixed seed, and the generator only keeps targets that a brute-force solver can reach, so every puzzle has an answer. The solver is our ground truth; the model never sees it.

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 3
OPS = {"+": lambda a, b: a + b, "-": lambda a, b: a - b if a >= b else None,
       "*": lambda a, b: a * b, "/": lambda a, b: a // b if b and a % b == 0 else None}

@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def solve(values: tuple, target: int):
    """Brute force: return an expression that reaches target, or None. values is a sorted tuple."""
    if len(values) == 1:
        return str(values[0]) if values[0] == target else None
    vals = list(values)
    for i in range(len(vals)):
        for j in range(len(vals)):
            if i == j:
                continue
            rest = [v for k, v in enumerate(vals) if k not in (i, j)]
            for op, f in OPS.items():
                r = f(vals[i], vals[j])
                if r is None:
                    continue
                sub = solve(tuple(sorted(rest + [r])), target)
                if sub is not None:
                    return f"[{vals[i]} {op} {vals[j]} = {r}] then {sub}"
    return None

@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def reachable(values: tuple) -> frozenset:
    if len(values) == 1:
        return frozenset(values)
    out = set()
    for i in range(len(values)):
        for j in range(len(values)):
            if i != j:
                rest = [v for k, v in enumerate(values) if k not in (i, j)]
                for f in OPS.values():
                    r = f(values[i], values[j])
                    if r is not None:
                        out |= reachable(tuple(sorted(rest + [r])))
    return frozenset(out)

def good_first_steps(nums, target):
    """How many legal first steps keep the target reachable. More = a friendlier puzzle."""
    n = 0
    for i in range(4):
        for j in range(4):
            if i != j:
                rest = [v for k, v in enumerate(nums) if k not in (i, j)]
                for f in OPS.values():
                    r = f(nums[i], nums[j])
                    n += r is not None and target in reachable(tuple(sorted(rest + [r])))
    return n

def make_puzzles(n, seed=7):
    """Random numbers 1-9; the target is one of the friendliest reachable targets (several paths work, but not all)."""
    rng, out = random.Random(seed), []
    while len(out) < n:
        nums = sorted(rng.randint(1, 9) for _ in range(4))
        cands = [t for t in reachable(tuple(nums)) if 12 <= t <= 40 and t != sum(nums)]   # not just "add everything"
        if not cands:
            continue
        ranked = sorted(cands, key=lambda t: -good_first_steps(nums, t))
        out.append({"numbers": nums, "target": rng.choice(ranked[:3])})
    return out

def check_expr(expr, numbers, target):
    """Exact check of a full expression. Returns (ok, value_or_None, message)."""
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except Exception:
        return False, None, "not a valid arithmetic expression"
    used = []
    def ev(n):
        if isinstance(n, ast.Expression): return ev(n.body)
        if isinstance(n, ast.Constant) and isinstance(n.value, int):
            used.append(n.value); return Fraction(n.value)
        if isinstance(n, ast.BinOp) and type(n.op) in (ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult, ast.Div):
            a, b = ev(n.left), ev(n.right)
            if isinstance(n.op, ast.Add): return a + b
            if isinstance(n.op, ast.Sub): return a - b
            if isinstance(n.op, ast.Mult): return a * b
            if b == 0: raise ZeroDivisionError
            return a / b
        raise ValueError("only numbers and + - * / are allowed")
    try:
        val = ev(tree)
    except Exception as e:
        return False, None, f"could not evaluate: {e}"
    if sorted(used) != sorted(numbers):
        return False, val, f"must use exactly the numbers {sorted(numbers)}, but used {sorted(used)}"
    if val != target:
        return False, val, f"it equals {float(val):g}, not {target}"
    return True, val, "correct"

def clean_expr(text):
    """Model replies often add words or '= 24'. Keep the last line that looks like arithmetic."""
    text = (text or "").replace("×", "*").replace("÷", "/").replace("x", "*").replace("`", "")
    lines = [l.split("=")[0] for l in text.splitlines() if re.search(r"\d\s*[-+*/]\s*\(?\d", l)]
    return re.sub(r"[^\d+\-*/() ]", "", lines[-1]).strip() if lines else ""

PUZZLES = make_puzzles(6)
P = PUZZLES[:3]                               # 3 puzzles keep us inside the call budget
for p in PUZZLES:
    print(p, "| solver:", solve(tuple(p["numbers"]), p["target"]))
print(check_expr("(9 - 1) * 3 + 2", [1, 2, 3, 9], 26), check_expr("9 * 3", [1, 2, 3, 9], 27)[2])

2. A step, a state, and a greedy chain

A state is the list of numbers still left (each remembers the expression that made it). A step combines two of them. Four numbers need three steps. When two numbers are left, the last step is easy to check in code, like running a unit test, so every method below gets that check for free. The greedy chain asks for one step at a time and never looks back.

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 4
class Step(BaseModel):
    a: int
    op: Literal["+", "-", "*", "/"]
    b: int

class Proposals(BaseModel):
    steps: list[Step]

def start(p):
    return [(n, str(n)) for n in p["numbers"]]

def apply(state, s: Step):
    """Return the new state, or None if the step is not allowed."""
    vals = [v for v, _ in state]
    if s.a not in vals:
        return None
    i = vals.index(s.a)
    rest = state[:i] + state[i + 1:]
    j = next((k for k, (v, _) in enumerate(rest) if v == s.b), None)
    if j is None:
        return None
    ea, eb = state[i][1], rest[j][1]
    r = OPS[s.op](s.a, s.b)
    if r is None:
        return None
    return rest[:j] + rest[j + 1:] + [(r, f"({ea} {s.op} {eb})")]

def finish(state, target):
    """Two numbers left: try every last step in code. Returns the full expression or None."""
    (a, ea), (b, eb) = state
    for (x, ex), (y, ey) in (((a, ea), (b, eb)), ((b, eb), (a, ea))):
        for op, f in OPS.items():
            if f(x, y) == target:
                return f"{ex} {op} {ey}"
    return None

def values(state):
    return [v for v, _ in state]

def propose(state, target, k=1, memory="", temperature=0.0):
    prompt = (f"Number puzzle. Numbers left: {values(state)}. Target: {target}.\n"
              "Pick TWO of the numbers and one operation (+, -, *, /) to combine them into one new number. "
              "Results must be whole numbers and not negative. Choose steps that keep it possible to reach the target "
              f"with the numbers that remain.\n{memory}Give {k} different option(s), best first, as a, op, b.")
    out = llm_struct(prompt, Proposals, temperature=temperature, max_tokens=300)
    return (out.steps if out else [])[:k]

def greedy(p):
    state, traj = start(p), []
    for _ in range(2):                                   # 4 numbers -> 3 -> 2
        steps = [s for s in propose(state, p["target"], k=1) if apply(state, s)]
        if not steps:
            return {"ok": False, "traj": traj, "expr": None}
        nxt = apply(state, steps[0])
        traj.append({"state": values(state), "action": steps[0].model_dump(), "next": values(nxt)})
        state = nxt
    expr = finish(state, p["target"])
    return {"ok": expr is not None, "traj": traj, "expr": expr}

greedy_runs = [greedy(p) for p in P]
for p, r in zip(P, greedy_runs):
    print(p["numbers"], "->", p["target"], "| greedy:", "SOLVED " + r["expr"] if r["ok"] else "failed",
          "| path:", [t["next"] for t in r["traj"]])
print(CALLS[0], "calls so far")

3. Tree search with backtracking (LATS, simplified)

LATS (Zhou et al. 2023, Language Agent Tree Search Unifies Reasoning, Acting, and Planning in Language Models) runs Monte Carlo Tree Search over agent steps: select a promising node, expand it with n sampled actions (n = 5 in the paper), evaluate children with a value V = λ·(LM score) + (1-λ)·(self-consistency score), backpropagate, and store a reflection after a failed path. It reached 92.7% pass@1 on HumanEval with GPT-4; on Game of 24 it scored 0.44 against 0.20 for Tree-of-Thoughts.

Our version is a small best-first search, which is the "select the highest value and backtrack" core of MCTS without the visit-count bonus: - expand: one call returns k=3 candidate steps; code drops illegal ones; - evaluate: one call rates the new states 1-10; self-consistency = how many proposals led to the same state; - terminal: with two numbers left, code checks the last step (the environment's reward); - reflection: dead ends are written into the next prompt (LATS asks the LM to write them; we let code write them, which is free).

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 5
class Scores(BaseModel):
    scores: list[int]

def rate(states, target):
    lists = "\n".join(f"{i+1}. {values(s)}" for i, s in enumerate(states))
    out = llm_struct(f"Target: {target}. For each list of numbers below, rate from 1 (impossible) to 10 (surely possible) "
                     f"how likely it is to reach the target using each number once with + - * /.\n{lists}\n"
                     f"Return exactly {len(states)} scores in order.", Scores, max_tokens=150)
    s = (out.scores if out else [])
    return [min(10, max(1, s[i])) / 10 if i < len(s) else 0.5 for i in range(len(states))]

def tree_search(p, k=3, max_expansions=4, lam=0.5):
    target, root = p["target"], start(p)
    frontier = [(1.0, 0, root, [])]                     # (value, tie-break id, state, trajectory)
    dead, expansions, uid, last = [], 0, 1, []
    while frontier and expansions < max_expansions:
        frontier.sort(key=lambda x: -x[0])
        v, _, state, traj = frontier.pop(0)             # SELECT the best open node
        memory = ("Dead ends already tried (do not repeat): " + "; ".join(dead[-4:]) + "\n") if dead else ""
        steps = propose(state, target, k=k, memory=memory, temperature=0.7)     # EXPAND
        expansions += 1
        kids = {}
        for s in steps:
            nxt = apply(state, s)
            if nxt is None:
                continue
            key = tuple(sorted(values(nxt)))
            kids.setdefault(key, [nxt, s, 0])[2] += 1     # count duplicates for self-consistency
        if not kids:
            continue
        open_kids = []
        for key, (nxt, s, count) in kids.items():
            t2 = traj + [{"state": values(state), "action": s.model_dump(), "next": values(nxt)}]
            last = t2                                     # keep the latest path, for the SWiRL section
            if len(nxt) == 2:                             # TERMINAL: code checks the last step
                expr = finish(nxt, target)
                if expr:
                    return {"ok": True, "traj": t2, "expr": expr, "expansions": expansions}
                dead.append(str(list(key)))               # REFLECTION (code-written)
            else:
                open_kids.append((key, nxt, s, count, t2))
        if open_kids:
            lm = rate([nk[1] for nk in open_kids], target)                       # EVALUATE
            for (key, nxt, s, count, t2), score in zip(open_kids, lm):
                value = lam * score + (1 - lam) * count / max(1, len(steps))
                frontier.append((value, uid, nxt, t2)); uid += 1
    return {"ok": False, "traj": last, "expr": None, "expansions": expansions, "dead": dead}

t0 = time.time()
tree_runs = [tree_search(p) for p in P]
for p, r in zip(P, tree_runs):
    print(p["numbers"], "->", p["target"], "| tree:", "SOLVED " + r["expr"] if r["ok"] else "failed",
          f"after {r['expansions']} expansions")
print(f"greedy solved {sum(r['ok'] for r in greedy_runs)}/{len(P)}, tree solved {sum(r['ok'] for r in tree_runs)}/{len(P)} "
      f"| {time.time()-t0:.0f}s, {CALLS[0]} calls so far")

The tree spends more calls, but on a dead end it can go back instead of failing. LATS's own limits apply here too: it costs more than a single chain, and it needs states you can go back to. A puzzle can; an agent that already sent an email cannot.

4. ADaPT: decompose only when needed

ADaPT (Prasad et al. 2024, ADaPT: As-Needed Decomposition and Planning with Language Models) first lets an executor try the whole task. Only if it fails does a planner split the task, and then ADaPT recurses on each piece, up to a max depth. With GPT-3.5 it beat ReAct, Plan-and-Execute and Reflexion: 71.6% vs 43.3% for ReAct on ALFWorld, 52% vs 19% on TextCraft.

Here the executor writes a whole expression in one go. If the checker rejects it, the planner picks one step, and we recurse on the smaller puzzle. (ADaPT's executor judged its own success; our checker is stricter and more honest.)

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 6
def direct(state, target, temperature=0.0, extra=""):
    nums = values(state)
    reply = llm(f"Use each of the numbers {nums} exactly once, with + - * / and parentheses, to make {target}. "
                f"{extra}Reply with only the expression, for example (8 - 2) * (3 + 1).", temperature=temperature, max_tokens=80)
    return clean_expr(reply)

def substitute(expr, state):
    """Rewrite an expression over the state's values into one over the ORIGINAL numbers."""
    pools = {}
    for v, e in state:
        pools.setdefault(v, []).append(e)
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
        class Sub(ast.NodeTransformer):
            def visit_Constant(self, node):
                return ast.parse(pools[node.value].pop(0), mode="eval").body
        return ast.unparse(Sub().visit(tree))
    except Exception:
        return None

def adapt(state, p, depth=0, d_max=2, log=None):
    log = [] if log is None else log
    expr = direct(state, p["target"])                                # 1. try directly
    full = substitute(expr, state) if expr else None
    ok = bool(full) and check_expr(full, p["numbers"], p["target"])[0]
    log.append({"depth": depth, "numbers": values(state), "try": expr, "ok": ok})
    if ok:
        return full, log
    if depth >= d_max or len(state) <= 2:
        return None, log
    steps = [s for s in propose(state, p["target"], k=1) if apply(state, s)]    # 2. only now: plan one sub-step
    if not steps:
        return None, log
    return adapt(apply(state, steps[0]), p, depth + 1, d_max, log)              # 3. recurse on the smaller task

adapt_runs = []
for p in P:
    expr, log = adapt(start(p), p)
    adapt_runs.append({"ok": expr is not None, "expr": expr, "log": log})
    print(p["numbers"], "->", p["target"], "|", " -> ".join(f"d{l['depth']}:{'OK' if l['ok'] else 'x'}" for l in log),
          "|", expr or "failed")
direct_only = sum(r["log"][0]["ok"] for r in adapt_runs)
print(f"direct only: {direct_only}/{len(P)} | ADaPT: {sum(r['ok'] for r in adapt_runs)}/{len(P)} | {CALLS[0]} calls so far")

The point is cost: easy puzzles stop after one call; only the hard ones pay for planning.

5. Wider or deeper? (AB-MCTS)

With a fixed budget of calls, you can sample new answers (wider) or refine the best one using feedback (deeper). AB-MCTS (Inoue et al. 2025, Wider or Deeper? Scaling LLM Inference-Time Compute with Adaptive Branching Tree Search) decides at every node, using Thompson sampling on the scores so far. With 128 calls of GPT-4o on CodeContests, pure refinement scored 30.1%, repeated sampling 37.9%, and AB-MCTS 40.6%. Pure "deeper" can be worse than pure "wider".

We give each strategy the same budget of 3 calls per puzzle and score answers with the checker (1.0 if correct, partial credit if it uses the right numbers and lands close).

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 7
BUDGET = 3

def score(expr, p):
    ok, val, msg = check_expr(expr, p["numbers"], p["target"]) if expr else (False, None, "empty answer")
    if ok:
        return 1.0, msg
    if val is None or "must use" in msg:
        return 0.0, msg
    return 0.5 * max(0.0, 1 - abs(float(val) - p["target"]) / p["target"]), msg

def wider(p):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        exprs = list(ex.map(lambda _: direct(start(p), p["target"], temperature=0.9), range(BUDGET)))
    return max(score(e, p)[0] for e in exprs)

def deeper(p):
    best = direct(start(p), p["target"], temperature=0.9)
    best_s, msg = score(best, p)
    for _ in range(BUDGET - 1):
        if best_s == 1.0:
            break
        e = direct(start(p), p["target"], temperature=0.7,
                   extra=f"Your last try was {best or '(empty)'}: {msg}. Fix it. ")
        s, m = score(e, p)
        if s >= best_s:
            best, best_s, msg = e, s, m
    return best_s

wd = {"wider": [wider(p) for p in P], "deeper": [deeper(p) for p in P]}
for name, ss in wd.items():
    print(f"{name:7s} solved {sum(s == 1.0 for s in ss)}/{len(P)} | mean best score {sum(ss)/len(ss):.2f}")
print(CALLS[0], "calls so far")

Three puzzles and three calls each is far too little to pick a winner; the paper needed up to 128 calls per problem. What you should take away is the knob itself: when feedback is informative ("it equals 30, not 24"), deeper helps; when the first idea is wrong at the root, only wider escapes it. AB-MCTS learns which one is paying off.

6. SPRINT: run independent steps at the same time

Reasoning models usually think in one long sequence. SPRINT (Biju et al. 2025, SPRINT: Enabling Interleaved Planning and Parallelized Execution in Reasoning Models) fine-tunes a model to alternate a plan (a list of independent subtasks) with parallel execution of those subtasks. It matched its baselines on math while using up to 39% fewer sequential tokens on long problems (and up to 45% on GPQA, 65% on Countdown).

The same idea works at the agent level. A contract review plan has four independent rule checks, then one summary that needs all four. Independent checks can run together.

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 8
PB = playbook()
WORD = {"liability_cap": "Liability", "governing_law": "Governing", "auto_renewal": "Renewal", "termination_notice": "Renewal"}
PLAN = {"liability_cap": [], "governing_law": [], "auto_renewal": [], "termination_notice": [],
        "summary": ["liability_cap", "governing_law", "auto_renewal", "termination_notice"]}

def stages(dag):
    """Group steps into rounds: a step runs once everything it depends on is done."""
    done, out = set(), []
    while len(done) < len(dag):
        ready = [s for s, deps in dag.items() if s not in done and all(d in done for d in deps)]
        if not ready:
            raise ValueError("cycle in plan")
        out.append(ready); done |= set(ready)
    return out

def check_rule(cid, rule):
    sec = next((s for s in sections(contract(cid)) if WORD[rule] in s["title"]), None)
    v = llm(f"Playbook rule: {PB['rules'][rule]}\n\nClause: {sec['text'] if sec else contract(cid)}\n\n"
            "Does the clause follow the rule? Answer PASS or FAIL only.", max_tokens=5).strip().upper()
    return rule, "FAIL" if "FAIL" in v else "PASS"

print("plan rounds:", stages(PLAN))
cid = "msa_15"
t0 = time.time(); seq = [check_rule(cid, r) for r in stages(PLAN)[0]]; t_seq = time.time() - t0
t0 = time.time()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    par = list(ex.map(lambda r: check_rule(cid, r), stages(PLAN)[0]))
t_par = time.time() - t0
summary = "Cannot sign as written: " + ", ".join(r for r, v in par if v == "FAIL") if any(v == "FAIL" for _, v in par) else "OK to sign"
print(f"sequential {t_seq:.1f}s | parallel(2) {t_par:.1f}s | {summary}")
print("truth issues:", [i["rule"] for i in labels()[cid]["issues"]], "|", CALLS[0], "calls so far")

With 2 parallel slots, 4 checks take about 2 rounds instead of 4. The planner's job is to find the independent steps; here the plan was written by hand, SPRINT trains the model to write it.

7. SWiRL: turn logged runs into step-level training data

SWiRL (Goldie et al. 2025, Synthetic Data Generation & Multi-Step RL for Reasoning & Tool Use) generates multi-step trajectories, cuts each one with k actions into k sub-trajectories (context so far → next action), filters them, and trains with RL where a model judges each step. Two findings worth knowing: with Gemma 2 27B it beat baselines by 21.5% (GSM8K) and 12.3% (HotPotQA) relative accuracy; and process-only filtering (keep steps a judge calls reasonable, even when the final answer was wrong) worked better for RL than keeping only trajectories with correct answers.

We already logged trajectories in sections 2 and 3. Our "process judge" is the solver: a step is good if the target is still reachable after it. (SWiRL used Gemini 1.5 Pro as the judge; a code judge is cheaper when you have one.)

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 9
trajectories = []
for p, g, t in zip(P, greedy_runs, tree_runs):
    for method, r in (("greedy", g), ("tree", t)):
        if r["traj"]:
            trajectories.append({"puzzle": p, "method": method, "steps": r["traj"], "outcome_ok": r["ok"]})

examples = []
for tr in trajectories:
    p = tr["puzzle"]
    for i, st in enumerate(tr["steps"]):                       # one example per action
        context = f"Numbers: {p['numbers']}. Target: {p['target']}. Steps so far: " + \
                  "; ".join(f"{s['action']['a']} {s['action']['op']} {s['action']['b']}" for s in tr["steps"][:i])
        step_ok = p["target"] in reachable(tuple(sorted(st["next"])))     # process label from the solver
        examples.append({"context": context, "action": st["action"], "process_ok": step_ok, "outcome_ok": tr["outcome_ok"]})

filters = {"no filter": lambda e: True, "outcome only": lambda e: e["outcome_ok"],
           "process only": lambda e: e["process_ok"], "process + outcome": lambda e: e["process_ok"] and e["outcome_ok"]}
for name, f in filters.items():
    kept = [e for e in examples if f(e)]
    print(f"{name:18s} keeps {len(kept):2d} of {len(examples)} step examples")
good_steps_failed_runs = sum(e["process_ok"] and not e["outcome_ok"] for e in examples)
print("good steps that live inside FAILED runs:", good_steps_failed_runs, "(an outcome filter throws these away)")
if examples:
    print("example:", json.dumps(examples[0]))

That last number is SWiRL's point in miniature: a failed run can still contain good steps, and step-level labels let you keep them.

a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 10
check("puzzle checker accepts a right answer and rejects a wrong one",
      check_expr("(9 - 1) * 3 + 2", [1, 2, 3, 9], 26)[0] and not check_expr("9 * 3", [1, 2, 3, 9], 27)[0])
check("tree search is not worse than greedy (allowing one puzzle of noise)",
      sum(r["ok"] for r in tree_runs) >= sum(r["ok"] for r in greedy_runs) - 1)
check("some method solves at least one puzzle",
      any(r["ok"] for r in greedy_runs + tree_runs + adapt_runs) or any(s == 1.0 for ss in wd.values() for s in ss))
check("ADaPT solves at least one puzzle", any(r["ok"] for r in adapt_runs))
check("SPRINT: parallel run mostly gives the same verdicts as sequential", len(set(par) & set(seq)) >= 3)
check("SWiRL: built step-level examples from logged runs", len(examples) >= 3)

Your turn

  1. Adaptive branching. Write adaptive(p): at each call, Thompson-sample between GEN (a new answer) and REFINE (fix the best answer), using Beta posteriors built from the scores each choice has produced. Same budget of 3. Compare with wider and deeper.
  2. What does the filter keep? Print the step examples that the process filter keeps but the outcome filter drops. Do they look like good steps?
  3. Plan rounds. A bigger review plan: extract parties and fees (no deps), check each of the 4 rules (each needs the extraction), compute the dollar cap (needs fees and the liability check), write the summary (needs everything). Use stages() to count rounds, and compute the best possible speedup = steps / rounds with unlimited parallel slots.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 11
# Solution 1: a tiny AB-MCTS-style choice between wider (GEN) and deeper (REFINE) at each step.
def adaptive(p, seed=0):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    stats = {"GEN": [], "REFINE": []}
    best, best_s, msg = None, -1.0, ""
    for call in range(BUDGET):
        if best is None:
            arm = "GEN"
        else:
            draws = {a: rng.betavariate(1 + sum(s), 1 + len(s) - sum(s)) for a, s in stats.items()}
            arm = max(draws, key=draws.get)
        extra = f"Your last try was {best}: {msg}. Fix it. " if arm == "REFINE" else ""
        e = direct(start(p), p["target"], temperature=0.9 if arm == "GEN" else 0.7, extra=extra)
        s, m = score(e, p)
        stats[arm].append(s)
        if s > best_s:
            best, best_s, msg = e, s, m
        if best_s == 1.0:
            break
    return best_s, {a: len(s) for a, s in stats.items()}

for p in P:
    s, used = adaptive(p)
    print(p["numbers"], "->", p["target"], f"| adaptive best score {s:.2f} | calls per arm {used}")
# Notice: the first call is always GEN (there is nothing to refine). With a budget of 3 the posterior barely moves;
# AB-MCTS shines at budgets of 32-128+ where it can learn which arm pays off on each problem.
a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 2: steps the process filter keeps but the outcome filter drops.
for e in examples:
    if e["process_ok"] and not e["outcome_ok"]:
        print(e["context"], "->", e["action"])
# Notice: these are legal steps after which the target is still reachable. The run failed LATER. Throwing them away wastes signal.
a04_planning_search.ipynb · cell 13
# Solution 3: rounds for a bigger plan.
BIG = {"extract": [], "liability_cap": ["extract"], "governing_law": ["extract"], "auto_renewal": ["extract"],
       "termination_notice": ["extract"], "dollar_cap": ["extract", "liability_cap"],
       "summary": ["liability_cap", "governing_law", "auto_renewal", "termination_notice", "dollar_cap"]}
rounds = stages(BIG)
print(rounds)
print(f"{len(BIG)} steps in {len(rounds)} rounds -> best speedup {len(BIG)/len(rounds):.2f}x")
# Notice: the longest chain of dependencies (extract -> liability -> dollar_cap -> summary) sets the floor on time,
# no matter how many parallel slots you have. This "critical path" is what SPRINT's sequential-token count measures.
print("total model calls in this notebook:", CALLS[0])
Traps
  • Searching where you can't undo actions. LATS assumes you can return to earlier states; a sent email or a database write can't be rewound.
  • Trusting the model's value scores alone. Use a real verifier when one exists; LM ratings are noisy.
  • Planning everything up front. A long fixed plan fails when any step fails; ADaPT decomposes only where needed.
  • Trusting self-reported success. ADaPT's executor overestimated success by more than 30 points on WebShop.
  • Judging wider vs deeper at tiny budgets. AB-MCTS's gains showed up at tens to hundreds of calls, and some gaps were within noise.
  • Parallelizing steps that depend on each other. Map dependencies first; the longest chain sets the floor on latency.
  • Keeping only successful runs as training data. SWiRL found process filtering beat outcome filtering for RL.

Check yourself

What does ADaPT do differently from plan-and-execute?

It tries the whole task first and only plans and splits it when the executor fails, recursing on failed parts up to a max depth, so easy tasks cost one call.

In AB-MCTS, what is the choice made at each node, and what decides it?

Whether to generate a new answer (wider) or refine an existing one (deeper); Thompson sampling on the feedback scores decides.

Why might SWiRL keep a step from a trajectory whose final answer was wrong?

Because the step itself was judged reasonable. Process-only filtering gave the best RL results, and failed runs still contain useful good steps.

Part 2 · A05

Train-time scaling and RL

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 6 (Oct 10)

If a program can check the answer, the model can improve by training on its own attempts: keep or reward what the checker accepts, and push away from what it rejects. STaR does this with supervised fine-tuning; GRPO and DAPO do it with reinforcement learning.

Key terms

Rationalization
In STaR, asking the model to explain a problem it got wrong while showing it the correct answer as a hint, then training on that explanation without the hint.Source: Zelikman et al. 2022
Verifiable reward
A reward computed by a program (exact answer match, unit tests), not by a human or a learned judge. It is what makes RL on reasoning cheap and hard to fool.Source: lecture
GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization)
Sample a group of answers per prompt; each answer's advantage is its reward minus the group mean, divided by the group std. No critic model needed.Source: Shao et al. 2024
Advantage
How much better one answer did than expected. Positive means 'do more of this', negative means 'do less'.Source: RL basics
Clip-higher
Use a larger upper clip bound than lower bound on the probability ratio (0.28 vs 0.2), so rare good tokens can grow and the model keeps exploring.Source: Yu et al. 2025
Dynamic sampling
Drop prompt groups where every answer got the same reward (zero advantage, zero gradient) and sample new prompts to fill the batch.Source: Yu et al. 2025
LoRA
Train small low-rank add-on matrices instead of all model weights. Cuts memory so RL fits a free GPU.Source: Hu et al. 2021

The papers

STaR: Bootstrapping Reasoning With Reasoning

Zelikman, Wu, Mu & Goodman · 2022

What
A loop on GPT-J (6B): generate step-by-step rationales with a few examples, keep the ones that reach the right answer, and for wrong ones generate a rationale again with the correct answer given as a hint ('rationalization'), stored without the hint. Fine-tune the original model on everything kept, then repeat.
Result
CommonsenseQA dev accuracy 72.5% with rationalization (68.8% without), vs 60.0% for GPT-J fine-tuned to answer directly and 73.0% for a fine-tuned GPT-3 that is 30x larger. GSM8K: 10.7% vs 5.8% for direct fine-tuning.
Takeaway
Before buying labeled reasoning data, let your model write it and filter by a checker. Add the answer-as-hint step to cover the problems it cannot yet solve, and watch for right answers with wrong reasoning.

DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning in Open Language Models

Shao et al. · 2024

What
Continued pre-training of a 7B model on 120B math tokens, then introduces GRPO: a PPO variant with no critic (value) model. For each question it samples a group of answers (64 in the paper) and uses each answer's reward relative to the group mean, divided by the group standard deviation, as its advantage, with a KL penalty (0.04) added directly to the loss.
Result
51.7% on MATH without tools or voting (60.9% with self-consistency over 64 samples). The GRPO stage alone took DeepSeekMath-Instruct 7B from 82.9% to 88.2% on GSM8K and 46.8% to 51.7% on MATH. RL raised Maj@K but not Pass@K.
Takeaway
GRPO is the cheapest RL recipe to start with: no critic network, only a reward function and a group of samples per prompt. Expect it to make good answers more reliable more than to teach brand-new skills (the Maj@K vs Pass@K finding).

DAPO: An Open-Source LLM Reinforcement Learning System at Scale

Yu et al. · 2025

What
Four fixes to GRPO for long reasoning: Clip-Higher (clip ratio at 0.2 below but 0.28 above, to avoid entropy collapse), Dynamic Sampling (drop prompts where all answers are right or all wrong), a token-level loss (average over all tokens, not per answer), and Overlong Reward Shaping (filter or softly penalize answers that hit the length limit). It also removes the KL penalty.
Result
50 points on AIME 2024 with Qwen2.5-32B base, vs 47 for DeepSeek-R1-Zero-Qwen-32B, using 50% of the training steps. Ablation: naive GRPO 30, +overlong filtering 36, +clip-higher 38, +soft overlong punishment 41, +token-level loss 42, +dynamic sampling 50.
Takeaway
When you run GRPO, turn on these fixes: in TRL, loss_type='dapo' (now the default), epsilon_high=0.28, a length penalty or mask_truncated_completions=True, and keep training prompts at the edge of what the model can solve so groups are not all-equal.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a05_rl_training.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 2
import json, random, re, time, urllib.request
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
import numpy as np
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.checks import check

STUDENT = "llama3.2:3b"     # a small Ollama model that still makes mistakes, so STaR has something to fix
RUN_LONG = False            # True = a longer GRPO run (about 30-40 minutes on a T4)

1. A task with exact answers

RL from rewards only works when a program can grade the answer. We generate arithmetic problems with a fixed seed, so every run sees the same problems and every answer is either right or wrong. The model must end with a line Answer: <number>. We read that line with a regex. If it is missing we fall back to the last number in the text.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 3
def make_problems(n, seed, hard=False):
    """Seeded arithmetic problems. hard=True gives 3-step problems that trip up small models."""
    rng, out = random.Random(seed), []
    for _ in range(n):
        if hard:
            a, b, c, d = rng.randint(12, 49), rng.randint(3, 9), rng.randint(10, 99), rng.randint(10, 99)
            q, ans = f"What is {a} * {b} - {c} + {d}?", a * b - c + d
        else:
            op = rng.choice(["+", "-", "*"])
            if op == "*":
                a, b = rng.randint(3, 9), rng.randint(11, 39)
            else:
                a, b = rng.randint(10, 99), rng.randint(10, 99)
            q, ans = f"What is {a} {op} {b}?", eval(f"{a}{op}{b}")       # safe: we built the string ourselves
        out.append({"question": q, "answer": int(ans)})
    return out

def extract_int(text):
    """Number after 'Answer:' if present, otherwise the last integer in the text. None if no number."""
    text = text or ""
    m = re.findall(r"Answer:\s*\**\s*(-?\d[\d,]*)", text, re.I)
    if not m:
        m = re.findall(r"-?\d[\d,]*", text)
    if not m:
        return None
    try:
        return int(m[-1].replace(",", ""))
    except ValueError:
        return None

def has_answer_line(text):
    return bool(re.search(r"Answer:\s*\**\s*-?\d", text or "", re.I))

INSTR = "Think step by step in one or two short lines, then finish with the line 'Answer: <number>'."
print(make_problems(3, seed=0), extract_int("so 12*3=36. Answer: 36"), extract_int("I think 41"))

2. STaR: the model writes its own training data

STaR (Zelikman et al. 2022, STaR: Bootstrapping Reasoning With Reasoning) is a simple loop: 1. Ask the model to reason and answer many problems. 2. Keep the reasoning only when the final answer is right. 3. For the problems it got wrong, show it the correct answer as a hint and ask it to explain. This is called rationalization. If the explanation now ends at the right answer, keep it, but store it without the hint, as if the model had found it alone. 4. Fine-tune on the kept examples and repeat.

In the paper, GPT-J (6B) with STaR reached 72.5% on CommonsenseQA, close to 73.0% for a fine-tuned GPT-3 that is 30 times larger. Here we do steps 1-3 with the 3B Ollama model and save the dataset. Step 4 is the same supervised fine-tuning you would do with any chat dataset.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 4
star_problems = make_problems(16, seed=1, hard=True)

def rationale(p, hint=None):
    prompt = f"{p['question']}\n{INSTR}"
    if hint is not None:
        prompt += f"\n(Hint: the correct answer is {hint}. Explain the steps that lead to it.)"
    try:
        return ask(prompt, model_name=STUDENT, temperature=0.7, max_tokens=200)
    except Exception as e:
        return f"(error: {e})"

t0 = time.time()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    first = list(ex.map(rationale, star_problems))                       # 16 calls
wrong = [i for i, (p, r) in enumerate(zip(star_problems, first)) if extract_int(r) != p["answer"]]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    hinted = dict(zip(wrong, ex.map(lambda i: rationale(star_problems[i], hint=star_problems[i]["answer"]), wrong)))  # <= 16 calls

sft = []
for i, (p, r) in enumerate(zip(star_problems, first)):
    if i not in wrong:
        sft.append({"source": "self", "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": f"{p['question']}\n{INSTR}"},
                                                   {"role": "assistant", "content": r.strip()}]})
    elif extract_int(hinted[i]) == p["answer"] and "hint" not in hinted[i].lower():
        # the prompt we store has NO hint: the model learns to produce this reasoning on its own
        sft.append({"source": "rationalized", "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": f"{p['question']}\n{INSTR}"},
                                                           {"role": "assistant", "content": hinted[i].strip()}]})
with open("star_sft.jsonl", "w") as f:
    for row in sft:
        f.write(json.dumps(row) + "\n")

n_self = sum(r["source"] == "self" for r in sft)
print(f"{len(star_problems)} problems: {len(star_problems) - len(wrong)} right on the first try, {len(wrong)} wrong")
print(f"kept {n_self} self-made + {len(sft) - n_self} rationalized examples -> star_sft.jsonl  [{time.time() - t0:.0f}s]")
if sft:
    print("\nexample:", sft[0]["messages"][1]["content"][:300])

Two things to notice: - The checker is the teacher. No human wrote any of these explanations. We only kept the ones whose final number was right. - A right answer can still have a wrong explanation. Rationalized examples are the riskiest: the model knew the answer and may have bent the steps to fit. We drop any reply that mentions the hint, and in real use you would also check the steps (for arithmetic you can recompute each line).

3. GRPO by hand: grade against the group, not against a critic

GRPO (Shao et al. 2024, DeepSeekMath) is the RL method behind many recent reasoning models. For each prompt: 1. Sample a group of G answers (the paper used G = 64). 2. Score each one with the reward. 3. The advantage of an answer is how much better it did than its group: (reward - group mean) / group std. 4. Raise the probability of tokens in answers with positive advantage, lower it for negative ones.

PPO needs a second network (a "critic" or value model) to guess the expected reward. GRPO uses the group mean instead, which saves a lot of memory. In the paper, GRPO lifted DeepSeekMath-Instruct 7B from 82.9% to 88.2% on GSM8K and from 46.8% to 51.7% on MATH.

Let us get real rewards: 4 samples each for 3 problems from the 3B model (12 calls).

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 5
REWARD_CORRECT, REWARD_FORMAT = 1.0, 0.2

def score(text, gold):
    return (REWARD_CORRECT if extract_int(text) == gold else 0.0) + (REWARD_FORMAT if has_answer_line(text) else 0.0)

G = 4
group_problems = make_problems(3, seed=2, hard=True)
jobs = [p for p in group_problems for _ in range(G)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    outs = list(ex.map(rationale, jobs))
groups = [np.array([score(o, p["answer"]) for o, p in zip(outs[k * G:(k + 1) * G], jobs[k * G:(k + 1) * G])])
          for k in range(len(group_problems))]

def grpo_advantages(r, eps=1e-4):
    r = np.asarray(r, dtype=float)
    return (r - r.mean()) / (r.std() + eps)

for p, r in zip(group_problems, groups):
    print(f"{p['question']:<32} rewards {r}  ->  advantages {grpo_advantages(r).round(2)}")

Read the output like this: a group where every answer got the same reward has advantage 0 for all of them. That prompt teaches the model nothing, even though we paid to generate it. DAPO fixes exactly this.

4. What DAPO changes (Yu et al. 2025, DAPO)

DAPO starts from GRPO and adds four fixes. In their ablation on AIME 2024 with Qwen2.5-32B, plain GRPO scored 30, and the full DAPO recipe scored 50. That beat DeepSeek-R1-Zero-Qwen-32B (47) with 50% of its training steps. DAPO also drops the KL penalty.

Fix Problem it solves In TRL's GRPOConfig
Clip-higher: clip the probability ratio at 1-0.2 below but 1+0.28 above With symmetric clipping, rare but good tokens cannot grow fast, so the model stops exploring ("entropy collapse") epsilon=0.2, epsilon_high=0.28
Dynamic sampling: drop groups where all rewards are equal, sample more until the batch is full Those groups give zero gradient and waste the batch not built in; we measure it below
Token-level loss: average the loss over all tokens in the batch, not per answer first Per-answer averaging lets a long bad answer count the same as a short one loss_type="dapo" (TRL's default now)
Overlong reward shaping: soft penalty that grows as an answer nears the length limit Cut-off answers get a reward of 0 for the wrong reason, which adds noise a reward function, below; or mask_truncated_completions=True

Here is dynamic sampling on the rewards we just collected, and the soft overlong penalty as a function.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 6
def dynamic_sampling(groups):
    """Keep only groups whose rewards are not all equal (DAPO)."""
    return [g for g in groups if np.ptp(g) > 0]

def overlong_penalty(n_tokens, l_max=96, l_cache=32):
    """DAPO soft overlong punishment: 0 until l_max - l_cache tokens, then down to -1 at l_max."""
    if n_tokens <= l_max - l_cache:
        return 0.0
    if n_tokens <= l_max:
        return (l_max - l_cache - n_tokens) / l_cache
    return -1.0

kept = dynamic_sampling(groups)
print(f"dynamic sampling keeps {len(kept)} of {len(groups)} groups")
print("overlong penalty at 40, 70, 90, 96 tokens:", [round(overlong_penalty(n), 2) for n in (40, 70, 90, 96)])

5. A real GRPO run: SmolLM2-360M + LoRA on a T4

Now we train for real with Hugging Face TRL's GRPOTrainer. - Model: HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M-Instruct, a small open model. We train only a LoRA adapter (low-rank extra weights, about 1% of the model), so memory stays small. - Rewards: +1.0 for the exact answer, +0.2 for the Answer: line, and DAPO's soft overlong penalty (scaled by 0.5). - Group size 8, completions of at most 96 tokens, 120 steps of 2 prompts each. - Precision: the T4 has no bfloat16, so we keep the weights in float32 and use float16 mixed precision. TRL's config turns bf16 on by default, so we switch it off explicitly.

First we free the GPU: Ollama keeps the Llama models loaded, and we no longer need them.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 7
def unload_ollama(models):
    for m in models:
        try:
            req = urllib.request.Request("http://localhost:11434/api/generate",
                                         data=json.dumps({"model": m, "keep_alive": 0}).encode(),
                                         headers={"Content-Type": "application/json"})
            urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=30).read()
        except Exception as e:
            print("could not unload", m, e)
unload_ollama([MODEL, STUDENT])

import torch
from datasets import Dataset
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
from peft import LoraConfig
from trl import GRPOConfig, GRPOTrainer

HAS_GPU = torch.cuda.is_available()
BF16_OK = HAS_GPU and torch.cuda.get_device_capability()[0] >= 8     # Ampere or newer. T4 is 7.5 -> False
MAX_STEPS = (400 if RUN_LONG else 120) if HAS_GPU else 5              # on CPU, just prove the code runs
MAX_NEW = 96
MID = "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M-Instruct"

tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MID)
tok.padding_side = "left"                     # GRPO and batched generation need left padding
if tok.pad_token is None:
    tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token
policy = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(MID).float()       # float32 weights; .float() also undoes any bf16 default
if HAS_GPU:
    policy = policy.to("cuda")
print("GPU:", torch.cuda.get_device_name(0) if HAS_GPU else "none", "| bf16 supported:", BF16_OK, "| steps:", MAX_STEPS)

def to_prompt(q):
    return [{"role": "user", "content": f"{q}\n{INSTR}"}]

train_rows = make_problems(MAX_STEPS * 2 + 50, seed=100)
test_rows = make_problems(100 if RUN_LONG else 60, seed=999)        # different seed = held-out problems
train_ds = Dataset.from_list([{"prompt": to_prompt(r["question"]), "answer": r["answer"], "question": r["question"]}
                              for r in train_rows])

A greedy evaluation on the held-out problems, so we can compare before and after.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 8
@torch.no_grad()
def evaluate(model, rows, batch=30):
    model.eval()
    right, fmt = 0, 0
    for i in range(0, len(rows), batch):
        chunk = rows[i:i + batch]
        texts = [tok.apply_chat_template(to_prompt(r["question"]), tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True) for r in chunk]
        enc = tok(texts, return_tensors="pt", padding=True).to(model.device)
        out = model.generate(**enc, max_new_tokens=MAX_NEW, do_sample=False, pad_token_id=tok.pad_token_id)
        for r, ids in zip(chunk, out[:, enc["input_ids"].shape[1]:]):
            t = tok.decode(ids, skip_special_tokens=True)
            right += extract_int(t) == r["answer"]
            fmt += has_answer_line(t)
    return right / len(rows), fmt / len(rows)

acc_before, fmt_before = evaluate(policy, test_rows)
print(f"BEFORE training: accuracy {acc_before:.0%}, uses 'Answer:' line {fmt_before:.0%}")

The reward functions. TRL calls each one with the batch of completions and every extra dataset column (here answer and question) as keyword arguments, and expects one float per completion. Our prompts are chat messages, so each completion arrives as a list of messages. The helper handles both shapes. We also log every reward with its question so we can study the groups afterwards.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 9
reward_log = []          # (question, total reward) for every completion TRL generates

def _text(c):
    if isinstance(c, list):                                   # conversational format
        return " ".join(m.get("content", "") for m in c if isinstance(m, dict))
    return c or ""

def correct_reward(completions, answer, question=None, **kwargs):
    texts = [_text(c) for c in completions]
    rs = [REWARD_CORRECT if extract_int(t) == int(a) else 0.0 for t, a in zip(texts, answer)]
    fm = [REWARD_FORMAT if has_answer_line(t) else 0.0 for t in texts]
    for q, r, f in zip(question or [""] * len(rs), rs, fm):
        reward_log.append((q, r + f))
    return rs

def format_reward(completions, **kwargs):
    return [REWARD_FORMAT if has_answer_line(_text(c)) else 0.0 for c in completions]

def overlong_reward(completion_ids, **kwargs):
    return [0.5 * overlong_penalty(len(ids), l_max=MAX_NEW, l_cache=32) for ids in completion_ids]

# quick self-test on fake completions, so a typo fails here and not after 10 minutes
fake = [[{"role": "assistant", "content": "7*12=84. Answer: 84"}], [{"role": "assistant", "content": "maybe 80"}]]
print(correct_reward(fake, answer=[84, 84], question=["q", "q"]), format_reward(fake), overlong_reward([[0] * 10, [0] * 90]))
reward_log.clear()
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 10
config = GRPOConfig(
    output_dir="grpo_smollm2",
    max_steps=MAX_STEPS,
    per_device_train_batch_size=16,     # 16 completions per step ...
    num_generations=8,                  # ... = 2 prompts x 8 samples. The batch must divide by num_generations.
    gradient_accumulation_steps=1,
    max_completion_length=MAX_NEW,
    temperature=1.0,
    learning_rate=3e-5,                 # LoRA tolerates a higher rate than full fine-tuning (TRL default is 1e-6)
    beta=0.0,                           # no KL penalty, as in DAPO; also means no reference model in memory
    epsilon=0.2, epsilon_high=0.28,     # DAPO clip-higher
    loss_type="dapo",                   # DAPO token-level loss
    scale_rewards="group",              # divide by the group std, as in the GRPO paper
    bf16=BF16_OK,                       # must be False on a T4
    fp16=HAS_GPU and not BF16_OK,
    gradient_checkpointing=False,       # the model is small; this keeps the PEFT setup simple
    logging_steps=10,
    save_strategy="no",
    report_to="none",
    seed=0,
)
lora = LoraConfig(r=16, lora_alpha=32, lora_dropout=0.0, task_type="CAUSAL_LM",
                  target_modules=["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj"])
trainer = GRPOTrainer(model=policy, reward_funcs=[correct_reward, format_reward, overlong_reward],
                      args=config, train_dataset=train_ds, processing_class=tok, peft_config=lora)
try:
    trainer.model.print_trainable_parameters()
except Exception:
    pass

t0 = time.time()
trainer.train()
train_minutes = (time.time() - t0) / 60
print(f"trained {MAX_STEPS} steps in {train_minutes:.1f} min")
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 11
acc_after, fmt_after = evaluate(trainer.model, test_rows)
print(f"held-out accuracy:   {acc_before:.0%} -> {acc_after:.0%}")
print(f"'Answer:' line rate: {fmt_before:.0%} -> {fmt_after:.0%}")
hist = [h for h in trainer.state.log_history if "reward" in h]
if hist:
    print("mean training reward every 10 steps:", [round(h["reward"], 2) for h in hist])

What to expect: the format reward is learned first (the Answer: rate jumps), and accuracy moves a few points. 120 steps of 2 prompts is a very small run. The point is that the whole loop, sample, grade, compare to the group, update, fits on a free GPU.

6. Dynamic sampling on the real training data

How much of our training batch was wasted on groups where all 8 answers scored the same? TRL also logs this as frac_reward_zero_std.

a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 12
from collections import defaultdict
by_q = defaultdict(list)
for q, r in reward_log:
    by_q[q].append(r)
train_groups = [np.array(v) for v in by_q.values() if len(v) >= 2]
zero_var = sum(np.ptp(g) == 0 for g in train_groups)
print(f"{len(train_groups)} groups, {zero_var} ({zero_var / max(1, len(train_groups)):.0%}) had identical rewards -> zero gradient")
print(f"DAPO dynamic sampling would drop them and sample new prompts, keeping {len(dynamic_sampling(train_groups))} useful groups")
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 13
check("STaR produced at least 4 training examples", len(sft) >= 4)
check("GRPO advantages sum to about 0 in each group", all(abs(grpo_advantages(g).sum()) < 1e-6 for g in groups))
check("training ran and logged rewards", len(reward_log) > 0)
check("held-out accuracy did not drop by more than 5 points", acc_after >= acc_before - 0.05)

Your turn

  1. Turn off the format reward (remove format_reward and set REWARD_FORMAT = 0). Does accuracy still rise, or does the model never learn the Answer: line?
  2. Change the training problems so they are all very easy (only + with one-digit numbers). What happens to the share of zero-variance groups, and why does that matter?
  3. Fine-tune SmolLM2 on star_sft.jsonl with TRL's SFTTrainer. That is the last step of the STaR loop.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 14
# Solution 1: correctness reward only. Retrains the model, so it is off by default (~8 minutes).
RUN_NO_FORMAT = False
if RUN_NO_FORMAT:
    p2 = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(MID).float().to("cuda" if HAS_GPU else "cpu")
    t2 = GRPOTrainer(model=p2, reward_funcs=[correct_reward], args=config, train_dataset=train_ds,
                     processing_class=tok, peft_config=lora)
    t2.train()
    print("no format reward -> accuracy, format rate:", evaluate(t2.model, test_rows))
# Notice: the fallback parser (last number) still pays for right answers, so accuracy can rise,
# but the Answer: line appears less often. A small shaping reward makes the output easy to grade.
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 15
# Solution 2: easy problems give mostly all-correct groups -> zero gradient.
fake_groups = [np.array([1.2] * 8)] * 15 + [np.array([1.2] * 7 + [0.2])] * 5     # 20 easy prompts, 8 samples each
print(f"if 15 of 20 easy groups are all correct, dynamic sampling keeps {len(dynamic_sampling(fake_groups))} of 20")
# Notice: RL learns only where the model is sometimes right and sometimes wrong. Pick training
# problems near the edge of what the model can do. This is why DAPO's dynamic sampling helps.
a05_rl_training.ipynb · cell 16
# Solution 3: the last STaR step, supervised fine-tuning on the kept rationales (~2 minutes on a T4).
RUN_STAR_SFT = False
if RUN_STAR_SFT:
    from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer
    sft_ds = Dataset.from_list([{"messages": r["messages"]} for r in sft])
    base = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(MID).float().to("cuda" if HAS_GPU else "cpu")
    st = SFTTrainer(model=base, train_dataset=sft_ds, processing_class=tok, peft_config=lora,
                    args=SFTConfig(output_dir="star_sft", num_train_epochs=3, per_device_train_batch_size=4,
                                   learning_rate=1e-4, bf16=BF16_OK, fp16=HAS_GPU and not BF16_OK,
                                   save_strategy="no", report_to="none"))
    st.train()
    print("after STaR SFT -> accuracy, format rate:", evaluate(st.model, test_rows))
# Notice: STaR restarts from the BASE model each outer loop and trains on the growing dataset,
# rather than training the same model again and again. That limits drift.
Traps
  • Training on problems that are too easy or too hard: every group is all-correct or all-wrong, the advantages are all zero, and nothing is learned even though the run looks busy.
  • A reward that can be gamed. If the parser takes 'the last number in the text', the model may learn to print many numbers. Check the format strictly or reward it separately.
  • Right answer, wrong reasoning. STaR and outcome rewards both accept lucky or bent reasoning; spot-check rationales, especially rationalized ones.
  • bf16 on a T4. The T4 has no bfloat16 and TRL's GRPOConfig turns bf16 on by default; set bf16=False and use fp16 mixed precision or fp32.
