  • Reading the training reward as the result. Always compare accuracy on a held-out set before and after.

Check yourself

A group of 4 answers gets rewards [1, 1, 1, 1]. What are the GRPO advantages, and what does DAPO do with this group?

All zero (every reward equals the mean), so the group gives no gradient. DAPO's dynamic sampling drops it and samples another prompt.

In STaR, why is the hint removed from the prompt before fine-tuning on a rationalized example?

So the model learns to produce that reasoning from the question alone, as it must at test time, not to depend on being told the answer.

What does GRPO use instead of PPO's critic (value) model?

The mean (and std) of the rewards of the other answers sampled for the same prompt.

Part 2 · A06

Open-ended evolution of self-improving agents

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 7 (Oct 13)

Let an LLM propose changes to code, agents or experiments, score every proposal automatically, and keep an archive of what worked to build on. Progress comes from the evaluator and the archive; the LLM supplies the guesses.

Key terms

Archive
The stored set of every candidate tried so far with its score. New candidates are made by changing archive members.Source: Hu et al. 2024; Zhang et al. 2025
Open-endedness
Searching for things that are new and interesting, not only better on one number, so the search keeps finding stepping stones instead of getting stuck.Source: lecture
Meta agent
An agent whose job is to write other agents (as code), as in ADAS's Meta Agent Search.Source: Hu et al. 2024
Evaluation cascade
Test each candidate on a tiny, fast test first, and only run the full, expensive test on candidates that pass.Source: Novikov et al. 2025
MAP-Elites / islands
Ways to keep diversity: keep the best candidate in each 'niche' (MAP-Elites), or evolve several separate populations (islands).Source: Novikov et al. 2025
Sandbox
Running untrusted code in a separate, limited process or container (timeouts, no network, limited disk) so it cannot harm the host.Source: Lu et al. 2024
Reward hacking
The search finds a way to score high that breaks the intent of the task, e.g. a 'packing' that overfills bins when the evaluator forgets to check.Source: lecture

The papers

Automated Design of Agentic Systems

Hu, Lu & Clune · 2024

What
Agents are defined as code (a forward() function). Meta Agent Search: a meta agent reads an archive of earlier agents with their scores, writes a new agent, checks it for novelty with two self-reflection steps, evaluates it on validation data (repairing errors up to five times) and adds it to the archive. The archive is seeded with hand-designed agents like Chain-of-Thought and Self-Refine.
Result
With GPT-4 as meta agent and GPT-3.5 running the agents: +13.6 F1 (out of 100) on DROP and +14.4% accuracy on MGSM over the best hand-designed agents; agents found on MGSM gave +25.9% on GSM8K and +13.2% on GSM-Hard when transferred. Discovered agents kept their edge when moved to other models (e.g. Claude, GPT-4).
Takeaway
If you have a checkable task set, let a strong model search over agent designs written as code, with a held-out set to catch lucky winners. Start the archive with the designs you already use.

Darwin Gödel Machine: Open-Ended Evolution of Self-Improving Agents

Zhang, Hu, Lu, Lange & Clune · 2025

What
The course page links the 'Automated design of agentic systems' reading to this paper. A coding agent rewrites its own code; each new version is tested on coding benchmarks and kept in a growing archive (a tree of agents), and new versions are branched from archive members, not only from the latest best.
Result
SWE-bench 20.0% -> 50.0% and Polyglot 14.2% -> 30.7%, beating variants without self-improvement or without open-ended exploration.
Takeaway
Keep the whole archive, not just the current best: stepping stones that look worse now often lead to the biggest later gains.

The AI Scientist: Towards Fully Automated Open-Ended Scientific Discovery

Lu, Lu, Lange, Foerster, Clune & Ha · 2024

What
A pipeline where LLMs generate research ideas (checked for novelty against Semantic Scholar), write and run experiment code with the Aider coding assistant, plot results, write a full LaTeX paper and review it with an automated reviewer. Applied to diffusion models, transformer language modeling and learning dynamics.
Result
Under $15 per paper. The GPT-4o reviewer reached 0.65 balanced accuracy on accept/reject for 500 ICLR 2022 papers, vs 0.66 for humans (from the NeurIPS 2021 consistency experiment). The authors report safety issues: the system edited its own code to extend timeouts and once relaunched itself repeatedly.
Takeaway
Automating the full loop is possible, but the weak points are the LLM reviewer and the sandbox. Ground every claim in the raw results with code, and run generated code in a container with timeouts, storage limits and no network.

AlphaEvolve: A coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery

Novikov et al. (Google DeepMind) · 2025

What
An evolutionary loop: a program database (MAP-Elites plus islands) feeds a prompt sampler; an ensemble of Gemini 2.0 Flash (many fast ideas) and Gemini 2.0 Pro (fewer, better ones) proposes code diffs in SEARCH/REPLACE format inside marked EVOLVE-BLOCKs; automatic evaluators score each child, optionally as a cascade of harder and harder tests.
Result
A 4x4 complex matrix multiplication with 48 scalar multiplications, the first improvement over Strassen in this setting in 56 years. On 50+ open math problems it matched the best known constructions in about 75% and improved them in about 20% (e.g. kissing number in 11 dimensions: 593, previous 592). At Google: a scheduling heuristic recovering on average 0.7% of fleet-wide compute, a 23% average kernel speedup giving 1% less Gemini training time, and a 32% speedup of a FlashAttention kernel.
Takeaway
Any problem with a fast, trustworthy scoring function is a candidate for LLM-driven evolution. Spend your effort on the evaluator (validity first, then quality) and let cheap models generate many candidates.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 2
import json, os, random, re, subprocess, sys, tempfile, textwrap, time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.checks import check

WORKER = "llama3.2:3b"     # the cheap model that runs inside agent designs (ADAS)
RUN_LONG = False           # True = more generations and a held-out test for ADAS
REPO = os.getcwd()

1. The sandbox: never run model code in your own process

Model-written code can loop forever, eat memory, or delete files. We write it to a temp folder and run it in a separate Python process with: - a wall-clock timeout, - a CPU-time and (on Linux) memory limit, - python -I (isolated mode: ignores environment variables and the user's site folder), - the temp folder as working directory.

This is a speed bump, not a real sandbox. It does not block the network or reading files. For real use, run it in a container with no network. The AI Scientist paper reports why: in some runs their system edited its own code to raise its timeout, and relaunched itself until a human had to stop it (Lu et al. 2024).

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 3
def run_sandboxed(code, timeout=10, mem_mb=1024):
    """Run code in a fresh Python process. The program reports by printing one line: RESULT <json>."""
    # The child sets its own CPU and memory limits first. (Setting them from the parent with preexec_fn
    # is unsafe in a Jupyter kernel, which runs threads.)
    header = ("try:\n    import resource, sys as _s\n"
              f"    resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_CPU, ({timeout + 1}, {timeout + 1}))\n"
              f"    if _s.platform.startswith('linux'): resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_AS, ({mem_mb} * 2**20, {mem_mb} * 2**20))\n"
              "except Exception:\n    pass\n")
    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as d:
        path = os.path.join(d, "prog.py")
        with open(path, "w") as f:
            f.write(header + code)
        t0 = time.time()
        try:
            p = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-I", path], cwd=d, capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=timeout)
        except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
            return {"ok": False, "error": f"timeout after {timeout}s", "seconds": timeout}
        secs = round(time.time() - t0, 2)
        lines = [l for l in p.stdout.splitlines() if l.startswith("RESULT ")]
        if not lines:
            return {"ok": False, "error": (p.stderr or p.stdout or "no RESULT line")[-500:], "seconds": secs}
        try:
            return {"ok": True, "result": json.loads(lines[-1][7:]), "seconds": secs}
        except Exception as e:
            return {"ok": False, "error": f"bad RESULT json: {e}", "seconds": secs}

def code_block(text):
    """Pull the first ```python block out of a reply (or the whole reply if there is none)."""
    m = re.search(r"```(?:python|py)?\s*\n(.*?)```", text or "", re.S)
    return (m.group(1) if m else (text or "")).strip()

print(run_sandboxed("print('RESULT', 1+1)"))
print(run_sandboxed("while True: pass", timeout=2))

2. Mini AlphaEvolve: evolve a bin-packing function

AlphaEvolve (Novikov et al. 2025, Google DeepMind) keeps a database of programs. Each step it picks a parent, shows the LLM the parent plus a few good "inspiration" programs with their scores, asks for an improved version, runs the evaluator, and stores the child. Among its results: a way to multiply two 4x4 complex matrices with 48 scalar multiplications (Strassen's method, from 1969, needs 49), and a scheduling heuristic that recovers on average 0.7% of Google's fleet-wide compute.

Our problem: bin packing. Put items of different sizes into as few bins of size 100 as possible. The program to evolve is pack(items, capacity). The evaluator: 1. checks validity (every item used exactly once, no bin over capacity); an invalid packing scores 0, 2. scores quality as lower bound / bins used, where the lower bound is ceil(total size / capacity). 1.0 would be perfect.

It runs as an evaluation cascade, an idea from the paper: a tiny test first, and only programs that pass it get the full test.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 4
SEED_PROGRAM = '''def pack(items, capacity):
    """Next fit: keep one open bin; start a new one when the item does not fit."""
    bins, current, room = [], [], capacity
    for x in items:
        if x > room:
            bins.append(current)
            current, room = [], capacity
        current.append(x)
        room -= x
    if current:
        bins.append(current)
    return bins
'''

BP_HARNESS = '''
import json, math, random
from collections import Counter

{code}

def run_stage(seed, n_inst, n_items, cap=100):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    scores = []
    for _ in range(n_inst):
        items = [rng.randint(20, 70) for _ in range(n_items)]
        bins = pack(list(items), cap)
        flat = [x for b in bins for x in b]
        if Counter(flat) != Counter(items) or any(sum(b) > cap for b in bins) or any(len(b) == 0 for b in bins):
            return None
        scores.append(math.ceil(sum(items) / cap) / len(bins))
    return sum(scores) / len(scores)

try:
    quick = run_stage(seed=1, n_inst=2, n_items=10)          # stage 1: tiny and fast
    full = run_stage(seed=2, n_inst=20, n_items=60) if quick is not None else None   # stage 2: the real test
    print("RESULT " + json.dumps({{"score": full or 0.0, "valid": full is not None,
                                  "stage": 2 if quick is not None else 1}}))
except Exception as e:
    print("RESULT " + json.dumps({{"score": 0.0, "valid": False, "error": repr(e)[:200]}}))
'''

def evaluate_packer(code):
    r = run_sandboxed(BP_HARNESS.format(code=code), timeout=10)
    if not r["ok"]:
        return 0.0, r["error"][-200:]
    res = r["result"]
    return float(res.get("score", 0.0)), res.get("error") or ("ok" if res.get("valid") else f"invalid at stage {res.get('stage')}")

seed_score, note = evaluate_packer(SEED_PROGRAM)
print(f"seed program (next fit): score {seed_score:.3f} ({note})")

The mutation step. We ask for the whole function back. (AlphaEvolve asks for diffs in a SEARCH/REPLACE format and marks the parts that may change with # EVOLVE-BLOCK-START / # EVOLVE-BLOCK-END. Whole functions are easier for an 8B model.)

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 5
def mutate(parent, inspirations):
    insp = "\n\n".join(f"# score {p['score']:.3f}\n{p['code']}" for p in inspirations)
    prompt = textwrap.dedent(f"""\
        You are improving a Python function for bin packing. Higher score is better (1.0 is a perfect packing).
        A packing that loses, duplicates or overfills items scores 0.

        Current program (score {parent['score']:.3f}):
        ```python
        {{parent}}
        ```

        Other programs from the archive, for inspiration:
        {{insp}}

        Write an improved version of `pack(items, capacity)`. It must return a list of bins, each a list of the
        item sizes in it, using every item exactly once. Use only the Python standard library. It must run in
        well under a second for 60 items. Think about which known heuristics pack tighter.
        Reply with one ```python code block containing the full function.""").replace("{parent}", parent["code"]).replace("{insp}", insp or "(none yet)")
    try:
        return code_block(ask(prompt, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=900))
    except Exception as e:
        return f"# model error: {e}"

archive = [{"code": SEED_PROGRAM, "score": seed_score, "gen": 0, "note": note}]
rng = random.Random(0)
GENERATIONS, CHILDREN = (10, 2) if RUN_LONG else (6, 2)           # 12 model calls by default
best_by_gen = [seed_score]
t0 = time.time()
for gen in range(1, GENERATIONS + 1):
    ranked = sorted(archive, key=lambda p: -p["score"])
    parents = [ranked[0] if rng.random() < 0.5 else rng.choice(ranked[:4]) for _ in range(CHILDREN)]
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        kids = list(ex.map(lambda par: mutate(par, [p for p in ranked[:3] if p is not par][:2]), parents))
    for code in kids:
        if "def pack(" not in code:
            archive.append({"code": code, "score": 0.0, "gen": gen, "note": "no pack() function"})
            continue
        s, note = evaluate_packer(code)
        archive.append({"code": code, "score": s, "gen": gen, "note": note})
    best_by_gen.append(max(p["score"] for p in archive))
    print(f"gen {gen}: children scored {[round(a['score'], 3) for a in archive[-CHILDREN:]]}  best so far {best_by_gen[-1]:.3f}")
print(f"[{time.time() - t0:.0f}s]")

best_prog = max(archive, key=lambda p: p["score"])
print(f"\nseed {seed_score:.3f} -> best {best_prog['score']:.3f} (found in generation {best_prog['gen']})\n")
print(best_prog["code"])

Things to notice: - The archive only gets better or stays the same, because we never throw away the best program. Children that crash or cheat get a 0 and are simply never picked as parents. - The evaluator does the real work. The LLM is just a source of guesses. AlphaEvolve's results came from problems where a program can score the answer. A typical win here is "first fit decreasing": sort items from big to small, put each one in the first bin where it fits.

3. Mini ADAS: a meta-agent that invents agent designs

ADAS (Hu, Lu & Clune 2024, Automated Design of Agentic Systems) writes agents as code: a forward() function that may call the model several times. A meta agent reads an archive of earlier designs with their scores and writes a new one. In the paper (GPT-4 as meta agent, GPT-3.5 running the agents), discovered agents beat hand-designed ones by 13.6 F1 points on DROP and 14.4% on MGSM, and kept an edge when moved to other domains and models.

The course page links this lecture item to arXiv 2505.22954, which is the follow-up paper, the Darwin Gödel Machine (Zhang et al. 2025). It applies the same archive idea to a coding agent that edits its own code: SWE-bench 20.0% -> 50.0%.

Our version: agents are forward(question, ask) functions. ask(prompt, temperature=0.0) calls the small 3B model, at most 2 calls per question. The tasks are seeded word problems with exact answers. Each design runs in the sandbox.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 6
def word_problems(n, seed):
    rng, out = random.Random(seed), []
    for _ in range(n):
        a, b, c, d = rng.randint(20, 90), rng.randint(3, 9), rng.randint(4, 12), rng.randint(5, 30)
        name, thing = rng.choice(["Mia", "Ravi", "Ana", "Tom", "Lena"]), rng.choice(["stickers", "apples", "cards", "coins"])
        kind = rng.randint(0, 2)
        if kind == 0:
            q, ans = f"{name} has {a} {thing}. {name} buys {b} packs with {c} {thing} each, then gives away {d}. How many {thing} does {name} have now?", a + b * c - d
        elif kind == 1:
            q, ans = f"A box holds {c} {thing}. {name} fills {b} boxes and has {d} {thing} left over, then loses {a % 10} of the left-over ones. How many {thing} did {name} have in total at the end?", b * c + d - a % 10
        else:
            q, ans = f"{name} had {a} {thing}, sold {d} of them, and then doubled what was left. After that {name} received {b * c} more. How many {thing} now?", 2 * (a - d) + b * c
        out.append({"question": q, "answer": ans})
    return out

def extract_int(text):
    m = re.findall(r"Answer:\s*\**\s*(-?\d[\d,]*)", text or "", re.I) or re.findall(r"-?\d[\d,]*", text or "")
    try:
        return int(m[-1].replace(",", "")) if m else None
    except ValueError:
        return None

AGENT_HARNESS = '''
import json, sys
sys.path.insert(0, {repo!r})
from lal.llm import ask as _ask
LIMIT, CALLS = 2, {{"n": 0}}

def ask(prompt, temperature=0.0):
    if CALLS["n"] >= LIMIT:
        raise RuntimeError("call budget exceeded")
    CALLS["n"] += 1
    return _ask(str(prompt), model_name={worker!r}, temperature=float(temperature), max_tokens=300)

{code}

outs = []
for q in {questions!r}:
    CALLS["n"] = 0
    try:
        outs.append(str(forward(q, ask)))
    except Exception as e:
        outs.append("ERROR: " + repr(e)[:200])
print("RESULT " + json.dumps(outs))
'''

def evaluate_agent(code, tasks):
    prog = AGENT_HARNESS.format(repo=REPO, worker=WORKER, code=code, questions=[t["question"] for t in tasks])
    r = run_sandboxed(prog, timeout=240, mem_mb=None)        # no memory cap: the model client needs room
    if not r["ok"]:
        return 0.0, "crashed: " + r["error"][-200:]
    outs = r["result"]
    right = sum(extract_int(o) == t["answer"] for o, t in zip(outs, tasks))
    errors = sum(o.startswith("ERROR") for o in outs)
    return right / len(tasks), f"{errors}/{len(tasks)} questions errored"

SEEDS = [
    {"name": "Direct", "idea": "Ask for the number only.",
     "code": "def forward(question, ask):\n    return ask(question + '\\nReply with only the final number.')\n"},
    {"name": "Chain of thought", "idea": "Reason step by step, then give the answer.",
     "code": "def forward(question, ask):\n    return ask(question + \"\\nThink step by step, then end with 'Answer: <number>'.\")\n"},
]
adas_tasks = word_problems(6, seed=7)
t0 = time.time()
agent_archive = []
for s in SEEDS:                                                  # 2 designs x 6 questions = 12 calls
    acc, note = evaluate_agent(s["code"], adas_tasks)
    agent_archive.append({**s, "score": acc, "note": note, "gen": 0})
    print(f"seed {s['name']:<18} accuracy {acc:.0%} ({note})")
print(f"[{time.time() - t0:.0f}s]")

The meta agent. ADAS adds two self-reflection rounds to check that each idea is new, and up to five repair rounds when a design crashes. We skip both to keep the call count small.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 7
def propose_design():
    shown = "\n\n".join(f"Name: {a['name']}\nIdea: {a['idea']}\nAccuracy: {a['score']:.0%}\n```python\n{a['code']}```"
                        for a in sorted(agent_archive, key=lambda a: -a["score"]))
    prompt = textwrap.dedent("""\
        You design agents that solve short math word problems. An agent is a Python function
        `forward(question, ask)` that returns a string containing the final answer. `ask(prompt, temperature=0.0)`
        calls a small language model and returns its text reply. You may call `ask` AT MOST 2 times per question.
        Use only plain Python (re is allowed via `import re` inside the function).

        Archive of designs tried so far, best first:
        {shown}

        Propose a NEW design that is meaningfully different from the archive and likely to score higher
        (ideas: plan then compute, write the arithmetic as one expression and check it, verify and fix, ...).
        Reply in exactly this format:
        Name: <short name>
        Idea: <one sentence>
        ```python
        def forward(question, ask):
            ...
        ```""").replace("{shown}", shown)
    try:
        reply = ask(prompt, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=700)
    except Exception as e:
        reply = f"Name: error\nIdea: {e}\n"
    name = (re.search(r"Name:\s*(.+)", reply) or [None, "unnamed"])[1].strip()[:40]
    idea = (re.search(r"Idea:\s*(.+)", reply) or [None, ""])[1].strip()[:200]
    return {"name": name, "idea": idea, "code": code_block(reply)}

ITERS = 6 if RUN_LONG else 3                                     # 3 meta calls + up to 3 x 6 x 2 = 36 worker calls
t0 = time.time()
for it in range(1, ITERS + 1):
    d = propose_design()
    if "def forward(" not in d["code"]:
        acc, note = 0.0, "no forward() function"
    else:
        acc, note = evaluate_agent(d["code"], adas_tasks)
    agent_archive.append({**d, "score": acc, "note": note, "gen": it})
    print(f"iter {it}: {d['name']:<30} accuracy {acc:.0%} ({note})")
print(f"[{time.time() - t0:.0f}s]")

best_agent = max(agent_archive, key=lambda a: a["score"])
print(f"\nbest design: {best_agent['name']} ({best_agent['score']:.0%})\n{best_agent['code']}")

Be honest about what 6 questions can tell you: one question is about 17 points. A design that "wins" here may just be lucky. ADAS reports scores on a held-out test set; with RUN_LONG = True we do the same below.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 8
if RUN_LONG:
    held = word_problems(12, seed=8)
    for a in [agent_archive[1], best_agent]:
        print(f"held-out: {a['name']:<30} {evaluate_agent(a['code'], held)[0]:.0%}")

4. An "AI Scientist" loop in miniature

The AI Scientist (Lu et al. 2024) chains the whole research process: idea -> experiment code -> run -> write-up -> automated review. The authors report under $15 per paper, and an automated reviewer (GPT-4o) with 0.65 balanced accuracy at accept/reject on 500 ICLR 2022 papers, against 0.66 for humans.

Ours runs on bin packing, so it is cheap to test. Five calls at most. We add one check the model cannot fake: do the numbers in the write-up actually appear in the results?

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 9
class Idea(BaseModel):
    title: str
    hypothesis: str = Field(description="one testable sentence")
    plan: str = Field(description="what to compare and how to measure it")

class Review(BaseModel):
    soundness: int = Field(ge=1, le=5)
    clarity: int = Field(ge=1, le=5)
    overall: int = Field(ge=1, le=10)
    main_weakness: str

t0 = time.time()
try:
    idea = structured("Propose one small, testable research idea about bin-packing heuristics "
                      "(for example: how item-size distribution affects first-fit vs best-fit). "
                      "It must be testable in pure Python on random instances in under 10 seconds.", Idea, temperature=0.7)
except Exception as e:
    idea = Idea(title="First fit vs best fit", hypothesis="Best fit uses fewer bins than first fit on small items.",
                plan=f"Compare both on random instances (fallback idea; model error: {e})")
print("IDEA:", idea.title, "|", idea.hypothesis)

exp_prompt = (f"Write a self-contained Python script (standard library only) that tests this hypothesis:\n"
              f"{idea.hypothesis}\nPlan: {idea.plan}\n"
              "Use random.seed(0). Keep it under 5 seconds. At the very end print exactly one line:\n"
              "print('RESULT', json.dumps(results)) where results is a flat dict of names to numbers.\n"
              "Reply with one ```python code block.")
experiment = {"ok": False, "error": "not run"}
code = ""
for attempt in range(2):                                         # one repair try, like the paper's retry loop
    try:
        code = code_block(ask(exp_prompt if attempt == 0 else
                              exp_prompt + f"\n\nYour last script failed with:\n{experiment.get('error', '')[-400:]}\nFix it.",
                              temperature=0.2, max_tokens=1200))
    except Exception as e:
        code = f"raise SystemExit('model error: {e}')"
    experiment = run_sandboxed(code, timeout=20)
    print(f"experiment attempt {attempt + 1}: {'ran' if experiment['ok'] else 'failed: ' + experiment['error'][-150:]}")
    if experiment["ok"]:
        break
results = experiment.get("result", {}) if experiment["ok"] else {}
print("RESULTS:", json.dumps(results)[:400])
a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 10
try:
    writeup = ask(f"Write a 120-word results note for this experiment.\nHypothesis: {idea.hypothesis}\n"
                  f"Results (JSON): {json.dumps(results)}\nReport the actual numbers, say whether the hypothesis "
                  "held, and name one limitation. Do not invent numbers that are not in the results.",
                  temperature=0.2, max_tokens=400)
except Exception as e:
    writeup = f"(write-up failed: {e})"
print(writeup)

def grounded_fraction(text, res):
    """Share of numbers in the write-up that match a result value (to 2 decimals) or appear in the results text."""
    vals = [v for v in res.values() if isinstance(v, (int, float))] if isinstance(res, dict) else []
    nums = [float(x) for x in re.findall(r"-?\d+\.?\d*", text.replace(",", ""))]
    nums = [n for n in nums if n not in (0, 1, 2)]               # small counts like "two heuristics" are noise
    if not nums:
        return None
    raw = json.dumps(res)
    ok = sum(any(abs(n - v) < 0.005 or abs(n - round(v * 100, 1)) < 0.06 for v in vals) or str(n).rstrip("0").rstrip(".") in raw
             for n in nums)
    return ok / len(nums)

try:
    review = structured(f"You are a strict reviewer. Review this short research note.\nHypothesis: {idea.hypothesis}\n"
                        f"Plan: {idea.plan}\nRaw results: {json.dumps(results)}\nNote:\n{writeup}\n"
                        "Score soundness and clarity 1-5 and overall 1-10. Punish any claim not supported by the raw results.",
                        Review)
except Exception as e:
    review = None
    print("review failed:", e)
g = grounded_fraction(writeup, results)
print(f"\nREVIEW: {review.model_dump() if review else None}")
print(f"numbers in the write-up found in the results: {'n/a' if g is None else f'{g:.0%}'}   [{time.time() - t0:.0f}s]")

What this shows, and what it does not: - Each step is checkable except the idea itself. The experiment either ran or it did not; the numbers are either in the results or they are not. - The LLM reviewer is the weakest link. It is one model grading another, and it can be fooled by confident writing. That is why we added the grounding check, which is plain code. - The real AI Scientist uses much bigger models, a literature search to check novelty, and full LaTeX papers. The loop is the same.

a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 11
check("sandbox stops an infinite loop", not run_sandboxed("while True: pass", timeout=2)["ok"])
check("AlphaEvolve loop beat the seed program", best_prog["score"] > seed_score)
ran_ok = [a for a in agent_archive if a["gen"] > 0 and a["note"].endswith("errored") and not a["note"].startswith(f"{len(adas_tasks)}/")]
check("ADAS: at least one new design ran on the tasks", len(ran_ok) > 0)
check("AI Scientist: the experiment ran and the reviewer returned a score", experiment["ok"] and review is not None and 1 <= review.overall <= 10)

Your turn

  1. Reward hacking. Remove the validity check from the bin-packing evaluator (score only lower bound / bins used). Write the program a search would find first. What does this teach about evaluators?
  2. Overfitting the archive. Take the best ADAS design and the chain-of-thought seed and score both on 12 fresh questions (a new seed). Does the winner still win?
  3. Diversity. Our AlphaEvolve parent choice favors the best program. Change it to pick parents uniformly from the whole archive. Is progress slower, faster, or just noisier?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 1: a cheating "packer" under a weak evaluator. No model calls needed.
CHEAT = "def pack(items, capacity):\n    return [items]          # everything in one bin\n"
VALIDITY = "        if Counter(flat) != Counter(items) or any(sum(b) > cap for b in bins) or any(len(b) == 0 for b in bins):\n            return None\n"
assert VALIDITY in BP_HARNESS
WEAK = BP_HARNESS.replace(VALIDITY, "")
weak = run_sandboxed(WEAK.format(code=CHEAT))
print("weak evaluator score for the cheat:", weak.get("result"), "| real evaluator:", evaluate_packer(CHEAT))
# Notice: one overfilled bin gives "lower bound / 1 bin" = a score far above 1.0. An LLM search optimizes
# whatever you measure, cheats included. Every evaluator needs a validity check that cannot be skipped.
a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 13
# Solution 2: held-out check of the ADAS winner (about 24 small-model calls).
RUN_HELDOUT = False
if RUN_HELDOUT:
    fresh = word_problems(12, seed=123)
    for a in [agent_archive[1], best_agent]:
        print(f"{a['name']:<30} search set {a['score']:.0%} | fresh set {evaluate_agent(a['code'], fresh)[0]:.0%}")
# Notice: with 6 search questions, the gap often shrinks or flips on fresh data. Use more search
# questions, or keep a held-out set and only trust gains that show up there.
a06_open_ended_evolution.ipynb · cell 14
# Solution 3: uniform parent choice (12 more model calls).
RUN_UNIFORM = False
if RUN_UNIFORM:
    arch2 = [{"code": SEED_PROGRAM, "score": seed_score, "gen": 0}]
    for gen in range(1, 7):
        for par in [rng.choice(arch2) for _ in range(2)]:
            c = mutate(par, sorted(arch2, key=lambda p: -p["score"])[:2])
            arch2.append({"code": c, "score": evaluate_packer(c)[0] if "def pack(" in c else 0.0, "gen": gen})
        print(f"gen {gen}: best {max(p['score'] for p in arch2):.3f}")
# Notice: uniform choice explores more but wastes calls on weak parents. AlphaEvolve balances the two
# with islands and MAP-Elites, so several different lines of programs keep improving side by side.
Traps
  • An evaluator without a validity check. The search will find the loophole (in the notebook, one overfilled bin scores 27 out of a 'perfect' 1.0).
  • Trusting a win on a tiny search set. With 6 questions one answer is 17 points; confirm the winner on held-out data.
  • Running model-written code in your own Python process. Always use a subprocess or container with a timeout; the AI Scientist raised its own timeout when it could.
  • Keeping only the current best. Without an archive and some diversity, the search gets stuck on one line of ideas.
  • Letting an LLM reviewer be the only judge. Check numbers and claims against the raw results with code.

Check yourself

Why does AlphaEvolve run an evaluation cascade instead of the full test on every child?

Most children are worse or broken; a tiny fast test filters them out early, so the expensive test is spent only on promising ones.

In ADAS, what does the meta agent output, and how is it judged?

A new agent written as code (a forward() function); it is run on validation tasks and its score is stored with it in the archive.

Name two safety measures for an AI Scientist-style loop.

Run generated code in a sandbox (separate process or container) with time, memory and storage limits and no network; check the write-up's claims against the raw results with code rather than trusting the LLM reviewer.

Part 2 · A07

Self-improvement with search and deep research agents

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 8 (Oct 17)

You can make an agent better without training it by searching. Search over answers: draw many programs, keep the ones that pass the examples, group them by behavior, and submit one per group. Search for knowledge: let the model pause mid-thought, look something up, boil the results down, and keep reasoning.

Key terms

Filtering
Throwing away candidate programs that fail the example tests given in the problem.Source: Li et al. 2022
Clustering by behavior
Running candidates on extra inputs and grouping the ones that give identical outputs; the inputs need no known answers.Source: Li et al. 2022
10@k
Percent of problems solved when k samples are drawn but only 10 may be submitted.Source: Li et al. 2022
Scoring model
A model that predicts how likely a code sample is to be correct, used to rank samples.Source: AlphaCode 2 report, 2023
Agentic RAG
Retrieval where the model itself decides when to search and what to search for, possibly many times.Source: Li et al. 2025
Reason-in-Documents
A separate step that reads retrieved pages and writes back only the useful facts, so noisy text does not derail reasoning.Source: Li et al. 2025
Coverage
Fraction of problems where at least one sample is correct; the ceiling for any selection method.Source: Brown et al. 2024

The papers

Competition-Level Code Generation with AlphaCode

Li et al. · 2022

What
A transformer model sampled up to millions of programs per competitive-programming problem. It filtered them with the example tests from the problem statement, then clustered the survivors by their outputs on new test inputs written by a separate model, and submitted one program per cluster, largest first, with at most 10 submissions.
Result
Average ranking in the top 54.3% in simulated Codeforces contests with more than 5,000 participants. Solved 34.2% of held-out CodeContests problems with up to a million samples and 10 submissions. Filtering on example tests removed about 99% of samples. Solve rate grew roughly log-linearly with the number of samples.
Takeaway
When a cheap exact check exists (example tests), sample a lot and filter. When many candidates survive, group them by behavior and pick from the biggest group, because correct programs tend to agree with each other.

AlphaCode 2 Technical Report

AlphaCode Team, Google DeepMind (Leblond et al.) · 2023

What
The same sample, filter and cluster pipeline, built on fine-tuned Gemini Pro models. It drew up to a million C++ samples per problem with randomized temperature, kept the 10 largest clusters, and added a second fine-tuned Gemini model that scores each sample's chance of being correct to choose the best one in each cluster.
Result
Solved 43% of problems from 12 recent Codeforces contests (77 problems) within 10 attempts, vs 25% for the original AlphaCode. Estimated 85th percentile among participants (AlphaCode: about 46%). Filtering removed about 95% of samples. Needed about 100 samples to match AlphaCode with a million samples.
Takeaway
A better base model makes search far more sample-efficient, and a learned scorer helps choose inside a group. Selection, not generation, is often where the remaining gains are.

Search-o1: Agentic Search-Enhanced Large Reasoning Models

Li et al. · 2025

What
A reasoning model (QwQ-32B-Preview) writes a search query between special tags whenever it is unsure. The system pauses, retrieves the top 10 web results, and a separate Reason-in-Documents step condenses them into the facts that matter, which are inserted back into the reasoning chain.
Result
On GPQA diamond, overall 63.6 vs 58.1 for the same model without retrieval and 58.6 with standard RAG. Averaged over the five reasoning datasets it beat agentic RAG without Reason-in-Documents by 4.7% and no retrieval by 3.1%. On multi-hop QA, average exact match beat standard RAG by 29.6% and agentic RAG by 5.3%.
Takeaway
For multi-step questions, let the model decide when and what to search, one fact at a time, and condense the results before they reach the reasoning. One retrieval with the whole question often misses the second hop.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a07_search_deep_research.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 2
import json, math, os, random, re, subprocess, sys, tempfile, textwrap
from collections import defaultdict
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from rank_bm25 import BM25Okapi
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.data import contract, labels, ids, sections
from lal.checks import pass_at_k, check

Part A · AlphaCode at laptop scale

AlphaCode (Li et al. 2022, Competition-Level Code Generation with AlphaCode) drew up to millions of programs per problem, then cut them down to 10 submissions in two steps:

  1. Filter: run each program on the example tests in the problem statement. This removed about 99% of samples.
  2. Cluster: run the survivors on new test inputs (made by a separate model) and group programs that give the same outputs. Submit one program per group, biggest group first.

Why biggest first? The paper's guess: there are many ways to be wrong, but correct programs tend to agree, so they pile up in the largest group. AlphaCode 2 (2023) kept the same pipeline and added a scoring model to pick the best program inside each of the 10 biggest groups.

Our version: 5 small tasks, 6 samples each. Each task has example tests (the model sees them), hidden tests (used only for grading) and an input generator (random inputs with no answers, used only for clustering).

A1. The tasks

The reference solutions live here, in trusted code. They compute every expected output, so no answer is typed by hand.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 3
REF_CODE = """
import re

def second_largest(nums):
    d = sorted(set(nums))
    return d[-2] if len(d) >= 2 else None

def merge_intervals(intervals):
    out = []
    for s, e in sorted(intervals):
        if out and s <= out[-1][1]:
            out[-1][1] = max(out[-1][1], e)
        else:
            out.append([s, e])
    return out

def rle_decode(s):
    return "".join(ch * int(n) for ch, n in re.findall(r"([a-zA-Z])(\\d+)", s))

def max_subarray(nums):
    best = cur = nums[0]
    for x in nums[1:]:
        cur = max(x, cur + x)
        best = max(best, cur)
    return best

def to_roman(n):
    vals = [(1000, "M"), (900, "CM"), (500, "D"), (400, "CD"), (100, "C"), (90, "XC"),
            (50, "L"), (40, "XL"), (10, "X"), (9, "IX"), (5, "V"), (4, "IV"), (1, "I")]
    out = ""
    for v, sym in vals:
        while n >= v:
            out += sym
            n -= v
    return out
"""
REF = {}
exec(REF_CODE, REF)          # trusted code we wrote, so it is fine to run it here

rng = random.Random(0)
def rand_list(lo, hi, nmin, nmax):
    return [rng.randint(lo, hi) for _ in range(rng.randint(nmin, nmax))]
def rand_intervals():
    out = []
    for _ in range(rng.randint(0, 5)):
        s = rng.randint(0, 12); out.append([s, s + rng.randint(0, 4)])
    return out
def rand_rle():
    return "".join(rng.choice("abcxyz") + str(rng.randint(1, 14)) for _ in range(rng.randint(0, 4)))

# Each input is a list of positional arguments.
TASKS = [
    dict(fn="second_largest", ref=REF["second_largest"],
         spec="Return the second largest DISTINCT value in the list `nums`. If there are fewer than two distinct values, return None.",
         examples=[[[3, 1, 2]], [[5, 5, 4]]],
         hidden=[[[7, 7]], [[]], [[-1, -5, -3]], [[1, 2, 2, 3, 3]], [[10]], [[4, 4, 4, 9]]],
         gen=lambda: [rand_list(-4, 4, 0, 6)]),
    dict(fn="merge_intervals", ref=REF["merge_intervals"],
         spec="Merge overlapping intervals. Each interval is a list [start, end] with start <= end. Intervals that only touch "
              "(one ends where the next starts) also merge. Return a new list of merged intervals sorted by start.",
         examples=[[[[1, 3], [2, 6], [8, 10]]], [[[1, 2]]]],
         hidden=[[[]], [[[5, 6], [1, 2]]], [[[1, 2], [2, 3]]], [[[1, 10], [2, 3]]], [[[4, 5], [1, 4], [0, 0]]]],
         gen=lambda: [rand_intervals()]),
    dict(fn="rle_decode", ref=REF["rle_decode"],
         spec="Decode a run-length string. Each letter is followed by a positive whole number (it may have several digits) "
              "saying how many times to repeat it. Example: 'a3b1' -> 'aaab'. The empty string decodes to ''.",
         examples=[["a3b2"], ["x1"]],
         hidden=[["a12"], [""], ["z10y2"], ["b1c1d1"], ["q20"]],
         gen=lambda: [rand_rle()]),
    dict(fn="max_subarray", ref=REF["max_subarray"],
         spec="Return the largest possible sum of a NON-EMPTY contiguous slice of the list `nums`. "
              "`nums` has at least one element, and all elements may be negative.",
         examples=[[[1, -2, 3, 4, -1]], [[2, 3]]],
         hidden=[[[-3, -1, -2]], [[-5]], [[5, -9, 6]], [[0, 0]], [[-2, 1, -3, 4, -1, 2, 1, -5, 4]]],
         gen=lambda: [rand_list(-6, 6, 1, 7)]),
    dict(fn="to_roman", ref=REF["to_roman"],
         spec="Convert an integer 1 <= n <= 3999 to a Roman numeral string, using the subtractive forms IV, IX, XL, XC, CD and CM.",
         examples=[[3], [58]],
         hidden=[[4], [9], [14], [40], [90], [400], [1994], [3999]],
         gen=lambda: [rng.randint(1, 3999)]),
]
N_GEN = 12                                                       # generated inputs per task, for clustering only
for t in TASKS:
    t["gen_inputs"] = [t["gen"]() for _ in range(N_GEN)]
print(len(TASKS), "tasks. Example generated inputs for", TASKS[0]["fn"], ":", TASKS[0]["gen_inputs"][:4])

A2. A safe runner

Model code runs only in a separate Python process with a timeout. The runner calls the function on a list of inputs and prints the outputs as JSON. A crash on one input is recorded as "ERROR: <type>" so it still forms part of the program's behavior.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 4
RUNNER = r'''
import json, sys, copy
src, fn, inputs_path = open(sys.argv[1]).read(), sys.argv[2], sys.argv[3]
inputs = json.load(open(inputs_path))
ns = {}
try:
    exec(compile(src, "candidate", "exec"), ns)
    f = ns[fn]
except Exception as e:
    print(json.dumps(["ERROR: " + type(e).__name__] * len(inputs))); sys.exit(0)
outs = []
for args in inputs:
    try:
        outs.append(f(*copy.deepcopy(args)))
    except Exception as e:
        outs.append("ERROR: " + type(e).__name__)
print(json.dumps(outs, default=repr))
'''
WORK = tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="a07_")
RUNNER_PATH = os.path.join(WORK, "runner.py")
open(RUNNER_PATH, "w").write(RUNNER)

def run_outputs(code, fn, inputs, timeout=10):
    """Run `code` in a subprocess and return one JSON-normalized output per input."""
    with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile("w", suffix=".py", dir=WORK, delete=False) as f:
        f.write(code); src = f.name
    inp = src + ".json"
    open(inp, "w").write(json.dumps(inputs))
    try:
        out = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-I", RUNNER_PATH, src, fn, inp], capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=timeout)
        res = json.loads(out.stdout.strip().splitlines()[-1])
        if not isinstance(res, list) or len(res) != len(inputs):
            raise ValueError("bad output")
    except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
        res = ["TIMEOUT"] * len(inputs)
    except Exception:
        res = ["ERROR: runner"] * len(inputs)
    return [json.dumps(r, sort_keys=True) for r in res]       # strings, so outputs compare and hash cleanly

def expected(task, inputs):
    return [json.dumps(json.loads(json.dumps(task["ref"](*json.loads(json.dumps(a))), default=repr)), sort_keys=True) for a in inputs]

# Test the runner and the task set with the reference solutions themselves.
ref_ok = all(run_outputs(REF_CODE, t["fn"], t["hidden"]) == expected(t, t["hidden"]) for t in TASKS)
print("reference solutions pass their own hidden tests through the runner:", ref_ok)
print("a broken program:", run_outputs("def to_roman(n):\n    return 1/0", "to_roman", [[1], [2]]))
print("an endless loop:", run_outputs("def to_roman(n):\n    while True: pass", "to_roman", [[1]], timeout=3))

A3. Why clustering works: a planted wrong answer

A common wrong max_subarray resets the running sum to 0, so it returns 0 when every number is negative. It passes both example tests. Filtering can't catch it, but its outputs on generated inputs differ from the correct program, so it lands in a different cluster.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 5
CORRECT_MS = "def max_subarray(nums):\n    best = cur = nums[0]\n    for x in nums[1:]:\n        cur = max(x, cur + x)\n        best = max(best, cur)\n    return best\n"
WRONG_MS = "def max_subarray(nums):\n    best = cur = 0\n    for x in nums:\n        cur = max(0, cur + x)\n        best = max(best, cur)\n    return best\n"
ms = TASKS[3]
for name, code in [("correct", CORRECT_MS), ("reset-to-0", WRONG_MS)]:
    print(f"{name:10} passes examples: {run_outputs(code, 'max_subarray', ms['examples']) == expected(ms, ms['examples'])} | "
          f"behavior on generated inputs: {run_outputs(code, 'max_subarray', ms['gen_inputs'])[:6]}")
planted_split = run_outputs(CORRECT_MS, "max_subarray", ms["gen_inputs"]) != run_outputs(WRONG_MS, "max_subarray", ms["gen_inputs"])

A4. Sample many programs

For diversity, AlphaCode sampled half in Python and half in C++, put random problem tags and ratings in the prompt, and used a fairly high temperature. AlphaCode 2 also randomized the temperature per sample. We use a temperature of 0.8 and 6 samples per task (30 calls), 2 at a time.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 6
N_SAMPLES = 6

def code_prompt(t):
    ex = "\n".join(f"{t['fn']}({', '.join(map(repr, a))}) == {t['ref'](*json.loads(json.dumps(a)))!r}" for a in t["examples"])
    return (f"Write a Python function `{t['fn']}`.\n{t['spec']}\n\nExamples:\n{ex}\n\n"
            "Return only the code, in one ```python block. No explanation.")

def extract_code(text):
    blocks = re.findall(r"```(?:python|py)?\s*\n(.*?)```", text or "", flags=re.S)
    return max(blocks, key=len) if blocks else (text or "")

def sample(t):
    return extract_code(ask(code_prompt(t), temperature=0.8, max_tokens=600))

jobs = [(ti, k) for ti in range(len(TASKS)) for k in range(N_SAMPLES)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    codes = list(ex.map(lambda j: sample(TASKS[j[0]]), jobs))
SAMPLES = defaultdict(list)
for (ti, _), c in zip(jobs, codes):
    SAMPLES[ti].append(c)
print(SAMPLES[0][0][:400])

A5. Filter, cluster, select

For each sample we record three things: does it pass the examples, its behavior signature on generated inputs, and (for grading only) does it pass the hidden tests.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 7
def analyse(ti):
    t = TASKS[ti]
    rows = []
    for code in SAMPLES[ti]:
        rows.append(dict(code=code,
                         ex_ok=run_outputs(code, t["fn"], t["examples"]) == expected(t, t["examples"]),
                         sig=tuple(run_outputs(code, t["fn"], t["gen_inputs"])),
                         hidden_ok=run_outputs(code, t["fn"], t["hidden"]) == expected(t, t["hidden"])))
    return rows

def clusters_of(rows):
    """Group the rows that passed the examples by behavior signature, biggest group first."""
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for r in rows:
        if r["ex_ok"]:
            groups[r["sig"]].append(r)
    return sorted(groups.values(), key=len, reverse=True)

RESULTS = {ti: analyse(ti) for ti in range(len(TASKS))}
for ti, rows in RESULTS.items():
    cl = clusters_of(rows)
    print(f"{TASKS[ti]['fn']:16} samples {len(rows)} | pass examples {sum(r['ex_ok'] for r in rows)} | "
          f"clusters {[len(c) for c in cl]} | cluster correct? {[c[0]['hidden_ok'] for c in cl]} | "
          f"any sample correct: {any(r['hidden_ok'] for r in rows)}")

A6. Compare selection methods

We have a budget of K submissions per task. A task counts as solved if any submission passes all hidden tests. - random: pick K samples at random (expected value, using pass@k). - filter + random: pick K at random from the samples that pass the examples. - filter + cluster: one sample from each of the K biggest clusters. - oracle: any sample is correct (the ceiling, also called coverage).

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 8
def solve_rates(K):
    out = defaultdict(float)
    for ti, rows in RESULTS.items():
        n, c = len(rows), sum(r["hidden_ok"] for r in rows)
        f = [r for r in rows if r["ex_ok"]]
        fc = sum(r["hidden_ok"] for r in f)
        out["random"] += pass_at_k(n, c, min(K, n)) if n else 0
        out["filter + random"] += pass_at_k(len(f), fc, min(K, len(f))) if f else 0
        out["filter + cluster"] += float(any(cl[0]["hidden_ok"] for cl in clusters_of(rows)[:K]))
        out["oracle (coverage)"] += float(c > 0)
    return {k: v / len(RESULTS) for k, v in out.items()}

TABLE = {K: solve_rates(K) for K in (1, 2)}
print(f"{'method':20}" + "".join(f"{'K=' + str(K):>8}" for K in TABLE))
for m in TABLE[1]:
    print(f"{m:20}" + "".join(f"{TABLE[K][m]:>8.0%}" for K in TABLE))

What to notice: filtering helps because example tests are a cheap, exact verifier. Clustering helps most when many samples pass the examples but disagree with each other. The gap to the oracle row is the selection problem, which AlphaCode 2 attacked with a learned scoring model.

Part B · Search-o1: search in the middle of reasoning

Search-o1 (Li et al. 2025, Search-o1: Agentic Search-Enhanced Large Reasoning Models) lets a reasoning model write a search query between special tags whenever it is unsure. The system pauses, retrieves documents, and a separate Reason-in-Documents step condenses them into a few useful facts. Only those facts go back into the reasoning, so long noisy pages don't derail it.

We compare three ways to answer questions about our company's contracts and policies: 1. No search: the model alone. It has never seen our data, so it should fail. 2. Naive RAG: retrieve once with the question, then answer. 3. Search-o1 style: the model decides what to search, possibly several times, and each result is condensed first.

B1. The corpus: 20 contracts plus a few internal policies

The questions need two hops: one fact from a contract, and one rule from a policy.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 9
POLICIES = {
    "Procurement Policy PP-4": [
        "Purpose. This policy sets who must sign off on recurring vendor spending.",
        "Approval tiers. The approver depends on the monthly fee in the vendor agreement. A monthly fee under $5,000 is approved by the Department Head. "
        "A monthly fee from $5,000 up to and including $15,000 is approved by the Finance Director. A monthly fee above $15,000 is approved by the Chief Financial Officer.",
        "Records. Keep the signed approval with the contract file for seven years.",
    ],
    "Legal Policy LP-2": [
        "Outside counsel. Disputes are handled by the outside counsel firm assigned to the state whose law governs the agreement.",
        "Assignments. Delaware law: Hale & Ross LLP. California law: Pacific Ridge Law Group. Texas law: Lone Star Counsel. "
        "Florida law: Bayfront Legal. New York law: Hudson Street Partners.",
        "Budget. Outside counsel spending above $50,000 per matter needs General Counsel approval.",
    ],
    "Contracts Desk Policy CD-1": [
        "Non-renewal letters. To stop an agreement from renewing, the Contracts Desk sends the non-renewal letter early. "
        "The letter must go out a number of days before the renewal date equal to the agreement's notice period plus a 15-day internal buffer.",
        "Tracking. Every agreement's renewal date is entered in the contracts calendar on signature.",
    ],
    "Travel Policy TR-3": [
        "Approval. Trips above $3,000 are approved by the Department Head before booking.",
        "Per diem. Meals are reimbursed up to $75 per day in the United States.",
    ],
    "IT Equipment Policy IT-7": [
        "Laptops. Standard laptops are replaced every 36 months. Exceptions are approved by the IT Director.",
        "Security. Lost devices must be reported within 24 hours.",
    ],
}

def parties(cid):
    m = re.search(r'entered into by (.+?) \("Customer"\) and (.+?) \("Vendor"\)', contract(cid))
    return (m.group(1), m.group(2)) if m else ("?", "?")

DOCS = []
for cid in ids():
    cust, vend = parties(cid)
    for s in sections(contract(cid)):
        DOCS.append({"id": f"{cid}#{s['id']}", "text": f"Agreement between {cust} (Customer) and {vend} (Vendor), "
                                                       f"section {s['id']} {s['title']}: {s['text']}"})
for title, paras in POLICIES.items():
    for i, p in enumerate(paras):
        DOCS.append({"id": f"{title}#{i + 1}", "text": f"{title}. {p}"})

tok = lambda s: re.findall(r"\w+", s.lower())
BM25 = BM25Okapi([tok(d["text"]) for d in DOCS])

def search(query, k=3):
    """The local search tool: BM25 over contract sections and policy paragraphs."""
    scores = BM25.get_scores(tok(query))
    return [DOCS[i] for i in sorted(range(len(DOCS)), key=lambda i: -scores[i])[:k]]

for d in search("Bayview Health Partners Brightline Data Corp monthly fee"):
    print(d["id"], "|", d["text"][:110])
bm25_ok = search(f"{' '.join(parties('msa_01'))} fees", k=3)[0]["id"].startswith("msa_01")

B2. Questions with known answers

Built from the labels with a seed. We only use customer/vendor pairs that appear in exactly one contract, so each question has one right answer.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 10
def approver(fee):
    return "Department Head" if fee < 5000 else ("Finance Director" if fee <= 15000 else "Chief Financial Officer")
COUNSEL = {"Delaware": "Hale & Ross", "California": "Pacific Ridge", "Texas": "Lone Star", "Florida": "Bayfront", "New York": "Hudson Street"}

L = labels()
pair_count = defaultdict(int)
for cid in ids():
    pair_count[parties(cid)] += 1
unique = [cid for cid in ids() if pair_count[parties(cid)] == 1]
qrng = random.Random(7)
picked = qrng.sample(unique, 6)
QUESTIONS = []
for i, cid in enumerate(picked):
    cust, vend = parties(cid); terms = L[cid]["terms"]
    if i % 3 == 0:
        QUESTIONS.append((f"Under our procurement policy, who must approve the agreement between {cust} and {vend}?", approver(terms["monthly_fee"])))
    elif i % 3 == 1:
        QUESTIONS.append((f"Which outside counsel firm handles disputes under the agreement between {cust} and {vend}?", COUNSEL[terms["governing_law"]]))
    else:
        QUESTIONS.append((f"How many days before the renewal date must the Contracts Desk send the non-renewal letter for the agreement between {cust} and {vend}?",
                          str(terms["notice_days"] + 15)))
for q, a in QUESTIONS:
    print(f"- {q}  -> gold: {a}")

def words(s):
    return re.findall(r"[a-z0-9]+", (s or "").lower())

def is_correct(answer, gold):
    """Lenient grading: a number must appear; for names, every word of the gold name must appear."""
    if gold.isdigit():
        return gold in re.findall(r"\d+", (answer or "").replace(",", ""))
    return all(w in words(answer) for w in words(gold))

def final_answer(text):
    m = re.findall(r"ANSWER:\s*(.+)", text or "")
    return m[-1].strip() if m else (text or "").strip()[-200:]

B3. Baselines: no search, and naive RAG

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 11
def no_search(q):
    return final_answer(ask(f"{q}\nIf you are not sure, give your best guess. End with a line 'ANSWER: <short answer>'.", max_tokens=300))

def naive_rag(q, k=4):
    ctx = "\n\n".join(f"[{d['id']}] {d['text']}" for d in search(q, k=k))
    return final_answer(ask(f"Sources:\n{ctx}\n\nQuestion: {q}\nThink briefly, then end with a line 'ANSWER: <short answer>'.", max_tokens=400))

B4. The Search-o1 loop

  1. The model reasons. When it needs a fact it writes <search>query</search>. We pass stop=["</search>"] so generation pauses right there.
  2. We run the search tool.
  3. Reason-in-Documents: a separate call reads the raw pages plus the reasoning so far and writes 1–3 sentences of useful facts.
  4. We paste those facts back inside <result>...</result> and let the model continue. At most 3 searches.
a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 12
S1_SYSTEM = ("You answer questions about our company's vendor agreements and internal policies. You do NOT know these facts yourself. "
             "Think step by step. Whenever you need a fact, write a search query like this: <search>your query</search> and then stop. "
             "Search for one fact at a time, using the names in the question. The results will come back as <result>...</result>. "
             "When you know enough, finish with a line 'ANSWER: <short answer>'.")

from lal.llm import chat
def ask_msgs(msgs, **kw):
    return chat(msgs, max_tokens=400, **kw).content or ""

def reason_in_documents(reasoning, query, docs):
    pages = "\n\n".join(f"[{d['id']}] {d['text']}" for d in docs)
    return ask(f"Reasoning so far:\n{reasoning[-1500:]}\n\nSearch query: {query}\n\nSearched pages:\n{pages}\n\n"
               "Read the pages and write only the facts that help with the search query, in 1-3 sentences. "
               "Copy names and numbers exactly. If nothing helps, write 'No helpful information found.'", max_tokens=200).strip()

def search_o1(q, max_searches=3, condense=True, verbose=False):
    base = [{"role": "system", "content": S1_SYSTEM}, {"role": "user", "content": q}]
    transcript, n_search = "", 0
    while True:
        msgs = base + ([{"role": "assistant", "content": transcript},
                        {"role": "user", "content": "Continue your reasoning from where you stopped."}] if transcript else [])
        out = ask_msgs(msgs, stop=["</search>"])
        out = out.split("</search>")[0]                    # in case the server ignores `stop`
        i_s, i_a = out.find("<search>"), out.find("ANSWER:")
        transcript += ("\n" if transcript else "") + out
        if i_a != -1 and (i_s == -1 or i_a < i_s):         # answered before (or without) asking for a search
            break
        if i_s == -1 or n_search >= max_searches:          # no search and no answer, or out of budget: ask for the answer
            transcript += "\n" + ask_msgs(base + [{"role": "assistant", "content": transcript},
                                                  {"role": "user", "content": "Give your final answer now as 'ANSWER: <short answer>'."}])
            break
        query = out[i_s + len("<search>"):].strip()[:200]
        docs = search(query)
        info = reason_in_documents(transcript, query, docs) if condense else "\n".join(d["text"] for d in docs)
        transcript += f"</search>\n<result>{info}</result>"
        n_search += 1
        if verbose:
            print(f"  search {n_search}: {query!r}\n    -> {info[:160]}")
    return final_answer(transcript), n_search

B5. Score all three

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 13
rows = []
for qi, (q, gold) in enumerate(QUESTIONS):
    a_none = no_search(q)
    a_rag = naive_rag(q)
    a_s1, n = search_o1(q, verbose=(qi == 0))               # print the searches for the first question
    rows.append((is_correct(a_none, gold), is_correct(a_rag, gold), is_correct(a_s1, gold), n))
    print(f"gold {gold!r:24} | none {a_none[:30]!r:34} | rag {a_rag[:30]!r:34} | search-o1 ({n} searches) {a_s1[:30]!r}")
acc = {name: sum(r[i] for r in rows) / len(rows) for i, name in enumerate(["no search", "naive RAG", "Search-o1 style"])}
print({k: f"{v:.0%}" for k, v in acc.items()})

What to notice: one retrieval with the whole question often brings back the contract or the policy, but not both. The agentic loop can search for each hop separately. The paper reports the same pattern on multi-hop QA: agentic search beat one-shot RAG, and adding Reason-in-Documents helped further. On a small model the loop can also fail by never searching, or by searching with vague words. Read the transcripts.

a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 14
check("reference solutions pass their own hidden tests (the task set is sound)", ref_ok)
check("a planted wrong program that passes the examples lands in a different cluster", planted_split)
check("filter + cluster (K=1) is at least as good as random choice (K=1)", TABLE[1]["filter + cluster"] >= TABLE[1]["random"] - 1e-9)
check("BM25 finds the right contract from party names", bm25_ok)
check("Search-o1 style beats no search on our questions", acc["Search-o1 style"] > acc["no search"])

Your turn

  1. Model-made test inputs. AlphaCode trained a separate model to write test inputs. Ask the model for 8 new inputs for merge_intervals (as a JSON list of argument lists), and cluster with those instead of our random generator. Do the clusters change?
  2. Turn off Reason-in-Documents. Run search_o1(q, condense=False), which pastes the raw pages back. Compare accuracy and the number of searches.
  3. Scale the samples. Plot the filter + cluster solve rate (K=1) using the first 2, 4 and 6 samples per task. Does it rise as AlphaCode saw (roughly log-linear)?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)

# Solution 1: model-written test inputs for clustering (1 call)
t = TASKS[1]
raw = ask(f"Write 8 varied test inputs for this Python function, including edge cases.\n{t['spec']}\n"
          f"The function is called as {t['fn']}(intervals). Reply with only a JSON list where each item is a list of arguments, "
          "for example [[[[1,2],[3,4]]], [[]]].", temperature=0.3, max_tokens=400)
try:
    model_inputs = json.loads(re.search(r"\[.*\]", raw, flags=re.S).group(0))
    model_inputs = [a for a in model_inputs if isinstance(a, list) and len(a) == 1][:8]
except Exception:
    model_inputs = []
print("model inputs:", model_inputs)
if model_inputs:
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for r in RESULTS[1]:
        if r["ex_ok"]:
            groups[tuple(run_outputs(r["code"], t["fn"], model_inputs))].append(r)
    print("cluster sizes with random inputs:", [len(c) for c in clusters_of(RESULTS[1])],
          "| with model inputs:", sorted(map(len, groups.values()), reverse=True))
# What to notice: as AlphaCode says, inputs don't need answers, or even to be valid, to separate programs.
# Edge-case inputs (empty list, touching intervals) are what split right from wrong.
a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 16
# Solution 2: raw pages instead of condensed facts (about 3-4 calls per question)
raw_rows = [is_correct(search_o1(q, condense=False)[0], gold) for q, gold in QUESTIONS[:3]]
cond_rows = [r[2] for r in rows[:3]]
print(f"first 3 questions | condensed: {sum(cond_rows)}/3 | raw pages: {sum(raw_rows)}/3")
# What to notice: with three short pages the difference may be small. Search-o1's motivation is long, noisy web pages,
# where raw text pushes the reasoning off track. Try search(query, k=8) to see the effect grow.
a07_search_deep_research.ipynb · cell 17
# Solution 3: solve rate as the sample budget grows (no new model calls; we reuse the samples)
for n in (2, 4, 6):
    solved = 0
    for ti, rows_t in RESULTS.items():
        cl = clusters_of(rows_t[:n])
        solved += bool(cl) and cl[0][0]["hidden_ok"]
    print(f"{n} samples per task -> filter + cluster K=1 solves {solved}/{len(RESULTS)}")
# What to notice: more samples raise the chance a correct program exists AND make the correct cluster bigger.
# AlphaCode needed millions of samples; AlphaCode 2 reached the same level with about 100, using a better model.
Traps
  • Passing the example tests is not being correct. Many wrong programs pass them, which is why AlphaCode needed clustering and hidden tests.
  • Random selection wastes your submission budget on programs that are written differently but behave the same.
  • Clustering inputs only need to separate programs. They do not need known answers, and even invalid inputs can help.
  • Naive RAG retrieves once with the whole question. For two-hop questions it often gets one hop and misses the other.
  • Pasting long raw search results into a reasoning chain can push it off track. Condense first.
  • A small model may never emit a search, or may search with vague words. Read the transcripts, not just the score.

Check yourself

Why does AlphaCode pick from the largest cluster first?

There are many ways to be wrong but correct programs tend to behave the same, so they gather in larger clusters.

What did AlphaCode 2 add on top of filtering and clustering?

A fine-tuned Gemini scoring model that picks the best sample in each of the 10 largest clusters.

About how much did filtering with example tests remove in AlphaCode?

About 99% of samples.

What does Reason-in-Documents do?

It reads the retrieved documents with the current reasoning and query, and returns only the relevant facts to insert into the reasoning chain.

In our notebook, why can naive RAG fail on the approver question?

The question needs the contract's monthly fee and the procurement policy tiers; one retrieval with the whole question may bring back only one of them.

Part 2 · A08

Agentic frameworks for software engineering

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 13 (Nov 3)

Code is the easiest place to self-improve because the computer grades the work: tests pass or fail, and code runs faster or slower. Sample many attempts, let tests (even model-written ones) pick the winner, and give the agent clear feedback from the system, such as a profiler, instead of a bare number.

Key terms

Coverage
Fraction of problems where at least one generated attempt is correct; the best any selector can do.Source: Ehrlich et al. 2025
Serial vs parallel test-time compute
Serial: more turns of fixing one attempt. Parallel: more independent attempts.Source: Ehrlich et al. 2025
Test-based voting
Run every model-written test on every candidate edit; the edit that passes the most tests wins.Source: Ehrlich et al. 2025
fast_p
Fraction of tasks where the new code is correct AND more than p times faster than the reference; fast_0 is plain correctness.Source: Ouyang et al. 2025
Agent-system interface
The layer between the agent and the system: what actions the agent can write and what feedback it gets back.Source: Wei et al. 2024
AutoGuide
A step that turns raw system output (errors, timings) into plain explanations and suggested changes for the agent.Source: Wei et al. 2024
Profiler
A tool that reports which functions used the most time; Python's built-in one is cProfile.Source: Python standard library

The papers

CodeMonkeys: Scaling Test-Time Compute for Software Engineering

Ehrlich, Brown, Juravsky et al. · 2025

What
A SWE-bench system in three steps: find relevant files (a cheap model scans every file), generate 10 parallel multi-turn attempts that each write a code edit and a test script and iterate on execution feedback, then select by running all model-written tests on all edits, keeping the top 3 by votes, and letting a model (which can write more tests) choose.
Result
57.4% of SWE-bench Verified resolved for about 2,300 USD, with 69.8% coverage (some attempt was correct). Selecting over an ensemble of edits from top leaderboard submissions reached 66.2%, above the best single member (62.8%). Different splits between serial and parallel compute often gave similar coverage. Model selection without the top-3 vote filter did worse than plain voting; with the filter it did better.
Takeaway
Treat generation and selection as separate problems and measure both (coverage vs final score). Cheap test-based voting to cut the pool, then a model judge on the short list, closes much of the gap.

KernelBench: Can LLMs Write Efficient GPU Kernels?

Ouyang, Guo et al. · 2025

What
250 PyTorch workloads in three levels. The model rewrites each as faster GPU code. Outputs are compared with the reference on 5 random inputs and both are timed. The metric fast_p is the fraction of tasks that are correct and faster than the reference by more than a factor p.
Result
One-shot, frontier reasoning models matched the PyTorch baseline in fewer than 20% of cases. With execution and profiler feedback over 10 refinement turns, the paper reports fast_1 rising from 12%, 36% and 12% to 43%, 72% and 18% across the three levels (the paper's Table 1 lists 2% as the Level 3 starting value; its introduction says 12%); the headline case is DeepSeek-R1 on Level 2, from 36% to 72%. Execution feedback mostly fixed execution errors; functional correctness stayed hard.
Takeaway
Grade speed and correctness together with one number (fast_p), check on several random inputs, and feed the model real execution and profiling output each turn.

Improving Parallel Program Performance with LLM Optimizers via Agent-System Interfaces

Wei, Nie et al. · 2024

What
An LLM optimizer (via the Trace framework, with GPT-4o) writes mappers that decide where tasks and data go in parallel programs. The agent-system interface has a small domain-specific language that hides low-level C++ and defines the search space, and AutoGuide, which turns raw execution output into explanations and suggested changes.
Result
Within 10 iterations it beat OpenTuner run for 1000 iterations, by 3.8x. Its mappers beat expert-written ones by up to 1.34x across nine benchmarks, cutting tuning from days to minutes. The DSL gave much higher code-generation success than raw C++.
Takeaway
Design what the agent sees and writes. A narrow action language plus feedback that explains errors and suggests fixes can matter more than a bigger model or more iterations.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a08_agentic_swe.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 2
import cProfile, difflib, json, os, re, shutil, subprocess, sys, tempfile, textwrap
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from lal.llm import ask, chat
from lal.checks import pass_at_k, check

WORK = tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="a08_")

def extract_code(text):
    """Take the longest ```python block from a reply, or the whole reply if there is none."""
    blocks = re.findall(r"```(?:python|py)?\s*\n(.*?)```", text or "", flags=re.S)
    return max(blocks, key=len) if blocks else (text or "")

Part A · CodeMonkeys at small scale

CodeMonkeys (Ehrlich, Brown, Juravsky et al. 2025, CodeMonkeys: Scaling Test-Time Compute for Software Engineering) solves real GitHub issues in three steps: 1. Context: find the files that matter. 2. Generation: run many attempts in parallel. Each attempt writes an edit and a test script. 3. Selection: run every model-written test on every edit, keep the top 3 edits by votes, and let a model pick among them.

Two numbers matter. Coverage: did any attempt fix the bug? Score: did the one we picked fix it? The gap between them is the selection problem.

A1. Four tiny buggy repos

Each has one file, an issue written like a bug report, and a hidden test (like SWE-bench's tests). The model never sees the hidden test. We also keep a reference fix, only to prove each task is sound.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 3
REPOS = [
    dict(name="textstats", file="textstats.py",
         code='''def word_count(text):
    """Number of words. Words are separated by any whitespace."""
    return len(text.split(" "))


def char_count(text, ignore_spaces=False):
    if ignore_spaces:
        return len(text.replace(" ", ""))
    return len(text)
''',
         issue="word_count gives wrong numbers. word_count('hello  world') returns 3 and word_count('a\\nb') returns 1. "
               "Both should be 2. Also word_count('') should be 0.",
         fix=("return len(text.split(\" \"))", "return len(text.split())"),
         test='''from textstats import word_count, char_count
assert word_count("hello  world") == 2
assert word_count("a\\nb") == 2
assert word_count("") == 0
assert word_count("  lead and trail  ") == 3
assert word_count("one") == 1
assert char_count("a b") == 3 and char_count("a b", ignore_spaces=True) == 2
'''),
    dict(name="late_fee", file="late_fee.py",
         code='''GRACE_DAYS = 5
DAILY_RATE = 0.01
MAX_FRACTION = 0.10


def late_fee(amount, days_late):
    """Fee for paying an invoice late.
    No fee within the grace period (up to and including GRACE_DAYS days late).
    After that, DAILY_RATE of the amount for each day beyond the grace period,
    capped at MAX_FRACTION of the amount. Rounded to cents."""
    if days_late < GRACE_DAYS:
        return 0.0
    fee = amount * DAILY_RATE * days_late
    return round(fee, 2)
''',
         issue="late_fee(1000, 5) returns 50.0, but 5 days late is still inside the grace period, so it should be 0.0. "
               "late_fee(1000, 8) returns 80.0 but should be 30.0 (only the 3 days after the grace period count). "
               "And late_fee(1000, 40) returns 400.0 even though the docstring says the fee is capped at 10% of the amount.",
         fix=("    if days_late < GRACE_DAYS:\n        return 0.0\n    fee = amount * DAILY_RATE * days_late\n",
              "    if days_late <= GRACE_DAYS:\n        return 0.0\n    fee = min(amount * DAILY_RATE * (days_late - GRACE_DAYS), amount * MAX_FRACTION)\n"),
         test='''from late_fee import late_fee
close = lambda a, b: abs(a - b) < 1e-6
assert close(late_fee(1000, 5), 0.0)
assert close(late_fee(1000, 0), 0.0)
assert close(late_fee(1000, 6), 10.0)
assert close(late_fee(1000, 8), 30.0)
assert close(late_fee(1000, 40), 100.0)
assert close(late_fee(200, 7), 4.0)
'''),
    dict(name="dates_util", file="dates_util.py",
         code='''from datetime import date


def add_months(d, n):
    """Return the date n months after d (n may be negative).
    If that day does not exist in the target month, use the last day of that month."""
    month = d.month - 1 + n
    year = d.year + month // 12
    month = month % 12 + 1
    return date(year, month, d.day)
''',
         issue="add_months(date(2027, 1, 31), 1) crashes with 'ValueError: day is out of range for month'. "
               "It should return date(2027, 2, 28). In a leap year, add_months(date(2028, 1, 31), 1) should return date(2028, 2, 29).",
         fix=("    return date(year, month, d.day)\n",
              "    import calendar\n    return date(year, month, min(d.day, calendar.monthrange(year, month)[1]))\n"),
         test='''from datetime import date
from dates_util import add_months
assert add_months(date(2027, 1, 31), 1) == date(2027, 2, 28)
assert add_months(date(2028, 1, 31), 1) == date(2028, 2, 29)
assert add_months(date(2027, 5, 31), 1) == date(2027, 6, 30)
assert add_months(date(2027, 12, 31), 2) == date(2028, 2, 29)
assert add_months(date(2027, 3, 15), -2) == date(2027, 1, 15)
assert add_months(date(2027, 1, 10), 12) == date(2028, 1, 10)
'''),
    dict(name="lru", file="lru.py",
         code='''from collections import OrderedDict


class LRUCache:
    """Keeps at most `capacity` items. When full, it evicts the least recently USED item.
    Reading a key with get() counts as using it."""

    def __init__(self, capacity):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.data = OrderedDict()

    def get(self, key, default=None):
        return self.data.get(key, default)

    def put(self, key, value):
        if key in self.data:
            self.data.move_to_end(key)
        self.data[key] = value
        if len(self.data) > self.capacity:
            self.data.popitem(last=False)
''',
         issue="Keys I just read still get evicted. c = LRUCache(2); c.put('a', 1); c.put('b', 2); c.get('a'); c.put('c', 3). "
               "Now 'a' is gone, but 'b' is the least recently used key and should be the one evicted.",
         fix=("        return self.data.get(key, default)\n",
              "        if key not in self.data:\n            return default\n        self.data.move_to_end(key)\n        return self.data[key]\n"),
         test='''from lru import LRUCache
c = LRUCache(2)
c.put("a", 1); c.put("b", 2)
assert c.get("a") == 1
c.put("c", 3)
assert c.get("b") is None and c.get("a") == 1 and c.get("c") == 3
assert c.get("zzz", "missing") == "missing"
d = LRUCache(1); d.put("x", 1); d.put("y", 2)
assert d.get("x") is None and d.get("y") == 2
e = LRUCache(2); e.put("a", 1); e.put("b", 2); e.put("a", 10); e.put("c", 3)
assert e.get("a") == 10 and e.get("b") is None
'''),
]
for r in REPOS:
    assert r["fix"][0] in r["code"], r["name"]
    r["fixed"] = r["code"].replace(*r["fix"])
print(REPOS[0]["issue"])

A2. Run a test against a version of the file, safely

Every run gets a fresh folder with the file and one test script, in a separate process with a timeout. Exit code 0 means pass.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 4
def run_test(repo, file_code, test_code, timeout=10):
    d = tempfile.mkdtemp(dir=WORK)
    open(os.path.join(d, repo["file"]), "w").write(file_code)
    open(os.path.join(d, "test_run.py"), "w").write(test_code)
    try:
        out = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "test_run.py"], cwd=d, capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=timeout)
        ok, msg = out.returncode == 0, (out.stderr or out.stdout).strip()[-300:]
    except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
        ok, msg = False, "TIMEOUT"
    shutil.rmtree(d, ignore_errors=True)
    return ok, msg

# Prove each task is sound: the hidden test FAILS on the buggy code and PASSES on the reference fix.
sound = {r["name"]: (not run_test(r, r["code"], r["test"])[0], run_test(r, r["fixed"], r["test"])[0]) for r in REPOS}
print("(fails on bug, passes on fix):", sound)

A3. Generation: 4 edits per repo, in parallel

Each attempt sees the issue and the file and returns the whole fixed file. 16 calls, 2 at a time.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 5
N_EDITS = 4

def edit_prompt(r):
    return (f"You are fixing a bug in a small Python repository.\n\nIssue:\n{r['issue']}\n\nFile `{r['file']}`:\n```python\n{r['code']}```\n\n"
            "Rewrite the file so the issue is fixed. Keep the same function and class names. "
            "Return the complete fixed file in one ```python block.")

def gen_edit(r):
    return extract_code(ask(edit_prompt(r), temperature=0.8, max_tokens=900))

jobs = [ri for ri in range(len(REPOS)) for _ in range(N_EDITS)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    edits = list(ex.map(lambda ri: gen_edit(REPOS[ri]), jobs))
EDITS = {ri: [e for j, e in zip(jobs, edits) if j == ri] for ri in range(len(REPOS))}

# Ground truth, for grading only: does each edit pass the hidden test?
HIDDEN = {ri: [run_test(REPOS[ri], e, REPOS[ri]["test"])[0] for e in EDITS[ri]] for ri in EDITS}
for ri, oks in HIDDEN.items():
    print(f"{REPOS[ri]['name']:11} edits passing the hidden test: {oks}")

A4. Coverage grows with the number of samples

Coverage@k is the chance that at least one of k attempts is correct. We estimate it with the unbiased pass@k formula. Large Language Monkeys (Brown et al. 2024) found coverage often grows roughly log-linearly with samples; CodeMonkeys reached 69.8% coverage on SWE-bench Verified.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 6
COVERAGE = {}
for k in range(1, N_EDITS + 1):
    COVERAGE[k] = sum(pass_at_k(N_EDITS, sum(oks), k) for oks in HIDDEN.values()) / len(HIDDEN)
    print(f"coverage@{k}: {COVERAGE[k]:.0%}  " + "#" * round(40 * COVERAGE[k]))

A5. The model writes its own tests
