We ask for 3 short test scripts per repo (12 calls). A useful test must fail on the buggy code; otherwise it doesn't reproduce the issue. We drop tests that pass on the original file (our own filter, in the spirit of CodeMonkeys running tests before and after an edit).

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 7
N_TESTS = 3

def test_prompt(r):
    module = r["file"][:-3]
    return (f"Issue:\n{r['issue']}\n\nFile `{r['file']}`:\n```python\n{r['code']}```\n\n"
            f"Write a short Python test script that does `from {module} import ...` and uses plain assert statements to check "
            "the behavior the issue asks for. It must FAIL on the current buggy code and PASS once the bug is fixed. "
            "No test framework, no explanation. Return only code in one ```python block.")

tjobs = [ri for ri in range(len(REPOS)) for _ in range(N_TESTS)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    tests = list(ex.map(lambda ri: extract_code(ask(test_prompt(REPOS[ri]), temperature=0.8, max_tokens=500)), tjobs))
GEN_TESTS = {}
for ri in range(len(REPOS)):
    mine = [t for j, t in zip(tjobs, tests) if j == ri]
    GEN_TESTS[ri] = [t for t in mine if not run_test(REPOS[ri], REPOS[ri]["code"], t)[0]]   # keep tests that fail on the bug
    print(f"{REPOS[ri]['name']:11} model tests kept: {len(GEN_TESTS[ri])}/{len(mine)}")

A6. Selection: test votes, then a model judge

  • Votes: each edit scores one point per model test it passes.
  • Top-3 + judge: keep the 3 best by votes and show their diffs to the model, which picks one. CodeMonkeys found that the model judge did worse than plain voting without the top-3 filter, and better with it.
a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 8
def votes(ri):
    return [sum(run_test(REPOS[ri], e, t)[0] for t in GEN_TESTS[ri]) for e in EDITS[ri]]

def diff(a, b):
    return "".join(difflib.unified_diff(a.splitlines(True), b.splitlines(True), "before", "after", n=1))[:1500]

def judge(ri, idxs):
    r = REPOS[ri]
    cands = "\n\n".join(f"Candidate {n + 1}:\n```diff\n{diff(r['code'], EDITS[ri][i])}```" for n, i in enumerate(idxs))
    reply = ask(f"Issue:\n{r['issue']}\n\nThese are candidate fixes as diffs:\n\n{cands}\n\n"
                f"Which candidate fixes the issue correctly without breaking anything else? Reply with only the number (1-{len(idxs)}).",
                max_tokens=20)
    m = re.search(r"\d+", reply or "")
    n = int(m.group(0)) - 1 if m else 0
    return idxs[n] if 0 <= n < len(idxs) else idxs[0]

SEL = {}
for ri in range(len(REPOS)):
    v = votes(ri)
    order = sorted(range(len(v)), key=lambda i: -v[i])          # stable: ties keep the sampling order
    top3 = order[:3]
    distinct = len({EDITS[ri][i] for i in top3}) > 1
    SEL[ri] = {"votes": order[0], "votes + judge": judge(ri, top3) if distinct else order[0]}
    print(f"{REPOS[ri]['name']:11} votes {v} | vote pick {order[0]} | judge pick {SEL[ri]['votes + judge']}")

scores = {"random": sum(sum(o) / len(o) for o in HIDDEN.values()) / len(HIDDEN)}
for m in ("votes", "votes + judge"):
    scores[m] = sum(HIDDEN[ri][SEL[ri][m]] for ri in SEL) / len(SEL)
scores["oracle (coverage)"] = COVERAGE[N_EDITS]
for m, s in scores.items():
    print(f"{m:18} {s:.0%}")

Part B · KernelBench at CPU scale

KernelBench (Ouyang, Guo et al. 2025, KernelBench: Can LLMs Write Efficient GPU Kernels?) asks a model to rewrite a PyTorch program as faster GPU code. It checks the output against the reference on 5 random inputs and times both. Its metric is

$$\text{fast}_p = \frac{1}{N}\sum_i \mathbf{1}[\text{correct}_i \wedge \text{speedup}_i > p]$$

so fast_0 is just the correctness rate, and fast_1 means "correct and faster than the reference".

We have no GPU budget, so we do the same thing with slow Python/NumPy functions on the CPU.

B1. Four slow functions

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 9
KTASKS = [
    dict(fn="pairwise_dist", ref='''import numpy as np

def pairwise_dist(X, Y):
    """Euclidean distance between every row of X (n, d) and every row of Y (m, d). Returns (n, m)."""
    n, m = X.shape[0], Y.shape[0]
    out = np.zeros((n, m))
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            out[i, j] = np.sqrt(np.sum((X[i] - Y[j]) ** 2))
    return out
''', gen='''def make_inputs(rng, big, case):
    n, m, d = (150, 150, 8) if big else (int(rng.integers(1, 30)), int(rng.integers(1, 30)), int(rng.integers(1, 6)))
    return (rng.normal(size=(n, d)), rng.normal(size=(m, d)))
'''),
    dict(fn="moving_average", ref='''import numpy as np

def moving_average(x, w):
    """Mean of every window of length w in the 1-D array x. Returns len(x) - w + 1 values."""
    out = np.empty(len(x) - w + 1)
    for i in range(len(out)):
        out[i] = x[i:i + w].mean()
    return out
''', gen='''def make_inputs(rng, big, case):
    n = 40000 if big else int(rng.integers(1, 200))
    w = 25 if big else [1, n, int(rng.integers(1, n + 1)), int(rng.integers(1, n + 1)), 1][case]
    return (rng.normal(size=n) * 10, w)
'''),
    dict(fn="softmax_rows", ref='''import math
import numpy as np

def softmax_rows(M):
    """Softmax of each row of the 2-D array M."""
    out = np.empty(M.shape, dtype=float)
    for i in range(M.shape[0]):
        row = M[i]
        m = max(row)
        e = [math.exp(v - m) for v in row]
        s = sum(e)
        for j in range(len(row)):
            out[i, j] = e[j] / s
    return out
''', gen='''def make_inputs(rng, big, case):
    r, c = (500, 300) if big else (int(rng.integers(1, 20)), int(rng.integers(1, 30)))
    scale = [1, 5, 50, 400, 900][case % 5]          # large values break a softmax that skips the max-subtraction
    return (rng.normal(size=(r, c)) * scale,)
'''),
    dict(fn="user_scores", ref='''import numpy as np

def clean(amounts):
    return np.clip(amounts, 0, None)

def per_user_totals(user_ids, amounts, n_users):
    totals = np.zeros(n_users)
    for u, x in zip(user_ids, amounts):
        totals[u] += x
    return totals

def normalize(totals):
    return totals / (totals.max() + 1e-9)

def user_scores(user_ids, amounts, n_users):
    """Total positive spend per user, scaled so the top user is about 1."""
    return normalize(per_user_totals(user_ids, clean(amounts), n_users))
''', gen='''def make_inputs(rng, big, case):
    n_users = 1000 if big else int(rng.integers(1, 20))
    n = 200000 if big else int(rng.integers(1, 300))
    return (rng.integers(0, n_users, size=n), rng.normal(size=n) * 50, n_users)
'''),
]
print([t["fn"] for t in KTASKS])

B2. The evaluator: correctness on 5 random inputs, then timing

All of this runs in a subprocess with a timeout. Correctness uses np.allclose (relative 1e-5, absolute 1e-6) and requires the same shape. Timing takes the best of 3 runs on one large input. The profiler mode runs cProfile on the large input and returns the functions that used the most time.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 10
KB_RUNNER = r'''
import copy, cProfile, json, os, pstats, sys, time, traceback
import numpy as np
mode, fn, ref_path, cand_path, gen_path = sys.argv[1:6]
def load(path):
    ns = {}
    exec(compile(open(path).read(), os.path.basename(path), "exec"), ns)
    return ns
gen = load(gen_path)["make_inputs"]
ref = load(ref_path)[fn]
res = {"correct": False, "speedup": 0.0, "error": None, "detail": None}
try:
    cand = load(cand_path)[fn]
    if mode == "profile":
        args = gen(np.random.default_rng(123), True, 0)
        prof = cProfile.Profile(); prof.enable(); cand(*args); prof.disable()
        st = pstats.Stats(prof).stats
        total = sum(v[2] for v in st.values()) or 1e-9
        rows = sorted(((k[2] if k[0] != "~" else "builtin " + k[2], os.path.basename(k[0]), v[1], v[2], v[3])
                       for k, v in st.items() if "disable" not in k[2]),
                      key=lambda r: -r[3])[:8]
        print(json.dumps({"total": total, "rows": rows})); sys.exit(0)
    for case in range(5):
        args = gen(np.random.default_rng(case), False, case)
        want, got = ref(*copy.deepcopy(args)), np.asarray(cand(*copy.deepcopy(args)), dtype=float)
        want = np.asarray(want, dtype=float)
        if got.shape != want.shape:
            res["detail"] = f"case {case}: shape {got.shape}, expected {want.shape}"; break
        if not np.allclose(got, want, rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-6):
            bad = ~np.isfinite(got)
            res["detail"] = (f"case {case}: {int(bad.sum())} non-finite values (inf/nan)" if bad.any() else
                             f"case {case}: max abs error {float(np.max(np.abs(got - want))):.3g}")
            break
    else:
        args = gen(np.random.default_rng(123), True, 0)
        def best(f):
            ts = []
            for _ in range(3):
                t0 = time.perf_counter(); out = f(*copy.deepcopy(args)); ts.append(time.perf_counter() - t0)
            return min(ts), out
        t_ref, want = best(ref)
        t_new, got = best(cand)
        if np.asarray(got).shape == np.asarray(want).shape and np.allclose(got, want, rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-6):
            res.update(correct=True, speedup=t_ref / max(t_new, 1e-9), t_ref=t_ref, t_new=t_new)
        else:
            res["detail"] = "wrong values on the large input"
except Exception as e:
    res["error"] = traceback.format_exc().strip().splitlines()[-1][:300]
print(json.dumps(res))
'''
RUNNER_PATH = os.path.join(WORK, "kb_runner.py")
open(RUNNER_PATH, "w").write(KB_RUNNER)

def kb_run(task, cand_code, mode="eval", timeout=90):
    d = tempfile.mkdtemp(dir=WORK)
    paths = {}
    for name, src in (("ref", task["ref"]), ("cand", cand_code), ("gen", task["gen"])):
        paths[name] = os.path.join(d, f"{name}.py")
        open(paths[name], "w").write(src)
    out = None
    try:
        out = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-I", RUNNER_PATH, mode, task["fn"], paths["ref"], paths["cand"], paths["gen"]],
                             capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=timeout)
        return json.loads(out.stdout.strip().splitlines()[-1])
    except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
        return {"correct": False, "speedup": 0.0, "error": f"timed out after {timeout}s", "detail": None}
    except Exception:
        return {"correct": False, "speedup": 0.0, "error": "runner failed: " + ((out.stderr or "")[-200:] if out else ""), "detail": None}
    finally:
        shutil.rmtree(d, ignore_errors=True)

def fast_p(results, p):
    return sum(r["correct"] and r["speedup"] > p for r in results) / len(results)

# Test the evaluator before trusting it: the reference vs itself, a known-wrong version, a known-fast version.
self_check = kb_run(KTASKS[0], KTASKS[0]["ref"])
naive_softmax = "import numpy as np\ndef softmax_rows(M):\n    e = np.exp(M)\n    return e / e.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n"
wrong_check = kb_run(KTASKS[2], naive_softmax)
fast_users = ("import numpy as np\ndef user_scores(user_ids, amounts, n_users):\n"
              "    t = np.bincount(user_ids, weights=np.clip(amounts, 0, None), minlength=n_users)\n    return t / (t.max() + 1e-9)\n")
fast_check = kb_run(KTASKS[3], fast_users)
print("reference vs itself:", self_check)
print("softmax without max-subtraction:", wrong_check)
print("bincount version of user_scores:", fast_check)

B3. The agent-system interface: scalar feedback vs. a profiler

Wei, Nie et al. (2024/2025, Improving Parallel Program Performance with LLM Optimizers via Agent-System Interfaces) found that what the optimizer sees matters as much as the model. Their interface had two parts: a small language that limits what the agent can write, and AutoGuide, which turns raw system output into plain advice. With it, an LLM optimizer beat OpenTuner (which only gets a number) within 10 iterations.

We compare two interfaces, 3 turns each (an attempt, then 2 rounds of feedback): - Scalar: the agent sees only "correct? speedup?", like a classic autotuner. - Profiler + guide: the agent also gets a cProfile table of the reference up front, and after each attempt a plain-language reading of the result: the error explained, which test case failed and how, and where its own code spends time.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 11
def profile_text(task, code):
    p = kb_run(task, code, mode="profile")
    if "rows" not in p:
        return f"(profiler failed: {p.get('error')})", None
    lines = [f"{'function':40} {'calls':>8} {'own time s':>10} {'with callees s':>14}"]
    lines += [f"{f[:40]:40} {n:>8} {tt:>10.4f} {ct:>14.4f}" for f, _, n, tt, ct in p["rows"]]
    hot = p["rows"][0]
    return "\n".join(lines), (hot[0], hot[3] / p["total"], hot[2])

def guide(task, code, r):
    """A tiny AutoGuide: turn raw results into actionable advice."""
    if r.get("error"):
        return f"Your code crashed: {r['error']}. Fix this error first and keep the same function name."
    if not r["correct"]:
        return (f"Your code returns wrong results: {r['detail']}. Compare with the reference on that kind of input "
                "(edge cases, very large or very small values, window sizes, shapes).")
    table, hot = profile_text(task, code)
    tip = (f"Hot spot: `{hot[0]}` takes {hot[1]:.0%} of the time ({hot[2]} calls). If it loops over array elements in Python, "
           "replace the loop with whole-array NumPy operations." if hot else "")
    return f"Correct. Speedup {r['speedup']:.2f}x over the reference.\nProfile of your version:\n{table}\n{tip}"

def scalar_feedback(task, code, r):
    return f"Result: correct={r['correct']}, speedup={r['speedup']:.2f}x."

def optimize(task, interface, turns=3):
    intro = (f"Here is a slow Python function:\n```python\n{task['ref']}```\n"
             f"Write a faster version with the SAME function name `{task['fn']}` and the same arguments. Use NumPy. "
             "It must return the same values (within 1e-6) for any valid input. Return the complete code in one ```python block.")
    if interface == "profiler":
        intro += "\n\nProfiler output for the reference on a large input:\n" + profile_text(task, task["ref"])[0]
    msgs, best, history = [{"role": "user", "content": intro}], {"correct": False, "speedup": 0.0}, []
    for turn in range(turns):
        reply = chat(msgs, temperature=0.3, max_tokens=900).content or ""
        code = extract_code(reply)
        r = kb_run(task, code)
        if r["correct"] and r["speedup"] > best["speedup"]:
            best = r
        history.append(dict(best))                                # best-so-far after each turn
        if r["correct"] and r["speedup"] >= 20:                    # good enough: save calls
            history += [dict(best)] * (turns - turn - 1)
            break
        fb = guide(task, code, r) if interface == "profiler" else scalar_feedback(task, code, r)
        msgs += [{"role": "assistant", "content": reply}, {"role": "user", "content": fb + "\nWrite an improved version. Return the complete code in one ```python block."}]
    return history

RUNS = {}
for interface in ("scalar", "profiler"):
    RUNS[interface] = [optimize(t, interface) for t in KTASKS]     # up to 4 tasks x 3 turns = 12 calls per interface
    for t, h in zip(KTASKS, RUNS[interface]):
        print(f"{interface:8} {t['fn']:15} best speedup by turn: {[round(x['speedup'], 1) if x['correct'] else 'x' for x in h]}")

B4. fast_p, one-shot and after feedback

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 12
print(f"{'interface':10} {'turns':>5} {'fast_0':>7} {'fast_1':>7} {'fast_2':>7} {'fast_10':>8}")
FAST = {}
for interface, runs in RUNS.items():
    for n in (1, 3):
        res = [h[min(n, len(h)) - 1] for h in runs]
        FAST[(interface, n)] = {p: fast_p(res, p) for p in (0, 1, 2, 10)}
        print(f"{interface:10} {n:>5} " + " ".join(f"{FAST[(interface, n)][p]:>7.0%}" for p in (0, 1, 2)) + f" {FAST[(interface, n)][10]:>8.0%}")

What to notice: fast_p drops as p rises, the same shape as in KernelBench. Feedback turns usually help most by fixing wrong answers (KernelBench saw the same: execution feedback mostly fixed errors). Whether the profiler beats scalar feedback on 4 tasks is noisy; on user_scores the profiler points straight at per_user_totals, which is where the paper's "interpret the raw output" idea pays off.

a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 13
check("each repo task is sound (hidden test fails on the bug, passes on the fix)", all(a and b for a, b in sound.values()))
check("evaluator: the reference matches itself", self_check["correct"])
check("evaluator: softmax without max-subtraction is caught as wrong", not wrong_check["correct"])
check("evaluator: a vectorized version is correct and faster", fast_check["correct"] and fast_check["speedup"] > 1)
check("at least one sampled edit fixes at least one repo (coverage > 0)", COVERAGE[N_EDITS] > 0)
check("test-vote selection is at least as good as picking at random", scores["votes"] >= scores["random"] - 1e-9)
check("the optimizer produced at least one correct, faster function", max(FAST[(i, 3)][1] for i in RUNS) > 0)

Your turn

  1. Serial compute. CodeMonkeys let each attempt see test results and try again. Add one feedback round to gen_edit: run the attempt against its own model test, and if it fails, send the error back and ask for a new file. Does coverage@1 rise?
  2. Sharper fast_p. Rerun B4 with p = 5. Which tasks still count? Why is a 1.0x threshold too easy for NumPy rewrites of Python loops (but hard on KernelBench, where the baseline is already optimized PyTorch)?
  3. A gaming check. Write a "fast" moving_average that is only correct when w == 25 (the size used for timing). Does our evaluator catch it? What does this say about checking on several random inputs?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 14
# Solution 1: one round of execution feedback per attempt (up to 4 more calls; we try the first edit of each repo)
def gen_edit_with_feedback(ri, first):
    r = REPOS[ri]
    if not GEN_TESTS[ri]:
        return first
    ok, msg = run_test(r, first, GEN_TESTS[ri][0])
    if ok:
        return first
    reply = ask(edit_prompt(r) + f"\n\nYour previous attempt:\n```python\n{first}```\nfailed this test:\n```python\n{GEN_TESTS[ri][0]}```\n"
                f"Error:\n{msg}\nNote the test itself may be wrong. Return the complete fixed file in one ```python block.",
                temperature=0.3, max_tokens=900)
    return extract_code(reply)
before = sum(HIDDEN[ri][0] for ri in HIDDEN)
after = sum(run_test(REPOS[ri], gen_edit_with_feedback(ri, EDITS[ri][0]), REPOS[ri]["test"])[0] for ri in HIDDEN)
print(f"first attempt fixes {before}/{len(HIDDEN)} repos; after one feedback round: {after}/{len(HIDDEN)}")
# What to notice: model tests can be wrong, so feedback from them can also mislead. CodeMonkeys found that spending
# compute on more serial iterations or more parallel samples often gave similar coverage for the same cost.
a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 15
# Solution 2: a higher speedup threshold (no new model calls)
for interface, runs in RUNS.items():
    res = [h[-1] for h in runs]
    print(interface, "fast_5:", f"{fast_p(res, 5):.0%}", [(t["fn"], round(r["speedup"], 1)) for t, r in zip(KTASKS, res) if r["correct"]])
# What to notice: replacing a Python loop with one NumPy call often gives 10-100x, so p=1 is easy here.
# KernelBench's baseline is PyTorch's own tuned kernels, so even p=1 is hard: one-shot, the best models matched PyTorch in under 20% of cases.
a08_agentic_swe.ipynb · cell 16
# Solution 3: a version that only works for the timing input's window size
cheat = ("import numpy as np\ndef moving_average(x, w):\n    k = np.ones(25) / 25\n    return np.convolve(x, k, mode='valid')\n")
print(kb_run(KTASKS[1], cheat))
# What to notice: it fails on the random small cases (other window sizes), so it scores as incorrect.
# If we had only checked the timing input, it would have looked perfect. Always check on varied random inputs.
Traps
  • High coverage is not a high score. More parallel samples can make selection harder.
  • Model-written tests can be wrong. Drop tests that already pass on the buggy code, and don't trust a single test.
  • A model judge on the whole pool can do worse than plain voting; filter to a short list first.
  • Checking speed on one input invites gaming. Check correctness on several random inputs, including edge cases like large values.
  • A speedup threshold of 1x is easy when the baseline is a Python loop and hard when the baseline is already tuned. Pick p for your baseline.
  • Timing is noisy. Take the best of several runs and keep timing in a separate process.
  • Never run model-written code in your own process. Use a subprocess with a timeout and a fresh folder.

Check yourself

What are CodeMonkeys' three subtasks?

Context (find relevant files), generation (sample edits and tests), and selection (choose one edit).

How does CodeMonkeys select an edit?

It runs model-written tests on all edits, keeps the top 3 by votes, and a model picks among them, optionally writing more tests.

What does fast_1 measure?

The fraction of tasks where the generated code is correct and faster than the reference.

What did profiler and execution feedback do for DeepSeek-R1 on KernelBench Level 2?

Raised fast_1 from 36% to 72% over 10 turns of refinement.

What are the two parts of the agent-system interface in Wei et al.?

A domain-specific language for mappers, and AutoGuide, which turns raw execution output into actionable feedback.

Part 2 · A09

Augmenting agents with memory

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 14 (Nov 7), guest lecturer Junchen Jiang (LMCache, UChicago)

A model only sees its context window, and long contexts are slow and costly. You can give an agent more memory at three layers: let the agent page facts in and out (MemGPT), let the server reuse work it already did on the same text (KV-cache reuse: CacheBlend, LMCache), or compress a corpus into a small trained memory ahead of time (Cartridges).

Key terms

Context window
The text a model can see in one call. Everything else is invisible to it.Source: general
Main context / external context
MemGPT's split: what is in the prompt now vs what is stored outside and must be fetched with a function call.Source: Packer et al. 2023
Working context
A small, writable block of notes inside the prompt that survives when old messages are evicted.Source: Packer et al. 2023
Archival storage
A searchable store of documents outside the window; the agent pages results in.Source: Packer et al. 2023
KV cache
The key and value vectors a transformer computes for every prompt token at every layer. Reusing them skips re-reading that text.Source: general
Prefill
The step where the model reads the whole prompt before writing; its time grows with prompt length.Source: general
Time to first token (TTFT)
How long a user waits before the first word appears; dominated by prefill on long prompts.Source: Yao et al. 2024
Prefix caching
Reusing the KV cache when a new prompt starts with exactly the same text as an earlier one.Source: general
KV deviation
How much a token's cached KV differs from what full recompute would give; CacheBlend recomputes the tokens with the largest deviation.Source: Yao et al. 2024
Cartridge
A small trained KV cache that stands in for a whole corpus at question time.Source: Eyuboglu et al. 2025
Self-study
Training data for a Cartridge: the model's own synthetic conversations about chunks of the corpus.Source: Eyuboglu et al. 2025
Context distillation
Training the model-with-Cartridge to match the next-token probabilities of the model-with-full-text.Source: Eyuboglu et al. 2025

The papers

Cartridges: Lightweight and general-purpose long context representations via self-study

Eyuboglu, Ehrlich, Arora, Guha, Zinsley, Liu, Tennien, Rudra, Zou, Mirhoseini, Ré · 2025

What
Train a small KV cache (a 'Cartridge') offline for each corpus and load it at question time instead of the whole corpus. Training on the raw text with next-token prediction generalized poorly, so they propose self-study: the model has synthetic conversations about chunks of the corpus (from generic seed prompts such as summarize, structure, ask a question), and the Cartridge is trained by context distillation to match the model's next-token distributions when the chunk is in context.
Result
Matched in-context learning quality on long-context benchmarks with 38.6x less memory and 26.4x higher throughput. Extended effective context on MTOB from 128k to 484k tokens. Distillation beat next-token training on MTOB (chrF 24.9 to 33.5). Initializing from the first p corpus tokens beat random vectors on LongHealth (55.3% vs 29.9%). Prefix-tuning kept MMLU near 54.7 while a memory-matched LoRA fell to 45.3. Cartridges can be composed at inference without retraining.
Takeaway
If one large corpus (a contract archive, a codebase) is queried thousands of times, paying once to train a compact memory can beat re-reading it each time. The training data must be varied synthetic Q&A, not the raw text.

MemGPT: Towards LLMs as Operating Systems

Packer, Wooders, Lin, Fang, Patil, Stoica, Gonzalez · 2023

What
Virtual context management modeled on an operating system's memory hierarchy. Main context = read-only system instructions, a writable working context, and a FIFO message queue headed by a recursive summary. External context = recall storage (all messages) and archival storage (vector-searched text). The model moves data with function calls; results are paginated; a memory-pressure warning fires at about 70% of the window and about half the queue is evicted at 100%.
Result
Deep memory retrieval in multi-session chat: GPT-4 accuracy 32.1% without MemGPT vs 92.5% with it (GPT-4 Turbo 35.3% vs 93.4%; GPT-3.5 Turbo 38.7% vs 66.9%). On nested key-value retrieval, GPT-4 and GPT-4 Turbo alone fell to 0% by 3 nesting levels, while MemGPT with GPT-4 was unaffected by depth. On document QA, MemGPT's accuracy stayed flat as more documents were retrieved, while fixed-context baselines were capped by the retriever.
Takeaway
Give the agent explicit memory tools (search, save a note) and a hard context budget, and keep every message in an append-only store. Check that the agent actually saves what it needs: the model decides what to remember.

CacheBlend: Fast Large Language Model Serving for RAG with Cached Knowledge Fusion

Yao, Li, Liu, Ray, Cheng, Zhang, Du, Lu, Jiang · 2024

What
Prefix caching only helps when a request starts with exactly the same text, which RAG rarely does. CacheBlend reuses each retrieved chunk's precomputed KV cache wherever it sits, and recomputes only a small share of tokens (15% per layer by default) chosen by KV deviation, the tokens whose KV values change most from cross-attention to earlier chunks. Recompute overlaps with loading caches from slower storage.
Result
Tested on Mistral-7B, Yi-34B and Llama-70B over 2WikiMQA, Musique, SAMSum and MultiNews. Versus full KV recompute: time to first token 2.2-3.3x lower and throughput 2.8-5x higher, with quality within 0.02 F1/Rouge-L. Recomputing 5-18% of tokens lost at most 0.002 F1/Rouge-L on Yi-34B. Full KV reuse without recompute was about as fast but lost 0.1-0.2 F1 on QA.
Takeaway
For RAG on your own GPUs, the prefill of retrieved chunks is the main latency cost. Cache chunk KV and use a serving stack that can blend it (LMCache with vLLM). Even without that, put stable text first so plain prefix caching hits.

LMCache: An Efficient KV Cache Layer for Enterprise-Scale LLM Inference (background reading, our pick)

Cheng, Liu, et al. (with Junchen Jiang) · 2025

What
The open-source KV cache layer from the guest lecturer's group. It moves KV caches out of GPU memory into CPU, disk or network storage and shares them across vLLM/SGLang engines and queries, supporting both prefix reuse across queries and prefill-decode disaggregation. Not an assigned course paper; added because the guest lecture is about it.
Result
The authors report up to 15x higher throughput when combined with vLLM on workloads such as multi-round QA and document analysis, and observe that context truncation, common in industry, can cut prefix cache hit ratio by half.
Takeaway
If you self-host models for repeated document work, a KV cache layer is often the cheapest speed-up available, before buying more GPUs.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a09_memory.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 2
import json, re, time, math
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from lal.llm import client, ask, chat
from lal.data import contract, labels, ids, sections
from lal.checks import check

LAB = labels()

1. MemGPT: an agent that manages its own memory

MemGPT (Packer et al. 2023, MemGPT: Towards LLMs as Operating Systems) splits memory into tiers, like RAM and disk: - Main context (what the model sees): fixed system instructions, a small working context the agent writes notes into, and a FIFO queue (first in, first out) of recent messages. - External context (outside the window): recall storage, every message ever, and archival storage, a searchable store of documents.

The agent moves data between tiers by calling functions. When the prompt reaches about 70% of the window, the system adds a "memory pressure" warning so the agent saves what matters. When it is full, old messages are evicted (they stay in recall storage). Search results come back in pages, so one search can never flood the window.

First the pure-Python parts: an archival store with a small TF-IDF search (a word-overlap score that weights rare words more), and a main context with a character budget.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 3
def tokenize(s):
    return re.findall(r"[a-z0-9_]+", s.lower())

class ArchivalStore:
    """Archival storage: text passages plus a tiny TF-IDF search. Results come back in pages."""
    def __init__(self, passages):
        self.passages = passages
        self.docs = [Counter(tokenize(p)) for p in passages]
        df = Counter(w for d in self.docs for w in d)
        self.idf = {w: math.log(1 + len(passages) / c) for w, c in df.items()}

    def search(self, query, page=0, page_size=3):
        q = set(tokenize(query))
        named = {w for w in q if re.fullmatch(r"msa_\d+", w)}      # a contract id in the query acts as a filter
        scores = [(sum(d[w] * self.idf.get(w, 0) for w in q) / (1 + sum(d.values())) ** 0.5, i)
                  for i, d in enumerate(self.docs) if not named or named & set(d)]
        ranked = [i for s, i in sorted(scores, reverse=True) if s > 0]
        hits = ranked[page * page_size:(page + 1) * page_size]
        more = len(ranked) > (page + 1) * page_size
        out = "\n".join(f"- {self.passages[i]}" for i in hits) or "(no results)"
        return out + (f"\n[page {page}; more results: call again with page={page + 1}]" if more else f"\n[page {page}; no more results]")

# One passage per contract section, tagged with the contract id so the search can find it.
PASSAGES = [f"[{cid} section {s['id']} {s['title']}] {s['text']}" for cid in ids() for s in sections(contract(cid))]
store = ArchivalStore(PASSAGES)
print(len(PASSAGES), "passages in archival storage")
print(store.search("msa_07 fees per month"))
a09_memory.ipynb · cell 4
class MainContext:
    """Main context = system prompt + working context + FIFO queue, kept under a character budget.
    The queue holds 'units' (one assistant turn plus its tool results), so we never split a tool call from its result."""
    def __init__(self, system, budget_chars=2500, warn_at=0.7):
        self.system, self.budget, self.warn_at = system, budget_chars, warn_at
        self.working = []          # notes the agent chose to keep (read/write via a tool)
        self.queue = []            # list of units; each unit is a list of messages
        self.recall = []           # recall storage: every message ever, never deleted
        self.evicted = 0

    def size(self):
        return len(self.render_system()) + sum(len(json.dumps(m)) for u in self.queue for m in u)

    def render_system(self):
        notes = "\n".join(f"- {n}" for n in self.working) or "(empty)"
        return f"{self.system}\n\nWORKING CONTEXT (your saved notes):\n{notes}"

    def add(self, unit):
        self.queue.append(unit)
        self.recall.extend(unit)
        while self.size() > self.budget and len(self.queue) > 2:   # keep the task and the newest unit
            self.queue.pop(1)                                      # queue[0] is the user's task: never evicted
            self.evicted += 1

    def messages(self):
        msgs = [{"role": "system", "content": self.render_system()}]
        msgs += [m for u in self.queue for m in u]
        if self.evicted:
            msgs.insert(2, {"role": "user", "content": f"[system note: {self.evicted} older steps were evicted to recall storage. Your notes are in WORKING CONTEXT.]"})
        if self.size() > self.warn_at * self.budget:
            msgs.append({"role": "user", "content": "[system warning: memory pressure. Save any facts you still need with working_context_append now.]"})
        return msgs

# Pure-Python test: fill the context past its budget and confirm it stays under budget.
mc = MainContext("test", budget_chars=600)
mc.add([{"role": "user", "content": "the task"}])
for i in range(10):
    mc.add([{"role": "user", "content": "x" * 100 + str(i)}])
print("size", mc.size(), "<= budget", mc.budget, "| evicted", mc.evicted, "| recall keeps", len(mc.recall), "messages")

Now the agent. It has three tools: archival_search, working_context_append and finish. The task needs facts from four different contracts, but the window budget (2,500 characters, about 600 tokens) is far smaller than the four contracts together (about 7,000 characters). So the agent must search, write a note, and let old results be evicted.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 5
TOOLS = [
    {"type": "function", "function": {"name": "archival_search",
        "description": "Search archival storage (all contract sections). Include the contract id and topic in the query, e.g. 'msa_07 fees'. Results are paged.",
        "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"query": {"type": "string"}, "page": {"type": "integer"}}, "required": ["query"]}}},
    {"type": "function", "function": {"name": "working_context_append",
        "description": "Save one short fact to your working context so it survives eviction, e.g. 'msa_07 fee = 4500'.",
        "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"fact": {"type": "string"}}, "required": ["fact"]}}},
    {"type": "function", "function": {"name": "finish",
        "description": "Give the final answer.",
        "parameters": {"type": "object", "properties": {"answer": {"type": "string"}}, "required": ["answer"]}}},
]
SYSTEM = ("You are an agent with a small memory. Facts live in archival storage; search it one contract at a time. "
          "After each search, save the key number with working_context_append, because old search results will be evicted. "
          "When you have every fact, call finish.")

def memgpt_agent(task, budget_chars=2500, max_steps=14, verbose=True):
    ctx = MainContext(SYSTEM, budget_chars)
    ctx.add([{"role": "user", "content": task}])
    peak = 0
    for step in range(max_steps):
        msgs = ctx.messages()
        peak = max(peak, sum(len(json.dumps(m)) for m in msgs))
        msg = chat(msgs, tools=TOOLS)
        calls = msg.tool_calls or []
        if not calls:                        # the model answered in plain text: treat it as final
            return {"answer": msg.content or "", "steps": step + 1, "peak_chars": peak, "notes": ctx.working, "evicted": ctx.evicted}
        unit = [{"role": "assistant", "content": msg.content or "",
                 "tool_calls": [{"id": c.id, "type": "function", "function": {"name": c.function.name, "arguments": c.function.arguments}} for c in calls]}]
        for c in calls:
            try:
                args = json.loads(c.function.arguments or "{}")
            except Exception:
                args = {}
            name = c.function.name
            if name == "finish":
                return {"answer": str(args.get("answer", "")), "steps": step + 1, "peak_chars": peak, "notes": ctx.working, "evicted": ctx.evicted}
            if name == "archival_search":
                try:
                    result = store.search(str(args.get("query", "")), int(args.get("page", 0) or 0))
                except Exception as e:
                    result = f"ERROR: {e}"
            elif name == "working_context_append":
                ctx.working.append(str(args.get("fact", ""))[:200])
                result = "saved"
            else:
                result = f"ERROR: unknown tool {name}"
            if verbose:
                print(f"  step {step+1}: {name}({c.function.arguments[:60]}) -> {result[:70]!r}")
            unit.append({"role": "tool", "tool_call_id": c.id, "content": result})
        ctx.add(unit)
    return {"answer": "STOPPED: step limit", "steps": max_steps, "peak_chars": peak, "notes": ctx.working, "evicted": ctx.evicted}

def first_number(s):
    m = re.findall(r"\d[\d,]*", s or "")
    return int(m[-1].replace(",", "")) if m else None

TARGETS = ["msa_03", "msa_07", "msa_12", "msa_18"]
TRUTH = sum(LAB[c]["terms"]["monthly_fee"] for c in TARGETS)
task = f"What is the total monthly fee, in dollars, across contracts {', '.join(TARGETS)}? Answer with one number."
t0 = time.time()
out = memgpt_agent(task)
print(f"\n{out['steps']} steps in {time.time()-t0:.0f}s | peak prompt {out['peak_chars']} chars | evicted {out['evicted']} units")
print("notes:", out["notes"])
print("answer:", out["answer"], "| truth:", TRUTH)
print("the 4 contracts in full would be", sum(len(contract(c)) for c in TARGETS), "chars")

What to notice: the prompt never grew past the budget, yet the agent used facts from four contracts. The working-context notes are what made that work: when a search result was evicted, the number survived as a note. The weak spot is also clear: if the agent forgets to save a note, the fact is gone from its view (it is still in recall storage, but only a search would bring it back).

2. KV-cache reuse: why CacheBlend and LMCache matter

Before a model writes its first word it must read the whole prompt (the prefill). While reading, it builds a KV cache: key and value vectors for every token at every layer. Prefill time grows with prompt length, so a long prompt means a long wait for the first token (time to first token, TTFT).

If the next request starts with the exact same text, the server can reuse the cached keys and values and skip that work. This is prefix caching. Ollama does it automatically within one model slot. We measure it: 1. a long shared prefix (8 contracts) + question A (cold: nothing cached); 2. the same prefix + question B (warm: the prefix is reused); 3. one changed word at the start of the prefix + question A (everything after the change must be recomputed); 4. one changed word at the end of the prefix + question A (most of the prefix is still reusable).

Caveat: Ollama runs 2 slots here. If a request lands in the other slot, it will look "cold". Run the cell twice if the numbers look odd.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 6
def ttft(prompt, max_tokens=8):
    """Time to the first streamed token, in seconds."""
    t0 = time.time()
    stream = client.chat.completions.create(model=MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": prompt}],
                                            temperature=0, max_tokens=max_tokens, stream=True)
    first = None
    for chunk in stream:
        if first is None and chunk.choices and (chunk.choices[0].delta.content or ""):
            first = time.time() - t0
    return first if first is not None else time.time() - t0

DOCS = ids()[:8]
PREFIX = "\n\n".join(f"=== {c} ===\n{contract(c)}" for c in DOCS)
QA = "\n\nQuestion: What governing law does msa_03 use? Answer in one word."
QB = "\n\nQuestion: What is the monthly fee in msa_05? Answer with a number."
ask("Say OK.", max_tokens=2)                      # load the model so loading time is not counted
results = {}
results["1 cold: new prefix"] = ttft(PREFIX + QA)
results["2 warm: same prefix, new question"] = ttft(PREFIX + QB)
results["3 changed first word of prefix"] = ttft("NOTE. " + PREFIX + QA)
results["4 changed last part of prefix"] = ttft(PREFIX + "\n(end of documents)" + QA)
for k, v in results.items():
    print(f"{k:38} TTFT {v:5.2f}s")
print(f"prefix is about {len(PREFIX)//4} tokens")

What to notice: the warm request should be much faster than the cold one, and changing the very first word should make it slow again. Prefix caching is all or nothing from the point of change: one new word at the start throws away the whole cache.

That is the problem in RAG (retrieval-augmented generation), where each request glues together different retrieved chunks in a different order. Each chunk may have been seen before, but never with the same prefix, so prefix caching misses. CacheBlend (Yao et al. 2024, CacheBlend: Fast LLM Serving for RAG with Cached Knowledge Fusion) reuses each chunk's cached KV even when it is not at the start, and recomputes only a small share of tokens (15% per layer by default), the ones whose KV values would change most because of the text before them. It cut TTFT 2.2-3.3x versus full recompute with about the same quality. LMCache (Cheng et al. 2025, LMCache: An Efficient KV Cache Layer for Enterprise-Scale LLM Inference) is the open-source layer that stores these caches outside GPU memory (CPU, disk, network) and shares them across vLLM/SGLang engines and queries.

Builder rule you can use today: put stable text first (system prompt, rules, documents) and changing text last (the question). Never put a timestamp at the top of a prompt.

3. Cartridges: study the document once, answer many questions cheaply

Cartridges (Eyuboglu et al. 2025, Cartridges: Lightweight and general-purpose long context representations via self-study) train a small KV cache for one document collection, offline. At question time the model loads this small cache instead of the whole document. The training recipe is self-study: 1. split the corpus into chunks; 2. the model talks to itself about each chunk: one copy asks (from generic seed prompts like "summarize", "structure", "ask a question"), another answers with the chunk in view; 3. train the small cache with context distillation: make the model with the cartridge produce the same next-token probabilities as the model with the full chunk in context.

The paper reports matching in-context quality with 38.6x less memory and 26.4x higher throughput. Training a cartridge needs gradient access to the KV vectors, which Ollama does not give us. So we build a text analogy: self-study generates Q&A, the Q&A becomes compact "study notes", and we compare answering from notes vs from the full document. The real method trains vectors, not text.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 7
CORPUS = ids()[6:12]                           # 6 contracts, mostly ones with issues
FULL_DOC = "\n\n".join(f"=== {c} ===\n{contract(c)}" for c in CORPUS)
SEEDS = {"structuring": "List the key commercial terms of this contract as 'term: value' lines (fees, term, renewal, notice, liability cap, governing law).",
         "question": "Write 3 questions a lawyer would ask about this contract, each followed by its answer from the text."}

def self_study(cid):
    """Self-study on one chunk: generic seed prompts, the chunk in view. Returns synthetic Q&A text."""
    chunk = f"=== {cid} ===\n{contract(cid)}"
    return cid, "\n".join(ask(f"{chunk}\n\n{p}", max_tokens=300) for p in SEEDS.values())

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:                # 6 contracts x 2 seeds = 12 calls
    studied = dict(ex.map(self_study, CORPUS))

notes = []
for cid in CORPUS:                               # compress each chunk's study output into a short card (6 calls)
    card = ask(f"Study notes for contract {cid}:\n{studied[cid]}\n\nCompress these into at most 6 short lines of the form "
               f"'{cid} <term>: <value>'. Keep numbers and state names exact.", max_tokens=200)
    notes.append(card.strip())
CARTRIDGE = "\n".join(notes)
print(CARTRIDGE[:800])
print(f"\nfull document: {len(FULL_DOC)} chars | cartridge: {len(CARTRIDGE)} chars ({len(FULL_DOC)/max(len(CARTRIDGE),1):.1f}x smaller)")

Now the test. The questions come from the labels, not from the study step, so the notes cannot "know" the questions in advance.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 8
QS = ([(f"What state's law governs {c}? Answer with the state name only.", LAB[c]["terms"]["governing_law"].lower()) for c in CORPUS[:4]]
      + [(f"How many days' notice of non-renewal does {c} require? Answer with a number only.", str(LAB[c]["terms"]["notice_days"])) for c in CORPUS[2:]])
print(len(QS), "questions, e.g.", QS[0])

def answer_with(context, q):
    return ask(f"{context}\n\n{q}", max_tokens=20).strip().lower()

def grade(pred, gold):
    words = re.sub(r"[^a-z0-9 ]", " ", pred).split()
    return gold in words if gold.isdigit() else gold in pred

def run(context):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        preds = list(ex.map(lambda qg: answer_with(context, qg[0]), QS))
    return sum(grade(p, g) for p, (_, g) in zip(preds, QS)) / len(QS), preds

acc_full, _ = run(FULL_DOC)                       # 8 calls
acc_cart, preds_cart = run(CARTRIDGE)             # 8 calls
print(f"full document in context: {acc_full:.0%} | cartridge notes: {acc_cart:.0%} | size ratio {len(FULL_DOC)/max(len(CARTRIDGE),1):.1f}x")
for (q, g), p in zip(QS, preds_cart):
    print(f"  {'ok ' if grade(p, g) else 'MISS'} {q[:55]:55} -> {p[:25]!r} (truth {g})")

What to notice: the notes are several times smaller, and on facts that the generic seeds happened to cover, they do about as well. Facts that self-study never touched are simply missing. The paper saw the same thing in a sharper form: training on the raw text with plain next-token prediction memorized it but generalized poorly, and the varied synthetic conversations of self-study were what made cartridges work across question types. Composition also works there: two cartridges can be loaded together without retraining.

When is each tool right? - MemGPT-style paging: many documents, the agent decides what to look at, and you want an audit trail of what it read. - Prefix / KV caching: the same long text is sent again and again (system prompt, playbook, a contract under review). Free speed-up; just keep stable text first. - Cartridges: one large corpus queried thousands of times (a codebase, a company's contract archive). Pay once to train, then serve cheaply.

a09_memory.ipynb · cell 9
check("archival search finds the right contract section", "msa_07" in store.search("msa_07 fees per month").split("\n")[0])
check("main context stays under budget after eviction", mc.size() <= mc.budget and mc.evicted > 0 and len(mc.recall) == 11)
check("MemGPT agent gets the total fee right", first_number(out["answer"]) == TRUTH)
check("warm prefix is faster than cold prefix", results["2 warm: same prefix, new question"] < results["1 cold: new prefix"])
check("cartridge is at least 2x smaller than the document", len(FULL_DOC) >= 2 * len(CARTRIDGE))
check("cartridge keeps at least half the accuracy of the full document", acc_cart >= 0.5 * acc_full)

Your turn

  1. Shrink the MemGPT budget to 1,500 characters. Does the agent still get the right total? Look at how many units were evicted and whether its notes were complete.
  2. Move the question to the start of the prompt (question, then the 8 contracts) and re-time two different questions. What happens to the cache, and why?
  3. Add a third self-study seed ("summarize the risks for the customer") and re-run the cartridge test. Does broader self-study raise accuracy?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a09_memory.ipynb · cell 10
# Solution 1: a tighter budget
small = memgpt_agent(task, budget_chars=1500, verbose=False)
print("answer:", small["answer"], "| truth:", TRUTH, "| evicted:", small["evicted"], "| notes:", small["notes"])
# Notice: with less room more units are evicted, so success depends entirely on whether the agent wrote each fee as a note.
# This is the MemGPT trade-off: the model, not the system, decides what to remember.
a09_memory.ipynb · cell 11
# Solution 2: question first breaks the prefix cache
a = ttft(QA.strip() + "\n\n" + PREFIX)
b = ttft(QB.strip() + "\n\n" + PREFIX)
print(f"question-first A: {a:.2f}s, question-first B: {b:.2f}s")
# Notice: B is slow too. The prompts differ from the first token, so nothing can be reused.
# CacheBlend's idea is to reuse the documents' KV anyway, recomputing only the tokens that need it.
a09_memory.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 3: one more seed prompt (6 more calls for study, 6 for cards, 8 for the test)
SEEDS["risks"] = "Summarize the risks of this contract for the customer, quoting the clause numbers and values."
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    studied2 = dict(ex.map(self_study, CORPUS))
CART2 = "\n".join(ask(f"Study notes for contract {c}:\n{studied2[c]}\n\nCompress into at most 6 short lines '{c} <term>: <value>'. Keep numbers and state names exact.", max_tokens=200).strip() for c in CORPUS)
acc2, _ = run(CART2)
print(f"2 seeds: {acc_cart:.0%} | 3 seeds: {acc2:.0%} | cartridge sizes {len(CARTRIDGE)} vs {len(CART2)} chars")
# Notice: more varied self-study usually covers more facts. The paper found the same: the diversity of the synthetic
# conversations matters more than training on the raw text.
Traps
  • Putting a timestamp, request ID or user name at the top of the prompt. It changes the first tokens, so prefix caching never hits.
  • Trusting the agent to save notes. In MemGPT-style paging the model decides what to remember; if it skips a note, the fact is gone from view. Log everything and test recall.
  • Training a compact memory on the raw text with next-token prediction. Cartridges found it memorizes but does not generalize; the varied synthetic conversations are what make it work.
  • Measuring TTFT with the first call after loading the model. Load time swamps everything; warm up first.
  • Assuming two requests share a cache. Servers with several slots or replicas may route them apart; LMCache exists partly to share caches across engines.

Check yourself

Why does changing the first word of a long prompt make it slow again?

Prefix caching only reuses KV for an exact matching prefix; a change at the start invalidates everything after it, so the whole prompt is prefilled again.

In MemGPT, what survives when old messages are evicted from the FIFO queue?

The working context notes, a recursive summary, and the full messages in recall storage (retrievable by search).

What does CacheBlend recompute, and why not nothing?

A small share of tokens (15% per layer by default) with the highest KV deviation; reusing caches with no recompute ignores cross-attention between chunks and loses quality.

How is a Cartridge trained?

With self-study: the model generates synthetic conversations about corpus chunks, and a small KV cache is trained by context distillation to match the model's outputs when the chunk is in context.

Our notebook's 'cartridge' is text notes. What is the real one?

A set of trainable key and value vectors (a prefix-tuning style KV cache), trained with gradients while the model weights stay frozen.

Part 2 · A10

Agentic evaluations and long-horizon tasks

Open in Colab

CS329A Lecture 17 (Nov 17)

Benchmark percentages do not tell a customer what an agent can do. Measure agents in human terms: the length of task (in human minutes) they finish half the time, whether an expert would prefer their work product over another expert's, and, for research reports, separate scores for coverage, retrieval and citations.

Key terms

Time horizon (50%)
The human time length of tasks that a model completes with 50% success, read from a fitted logistic curve.Source: Kwa et al. 2025
80% time horizon
The same, at 80% success; much shorter, and closer to what business use needs.Source: Kwa et al. 2025
Logistic curve
An S-shaped curve from 0 to 1; here, chance of success against log of task length.Source: general
Human baseline
A timed attempt by a skilled person; the task's difficulty is the geometric mean of successful times.Source: Kwa et al. 2025
Deliverable
The actual work product (memo, spreadsheet, slides) a task asks for, graded as a whole.Source: Patwardhan et al. 2025
Win-or-tie rate
Share of pairwise comparisons where the model's deliverable was judged as good as or better than the expert's.Source: Patwardhan et al. 2025
Order swap
Grading each pair twice with positions swapped, and treating a flip as a tie, to cancel position bias.Source: general
Nugget coverage
Share of key facts from the human-written reference that appear in the generated report.Source: Patel et al. 2025
Reference coverage
Share of the reference's important citations that the system found.Source: Patel et al. 2025
Citation precision
Share of citations whose source actually supports the sentence they are attached to.Source: Patel et al. 2025
Claim coverage
Share of sentences whose claims are all backed by cited sources.Source: Patel et al. 2025
Live benchmark
A benchmark refreshed with new items over time, so models cannot have trained on it.Source: Patel et al. 2025

The papers

Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Tasks

Kwa, West, Becker, Deng, et al. (METR) · 2025

What
Timed skilled humans (over 800 baselines, 2,529 hours) on 170 tasks: 97 from HCAST, 7 from RE-Bench and 66 new short software tasks. For each model, fit a logistic curve of success against log human time (task difficulty = geometric mean of successful human times; tasks weighted by inverse square root of family size; 8 runs per agent and task) and read off the 50% time horizon.
Result
Frontier 50% horizons doubled about every 7 months since 2019 (207 days, 95% CI 166-240), possibly faster in 2024. Claude 3.7 Sonnet was about 50 minutes; GPT-2 about 2 seconds; a later revision reports o3 at about 110 minutes. 80% horizons were roughly 4-6x shorter than 50% horizons. Gains came mainly from reliability, recovering from mistakes, logical reasoning and tool use.
Takeaway
Describe your agent to customers as 'finishes X-minute tasks Y% of the time', and quote the high-reliability horizon (80% or more), not the 50% one. Build your task set across a range of lengths and time real people.

GDPval: Evaluating AI Model Performance on Real-World Economically Valuable Tasks

Patwardhan, Dias, Proehl, Kim, Wang, Watkins, et al. (OpenAI) · 2025

What
1,320 tasks across 44 occupations in the 9 sectors contributing most to US GDP, built from the work of professionals with 14 years of experience on average. Each task asks for a real deliverable (documents, slides, spreadsheets). Occupation experts compare model and human deliverables blind and pairwise; a 220-task gold subset and an automated grader are public.
Result
On the gold subset, Claude Opus 4.1 was best, with 47.6% of deliverables rated as wins or ties against the expert. Strict win rates: GPT-4o 12.5%, o4-mini 29.1%, o3 35.2%, GPT-5 39.0%. Naively GPT-5 was about 90x faster and 474x cheaper than experts, but with expert review and fixing ('try once, then fix') the gain shrank to about 1.12x faster and 1.18x cheaper. The automated grader agreed with experts 65.7% of the time vs 70.8% human-human agreement. More reasoning effort, more task context and more scaffolding all helped.
Takeaway
Grade agents on the actual work product against an expert reference, pairwise and blind, and include review time when you claim savings. An LLM grader is useful for iteration but is not as reliable as an expert.

DeepScholar-Bench: A Live Benchmark and Automated Evaluation for Generative Research Synthesis

Patel, Arabzadeh, Gupta, Sundar, Stoica, Zaharia, Guestrin · 2025

What
A live benchmark: queries and human-written related-work sections are drawn from recent arXiv papers, so it stays fresh and hard to contaminate. Systems must research and write a cited related-work section. Automatic metrics on three axes: knowledge synthesis (organization via pairwise LLM judge; nugget coverage), retrieval quality (relevance rate; reference coverage; document importance) and verifiability (citation precision; claim coverage). Also releases DeepScholar-ref, an open reference pipeline on LOTUS.
Result
The first set has 63 papers from 18 arXiv domains. No system exceeded a geometric mean of 31% across metrics; OpenAI DeepResearch scored 0.309 but only 0.138 claim coverage and 0.187 reference coverage. A Claude-based search agent had the highest verifiability (citation precision 0.701, claim coverage 0.760). DeepScholar-ref reached 0.285 at lower cost.
Takeaway
Score research and summary agents with separate metrics for coverage, retrieval and citation support. One overall score hides which part failed, and the best system on one axis is often weak on another.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 2
import json, math, random, re, time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
import numpy as np
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.data import contract, labels, playbook, ids
from lal.tasks import extract_int
from lal.checks import check

LAB, PB = labels(), playbook()

1. METR's time horizon, on a toy

METR (Kwa et al. 2025, Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Tasks) timed skilled people on 170 software tasks, from seconds to many hours. For each model they fit a logistic curve: chance of success against the log of the human time. The 50% time horizon is the human task length where that curve crosses 50%. They report it doubled about every 7 months since 2019 (Claude 3.7 Sonnet: about 50 minutes in the abstract).

Our toy: multi-step contract checks. A task with N steps asks the agent to walk through N facts from a contract fact sheet, keeping a running total. Each extra step is one more lookup and one more chance to slip. We assume a person needs 1 minute to set up plus 1.5 minutes per step. That is our made-up estimate; METR timed real people.

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 3
FACTS = {c: LAB[c]["terms"] for c in ids()}
SHEET = "id | monthly fee ($) | notice days | renewal years | liability cap (months) | governing law\n" + "\n".join(
    f"{c} | {t['monthly_fee']} | {t['notice_days']} | {t['renewal_years']} | {t['liability_cap_months'] if t['liability_cap_months'] else 'none'} | {t['governing_law']}"
    for c, t in FACTS.items())
CAPPED = [c for c, t in FACTS.items() if t["liability_cap_months"]]

def make_task(n_steps, seed):
    """A chain of n_steps lookups with an exact answer."""
    rng = random.Random(f"metr-toy-{n_steps}-{seed}")
    c0 = rng.choice(ids())
    total = FACTS[c0]["monthly_fee"] // 100
    steps = [f"Start with the monthly fee of {c0} divided by 100."]
    for _ in range(n_steps - 1):
        kind = rng.randrange(4)
        if kind == 0:
            c = rng.choice(ids()); total += FACTS[c]["notice_days"]
            steps.append(f"Add the notice days of {c}.")
        elif kind == 1:
            c = rng.choice(ids()); total -= FACTS[c]["renewal_years"]
            steps.append(f"Subtract the renewal years of {c}.")
        elif kind == 2:
            c = rng.choice(CAPPED); total += FACTS[c]["liability_cap_months"]
            steps.append(f"Add the liability cap in months of {c}.")
        else:
            c = rng.choice(ids()); ok = FACTS[c]["governing_law"] in PB["allowed_law"]
            total += 10 if ok else -10
            steps.append(f"Add 10 if the governing law of {c} is California or Delaware; otherwise subtract 10.")
    return {"n": n_steps, "minutes": 1 + 1.5 * n_steps, "steps": steps, "answer": total}

STEP_COUNTS = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 16, 20, 24, 28, 32]     # 15 tasks, 2.5 to 49 "human minutes"
TASKS = [make_task(n, seed=0) for n in STEP_COUNTS]
print(TASKS[2])

def run_task(task, temperature=0.5):
    prompt = (f"Contract fact sheet:\n{SHEET}\n\nDo these steps in order. Write the running total after each step, "
              "then end with 'ANSWER: <number>'.\n" + "\n".join(f"{i+1}. {s}" for i, s in enumerate(task["steps"])))
    return extract_int(ask(prompt, temperature=temperature, max_tokens=1500)) == task["answer"]

Run each task twice (30 calls). METR ran each agent 8 times per task; we keep it small.

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 4
N_RUNS = 2
t0 = time.time()
jobs = [t for t in TASKS for _ in range(N_RUNS)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    outcomes = list(ex.map(run_task, jobs))
minutes = np.array([t["minutes"] for t in jobs])
success = np.array(outcomes, dtype=float)
print(f"{len(jobs)} runs in {time.time()-t0:.0f}s")
for t in TASKS:
    s = [o for j, o in zip(jobs, outcomes) if j is t]
    print(f"  {t['n']:2} steps ~{t['minutes']:4.1f} min: {sum(s)}/{len(s)}")

Fit the logistic curve

Model: P(success) = sigmoid(a + b · log2(minutes)). We fit a and b by maximum likelihood with Newton's method (a few exact steps instead of many small ones), plus a tiny penalty so the fit stays finite when the data separate perfectly. The 50% horizon is where a + b·x = 0, so x = -a/b and horizon = 2^x minutes. The 80% horizon solves a + b·x = log(0.8/0.2).

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 5
def fit_logistic(minutes, success, l2=1e-2, iters=50):
    x = np.log2(np.asarray(minutes, float)); y = np.asarray(success, float)
    X = np.stack([np.ones_like(x), x], 1)
    w = np.zeros(2)
    for _ in range(iters):
        p = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-(X @ w)))
        grad = X.T @ (p - y) + l2 * np.array([0, w[1]])
        H = X.T @ (X * (p * (1 - p))[:, None]) + l2 * np.diag([1e-6, 1.0])
        w = w - np.linalg.solve(H, grad)
    return w

def horizon(w, level=0.5):
    a, b = w
    if b >= 0:                       # success does not fall with length: no horizon in this range
        return float("inf")
    return float(2 ** ((math.log(level / (1 - level)) - a) / b))

# Test the fit on fake data with a known answer: true 50% horizon = 16 minutes.
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
fake_m = np.repeat(2 ** np.linspace(0, 7, 15), 40)
fake_y = (rng.random(fake_m.size) < 1 / (1 + np.exp(-(-1.2 * (np.log2(fake_m) - 4))))).astype(float)
w_fake = fit_logistic(fake_m, fake_y)
print(f"fake data: recovered 50% horizon {horizon(w_fake):.1f} min (true 16.0)")

w = fit_logistic(minutes, success)
h50, h80 = horizon(w, 0.5), horizon(w, 0.8)
print(f"our model: slope {w[1]:.2f}, 50% horizon {h50:.1f} min, 80% horizon {h80:.1f} min")

# Bootstrap over tasks for a rough interval (METR resampled families, tasks and runs).
task_idx = np.repeat(np.arange(len(TASKS)), N_RUNS)
boots = []
for _ in range(500):
    pick = rng.integers(0, len(TASKS), len(TASKS))
    mask = np.concatenate([np.where(task_idx == k)[0] for k in pick])
    boots.append(horizon(fit_logistic(minutes[mask], success[mask])))
finite = np.array([b for b in boots if np.isfinite(b)])
if finite.size:
    print(f"bootstrap 50% horizon: median {np.median(finite):.1f} min, 10th-90th percentile {np.percentile(finite, 10):.1f}-{np.percentile(finite, 90):.1f}")

How to read this. If the 50% horizon is, say, 12 minutes, the model finishes about half of tasks that would take a person 12 minutes, and fewer of the longer ones. The 80% horizon is always shorter: METR found 80% horizons about 4-6x shorter than 50% ones. For a business, the 80% (or higher) number is the one that matters, since a review step must catch every miss. Three caveats from the paper apply here too: the human times drive everything (ours are guesses); tasks within one family are related (METR down-weighted large families); and success on tidy tasks may not carry over to messy real work.

If the horizon prints as inf or is beyond 49 minutes, the model never dropped below 50% in our range: make longer tasks. If every run failed, make shorter ones.

2. GDPval: would an expert prefer this deliverable?

GDPval (Patwardhan et al. 2025, GDPval: Evaluating AI Model Performance on Real-World Economically Valuable Tasks) has 1,320 tasks across 44 occupations, written by professionals with about 14 years of experience on average. Each task asks for a real work product (a memo, a spreadsheet, a slide deck). Expert graders compare the model's deliverable against a human expert's, blind and pairwise, and the score is the win-or-tie rate. The best model on their gold set, Claude Opus 4.1, had 47.6% of deliverables rated as good as or better than the expert's. Their experimental automatic grader agreed with human experts 65.7% of the time, while human experts agreed with each other 70.8% of the time.

Our version: three client emails about contracts that break the playbook. We write the expert reference from the labels. An LLM grader compares the model's email with the expert's, in both orders.

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 6
RULE_TEXT = PB["rules"]
TASK_CIDS = ["msa_08", "msa_10", "msa_17"]
EXPERT = {
    "msa_08": "Subject: msa_08 - liability cap too high\n\nThe liability cap in clause 7.1 is fees paid in the prior 36 months. Our playbook allows at most 12 months. "
              "Recommendation: revise 7.1 to cap each party's liability at the fees paid in the 12 months preceding the claim. The rest of the agreement meets the playbook.",
    "msa_10": "Subject: msa_10 - governing law\n\nClause 10.1 chooses Florida law. Our playbook requires California or Delaware law. "
              "Recommendation: change 10.1 to Delaware (or California) law. The liability cap (12 months), one-year renewals and 30-day notice all meet the playbook.",
    "msa_17": "Subject: msa_17 - renewal and notice terms\n\nClause 4.1 has two problems. It renews automatically for five-year periods; the playbook allows one year at most. "
              "It also needs only 15 days' notice of non-renewal; the playbook requires at least 30 days. Recommendation: revise 4.1 to one-year renewals with at least 30 days' notice.",
}
TASK = ("Write a short email (under 120 words) to the client's general counsel about contract {cid}. Say which playbook rules it breaks, "
        "cite the clause numbers, and recommend a specific fix.\n\nPlaybook rules:\n{rules}\n\nContract:\n{doc}")

def deliverable(cid):
    return ask(TASK.format(cid=cid, rules=json.dumps(RULE_TEXT, indent=1), doc=contract(cid)), max_tokens=300)

GRADER = """You are a senior contracts lawyer grading two work products for the same task. You cannot see who wrote them.
Task: {task}
Playbook rules: {rules}
Contract:
{doc}

Email A:
{a}

Email B:
{b}

Judge correctness first (right rules, right clause numbers, workable fix), then clarity. Length is not quality.
Reply with one word: A, B, or TIE."""

def grade_once(cid, a, b):
    r = ask(GRADER.format(task=TASK.split("\n")[0].format(cid=cid), rules=json.dumps(RULE_TEXT), doc=contract(cid), a=a, b=b), max_tokens=5)
    m = re.search(r"\b(A|B|TIE)\b", r.upper())
    return m.group(1) if m else "TIE"

def pairwise(cid, model_text, expert_text):
    """Model vs expert, graded twice with the order swapped. Disagreement after the swap counts as a tie."""
    first = grade_once(cid, model_text, expert_text)     # model is A
    second = grade_once(cid, expert_text, model_text)    # model is B
    if first == "A" and second == "B": return "win"
    if first == "B" and second == "A": return "loss"
    return "tie"

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    drafts = dict(zip(TASK_CIDS, ex.map(deliverable, TASK_CIDS)))         # 3 calls
verdicts = {c: pairwise(c, drafts[c], EXPERT[c]) for c in TASK_CIDS}      # 6 calls
for c in TASK_CIDS:
    print(f"--- {c}: {verdicts[c]}\n{drafts[c][:300]}\n")
win_or_tie = sum(v in ("win", "tie") for v in verdicts.values()) / len(verdicts)
print(f"win-or-tie rate vs expert: {win_or_tie:.0%}  ({verdicts})")

# Sanity check of the grader: a confidently wrong email should lose to the expert.
WRONG = "Subject: msa_17 looks good\n\nI reviewed msa_17. It meets every playbook rule, so the client can sign it as is."
sanity = pairwise("msa_17", WRONG, EXPERT["msa_17"])                    # 2 calls
print("wrong email vs expert:", sanity)

What to notice: the swap matters. Small graders often pick the same position both times, and we count that as a tie rather than trusting it. GDPval also found that review time eats most of the speed gain: for GPT-5, the raw ratio was about 90x faster than an expert, but once an expert reviews the output and redoes it when needed ("try once, then fix"), the gain was about 1.12x faster and 1.18x cheaper. Report both numbers to a customer.

3. DeepScholar-Bench: score a research summary automatically

DeepScholar-Bench (Patel et al. 2025, DeepScholar-Bench: A Live Benchmark and Automated Evaluation for Generative Research Synthesis) asks systems to write the related-work section of a recent arXiv paper, and scores three things: knowledge synthesis (e.g. nugget coverage: how many key facts from the human-written section appear), retrieval quality (e.g. reference coverage: how many important references were found) and verifiability (citation precision: does each cited source support its sentence; claim coverage: is every sentence backed by a citation). No system passed a geometric mean of 31% across their metrics.

Our version uses code only, so it is cheap and repeatable. We give the model six sources: three on agent evaluation (relevant) and three on memory and serving (distractors). It writes a short cited summary about evaluating agents.

One simplification: the paper uses an LLM judge to decide whether a source supports a sentence. We use a word-overlap rule instead (a cited source "supports" a sentence if they share at least 3 content words). It is crude but deterministic.

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 7
SOURCES = {
    "S1": "METR measures AI ability to complete long tasks with the 50% task-completion time horizon: the length of tasks, timed on skilled humans, that a model completes with 50% success. The horizon has doubled about every seven months since 2019.",
    "S2": "GDPval evaluates models on real-world economically valuable tasks from 44 occupations, written by industry experts. Experts compare model deliverables with expert deliverables in blind pairwise grading and report win or tie rates.",
    "S3": "DeepScholar-Bench is a live benchmark for generative research synthesis. It scores generated related-work sections on knowledge synthesis, retrieval quality and verifiability, and no system exceeds a geometric mean of 31 percent.",
    "S4": "MemGPT manages memory tiers for language models like an operating system, paging data between main context and archival storage through function calls.",
    "S5": "CacheBlend reuses precomputed KV caches of retrieved text chunks and recomputes a small subset of tokens, reducing time to first token.",
    "S6": "Cartridges train a small KV cache offline for a corpus using self-study and context distillation, cutting serving memory.",
}
GOLD = {"S1", "S2", "S3"}
NUGGETS = {   # key facts a good summary should contain: (description, regex)
    "50% time horizon": r"50\s*%|half|time horizon",
    "doubling about every 7 months": r"(7|seven)\s*months|doubl",
    "44 occupations / real work": r"44|occupation",
    "blind pairwise expert grading": r"pairwise|blind|win",
    "three axes: synthesis, retrieval, verifiability": r"verifiab|retrieval quality|synthesis",
    "benchmarks far from saturated (31%)": r"31|saturat",
}
STOP = set("the a an of and or to for in on with by is are that this it as from at be its their they about each than model models".split())

def content_words(s):
    return {w for w in re.findall(r"[a-z0-9%]+", s.lower()) if w not in STOP and len(w) > 2}

def split_sentences(text):
    text = re.sub(r"([.!?])\s*((?:\[[^\]]+\]\s*)+)", lambda m: " " + m.group(2).strip() + m.group(1) + " ", text)   # "x. [S1]" -> "x [S1]."
    return [s.strip() for s in re.split(r"(?<=[.!?])\s+", text.strip()) if len(s.strip()) > 3]

def citations(sentence):
    return {f"S{n}" for group in re.findall(r"\[([^\]]+)\]", sentence) for n in re.findall(r"S\s*(\d+)", group)}

def scholar_metrics(text, sources=SOURCES, gold=GOLD, nuggets=NUGGETS):
    sents = split_sentences(text)
    cited = set().union(*[citations(s) for s in sents]) & set(sources) if sents else set()
    pairs = [(s, c) for s in sents for c in citations(s) if c in sources]
    supported = [len(content_words(re.sub(r"\[[^\]]+\]", "", s)) & content_words(sources[c])) >= 3 for s, c in pairs]
    sent_ok = [any(len(content_words(re.sub(r"\[[^\]]+\]", "", s)) & content_words(sources[c])) >= 3 for c in citations(s) if c in sources) for s in sents]
    m = {
        "nugget_coverage": sum(bool(re.search(p, text, re.I)) for p in nuggets.values()) / len(nuggets),
        "reference_coverage": len(cited & gold) / len(gold),
        "retrieval_precision": len(cited & gold) / len(cited) if cited else 0.0,
        "citation_precision": sum(supported) / len(supported) if supported else 0.0,
        "claim_coverage": sum(sent_ok) / len(sent_ok) if sent_ok else 0.0,
    }
    vals = list(m.values())
    m["geometric_mean"] = float(np.exp(np.mean(np.log(np.maximum(vals, 1e-3)))))
    return m

ORACLE = ("METR reports a 50% task-completion time horizon, the length of human-timed tasks a model completes with 50% success, which has doubled about every seven months since 2019 [S1]. "
          "GDPval evaluates real-world economically valuable tasks from 44 occupations, using blind pairwise grading by experts against expert deliverables [S2]. "
          "DeepScholar-Bench scores generated related-work sections on knowledge synthesis, retrieval quality and verifiability, and no system exceeds a geometric mean of 31 percent [S3].")
BAD = ("Agents are evaluated in many ways. Memory systems page data between main context and archival storage [S1]. "
       "Caching reduces time to first token [S5]. Benchmarks are important.")
for name, txt in [("oracle", ORACLE), ("bad", BAD)]:
    print(f"{name:7}", {k: round(v, 2) for k, v in scholar_metrics(txt).items()})
a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 8
SRC_BLOCK = "\n".join(f"[{k}] {v}" for k, v in SOURCES.items())
summary = ask(f"Sources:\n{SRC_BLOCK}\n\nWrite a 4-sentence related-work paragraph on how to evaluate AI agents on long, realistic tasks. "
              "Use only sources that are relevant. Cite sources inline like [S1] at the end of each sentence.", max_tokens=350)
print(summary, "\n")
model_m = scholar_metrics(summary)
print({k: round(v, 2) for k, v in model_m.items()})

What to notice: each metric catches a different failure. Citing a distractor (MemGPT, CacheBlend) lowers retrieval precision. A sentence with no citation lowers claim coverage. Citing a real source for a claim it does not make lowers citation precision. A single "quality" score would hide which one went wrong. DeepScholar-Bench's results show the same split: OpenAI DeepResearch led on the geometric mean (0.309), but a Claude-based search agent had much higher citation precision (0.701) and claim coverage (0.760).

a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 9
check("logistic fit recovers a known horizon (within 30%)", abs(horizon(w_fake) - 16) / 16 < 0.3)
check("80% horizon is shorter than 50% horizon on fake data", horizon(w_fake, 0.8) < horizon(w_fake))
check("the agent solves the shortest task at least once", any(o for j, o in zip(jobs, outcomes) if j["n"] <= 2))
check("success on long tasks is no better than on short tasks", success[minutes >= 25].mean() <= success[minutes <= 8].mean())
check("grader does not prefer the wrong email over the expert", sanity != "win")
check("oracle summary beats bad summary on every metric",
      all(scholar_metrics(ORACLE)[k] > scholar_metrics(BAD)[k] for k in scholar_metrics(ORACLE)))
check("model summary cites at least 2 of the 3 relevant sources", model_m["reference_coverage"] >= 2 / 3)

Your turn

  1. Make the toy tasks harder per step (for example, ask for the fee in dollars instead of fee / 100, so the numbers are bigger). Refit. How much does the 50% horizon move? What does that say about how much the human-time estimates matter?
  2. Run the GDPval comparison with llama3.2:3b as the writer and MODEL as the grader. Is the win-or-tie rate lower?
  3. Write a summary that games our DeepScholar metrics (high scores, but useless to a reader). Which metric would the paper's LLM-judge version catch it with?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 10
# Solution 1: bigger numbers per step (30 calls). We keep the same human-minute estimates.
def run_task_dollars(task, temperature=0.5):
    t = dict(task)
    first = t["steps"][0].replace(" divided by 100", "")
    c0 = re.search(r"msa_\d+", first).group(0)
    t["steps"] = [first] + t["steps"][1:]
    t["answer"] = task["answer"] - FACTS[c0]["monthly_fee"] // 100 + FACTS[c0]["monthly_fee"]
    return run_task(t, temperature)

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    out2 = np.array(list(ex.map(run_task_dollars, jobs)), dtype=float)
print(f"fee/100: 50% horizon {h50:.1f} min | dollars: {horizon(fit_logistic(minutes, out2)):.1f} min")
# Notice: the same 'human minutes' can hide very different difficulty. METR's number is only as good as its
# human baselines, which is why they timed real people instead of guessing.
a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 11
# Solution 2: a smaller writer (3 + 6 calls)
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    small = dict(zip(TASK_CIDS, ex.map(lambda c: ask(TASK.format(cid=c, rules=json.dumps(RULE_TEXT, indent=1), doc=contract(c)),
                                                       model_name="llama3.2:3b", max_tokens=300), TASK_CIDS)))
v_small = {c: pairwise(c, small[c], EXPERT[c]) for c in TASK_CIDS}
print("3B writer:", v_small, "| 8B writer:", verdicts)
# Notice: with only 3 tasks the rate moves in steps of 33%. GDPval used 220 gold tasks and three graders per sample
# for a reason. Never decide between models on a handful of comparisons.
a10_evals_long_horizon.ipynb · cell 12
# Solution 3: a summary that games word-overlap metrics
GAMED = " ".join(f"{s.rstrip('.')} [{k}]." for k in sorted(GOLD) for s in split_sentences(SOURCES[k]))   # copy the sources word for word
print({k: round(v, 2) for k, v in scholar_metrics(GAMED).items()})
# Notice: copying the sources scores near-perfect here. The paper's organization metric (an LLM judge comparing
# against the human-written section, pairwise) would catch that it is not a synthesis, just a list of abstracts.
Traps
  • Guessing human times. The time horizon is only as good as the human baselines; our toy's made-up 1.5 minutes per step is exactly what METR avoided by timing people.
  • Quoting the 50% horizon to a customer. Half of tasks failing is not a product; quote the 80% or 95% horizon and design review around it.
  • Claiming '90x faster' without review time. GDPval's speed-up nearly vanished once expert review and fixes were counted.
  • Trusting a single-order LLM grader. Small graders often pick the same position both times; swap and count disagreement as a tie.
  • Rewarding long or copied text. Word-overlap metrics can be gamed by pasting sources; keep an LLM or human judge for organization and synthesis.
  • Fitting a curve when every run succeeded or every run failed. There is no crossing point in your range; make longer or shorter tasks.

Check yourself

How does METR turn success rates into a time horizon?

Fit a logistic curve of success against log human time per model, then find the task length where the curve crosses 50%.

Why is the 80% horizon shorter than the 50% horizon?
