Success falls as tasks get longer, so to succeed 80% of the time the tasks must be shorter; METR found 4-6x shorter.

What does GDPval compare, and how?

The model's deliverable vs a human expert's for the same task, judged blind and pairwise by occupation experts; the score is the win-or-tie rate.

Why did GDPval's speed and cost advantage shrink?

Because an expert must review the model's output and fix or redo it when it is wrong; that review time counts.

Name the three axes DeepScholar-Bench scores.

Knowledge synthesis, retrieval quality and verifiability.

A summary cites a distractor source for a true sentence. Which of our metrics drops?

Retrieval precision (a non-relevant source was cited) and probably citation precision (the source does not support the sentence).

Part 2 · A11

Guest lectures and reasoning frontiers

Open in Colab

CS329A guest lectures: Lecture 9 (Oct 20) Melvin Johnson, 15 (Nov 10) Denny Zhou, 16 (Nov 14) Thang Luong, 18 (Nov 21) Misha Laskin, 19 (Dec 1) Danny Driess; Lecture 20 (Dec 5) Future research areas

The guest talks trace one arc: models that reason step by step, sample and vote, learn from checkable rewards, and act in software and the physical world. The course assigned no papers for these talks, so every reading here is our pick of the speaker's best-known public work on the talk's topic, not the course's reading list.

Key terms

Chain of thought (CoT)
The model writes its reasoning steps before the final answer.Source: Wei et al. 2022
Self-consistency
Sample several reasoning paths and take the majority answer.Source: Wang et al. 2022
Agreement rate
The share of samples that match the majority answer; a cheap confidence score.Source: Wang et al. 2022
Least-to-most prompting
Decompose into easier sub-questions, then solve them in order using earlier answers.Source: Zhou et al. 2022
Greedy decoding
Always picking the single most likely next token.Source: general
CoT-decoding
Branch on the top-k first tokens, continue each greedily, and pick by answer confidence.Source: Wang and Zhou 2024
Answer confidence (Δ)
Average gap between the top-1 and top-2 token probabilities over the answer's tokens.Source: Wang and Zhou 2024
Post-training
Everything after pre-training (instruction tuning, preference tuning, RL) that makes a model helpful and agentic.Source: general
Formal proof / Lean
A proof written in a language a computer can check exactly; Lean is one such language.Source: DeepMind 2024
Parallel thinking
Exploring and combining several candidate solutions at once instead of one chain.Source: DeepMind 2025
Vision-language-action (VLA) model
A model that takes images and text and outputs robot actions.Source: Black et al. 2024
Algorithm distillation
Training a sequence model on an RL algorithm's learning histories so it learns to improve in context.Source: Laskin et al. 2022

The papers

Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models (our pick for Denny Zhou's talk)

Wei, Wang, Schuurmans, Bosma, Ichter, Xia, Chi, Le, Zhou · 2022

What
Prompting with a few worked examples that show intermediate reasoning steps, tested on three large models across arithmetic, commonsense and symbolic reasoning.
Result
A 540B model with eight chain-of-thought examples reached state-of-the-art accuracy on GSM8K, beating a fine-tuned GPT-3 with a verifier.
Takeaway
Let the model show its work before the answer on multi-step tasks; then grade only the final answer with code.

Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models (our pick for Denny Zhou's talk)

Wang, Wei, Schuurmans, Le, Chi, Narang, Chowdhery, Zhou · 2022

What
Sample several diverse reasoning paths instead of one greedy path and take the most consistent final answer.
Result
Over chain-of-thought: GSM8K +17.9%, SVAMP +11.0%, AQuA +12.2%, StrategyQA +6.4%, ARC-challenge +3.9%.
Takeaway
Majority voting is a cheap accuracy boost, and the agreement rate is a free confidence score you can use to route low-agreement answers to a person.

Least-to-Most Prompting Enables Complex Reasoning in Large Language Models (our pick for Denny Zhou's talk)

Zhou, Schärli, Hou, Wei, Scales, Wang, Schuurmans, Cui, Bousquet, Le, Chi · 2022

What
Two stages: decompose a problem into simpler sub-problems, then solve them in order, feeding each answer into the next. Aimed at problems harder than the prompt examples.
Result
On SCAN with code-davinci-002 and 14 examples: at least 99% accuracy on every split, including the length split, vs 16% for chain-of-thought.
Takeaway
When tasks are longer than your examples, have the agent write a plan of sub-tasks first and execute them one by one.

Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Without Prompting (our pick for Denny Zhou's talk)

Wang, Zhou · 2024

What
Instead of greedy decoding, branch on the top-k alternatives for the first token (k = 10 by default), continue each greedily, and rank paths by answer confidence: the average gap between top-1 and top-2 token probabilities over the answer tokens.
Result
Mistral-7B (pre-trained) on GSM8K: greedy 9.9%, self-consistency without a CoT prompt 12.9%, CoT-decoding 25.1%. PaLM-2 Large: 34.8% greedy to 63.2%. Instruction-tuned Mistral-7B also improved (31.2% to 38.2%).
Takeaway
Reasoning paths often exist inside a model even when greedy decoding misses them; answer-token confidence is a useful signal for picking among candidates.

Solving olympiad geometry without human demonstrations (AlphaGeometry; our pick for Thang Luong's talk)

Trinh, Wu, Le, He, Luong · 2024

What
A neural language model proposes auxiliary constructions and a symbolic deduction engine does rigorous proofs, trained on 100 million synthetic theorems and proofs with no human demonstrations.
Result
Solved 25 of 30 recent olympiad geometry problems (IMO-AG-30); the previous best method solved 10, and an average human gold medalist about 25.9.
Takeaway
Pair a creative model with an exact checker, and generate your own training data when human data is scarce.

AI achieves silver-medal standard solving International Mathematical Olympiad problems (AlphaProof + AlphaGeometry 2 blog; our pick)

Google DeepMind · 2024

What
AlphaProof couples a pre-trained language model with AlphaZero-style reinforcement learning in the Lean proof language, trained on about a million problems auto-formalized by Gemini. AlphaGeometry 2 handled geometry.
Result
28 of 42 points at IMO 2024 (4 of 6 problems), silver level; some problems took up to three days. AlphaGeometry 2 solved 83% of IMO geometry problems from the past 25 years (predecessor 53%).
Takeaway
Reinforcement learning works best when a machine can check the answer exactly; formal verifiers are such checkers.

Advanced version of Gemini with Deep Think officially achieves gold-medal standard at the IMO (blog; our pick)

Google DeepMind · 2025

What
A Gemini model reasoning end to end in natural language, with 'parallel thinking' over several candidate solutions and new reinforcement-learning techniques for multi-step reasoning and proofs.
Result
35 of 42 points at IMO 2025 (5 of 6 problems), gold level, within the 4.5-hour limit, graded by IMO coordinators.
Takeaway
Parallel exploration plus RL on hard, checkable problems moved a general model past a specialized formal system in one year.

Google's Multilingual Neural Machine Translation System: Enabling Zero-Shot Translation (our pick for Melvin Johnson's talk)

Johnson, Schuster, Le, Krikun, Wu, Chen, Thorat, Viégas, Wattenberg, Corrado, Hughes, Dean · 2017

What
One translation model for many languages, with only a target-language token added to the input and no extra parameters. Background for the speaker, who now leads Gemini post-training (per his site); for his post-training work see the Gemini report (arXiv 2312.11805), where he is an author.
Result
Matched state-of-the-art English to French and beat it for English to German on WMT'14; translated between language pairs never seen together in training (zero-shot).
Takeaway
A single general model with the right training signal can replace many special-purpose ones; post-training is where that signal is added.

In-context Reinforcement Learning with Algorithm Distillation (our pick for Misha Laskin's talk)

Laskin, Wang, Oh, Parisotto, Spencer, Steigerwald, Strouse, Hansen, Filos, Brooks, Gazeau, Sahni, Singh, Mnih · 2022

What
Train a causal transformer on the learning histories of an RL algorithm, so it learns to improve its policy in context, with no weight updates. Laskin later co-founded Reflection AI (March 2025), which focuses on autonomous coding agents.
Result
Improved its policy entirely in context in sparse-reward, combinatorial and pixel-based environments, and learned a more data-efficient RL algorithm than the one that generated its data.
Takeaway
An agent can learn from its own experience inside the context window if it sees its past attempts and outcomes; keep those histories.

PaLM-E: An Embodied Multimodal Language Model (our pick for Danny Driess's talk)

Driess, Xia, Sajjadi, Lynch, Chowdhery, Ichter, et al. · 2023

What
Feed images and robot state directly into a pre-trained language model as 'multimodal sentences', trained end to end for robot planning, visual question answering and captioning.
Result
The largest model, PaLM-E-562B, reached state-of-the-art on OK-VQA and kept its general language skills; joint training on internet-scale data helped robot tasks.
Takeaway
Reuse a big pre-trained model for a new input type rather than training a specialist from scratch.

π0: A Vision-Language-Action Flow Model for General Robot Control (our pick for Danny Driess's talk)

Black, Brown, Driess, Esmail, Equi, Finn, et al. (Physical Intelligence) · 2024

What
A flow-matching action model built on a pre-trained vision-language model, trained on data from many robot types (single-arm, dual-arm, mobile).
Result
Evaluated zero-shot after pre-training, on following language instructions, and on learning new skills by fine-tuning, on tasks such as laundry folding, table cleaning and box assembly. (We did not extract per-task numbers.)
Takeaway
The recipe from language agents carries over to robots: broad pre-training, then fine-tuning on high-quality task data.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in a11_guest_lectures.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 2
import json, re, time
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
import httpx
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.tasks import math_problems, extract_int
from lal.checks import check

PROBS = math_problems(6, seed=11, difficulty="hard")      # 3-5 step word problems with exact answers
print(PROBS[0]["question"], "->", PROBS[0]["answer"])

def acc(preds):
    return sum(p == q["answer"] for p, q in zip(preds, PROBS)) / len(PROBS)

def pmap(fn, xs):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        return list(ex.map(fn, xs))

1. Direct answer vs chain of thought (Denny Zhou, Lecture 15)

Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting (Wei et al. 2022, Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models): let the model write its steps before the answer. With eight worked examples, a 540B model reached state-of-the-art on GSM8K grade-school math, beating a fine-tuned GPT-3 with a verifier. Today's chat models are trained to reason this way, so a plain "think step by step" is usually enough. We compare that with forcing the answer right away.

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 3
DIRECT = "{q}\nReply with only the final number, no words, no working. Format: ANSWER: <number>"
COT = "{q}\nThink step by step, showing each calculation. Then end with: ANSWER: <number>"

direct = pmap(lambda q: extract_int(ask(DIRECT.format(q=q["question"]), max_tokens=15)), PROBS)        # 6 calls
cot = pmap(lambda q: extract_int(ask(COT.format(q=q["question"]), max_tokens=500)), PROBS)             # 6 calls
print(f"direct: {acc(direct):.0%} | chain of thought: {acc(cot):.0%}")
for q, d, c in zip(PROBS, direct, cot):
    print(f"  truth {q['answer']:5} | direct {d} | cot {c}")

2. Self-consistency: sample several chains, take the majority

Self-consistency (Wang et al. 2022, Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models): sample several reasoning paths at a temperature above 0 and take the most common final answer. A hard problem has many paths to the right answer but the wrong answers tend to scatter. They report gains over single-chain CoT of +17.9% on GSM8K, +11.0% on SVAMP and +12.2% on AQuA. How often the samples agree is also a free confidence score.

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 4
K = 5
def sample_answers(q):
    return [extract_int(ask(COT.format(q=q["question"]), temperature=0.7, max_tokens=500)) for _ in range(K)]

samples = pmap(sample_answers, PROBS)                                                                   # 30 calls
votes = [Counter(a for a in s if a is not None).most_common(1) for s in samples]
sc = [v[0][0] if v else None for v in votes]
agree = [v[0][1] / K if v else 0 for v in votes]
print(f"single CoT: {acc(cot):.0%} | self-consistency@{K}: {acc(sc):.0%}")
for q, s, a, v in zip(PROBS, samples, agree, sc):
    print(f"  truth {q['answer']:5} | samples {s} | vote {v} | agreement {a:.0%} | {'right' if v == q['answer'] else 'wrong'}")

Look at the agreement column: when most samples agree, the majority is usually right. That is how you decide, in production, which answers to send to a person for review.

3. Least-to-most: split the problem, then solve the parts in order

Least-to-most prompting (Zhou et al. 2022, Least-to-Most Prompting Enables Complex Reasoning in Large Language Models): first ask the model to break the problem into simpler sub-questions, then solve them in order, each answer feeding the next. CoT struggles when test problems are harder than the examples; least-to-most reached at least 99% on every split of the SCAN benchmark with 14 examples, versus 16% for CoT.

The paper solves each sub-question in its own call. To save calls we do it in two: one call to decompose, one to solve all sub-questions in order.

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 5
def least_to_most(q):
    subs = ask(f"{q['question']}\n\nDo not solve it yet. List the simpler sub-questions, in order, that you need to answer to solve this. "
               "One per line, at most 5.", max_tokens=200)
    solved = ask(f"Problem: {q['question']}\n\nSub-questions:\n{subs}\n\nAnswer each sub-question in order, using earlier answers. "
                 "Then end with: ANSWER: <number>", max_tokens=600)
    return extract_int(solved), subs

l2m_out = pmap(least_to_most, PROBS)                                                                   # 12 calls
l2m = [a for a, _ in l2m_out]
print(f"least-to-most: {acc(l2m):.0%}")
print("example decomposition:\n" + l2m_out[0][1][:400])

4. CoT-decoding: reasoning without a prompt

CoT-decoding (Wang and Zhou 2024, Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Without Prompting) shows that a model often "knows" how to reason, but greedy decoding hides it. Give the plain format Q: ... A: with no instructions. At the first answer token, branch on the top-k alternatives (k = 10 in the paper), then continue each branch greedily. Branches that contain reasoning tend to end in an answer the model is more confident about. They measure confidence as Δ: the average gap between the top-1 and top-2 token probabilities over the answer's tokens. On GSM8K, Mistral-7B (pre-trained) went from 9.9% with greedy decoding to 25.1% with CoT-decoding.

We need the raw text-completion endpoint for this, so we call Ollama's own API (/api/generate with raw: true, which skips the chat template). If your Ollama returns token log-probabilities, we take the real top-k first tokens and compute Δ. If not, we approximate: sample several different first tokens at a high temperature, and use agreement between branches as the confidence instead of Δ.

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 6
OLLAMA = "http://localhost:11434"

def generate(prompt, num_predict, temperature=0.0, seed=0, logprobs=False):
    body = {"model": MODEL, "prompt": prompt, "raw": True, "stream": False,
            "options": {"temperature": temperature, "num_predict": num_predict, "seed": seed, "stop": ["\nQ:"]}}
    if logprobs:
        body.update({"logprobs": True, "top_logprobs": 5})
    r = httpx.post(f"{OLLAMA}/api/generate", json=body, timeout=180)
    r.raise_for_status()
    return r.json()

def first_tokens(prompt, k=5):
    """Top-k first tokens: from log-probs if the server gives them, else by sampling."""
    try:
        lp = generate(prompt, 1, logprobs=True).get("logprobs") or []
        tops = [t.get("token", "") for t in (lp[0].get("top_logprobs") or [])] if lp else []
        if len(tops) >= 2:
            return tops[:k], True
    except Exception as e:
        print("log-probs not available:", type(e).__name__)
    seen = []
    for s in range(2 * k):                         # sample until we have k different first tokens (cheap 1-token calls)
        tok = generate(prompt, 1, temperature=1.2, seed=s).get("response", "")
        if tok not in seen:
            seen.append(tok)
        if len(seen) == k:
            break
    return seen, False

def delta(lp_list, text):
    """Average top-1 minus top-2 probability over the tokens of the last number in the text (the paper's Δ)."""
    import math
    nums = re.findall(r"-?\d[\d,]*", text)
    if not nums or not lp_list:
        return None
    target, gaps, built = nums[-1], [], ""
    for t in reversed(lp_list):                    # walk back from the end until we have covered the answer tokens
        tok = t.get("token", "")
        built = tok + built
        tops = sorted((math.exp(x.get("logprob", -99)) for x in (t.get("top_logprobs") or [])), reverse=True) + [0, 0]
        if any(ch.isdigit() for ch in tok):
            gaps.append(tops[0] - tops[1])
        if target in built and gaps:
            break
    return sum(gaps) / len(gaps) if gaps else None

def cot_decode(q, k=5):
    prompt = f"Q: {q['question']}\nA:"
    toks, have_lp = first_tokens(prompt, k)
    branches = []
    for tok in toks:
        out = generate(prompt + tok, 250, logprobs=have_lp)
        text = tok + out.get("response", "")
        branches.append({"first": tok, "answer": extract_int(text), "text": text,
                         "delta": delta(out.get("logprobs") or [], text) if have_lp else None})
    counts = Counter(b["answer"] for b in branches if b["answer"] is not None)
    for b in branches:
        b["agreement"] = counts.get(b["answer"], 0) / len(branches)
    # Pick the answer: by summed Δ if we have it (the paper's weighted aggregation), else by agreement.
    score = Counter()
    for b in branches:
        if b["answer"] is not None:
            score[b["answer"]] += b["delta"] if b["delta"] is not None else 1
    return branches, (score.most_common(1)[0][0] if score else None), have_lp

try:
    cd_results = []
    for q in PROBS[:1]:                                                                                 # about 20 short calls
        branches, pick, have_lp = cot_decode(q)
        greedy = branches[0]["answer"] if have_lp else extract_int(generate(f"Q: {q['question']}\nA:", 250).get("response", ""))
        cd_results.append((q["answer"], greedy, pick))
        print(f"\ntruth {q['answer']} | greedy {greedy} | CoT-decoding pick {pick} | real log-probs: {have_lp}")
        for b in branches:
            d = f"{b['delta']:.2f}" if b["delta"] is not None else "n/a"
            print(f"  first token {b['first']!r:10} -> answer {b['answer']} | Δ {d} | agreement {b['agreement']:.0%} | {b['text'][:70]!r}")
except Exception as e:
    cd_results = []
    print("Skipped CoT-decoding (needs Ollama's /api/generate):", type(e).__name__, e)

What to notice: some first tokens start the answer right away ("12"), others start a sentence ("First", "The") and lead into worked steps. Our model is instruction-tuned, so it often reasons anyway; the effect is biggest on pre-trained (base) models, which is what the paper studied. The paper also found instruction-tuned Mistral-7B still gained (31.2% to 38.2% on GSM8K).

5. The other talks, in short (readings are our picks)

Melvin Johnson (Google DeepMind), Lecture 9: "Evolution of Post-training from Chatbots to Agents." His personal site describes him as leading Gemini post-training. Post-training is everything after pre-training: instruction tuning, preference tuning, and reinforcement learning, which together turn a text predictor into a helpful chat model and now into an agent that plans and calls tools. Readings we chose: the Gemini 1.0 report (Gemini Team 2023, Gemini: A Family of Highly Capable Multimodal Models), where he is an author and which describes the post-training stages; and his best-known earlier paper (Johnson et al. 2017, Google's Multilingual Neural Machine Translation System), where one model handles many languages with only a target-language token added, and can translate between pairs it never saw together ("zero-shot translation"). The lesson that carries over: one general model plus the right training signal beats many special-purpose models.

Thang Luong (Google DeepMind), Lecture 16: "Towards AI Superhuman Reasoning: AlphaProof, AlphaGeometry and Gemini IMO Gold." - AlphaGeometry (Trinh et al. 2024, Solving olympiad geometry without human demonstrations, Nature): a language model proposes helpful extra constructions (a new point or line) and a symbolic engine does the rigorous deduction. Trained on 100 million synthetic theorems and proofs, with no human demonstrations. It solved 25 of 30 olympiad geometry problems; the previous best method solved 10, and an average gold medalist about 25.9. - AlphaProof + AlphaGeometry 2 (DeepMind blog, July 2024): reinforcement learning in the formal proof language Lean, on about a million problems auto-translated from natural language. Together they scored 28 of 42 at IMO 2024 (4 of 6 problems), silver-medal level, but some problems took up to three days. - Gemini Deep Think (DeepMind blog, July 2025): 35 of 42 at IMO 2025 (5 of 6 problems), gold-medal level, end to end in natural language within the 4.5-hour limit, using "parallel thinking" (exploring several solution paths at once) and new RL techniques. The arc: from a verifier-backed narrow system to a general model that reasons in plain language. A checkable reward (a proof checker, a unit test, our contract labels) is what made the RL work.

Misha Laskin (Reflection AI), Lecture 18: "Building Agentic Systems for Autonomy: Lessons and Open Questions." Laskin co-founded Reflection AI in March 2025 after DeepMind work on RL and Gemini; its stated bet is that solving autonomous coding leads toward broader superintelligence. Its first product, Asimov, reads code plus team documents and messages to answer how a codebase works (as reported in July 2025). Reading we chose: his earlier paper (Laskin et al. 2022, In-context Reinforcement Learning with Algorithm Distillation), which trains a transformer on the learning histories of an RL algorithm; the model then improves its policy in context, with no weight updates. That is a clean early version of this course's theme: an agent that gets better from its own experience.

Danny Driess (Physical Intelligence), Lecture 19: "Multimodal AI Agents in Robotics." Readings we chose: PaLM-E (Driess et al. 2023, PaLM-E: An Embodied Multimodal Language Model), which feeds images and robot state straight into a language model as "multimodal sentences"; the largest version (562B parameters) did robot planning and reached state-of-the-art on the OK-VQA visual question benchmark. And π0 (Black et al. 2024, π0: A Vision-Language-Action Flow Model for General Robot Control), co-authored by Driess: a vision-language model extended with a flow-matching "action expert" that outputs smooth robot motions, trained on many robot types, doing tasks like folding laundry and assembling boxes. The pattern from the rest of the course holds: start from a pre-trained model, add the new input and output types, and fine-tune for the task.

Lecture 20: Future research areas. No readings were listed; this paragraph is our synthesis of open problems that run through the course, not the lecture's content: (1) rewards for work that has no unit test (most business work); (2) long-horizon reliability, since METR's 80% horizons are several times shorter than its 50% ones; (3) memory that stays correct over months; (4) cheaper inference so that search, voting and self-checking are affordable; (5) evaluations that keep up with models (live benchmarks like DeepScholar-Bench).

a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 7
check("chain of thought is at least as accurate as direct answers", acc(cot) >= acc(direct))
check("chain of thought solves at least half the problems", acc(cot) >= 0.5)
check("self-consistency is not worse than single CoT by more than one problem", acc(sc) >= acc(cot) - 1 / len(PROBS))
hi = [p == q["answer"] for a, p, q in zip(agree, sc, PROBS) if a >= 0.6]
lo = [p == q["answer"] for a, p, q in zip(agree, sc, PROBS) if a < 0.6]
check("high-agreement answers are at least as accurate as low-agreement ones",
      not hi or not lo or sum(hi) / len(hi) >= sum(lo) / len(lo))
check("least-to-most returns a number for most problems", sum(a is not None for a in l2m) >= len(PROBS) - 1)
check("CoT-decoding ran and produced a pick", bool(cd_results) and all(p is not None for _, _, p in cd_results))

Your turn

  1. Raise self-consistency to K = 9 on the two problems with the lowest agreement. Does the majority change? Does agreement go up?
  2. Run the direct vs CoT comparison with llama3.2:3b. Is the gain from CoT bigger or smaller for the smaller model?
  3. Use self-consistency agreement as a gate: send any answer with agreement below 60% to "human review". What share goes to review, and what is the accuracy of the rest?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 8
# Solution 1: more samples on the least certain problems (2 x 9 = 18 calls)
low = sorted(range(len(PROBS)), key=lambda i: agree[i])[:2]
for i in low:
    more = [extract_int(ask(COT.format(q=PROBS[i]["question"]), temperature=0.7, max_tokens=500)) for _ in range(9)]
    top = Counter(a for a in more if a is not None).most_common(1)
    print(f"truth {PROBS[i]['answer']} | K=5 vote {sc[i]} ({agree[i]:.0%}) | K=9 vote {top[0][0] if top else None} ({top[0][1]/9 if top else 0:.0%})")
# Notice: more samples make the vote more stable, but if the model is wrong in the same way each time,
# voting cannot fix it. Agreement measures consistency, not truth.
a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 9
# Solution 2: a smaller model (12 calls)
d3 = pmap(lambda q: extract_int(ask(DIRECT.format(q=q["question"]), model_name="llama3.2:3b", max_tokens=15)), PROBS)
c3 = pmap(lambda q: extract_int(ask(COT.format(q=q["question"]), model_name="llama3.2:3b", max_tokens=500)), PROBS)
print(f"3B direct {acc(d3):.0%} -> CoT {acc(c3):.0%} | 8B direct {acc(direct):.0%} -> CoT {acc(cot):.0%}")
# Notice: the CoT paper found the gain grows with model size; very small models can write steps that look
# right but are wrong. On a tiny sample like ours, expect noise.
a11_guest_lectures.ipynb · cell 10
# Solution 3: agreement as a review gate (no new calls)
auto = [i for i in range(len(PROBS)) if agree[i] >= 0.6]
print(f"sent to review: {1 - len(auto)/len(PROBS):.0%} | accuracy of auto-approved: "
      f"{(sum(sc[i] == PROBS[i]['answer'] for i in auto) / len(auto)) if auto else float('nan'):.0%}")
# Notice: this is the business use of self-consistency. You trade some review work for much higher accuracy
# on what you let through without a person.
Traps
  • Treating these readings as the course's. CS329A listed no papers for the guest lectures; we chose them.
  • Forcing a short answer on a multi-step problem. Asking for 'only the number' removes the reasoning the model needs.
  • Reading high agreement as truth. Self-consistency measures consistency; a model can be wrong the same way every time.
  • Running CoT-decoding through the chat endpoint. It needs raw completion and ideally token log-probs; without them our notebook approximates confidence by agreement.
  • Expecting a big CoT gain on tiny models. The CoT paper found the gain grows with model size; small models can write plausible but wrong steps.
  • Copying the IMO recipe without a checker. RL on reasoning worked because proofs and answers could be verified exactly.

Check yourself

What is the difference between self-consistency and CoT-decoding?

Self-consistency samples whole reasoning paths from a CoT prompt and votes; CoT-decoding uses no prompt, branches only on the first token's top-k alternatives, continues greedily, and picks by answer confidence.

Why does least-to-most help on problems harder than the examples?

Breaking the problem into easier sub-problems solved in order lets the model handle each piece at the difficulty it can manage, with earlier answers feeding later ones.

What changed between the 2024 IMO silver and the 2025 IMO gold?

2024 used formal Lean proofs and took up to three days (28/42); 2025 Gemini Deep Think worked end to end in natural language within 4.5 hours (35/42).

What made RL work in AlphaProof?

A formal checker (Lean) that verifies proofs exactly, giving a reliable reward.

Were the readings for these talks assigned by the course?

No. The course listed only titles and speakers; these are our picks of each speaker's public work.

Past project · AP1

AppBench: Benchmarking AI-Generated Web Applications

Open in Colab

CS329A Winter 2025 · Ethan Hellman, Brendan McLaughlin, Abhinav Lalwani, Belinda Mo

Paper · Poster

Problem
Code benchmarks check functions with unit tests. Nothing checked whether an AI-built web app is usable: does navigation work, does state survive a refresh, are errors handled?
Method
9 app requests from L0 (static page) to L4 (real-time collaborative whiteboard), spread over six axes (UI, features, state, API integration, cross-page navigation, data processing). Each request gets a checklist of explicit and implicit requirements, arranged as a dependency graph and turned into a scripted evaluator built from shared tools: screenshot + multimodal LLM judging, an LLM-driven browser (Stagehand on Playwright), and network interception for error states. Weighted scores scaled to 0-100. 7 builders tested one-shot: Replit, Bolt, Cursor Agent, Lovable, Claude Sonnet 3.7, GPT-4.5, OpenHands CodeAct 2.1.
Results
Grader vs. 3 human raters: mean MSE 0.0302, mean MAE 0.1313, Pearson 0.8173, Spearman 0.6827. Per-builder MSE from 0.0160 (Service 4) to 0.0501 (CodeAct); per-task MSE from 0.0042 (l2-add-to-cart) to 0.0751 (l3-food-order). All builder types scored above 75% up to L2, fell at L3 and dropped sharply at L4; 2 of 7 builders failed to produce a compiling app for one L3 and one L4 task. One-shot models were close to full agent products; Lovable and Bolt were the top builders.
Lessons
Grade AI-built software by what a user can do with it, not by whether it compiles. Split checks into what was asked and what a good result would also do, and skip checks whose prerequisites failed so one root failure doesn't look like ten. Validate your grader against humans (or at least known-good and known-broken examples) before trusting it. Don't assume an agent framework beats a strong one-shot model; measure it.
Our rebuild
The local model builds 5 single-file HTML apps (L0-L3: about page, theme switcher, tip calculator, food order, to-do list saved in localStorage). Python's HTML parser checks structure; Node.js runs each app's script in a fake page (subprocess with timeout) and calls the functions it exposes on window.app, including a 'page refresh' test for saved state. Checks are weighted, core vs. implicit, with dependencies. We validate the grader on known-good/broken apps, compare one-shot vs. one fix round with failure feedback, and (in the solutions) correlate the grader with a hand score and an LLM judge.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in ap1_appbench.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

The original project

AppBench: Benchmarking AI-Generated Web Applications by Ethan Hellman, Brendan McLaughlin, Abhinav Lalwani and Belinda Mo (Stanford CS329A, Winter 2025). Paper · Poster

Problem. Code benchmarks check functions with unit tests. Nobody checked whether an AI-built web app is usable: does navigation work, does state survive a refresh, does the page handle errors?

Method. 9 app requests from L0 (static page) to L4 (real-time collaborative whiteboard), spread over six axes: UI complexity, features, state management, API integration, cross-page navigation, data processing. For each request the authors wrote a checklist of explicit requirements (what the user asked for) and implicit ones (what a good app would also do, like a back button). They arranged the checklist as a dependency graph: you can't test "add to cart" before the product page opens. Each node becomes a scripted test built from shared tools: screenshot + multimodal LLM judging, an LLM-driven browser (Stagehand on Playwright), and network interception to test error states. Scores are weighted and scaled to 0–100.

Setup. 7 builders: Replit, Bolt, Cursor Agent, Lovable, Claude Sonnet 3.7, GPT-4.5 and OpenHands CodeAct 2.1, each in one shot. Three humans scored every app without seeing the automatic scores.

Key results (as reported). - Grader vs. humans: mean MSE 0.0302, mean MAE 0.1313, Pearson 0.8173, Spearman 0.6827. - Per-builder MSE ranged from 0.0160 (Service 4) to 0.0501 (CodeAct); per-task MSE from 0.0042 (l2-add-to-cart) to 0.0751 (l3-food-order). - All builder types scored above 75% up to level L2; scores fell at L3 and dropped sharply at L4. 2 of 7 builders could not produce a compiling app for one L3 and one L4 task. - One-shot models were "surprisingly competitive" with full agent products; the gap narrowed at higher levels. Lovable and Bolt were the top builders.

Limitations they state. Only 9 tasks, so no strong general claims; the graders need more calibration with UX experts.

Our mini version. Their apps ran in a real browser. Colab has no browser, so each app must expose its logic as plain JavaScript functions on window.app. We check the page structure with Python's built-in HTML parser, and we run the app's own script in Node.js (which Colab has) inside a fake page, then call its functions. Same ideas: explicit vs. implicit checks, a dependency graph, weighted 0–100 scores, and "page refresh" tests for saved state.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 2
import json, re, shutil, subprocess, tempfile
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from html.parser import HTMLParser
from pathlib import Path
from lal.llm import ask
from lal.checks import check

NODE = shutil.which("node")
print("node.js:", NODE or "not found (behavior tests fall back to weak text checks)")
OUT = Path("cs329a_projects/ap1_apps"); OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

1. Read the page like a browser would (pure Python)

Page collects tag counts, element ids, the text inside each tag, and the inline scripts.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 3
VOID = {"area", "base", "br", "col", "embed", "hr", "img", "input", "link", "meta", "source", "track", "wbr"}

class Page(HTMLParser):
    def __init__(self, html: str):
        super().__init__(convert_charrefs=True)
        self.tags, self.ids, self.text = Counter(), {}, defaultdict(str)
        self.scripts, self.stack, self._script = [], [], False
        self.attrs = []
        try:
            self.feed(html or "")
        except Exception as e:                      # very broken HTML: keep what we parsed
            print("parse warning:", e)

    def handle_starttag(self, tag, attrs):
        a = {k: (v or "") for k, v in attrs}
        self.tags[tag] += 1
        self.attrs.append((tag, a))
        if a.get("id"):
            self.ids[a["id"]] = tag
        if tag == "script":
            self._script = not a.get("src")         # external scripts are not run
            if self._script:
                self.scripts.append("")
        if tag not in VOID:
            self.stack.append(tag)

    def handle_endtag(self, tag):
        if tag == "script":
            self._script = False
        if tag in self.stack:
            while self.stack and self.stack.pop() != tag:
                pass

    def handle_data(self, data):
        if self._script:
            self.scripts[-1] += data
            return
        for t in set(self.stack):
            self.text[t] += data + " "
        self.text["*"] += data + " "

    def has(self, tag, n=1):
        return self.tags[tag] >= n

    def text_has(self, tag, s):
        return s.lower() in re.sub(r"\s+", " ", self.text[tag]).lower()

p = Page("<html><head><title>Hi</title></head><body><header>Acme Labs</header><input id='x'><script>var a=1;</script></body></html>")
print(p.tags["input"], p.ids, p.text_has("header", "acme"), p.scripts)

2. Run the app's own JavaScript in a fake page (Node.js, in a subprocess with a timeout)

The fake page answers every DOM call with a harmless stand-in object, so the app's setup code doesn't crash. localStorage is real (a dictionary), so we can test saving. A phase is one page load: to test a refresh, we run phase 1, keep localStorage, and load the page again for phase 2. The value of the last expression in the last phase is the result.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 4
HARNESS_JS = r"""
const vm = require('vm'), fs = require('fs');
const code = fs.readFileSync(process.argv[2], 'utf8');
const phases = JSON.parse(fs.readFileSync(process.argv[3], 'utf8'));
const store = {};
const TEXTY = ['value','textContent','innerHTML','innerText','className','id','src','href','checked'];
function any() {                       // a stand-in for any DOM object
  const props = {};
  return new Proxy(function(){}, {
    get(t, p) {
      if (p === Symbol.toPrimitive) return () => '';
      if (p in props) return props[p];
      if (p === 'toString' || p === 'valueOf') return () => '';
      if (TEXTY.includes(p)) return '';
      if (p === 'length') return 0;
      if (p === 'then') return undefined;
      if (p === Symbol.iterator) return function* () {};
      if (p === 'children' || p === 'childNodes') return [];
      const v = any(); props[p] = v; return v;
    },
    set(t, p, v) { props[p] = v; return true; },
    apply() { return any(); },
    construct() { return any(); },
  });
}
const localStorage = {
  getItem: k => (k in store ? store[k] : null),
  setItem: (k, v) => { store[k] = String(v); },
  removeItem: k => { delete store[k]; },
  clear: () => { for (const k in store) delete store[k]; },
  key: i => Object.keys(store)[i] ?? null,
  get length() { return Object.keys(store).length; },
};
let out = {load_error: null, error: null, value: null};
for (let i = 0; i < phases.length; i++) {
  const listeners = [];
  const document = any();
  document.readyState = 'loading';
  document.addEventListener = (ev, fn) => { if (typeof fn === 'function' && /load/i.test(ev)) listeners.push(fn); };
  const sb = {document, localStorage, console: {log(){}, error(){}, warn(){}, info(){}},
              alert(){}, confirm(){ return true; }, prompt(){ return ''; },
              setTimeout(){ return 0; }, clearTimeout(){}, setInterval(){ return 0; }, clearInterval(){},
              requestAnimationFrame(){ return 0; }, fetch(){ return new Promise(() => {}); },
              navigator: any(), location: any(), history: any()};
  sb.window = sb; sb.self = sb;
  sb.addEventListener = (ev, fn) => { if (typeof fn === 'function' && /load/i.test(ev)) listeners.push(fn); };
  const ctx = vm.createContext(sb);
  try {
    vm.runInContext(code, ctx, {timeout: 2000});
    for (const fn of listeners) fn.call(sb, any());
    if (typeof sb.onload === 'function') sb.onload(any());
  } catch (e) { out.load_error = String(e).slice(0, 200); }
  try {
    let v;
    for (const expr of phases[i]) v = vm.runInContext(expr, ctx, {timeout: 1000});
    out.value = (v === undefined) ? null : JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(v));
  } catch (e) { out.error = String(e).slice(0, 200); out.value = null; }
}
console.log(JSON.stringify(out));
"""
WORK = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="ap1_"))
(WORK / "harness.js").write_text(HARNESS_JS)

def run_js(script: str, phases: list) -> dict:
    """Run the app's inline script in the fake page. Untrusted code: subprocess + timeout."""
    if not NODE:
        return {"skipped": True}
    d = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(dir=WORK))
    (d / "app.js").write_text(script)
    (d / "phases.json").write_text(json.dumps(phases))
    try:
        r = subprocess.run([NODE, str(WORK / "harness.js"), str(d / "app.js"), str(d / "phases.json")],
                           capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=10)
        return json.loads(r.stdout.strip().splitlines()[-1])
    except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
        return {"load_error": "timeout", "error": "timeout", "value": None}
    except Exception as e:
        return {"load_error": f"harness failed: {e}", "error": "harness failed", "value": None}

print(run_js("window.app = {double: x => 2*x}; localStorage.setItem('k','1');",
             [["app.double(21)"]]))
print(run_js("const app = {n: () => Number(localStorage.getItem('k') || 0), inc(){ localStorage.setItem('k', this.n()+1); }};",
             [["app.inc()", "app.inc()"], ["app.n()"]]))       # survives the "refresh"

3. The checklist as a dependency graph

Each check has a weight, a flag for core (explicitly asked) vs. implicit (a good app would do it), and a list of checks it depends on. If a parent fails, its children are skipped and score 0, the same "conditional test execution" AppBench uses so one missing button doesn't cause a pile of confusing failures.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 5
def static(fn):
    return ("static", fn)

def js(phases, expected):
    return ("js", (phases, expected))

def same(got, want):
    if isinstance(want, (int, float)) and isinstance(got, (int, float)) and not isinstance(got, bool):
        return abs(got - want) < 0.011
    return got == want

APP_LOADS = js([["typeof app === 'object' && app !== null"]], True)

SPECS = {
 "l0-about-us": dict(level=0, spec=
   "Design a static 'About Us' page for a company called Acme Labs. It needs a <header> with the company name, "
   "a <main> section with at least two paragraphs about the company, and a <footer> with the company address: "
   "12 Market Street, Springfield. Make it look good on phones too.",
   checks=[
     ("header names the company", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: p.text_has("header", "Acme Labs"))),
     ("main with 2+ paragraphs", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: p.has("main") and p.has("p", 2))),
     ("footer has the address", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: p.text_has("footer", "12 Market Street"))),
     ("has a <title>", 1, False, [], static(lambda p: p.has("title") and p.text["title"].strip() != "")),
     ("mobile viewport meta", 1, False, [], static(lambda p: any(t == "meta" and a.get("name") == "viewport" for t, a in p.attrs))),
   ]),
 "l1-theme-switcher": dict(level=1, spec=
   "Create a theme switcher page. Three buttons with ids btn-light, btn-dark and btn-blue switch the whole page "
   "between a light, a dark and a blue theme. Show a heading and a short paragraph so the theme is visible. "
   "Expose window.app = { setTheme(name), getTheme() }: setTheme('dark') applies the dark theme and getTheme() then "
   "returns 'dark'. The default theme is 'light'. Unknown theme names must be ignored. The buttons must call app.setTheme.",
   checks=[
     ("three theme buttons", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: all(i in p.ids for i in ("btn-light", "btn-dark", "btn-blue")))),
     ("heading and paragraph", 1, False, [], static(lambda p: (p.has("h1") or p.has("h2")) and p.has("p"))),
     ("app loads", 1, True, [], APP_LOADS),
     ("default theme is light", 2, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.getTheme()"]], "light")),
     ("setTheme('blue') works", 3, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.setTheme('blue')", "app.getTheme()"]], "blue")),
     ("unknown theme ignored", 2, False, ["setTheme('blue') works"], js([["app.setTheme('dark')", "app.setTheme('purple')", "app.getTheme()"]], "dark")),
   ]),
 "l1-tip-calculator": dict(level=1, spec=
   "Build a tip calculator. It has an input with id bill, an input with id tip-pct (tip percent), a button with id "
   "calc, and an element with id result that shows the tip and the total. Each input has a <label>. "
   "Expose window.app = { tip(bill, pct), total(bill, pct) }: tip returns bill*pct/100 rounded to 2 decimals, "
   "total returns bill + tip rounded to 2 decimals. If bill is negative or not a number, both return 0.",
   checks=[
     ("inputs, button, result", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: all(i in p.ids for i in ("bill", "tip-pct", "calc", "result")))),
     ("inputs have labels", 1, False, [], static(lambda p: p.has("label", 2))),
     ("app loads", 1, True, [], APP_LOADS),
     ("tip(50, 20) = 10", 2, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.tip(50, 20)"]], 10)),
     ("total(80, 15) = 92", 2, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.total(80, 15)"]], 92)),
     ("rounds to cents", 2, True, ["tip(50, 20) = 10"], js([["Math.abs(app.tip(33.33, 18) - 6) < 1e-9"]], True)),
     ("negative bill gives 0", 1, False, ["app loads"], js([["app.tip(-10, 20)"]], 0)),
   ]),
 "l2-food-order": dict(level=2, spec=
   "Build a single-page restaurant menu with exactly these items and prices: Burger $8.50, Fries $3.00, "
   "Soda $1.75, Salad $6.25. Each item has an add button. The bottom of the page lists the current order and shows "
   "the total in an element with id order-total. No login. Expose window.app = { add(name), remove(name), "
   "count(name), total() }: total() returns the order total as a number. remove takes away one unit and never "
   "goes below zero. Unknown item names are ignored.",
   checks=[
     ("menu shows all items and prices", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: all(p.text_has("*", s) for s in
                                                    ("Burger", "8.50", "Fries", "3.00", "Soda", "1.75", "Salad", "6.25")))),
     ("order total element", 1, True, [], static(lambda p: "order-total" in p.ids)),
     ("app loads", 1, True, [], APP_LOADS),
     ("2 Burgers + Soda = 18.75", 3, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.add('Burger')", "app.add('Burger')", "app.add('Soda')", "app.total()"]], 18.75)),
     ("remove takes one unit", 2, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.add('Fries')", "app.add('Fries')", "app.remove('Fries')", "app.count('Fries')"]], 1)),
     ("remove never below zero", 1, False, ["remove takes one unit"], js([["app.remove('Salad')", "app.count('Salad')"]], 0)),
     ("unknown item ignored", 1, False, ["2 Burgers + Soda = 18.75"], js([["app.add('Pizza')", "app.total()"]], 0)),
   ]),
 "l3-todo-saved": dict(level=3, spec=
   "Build a to-do list app with an input with id new-task, a button with id add-btn and a list with id task-list. "
   "Expose window.app = { addTask(text), toggle(index), remaining(), all() }. all() returns an array of "
   "{text, done} objects. toggle flips done for the task at that index. remaining() counts tasks that are not done. "
   "Ignore empty or whitespace-only text. Save the tasks in localStorage under the key 'todos' as JSON after every "
   "change, and load them when the page opens, so tasks survive a page refresh.",
   checks=[
     ("input, button, list", 2, True, [], static(lambda p: all(i in p.ids for i in ("new-task", "add-btn", "task-list")))),
     ("app loads", 1, True, [], APP_LOADS),
     ("add + toggle + remaining", 3, True, ["app loads"], js([["app.addTask('a')", "app.addTask('b')", "app.toggle(0)", "app.remaining()"]], 1)),
     ("saves JSON under 'todos'", 2, True, ["add + toggle + remaining"], js([["app.addTask('a')", "JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('todos')).length"]], 1)),
     ("survives a refresh", 3, True, ["saves JSON under 'todos'"], js([["app.addTask('a')", "app.addTask('b')"], ["app.remaining()"]], 2)),
     ("ignores blank tasks", 1, False, ["app loads"], js([["app.addTask('   ')", "app.all().length"]], 0)),
   ]),
}
print({k: len(v["checks"]) for k, v in SPECS.items()})
ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 6
def grade(name: str, html: str, verbose=False) -> dict:
    page = Page(html)
    script = "\n;\n".join(page.scripts)
    results, earned, total, core_earned, core_total = {}, 0, 0, 0, 0
    for cname, w, core, deps, (kind, arg) in SPECS[name]["checks"]:
        total += w; core_total += w if core else 0
        if any(not results.get(d, (False,))[0] for d in deps):
            results[cname] = (False, "skipped: depends on " + ", ".join(deps))
        elif kind == "static":
            try:
                ok = bool(arg(page))
            except Exception as e:
                ok = False
            results[cname] = (ok, "")
        else:
            phases, want = arg
            r = run_js(script, phases)
            if r.get("skipped"):                     # no node: weak fallback, function names must at least appear
                names = set(re.findall(r"app\.(\w+)\(", " ".join(sum(phases, []))))
                ok = bool(script) and all(re.search(rf"\b{n}\b", script) for n in names)
                results[cname] = (ok, "weak check (no node)")
            else:
                ok = r.get("error") is None and same(r.get("value"), want)
                why = "" if ok else f"got {r.get('value')!r}, want {want!r}" + (f"; error: {r['error']}" if r.get("error") else "") \
                      + (f"; page script error: {r['load_error']}" if r.get("load_error") else "")
                results[cname] = (ok, why)
        if results[cname][0]:
            earned += w; core_earned += w if core else 0
    out = {"score": round(100 * earned / total, 1), "core": round(100 * core_earned / max(core_total, 1), 1), "checks": results}
    if verbose:
        for c, (ok, why) in results.items():
            print(f"  {'✓' if ok else '✗'} {c}  {why}")
    return out

4. Test the grader before you trust it

AppBench's main claim is that its grader agrees with humans. We can't hire three humans here, so we do the cheaper first step: a known-good app must score 100, and a known-broken one must score low.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 7
GOOD_TIP = """<!DOCTYPE html><html><head><meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width"><title>Tips</title></head>
<body><h1>Tip calculator</h1>
<label for="bill">Bill</label><input id="bill" type="number">
<label for="tip-pct">Tip %</label><input id="tip-pct" type="number" value="15">
<button id="calc">Calculate</button><p id="result"></p>
<script>
const r2 = x => Math.round(x * 100) / 100;
window.app = {
  tip(bill, pct) { bill = Number(bill); if (!(bill >= 0)) return 0; return r2(bill * Number(pct) / 100); },
  total(bill, pct) { bill = Number(bill); if (!(bill >= 0)) return 0; return r2(bill + this.tip(bill, pct)); },
};
document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', () => {
  document.getElementById('calc').addEventListener('click', () => {
    const b = document.getElementById('bill').value, p = document.getElementById('tip-pct').value;
    document.getElementById('result').textContent = `Tip ${app.tip(b, p)} · Total ${app.total(b, p)}`;
  });
});
</script></body></html>"""
BROKEN_TIP = GOOD_TIP.replace("<label", "<span").replace("bill * Number(pct) / 100", "bill * Number(pct)").replace('id="calc"', 'id="go"')
CRASHING_TIP = GOOD_TIP.replace("const r2", "undefinedFunction(); const r2")

ref = {n: grade("l1-tip-calculator", h) for n, h in [("good", GOOD_TIP), ("broken", BROKEN_TIP), ("crashes", CRASHING_TIP)]}
for n, g in ref.items():
    print(f"{n:8s} score {g['score']:5.1f}")
grade("l1-tip-calculator", BROKEN_TIP, verbose=True);

5. Let the model build the five apps (one shot)

Like the paper's "one-shot regime": same prompt shape for every task, one turn, no feedback.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 8
BUILD = ("You are an expert front-end developer. Build this web app as ONE complete HTML file with all CSS and "
         "JavaScript inline. Do not load any external scripts, fonts or libraries.\n\nAPP REQUEST:\n{spec}\n\n"
         "Return only the file inside a ```html code block.")

def extract_html(text: str) -> str:
    m = re.search(r"```(?:html)?\s*(.*?)```", text or "", re.S | re.I)
    if m and "<" in m.group(1):
        return m.group(1).strip()
    m = re.search(r"(<!DOCTYPE.*?</html>|<html.*?</html>)", text or "", re.S | re.I)
    return m.group(1) if m else (text or "")

def build(name):
    try:
        return extract_html(ask(BUILD.format(spec=SPECS[name]["spec"]), temperature=0, max_tokens=3000))
    except Exception as e:
        print(name, "generation failed:", e)
        return ""

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    apps = dict(zip(SPECS, ex.map(build, SPECS)))
one_shot = {}
for name, html in apps.items():
    (OUT / f"{name}.html").write_text(html)
    one_shot[name] = grade(name, html)
    print(f"\n{name} (L{SPECS[name]['level']}): score {one_shot[name]['score']} · core {one_shot[name]['core']}")
    grade(name, html, verbose=True)

6. Does an "agent loop" help? Build → grade → fix once

The paper found agent products only modestly better than one-shot models. Here the "agent" gets the names of its failed checks (not the hidden test code) and one chance to fix the file. Each fix is one more model call.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 9
FIX = ("Here is a web app you built and the user tests it failed.\n\nAPP REQUEST:\n{spec}\n\nFAILED CHECKS:\n{fails}\n\n"
       "CURRENT FILE:\n```html\n{html}\n```\n\nReturn the complete corrected file inside a ```html code block.")

def fix(name):
    g = one_shot[name]
    fails = [f"- {c}: {why}" for c, (ok, why) in g["checks"].items() if not ok]
    if not fails:
        return apps[name]                            # nothing to fix, no call spent
    try:
        return extract_html(ask(FIX.format(spec=SPECS[name]["spec"], fails="\n".join(fails), html=apps[name][:12000]),
                                temperature=0, max_tokens=3000))
    except Exception as e:
        print(name, "fix failed:", e)
        return apps[name]

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    fixed = dict(zip(SPECS, ex.map(fix, SPECS)))
with_fix = {n: grade(n, h) for n, h in fixed.items()}
print(f"{'task':20s} level  one-shot  after-fix")
for n in SPECS:
    print(f"{n:20s}  L{SPECS[n]['level']}    {one_shot[n]['score']:6.1f}    {with_fix[n]['score']:6.1f}")
mean = lambda d: sum(v["score"] for v in d.values()) / len(d)
print(f"mean                       {mean(one_shot):6.1f}    {mean(with_fix):6.1f}")

What to notice. Scores usually fall as the level goes up, as in the paper. Look at which checks fail: implicit checks (blank tasks, unknown items) and saved state across a refresh are the usual misses. A fix round with precise failure messages often helps a lot here, because our checks name the exact problem. In AppBench the agents got no such feedback, which is one reason their edge over one-shot was small.

ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 10
check("grader: known-good app scores 100", ref["good"]["score"] == 100)
check("grader: broken app scores under 60", ref["broken"]["score"] < 60)
check("grader: crashing script fails the behavior checks", ref["crashes"]["core"] < 50)
check("all five apps were built and graded", len(one_shot) == 5 and all((OUT / f"{n}.html").exists() for n in SPECS))
check("the model's apps score at least 30/100 on average", mean(one_shot) >= 30)

Your turn

  1. Add an implicit check. For l2-food-order, a good app would let the user clear the order. Add an implicit check that app.clear() (if it exists) sets the total to 0. How many of the model's apps pass a check nobody asked for?
  2. Calibrate against a human (you). Open two or three apps in cs329a_projects/ap1_apps/ (in Colab, use IPython.display.HTML). Score each 0–100 by hand, then compute the Spearman correlation with the grader. AppBench reported 0.68 with three humans. Where do you and the grader disagree?
  3. Add an LLM judge. AppBench also used a model to judge screenshots. Ask the local model to score each HTML file 0–10 against its spec, and compare its ranking with the checklist grader. Which one would you trust, and why?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)

# 1. An implicit "clear order" check. It depends on "app loads". Notice: the spec never asked for clear(),
#    so most apps fail it. That is exactly what implicit checks measure: going beyond the literal request.
SPECS["l2-food-order"]["checks"].append(
    ("can clear the order", 1, False, ["app loads"],
     js([["app.add('Burger')", "typeof app.clear === 'function' ? (app.clear(), app.total()) : -1"]], 0)))
g = grade("l2-food-order", fixed["l2-food-order"], verbose=True)
print("food-order score with the new check:", g["score"])
ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 12
# 2. Spearman correlation between your scores and the grader's. Replace my_scores with your own numbers.
def spearman(a, b):
    def ranks(x):
        order = sorted(range(len(x)), key=lambda i: x[i]); r = [0.0] * len(x)
        i = 0
        while i < len(x):                                  # average ranks for ties
            j = i
            while j + 1 < len(x) and x[order[j + 1]] == x[order[i]]:
                j += 1
            for k in range(i, j + 1):
                r[order[k]] = (i + j) / 2
            i = j + 1
        return r
    ra, rb = ranks(a), ranks(b); n = len(a)
    ma, mb = sum(ra) / n, sum(rb) / n
    cov = sum((x - ma) * (y - mb) for x, y in zip(ra, rb))
    sd = (sum((x - ma) ** 2 for x in ra) * sum((y - mb) ** 2 for y in rb)) ** 0.5
    return cov / sd if sd else 0.0

names = list(SPECS)
grader_scores = [with_fix[n]["score"] for n in names]
my_scores = [90, 70, 80, 60, 50]                           # <- your hand scores, same order as `names`
print(dict(zip(names, grader_scores)))
print("Spearman(you, grader) =", round(spearman(my_scores, grader_scores), 3))
ap1_appbench.ipynb · cell 13
# 3. A text-only LLM judge (5 calls). Notice how often it gives high scores to apps whose logic is broken:
#    a judge that reads code can't "click", which is why AppBench simulated real interaction.
def judge(name):
    reply = ask(f"APP REQUEST:\n{SPECS[name]['spec']}\n\nHTML FILE:\n```html\n{fixed[name][:8000]}\n```\n\n"
                "Score how well this file meets the request, 0 to 10. End with a line 'SCORE: <number>'.", max_tokens=400)
    m = re.findall(r"SCORE:\s*(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)", reply or "")
    return float(m[-1]) * 10 if m else 50.0
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    judge_scores = list(ex.map(judge, names))
for n, a, b in zip(names, grader_scores, judge_scores):
    print(f"{n:20s} grader {a:5.1f}   judge {b:5.1f}")
print("Spearman(judge, grader) =", round(spearman(judge_scores, grader_scores), 3))
Past project · AP2

PRIME: Planning with Reflective Iterative Multi-Agentic Exploration

Open in Colab

CS329A Winter 2025 · Chelsea Zou, Samuel Liu, Jui Khankari (the poster lists Chelsea Zou, Jui Khankari, Miriam Cheng)

Paper · Poster

Problem
Reasoning tricks such as reflection, debate and voting are applied ad hoc, with no principled way to choose the right one for each step of a multi-step plan.
Method
Treat each reasoning technique as an 'option' and search with Monte Carlo Tree Search. A planner splits the task into subgoals; at each node it can Drill down, Solve with the best-suited self-improvement tool (self-reflection, self-refinement, debate), or Backtrack. Values are backed up with a running mean and nodes chosen with UCT; non-leaf values mix an LM score with self-consistency. Built on LATS. GPT-4o-mini inside PRIME, compared with LATS, plain 4o-mini and one-shot o1 on 60 sampled questions each from PlanBench, WebShop and Game of 24, plus a reverse ablation that upgrades only the planner or only the executor.
Results
Table 1 success rates (PlanBench / WebShop / Game of 24): PRIME 36.2% / 40% / 75%; LATS 2.2% / 38% / 44%; GPT-4o-mini 0% / 0% / 0%; GPT-o1 100% / 100% / 100%; PRIME with upgraded planner 95% / 94% / 100%; with upgraded executor 96% / 92% / 100%. The text gives different figures for some cells (PRIME 'a 2.2% improvement over LATS' on PlanBench; o1 at 37.3% on PlanBench and 80% on WebShop), so these numbers are internally inconsistent. Estimated about 4x lower cost than o1 despite more API calls.
Lessons
Choosing the strategy per subgoal can beat one fixed strategy, but compare at equal call budgets: on WebShop, PRIME and LATS tied and reflections were generic. Tree search shines when each step's result is clear and checkable, and wanders when it isn't. A reverse ablation (upgrade one component at a time) is a cheap way to find which part of a pipeline deserves the expensive model.
Our rebuild
Game of 24 with an exact ast-based checker. PRIME-lite: the planner proposes first steps with a confidence (LM value); code drops impossible steps (backtrack); a bandit-style MCTS picks (subgoal, tool) arms with UCT across three tools (solve, reflect, drill down), uses the checker as reward, backs up values with the paper's running mean, and carries tool statistics across puzzles. Compared at a 5-call budget against single-shot and retry-with-feedback; solutions run a reverse ablation with a 3B executor and vary the exploration constant.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in ap2_prime.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

The original project

PRIME: Planning with Reflective, Iterative, Multi-agentic Exploration by Chelsea Zou, Samuel Liu and Jui Khankari (the poster lists Chelsea Zou, Jui Khankari and Miriam Cheng). Stanford CS329A, Winter 2025. Paper · Poster

Problem. Top-down planning with LLMs is unsolved. Reflection, debate and voting are used "ad hoc", with no principled way to pick the right one for a given step.

Method. Treat each reasoning technique as an option (a reusable sub-policy, from reinforcement learning) and search with Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). A planner splits the problem into N subgoals (tree children). At each node it chooses Drill down (split further), Solve (call the best-suited self-improvement tool: self-reflection, self-refinement or debate) or Backtrack (drop a useless subgoal). Results flow back up the tree with a running mean, V(s) ← (V·N + r)/(N+1), and the next node is chosen with UCT = w/n + C·√(ln N / n). Leaves are judged by ground truth when it exists, otherwise by V(s) = λ·LM(s) + (1−λ)·SC(s), a mix of a model's score and self-consistency. It builds on LATS (Zhou et al. 2024, Language Agent Tree Search).

Setup. GPT-4o-mini inside PRIME vs. LATS (4o-mini), plain 4o-mini, and one-shot GPT-o1, on 60 sampled questions each from PlanBench, WebShop and Game of 24. Plus a "reverse ablation": upgrade only the planner, or only the executor, to a stronger model.

Key results (as reported, Table 1, success rate).

Method PlanBench WebShop Game of 24
PRIME (4o-mini) 36.2% 40% 75%
LATS (4o-mini) 2.2% 38% 44%
GPT-4o-mini 0% 0% 0%
GPT-o1 100% 100% 100%
PRIME, upgraded planner 95% 94% 100%
PRIME, upgraded executor 96% 92% 100%

The text gives some different numbers for the same comparisons: PRIME is "a 2.2% improvement over LATS" on PlanBench, and o1 scores 37.3% on PlanBench and 80% on WebShop. We report both and can't tell which is right. The authors estimate PRIME costs about 4× less than o1, despite more API calls.

Limitations they state. PRIME wanders when the value of an action is unclear (obfuscated PlanBench); on WebShop, reflections were generic and the agent got stuck in local minima, so subgoals helped little.

Our mini version. Game of 24 only, because a checker can grade it exactly. The planner proposes first steps with a confidence (the LM value). Code rejects impossible steps (backtrack). A small bandit-style MCTS picks a (subgoal, tool) pair with UCT, where the tools are solve, reflect and drill down. The checker is the reward. Tool statistics carry over from puzzle to puzzle, a tiny version of "learning which option works".

ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 2
import ast, itertools, math, random, re
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from fractions import Fraction
from typing import List
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
from lal.llm import ask, chat, structured
from lal.checks import check

1. The task and an exact checker

Use each of four numbers once with + − × ÷ to make 24. The checker parses the expression with Python's ast module (no eval), checks the numbers used, and computes with exact fractions.

ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 3
OPS = {ast.Add: lambda a, b: a + b, ast.Sub: lambda a, b: a - b, ast.Mult: lambda a, b: a * b, ast.Div: lambda a, b: a / b}

def evaluate(node, used):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return evaluate(node.body, used)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and isinstance(node.value, int) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
        used.append(node.value); return Fraction(node.value)
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in OPS:
        return OPS[type(node.op)](evaluate(node.left, used), evaluate(node.right, used))
    raise ValueError("only whole numbers, + - * / and brackets are allowed")

def check24(expr: str, nums) -> tuple:
    """(ok, message). The message is what a reflecting agent gets to see."""
    try:
        used = []
        val = evaluate(ast.parse(expr, mode="eval"), used)
    except ZeroDivisionError:
        return False, "it divides by zero"
    except Exception as e:
        return False, f"it is not a valid expression ({e})"
    if sorted(used) != sorted(nums):
        return False, f"it uses the numbers {sorted(used)} but must use exactly {sorted(nums)}"
    if val != 24:
        return False, f"it equals {val}, not 24"
    return True, "correct"

def extract_expr(text: str) -> str:
    text = (text or "").replace("×", "*").replace("÷", "/").replace("−", "-")
    m = re.findall(r"ANSWER:\s*(.+)", text, re.I)
    cand = m[-1] if m else (text.strip().splitlines() or [""])[-1]
    cand = re.sub(r"(?<=[\d\)])\s*[xX]\s*(?=[\d\(])", "*", cand)
    parts = [p for p in cand.split("=") if re.search(r"\d", p)]
    if len(parts) > 1 and re.fullmatch(r"\s*24\s*\.?\s*", parts[-1]):
        parts = parts[:-1]
    cand = max(parts, key=lambda p: len(re.findall(r"\d+", p))) if parts else ""
    return re.sub(r"[^\d\s\+\-\*/\(\)]", "", cand).strip()

def solve24(nums):
    """Brute force: returns one solution or None. Used only to pick solvable puzzles, never by the agents."""
    items = [(Fraction(n), str(n)) for n in nums]
    def rec(items):
        if len(items) == 1:
            return items[0][1] if items[0][0] == 24 else None
        for i, j in itertools.permutations(range(len(items)), 2):
            (a, ea), (b, eb) = items[i], items[j]
            rest = [items[k] for k in range(len(items)) if k not in (i, j)]
            for v, e in [(a + b, f"({ea}+{eb})"), (a - b, f"({ea}-{eb})"), (a * b, f"({ea}*{eb})")] + \
                        ([(a / b, f"({ea}/{eb})")] if b != 0 else []):
                r = rec(rest + [(v, e)])
                if r:
                    return r
        return None
    return rec(items)

rng = random.Random(329)
PUZZLES = []
while len(PUZZLES) < 6:
    nums = sorted(rng.randint(1, 13) for _ in range(4))
    if nums not in PUZZLES and solve24(nums):
        PUZZLES.append(nums)
print(PUZZLES)
print(check24("(8-2)*(3+1)", [1, 2, 3, 8]), check24("8*3", [1, 2, 3, 8]), extract_expr("So: (8 − 2) × (3 + 1) = 24\nANSWER: (8-2)*(3+1) = 24"))

2. Baselines: one shot, and retry with feedback (same budget as PRIME)

The retry baseline is a plain reflection loop: try, get the checker's message, try again. If PRIME only wins because it makes more calls, this baseline will catch it.

ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 4
BUDGET = 5            # model calls per puzzle for the two search methods
ASK24 = ("Use each of the numbers {nums} exactly once, with + - * / and brackets, to make exactly 24.\n"
         "Think briefly, then end with one line: ANSWER: <expression>")

def single_shot(nums):
    out = ask(ASK24.format(nums=nums), max_tokens=600)
    ok, _ = check24(extract_expr(out), nums)
    return {"ok": ok, "calls": 1}

def retry_with_feedback(nums, budget=BUDGET):
    msgs = [{"role": "user", "content": ASK24.format(nums=nums)}]
    for call in range(1, budget + 1):
        out = chat(msgs, temperature=0.7, max_tokens=600).content or ""
        expr = extract_expr(out)
        ok, why = check24(expr, nums)
        if ok:
            return {"ok": True, "calls": call}
        msgs += [{"role": "assistant", "content": out},
                 {"role": "user", "content": f"Your expression '{expr}' is wrong: {why}. Reflect on the mistake and try a different approach."}]
    return {"ok": False, "calls": budget}

3. PRIME-lite

  • Plan (1 call): propose up to 4 first steps like 8 - 2 = 6, each with a confidence 1–10.
  • Backtrack for free: code drops steps that use numbers we don't have or have wrong arithmetic.
  • Options for each surviving subgoal: solve (1 call), reflect (solve, and if the checker says no, fix once: up to 2 calls), drill (propose the next step, code checks it, then solve with both steps fixed: 2 calls).
  • Select the (subgoal, option) arm with the highest UCT score, run it, get reward 1 or 0 from the checker, update the arm with the running mean from the paper. Stop on success or when the budget is used up.
  • Learn across puzzles: each option keeps a global success rate that becomes the starting value of new arms.
ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 5
class Subgoal(BaseModel):
    step: str = Field(description="one operation on two of the available numbers, like '8 - 2 = 6'")
    confidence: int = Field(ge=1, le=10, description="how likely this step leads to 24")

class Plan(BaseModel):
    subgoals: List[Subgoal] = Field(max_length=4)

def fmt(x: Fraction):
    return str(x.numerator) if x.denominator == 1 else f"{x.numerator}/{x.denominator}"

def apply_step(step: str, state):
    """Check one step against the available numbers. Returns (new_state, text) or None (= backtrack)."""
    m = re.search(r"(\d+(?:/\d+)?)\s*([\+\-\*/xX×÷])\s*(\d+(?:/\d+)?)", (step or "").replace("−", "-"))
    if not m:
        return None
    a, op, b = Fraction(m.group(1)), m.group(2), Fraction(m.group(3))
    pool = list(state)
    for v in (a, b):
        if v not in pool:
            return None
        pool.remove(v)
    op = {"x": "*", "X": "*", "×": "*", "÷": "/"}.get(op, op)
    if op == "/" and b == 0:
        return None
    val = {"+": a + b, "-": a - b, "*": a * b, "/": a / b if b else None}[op]
    return pool + [val], f"{fmt(a)} {op} {fmt(b)} = {fmt(val)}"

SOLVE = ("Numbers: {nums}. Goal: 24, using each number exactly once with + - * / and brackets.\n"
         "Follow this plan: {steps}. After these steps the numbers left are {left}.\n"
         "Write ONE expression with the ORIGINAL numbers {nums} that follows the plan and equals 24.\n"
         "End with one line: ANSWER: <expression>")

class Step(BaseModel):
    step: str

TOOL_STATS = {t: [1, 2] for t in ("solve", "reflect", "drill")}     # [successes+1, tries+2]: a gentle prior

def run_tool(tool, nums, steps, left, budget_left):
    """Returns (ok, calls_used)."""
    prompt = SOLVE.format(nums=nums, steps="; ".join(steps), left=[fmt(x) for x in left])
    if tool == "drill":
        try:
            nxt = structured(f"Numbers left: {[fmt(x) for x in left]}. Goal: reach 24 in the end. "
                             f"Propose the single best next step on two of these numbers, like 'a op b = c'.", Step, temperature=0.7)
            res = apply_step(nxt.step, left)
        except Exception:
            res = None
        if res is None or budget_left < 2:
            return False, 1
        left, txt = res
        prompt = SOLVE.format(nums=nums, steps="; ".join(steps + [txt]), left=[fmt(x) for x in left])
        ok, _ = check24(extract_expr(ask(prompt, temperature=0.3, max_tokens=500)), nums)
        return ok, 2
    msgs = [{"role": "user", "content": prompt}]
    out = chat(msgs, temperature=0.3, max_tokens=500).content or ""
    ok, why = check24(extract_expr(out), nums)
    if ok or tool == "solve" or budget_left < 2:
        return ok, 1
    msgs += [{"role": "assistant", "content": out},
             {"role": "user", "content": f"A checker says your expression is wrong: {why}. Reflect, then give a fixed ANSWER line."}]
    ok, _ = check24(extract_expr(chat(msgs, temperature=0.3, max_tokens=500).content), nums)
    return ok, 2

def prime(nums, budget=BUDGET, C=1.0, log=False):
    calls = 1
    try:
        plan = structured(f"Numbers: {nums}. Goal: make 24 using each number once with + - * /.\n"
                          "Propose up to 4 DIFFERENT promising first steps. Each step combines two of the numbers.",
                          Plan, temperature=0.7)
        subgoals = plan.subgoals
    except Exception:
        subgoals = []
    state0 = [Fraction(n) for n in nums]
    arms = []                                            # one arm per (valid subgoal, tool)
    for sg in subgoals:
        res = apply_step(sg.step, state0)
        if res is None:
            if log: print("   backtrack (invalid step):", sg.step)
            continue
        left, txt = res
        for tool, (s, n) in TOOL_STATS.items():
            prior = 0.5 * sg.confidence / 10 + 0.5 * s / n   # V = λ·LM + (1−λ)·(what this option did before)
            arms.append({"txt": txt, "left": left, "tool": tool, "N": 1, "V": prior})
    if not arms:                                         # nothing valid: fall back to solving from scratch
        arms = [{"txt": "(no plan)", "left": state0, "tool": "reflect", "N": 1, "V": 0.5}]
    total = sum(a["N"] for a in arms)
    while calls < budget:
        uct = lambda a: a["V"] + C * math.sqrt(math.log(total) / a["N"])
        arm = max(arms, key=uct)
        steps = [] if arm["txt"] == "(no plan)" else [arm["txt"]]
        ok, used = run_tool(arm["tool"], nums, steps, arm["left"], budget - calls)
        calls += used
        r = 1.0 if ok else 0.0
        arm["V"] = (arm["V"] * arm["N"] + r) / (arm["N"] + 1); arm["N"] += 1; total += 1     # backpropagation
        TOOL_STATS[arm["tool"]][0] += r; TOOL_STATS[arm["tool"]][1] += 1
        if log: print(f"   try {arm['tool']:7s} on [{arm['txt']}] -> {'✓' if ok else '✗'} (calls {calls})")
        if ok:
            return {"ok": True, "calls": calls}
    return {"ok": False, "calls": calls}

print(apply_step("8 - 2 = 6", [Fraction(n) for n in (1, 2, 3, 8)]), apply_step("9 - 2", [Fraction(n) for n in (1, 2, 3, 8)]))

4. Run all three methods

Puzzles run two at a time. Total model calls: at most 6 + 30 + 30.

ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 6
def run(method):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        return list(ex.map(method, PUZZLES))

results = {"single shot": run(single_shot), "retry + feedback": run(retry_with_feedback)}
print("PRIME-lite, puzzle by puzzle:")
results["PRIME-lite"] = []
for nums in PUZZLES:                                   # sequential so tool statistics carry over in order
    print(" ", nums)
    results["PRIME-lite"].append(prime(nums, log=True))

for name, rs in results.items():
    acc = sum(r["ok"] for r in rs) / len(rs)
    calls = sum(r["calls"] for r in rs) / len(rs)
    print(f"{name:18s} success {acc:4.0%}   avg calls {calls:.1f}")
print("option statistics (successes+1, tries+2):", TOOL_STATS)

What to notice. With an 8B model the single shot is often weak, like 4o-mini's 0% in the paper. Compare PRIME-lite with retry + feedback, not just with the single shot: both spend up to 5 calls. If PRIME-lite wins, the plan + strategy choice is doing work. If they tie, the gain came from more tries, which is the honest result the paper's WebShop row also shows. Also look at the option statistics: which tool did the search learn to prefer?

ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 7
acc = {k: sum(r["ok"] for r in v) / len(v) for k, v in results.items()}
check("all three methods ran on every puzzle", all(len(v) == len(PUZZLES) for v in results.values()))
check("search methods stay within the call budget", all(r["calls"] <= BUDGET for k in ("retry + feedback", "PRIME-lite") for r in results[k]))
check("checker rejects a wrong answer", not check24("8*3", [1, 2, 3, 8])[0] and check24("(8-2)*(3+1)", [1, 2, 3, 8])[0])
check("PRIME-lite solves at least one puzzle", acc["PRIME-lite"] > 0)

Your turn

  1. Reverse ablation. PRIME upgraded one part at a time to see where a better model helps. Use llama3.2:3b for the executor tools but keep llama3.1:8b as the planner. Does accuracy drop more than you expected?
  2. Turn off learning. Reset TOOL_STATS before every puzzle. Does carrying option statistics across puzzles help?
  3. Exploration constant. Try C=0.2 and C=2.0. What happens to the number of different arms tried?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
ap2_prime.ipynb · cell 8
# 1. Reverse ablation on 2 puzzles (up to 10 calls). We swap the model used by the executor tools only.
#    Notice: in the paper the executor upgrade mattered about as much as the planner upgrade.
import lal.llm as L
_ask, _chat = L.ask, L.chat
def small_ask(p, **kw): return _ask(p, model_name="llama3.2:3b", **kw)
def small_chat(m, **kw): return _chat(m, model_name="llama3.2:3b", **kw)
ask, chat = small_ask, small_chat            # run_tool looks these names up at call time
small = [prime(n) for n in PUZZLES[:2]]
ask, chat = _ask, _chat
print("small executor:", sum(r["ok"] for r in small), "/ 2   vs 8B executor:",
      sum(r["ok"] for r in results["PRIME-lite"][:2]), "/ 2")
