Past project · AP3

Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in LLMs through Reasoning Distillation, GRPO, and Multi-agent PRM Reranking

Open in Colab

CS329A Winter 2025 · Abhinav Agarwal, Shree Reddy, Carlo Baronio, Shubhra Mishra

Paper · Poster

Problem
Olympiad-level math (the AIMO prize) needs long, consistent reasoning, and models spend many tokens getting there.
Method
A 387K-problem dataset (OpenR1-Math-220K 55%, 150K newly generated 37.5%, Bespoke-Stratos-17K 7.5%; a GPT-4o-mini judge rejected about 15% of generated solutions). Chain-of-thought distillation from DeepSeek R1 into Qwen2.5 with a KL + cross-entropy loss and a falling temperature. GRPO with KL penalty beta = 0 (no reference model), reward 1.0 for correctness + 0.2 for format, DeepSpeed ZeRO-3 and colocated vLLM, scaled to 32B. At test time, several solvers, self-verification, and a (preference) reward model reranks candidates.
Results
AIME'24: 32B GRPO model 76.7% with 5,048 tokens per problem vs. 32K for baselines (reported as a 6.3x reduction); 79.9% with multi-agent reranking over 4 passes. Ablation: base 50.0%, distillation only 72.1% (+22.1), + GRPO 76.7% (+4.6), + reranking 79.9% (+3.2). Baselines: Qwen2.5-32B Instruct 26.7%, Qwen2.5-Math-72B 30.0% (40.0% with tool-integrated reasoning), R1-Distill-Qwen-32B 73.3%. Qwen2.5-1.5B GRPO 10.0%, 7B GRPO 16.7%; their 7B pipeline 65.2% (5,137 tokens) vs. 58.7% baseline. 2.1x faster inference. Context window had to grow from 8,192 to 16,384 tokens after about 320 GRPO steps on 7B. Reward-model selection beat majority voting at every size (e.g., Qwen2.5-Math-7B 70.0% rm@64 vs. 43.3% maj@64). Note: AIME'24 has 30 problems, and 79.9% is not a multiple of 1/30; the paper does not explain this.
Lessons
Most of the gain came from distillation (good data from a stronger model), not RL; RL then mostly bought token efficiency. Filter distillation data with a checker, including the steps, not just the final answer. A verifier that scores candidates beats majority voting when the right answer is rare. Setting the KL penalty to 0 saves a whole model's memory and worked here. Watch for truncated completions during RL.
Our rebuild
Seeded word problems from lal.tasks. Distillation data: llama3.1:8b samples 2 rationales per problem; we keep only those with the right answer and no wrong calculation, and choose the shortest, writing a ready-to-train SFT JSONL. Reranking: llama3.2:3b writes 4 solutions per test problem; we compare first sample, majority vote, a code step-checker used as a process reward (min over steps), an LLM judge that names the first wrong step, and the pass@4 oracle. Behind RUN_LONG: GRPO with TRL on SmolLM2-135M-Instruct using the paper's 1.0 + 0.2 reward and beta = 0 (fp32 on T4), and SFT on the distilled file.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

The original project

Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models through Reasoning Distillation, GRPO, and Multi-agent PRM Reranking by Abhinav Agarwal, Shree Reddy, Carlo Baronio and Shubhra Mishra (Stanford CS329A, Winter 2025). Paper · Poster · code: github.com/abhinav30219/math_reasoner

Problem. Olympiad-level math (the AIMO prize) needs long, consistent reasoning, and today's models spend a lot of tokens getting there.

Method. 1. Data: 387K problems: OpenR1-Math-220K (55%), 150K newly generated (37.5%), Bespoke-Stratos-17K (7.5%). A GPT-4o-mini judge plus format checks rejected about 15% of generated solutions. 2. Distillation: DeepSeek R1 as teacher, Qwen2.5 as student, loss L = α·KL(teacher‖student) + (1−α)·CE, with a temperature that starts high and drops, and extra weight on reasoning tokens. 3. GRPO (Shao et al. 2024, DeepSeekMath): sample a group of answers per problem and use the group's average reward as the baseline, so no separate value network is needed. Their key choice: KL penalty β = 0, so the reference model can be dropped from memory. Reward = 1.0 for a correct answer + 0.2 for the right format. 4. Multi-agent "PRM" reranking: several solvers with different settings, self-verification, then a reward model ranks the candidates and the best one is kept. (In this paper PRM stands for Preference Reward Model.)

Key results (as reported, AIME'24). - 32B model after GRPO: 76.7% with 5,048 tokens per problem, vs. 32K tokens for baselines (a "6.3× reduction"). - With multi-agent reranking over 4 inference passes: 79.9%. - Ablation: base model 50.0% → distillation only 72.1% (+22.1) → + GRPO 76.7% (+4.6) → + reranking 79.9% (+3.2). - Baselines: Qwen2.5-32B Instruct 26.7%, Qwen2.5-Math-72B 30.0% (40.0% with tool-integrated reasoning), R1-Distill-Qwen-32B 73.3%. - Scale: Qwen2.5-1.5B GRPO 10.0%, 7B GRPO 16.7%. Their 7B pipeline: 65.2% with 5,137 tokens vs. 58.7% baseline. - 2.1× faster inference. After about 320 GRPO steps on 7B, truncated answers hurt accuracy until they raised the context window from 8,192 to 16,384 tokens. - Reward-model selection beat majority voting at every size (for example Qwen2.5-Math-7B: 70.0% rm@64 vs. 43.3% maj@64).

Read with care (our note). AIME'24 has 30 problems, so scores move in steps of 3.3 points. 79.9% is not a multiple of 1/30; it may be an average over runs, but the paper doesn't say. Also, some baselines (such as R1-Distill) are known to be reasoning models given a 32K budget, so the token comparison mixes model types. The poster adds a neat fact: with β = 0, one gradient step per batch and normalized advantages, the GRPO loss is always 0 while the gradient is not.

Our mini version. This repo does not use Qwen or DeepSeek models, so: llama3.1:8b is the teacher, llama3.2:3b is the student at test time, and SmolLM2-135M-Instruct is the model we train with GRPO (optional). Problems come from lal.tasks (generated with a seed), so every answer is exact.

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 2
import ast, json, random, re
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from pathlib import Path
from typing import List
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.checks import check, pass_at_k

RUN_LONG = False          # True: also run the GRPO training (about 10 minutes on a T4)
TEACHER, STUDENT = MODEL, "llama3.2:3b"           # MODEL is llama3.1:8b on a GPU runtime

1. Problems with exact answers

We reuse the repo's seeded word problems (lal.tasks): "easy" ones take 2 steps, "hard" ones 3–5 steps. Training (for the teacher) and test (for the student) mix both.

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 3
from lal.tasks import math_problems, extract_int
to_pairs = lambda ps: [(p["question"], p["answer"]) for p in ps]
TRAIN = to_pairs(math_problems(5, seed=31, difficulty="easy") + math_problems(5, seed=31, difficulty="hard"))
TEST = to_pairs(math_problems(3, seed=32, difficulty="easy") + math_problems(3, seed=32, difficulty="hard"))
for q, a in TRAIN[4:7]:
    print(a, "←", q)

A step checker made of code: it recomputes every a op b = c it finds in a step. We use it twice: to filter teacher data, and later as a process reward for reranking.

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 4
def safe_eval(expr):
    node = ast.parse(expr.replace("×", "*").replace("÷", "/").replace("−", "-").strip(), mode="eval")
    def ev(n):
        if isinstance(n, ast.Expression): return ev(n.body)
        if isinstance(n, ast.Constant) and isinstance(n.value, (int, float)): return float(n.value)
        if isinstance(n, ast.UnaryOp) and isinstance(n.op, ast.USub): return -ev(n.operand)
        if isinstance(n, ast.BinOp) and isinstance(n.op, (ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult, ast.Div)):
            a, b = ev(n.left), ev(n.right)
            return {ast.Add: a + b, ast.Sub: a - b, ast.Mult: a * b, ast.Div: a / b if b else float("nan")}[type(n.op)]
        raise ValueError("unsupported")
    return ev(node)

CALC = re.compile(r"([\d\.\s\+\-\*/×÷\(\)]*\d[\d\.\s\+\-\*/×÷\(\)]*)=\s*(-?\d+(?:\.\d+)?)")

def step_scores(solution):
    """One score per step: 1.0 if every calculation in it is right, 0.0 if any is wrong."""
    scores = []
    for line in (solution or "").replace(",", "").replace("$", "").splitlines():
        if not re.match(r"\s*\**step", line, re.I):
            continue
        ok = True
        for left, right in CALC.findall(line):
            if not re.search(r"[\+\-\*/×÷]", left.strip().lstrip("-")):
                continue                                   # "x = 5" is not a calculation
            try:
                ok &= abs(safe_eval(left) - float(right)) < 0.01
            except Exception:
                pass                                       # can't parse: don't punish
        scores.append(1.0 if ok else 0.0)
    return scores

print(step_scores("Step 1: 12 * 7 = 84\nStep 2: 84 - 9 = 76\nStep 3: 76 / 4 = 19\nANSWER: 19"))   # step 2 is wrong

2. Stage 1: reasoning distillation data

The real recipe trains the student on the teacher's full reasoning. The data step is what we can do here: sample 2 rationales per problem from the teacher, keep only the correct ones (rejection sampling, like the paper's ~15% reject rate). "Correct" means the right final answer and no wrong calculation in any step: a right answer reached through a wrong step teaches the student bad habits. Among the correct ones keep the shortest. That last step pushes toward the token efficiency the paper reports. The output is a ready-to-train SFT file (20 calls).

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 5
STEP_PROMPT = ("Solve the problem step by step. Put each step on its own line as 'Step k: ...' and write every "
               "calculation as 'a op b = c'. End with a line 'ANSWER: <integer>'.\n\nProblem: {q}")

def sample(q, model, temperature):
    try:
        return ask(STEP_PROMPT.format(q=q), model_name=model, temperature=temperature, max_tokens=500)
    except Exception as e:
        print("call failed:", e); return ""

jobs = [(q, a) for q, a in TRAIN for _ in range(2)]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    teacher_out = list(ex.map(lambda qa: sample(qa[0], TEACHER, 0.7), jobs))

by_q = {}
for (q, a), out in zip(jobs, teacher_out):
    by_q.setdefault(q, []).append((extract_int(out) == a, out))
sft, lucky = [], 0
for q, cands in by_q.items():
    # Right answer AND no wrong calculation in any step (the step checker is defined in section 3).
    lucky += sum(ok and 0.0 in step_scores(o) for ok, o in cands)
    good = sorted((o for ok, o in cands if ok and 0.0 not in step_scores(o)), key=len)
    if good:
        sft.append({"prompt": [{"role": "user", "content": STEP_PROMPT.format(q=q)}],
                    "completion": [{"role": "assistant", "content": good[0].strip()}]})
n_ok = sum(ok for c in by_q.values() for ok, _ in c)
avg_all = sum(len(o) for o in teacher_out) / max(len(teacher_out), 1)
avg_kept = sum(len(r["completion"][0]["content"]) for r in sft) / max(len(sft), 1)
print(f"teacher correct: {n_ok}/{len(jobs)} (rejected {1 - n_ok / len(jobs):.0%})")
print(f"right answer but a wrong step inside (also rejected): {lucky}")
print(f"problems with at least one correct rationale: {len(sft)}/{len(TRAIN)}")
print(f"avg characters: all samples {avg_all:.0f} → kept (shortest correct) {avg_kept:.0f}")
DATA = Path("cs329a_projects/ap3_distill.jsonl"); DATA.parent.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
DATA.write_text("\n".join(json.dumps(r) for r in sft))
print("wrote", DATA, "\n\nexample:\n", sft[0]["completion"][0]["content"][:400] if sft else "(none)")

3. Stage 3: sample N answers, then rerank with step scores

The student (llama3.2:3b) writes N = 4 solutions per test problem (24 calls). We compare five ways to pick one: - first sample: what a single call gives you; - majority vote (self-consistency, Wang et al. 2023); - code step-checker: a process reward made by code. Every a op b = c in a step is recomputed; a step scores 1 if all its calculations are right. A solution's score is its weakest step (min), as in Lightman et al. 2023, Let's Verify Step by Step; - LLM judge: the 8B model reads all 4 solutions and names the first wrong step in each (1 call per problem); - oracle (pass@4): is any of the 4 right? This is the ceiling for any reranker.

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 6
N = 4
with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    cand = list(ex.map(lambda qi: sample(TEST[qi[0]][0], STUDENT, 0.8), [(i, j) for i in range(len(TEST)) for j in range(N)]))
CANDS = [cand[i * N:(i + 1) * N] for i in range(len(TEST))]
ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 7
class Verdict(BaseModel):
    solution: int
    first_wrong_step: int = Field(description="number of the first wrong step, or 0 if all steps are right")

class Verdicts(BaseModel):
    verdicts: List[Verdict]

def llm_judge(q, sols):
    text = "\n\n".join(f"=== Solution {i + 1} ===\n{s[:1200]}" for i, s in enumerate(sols))
    try:
        v = structured(f"Problem: {q}\n\n{text}\n\nCheck every step of every solution. For each solution, give the "
                       "number of the first wrong step (0 if all steps are right).", Verdicts, model_name=TEACHER, max_tokens=400)
        got = {x.solution: x.first_wrong_step for x in v.verdicts}
    except Exception:
        got = {}
    return [1.0 if got.get(i + 1, 1) == 0 else 0.0 for i in range(len(sols))]

with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
    JUDGE = list(ex.map(lambda i: llm_judge(TEST[i][0], CANDS[i]), range(len(TEST))))

def pick(answers, scores):
    """Highest score wins; ties go to the most common answer among the tied."""
    best = max(scores)
    tied = [a for a, s in zip(answers, scores) if s == best and a is not None]
    return Counter(tied).most_common(1)[0][0] if tied else answers[0]

def evaluate(cands, judge, agg=min):
    res = Counter()
    for (q, gold), sols, js in zip(TEST, cands, judge):
        ans = [extract_int(s) for s in sols]
        prm = [agg(step_scores(s)) if step_scores(s) else 0.5 for s in sols]
        votes = Counter(a for a in ans if a is not None)
        res["first sample"] += ans[0] == gold
        res["majority vote"] += (votes.most_common(1)[0][0] if votes else None) == gold
        res["code step-checker"] += pick(ans, prm) == gold
        res["LLM judge"] += pick(ans, js) == gold
        res["oracle (pass@N)"] += gold in ans
    return {k: v / len(TEST) for k, v in res.items()}

ACC = evaluate(CANDS, JUDGE)
for k, v in ACC.items():
    print(f"{k:18s} {v:.0%}")
correct = [sum(extract_int(s) == g for s in sols) for (q, g), sols in zip(TEST, CANDS)]
print("unbiased pass@1 over all samples:", f"{sum(pass_at_k(N, c, 1) for c in correct) / len(TEST):.0%}")

What to notice. The gap between first sample and oracle is the room a reranker has. Majority vote needs the right answer to be common; a step checker can rescue a right answer that appears only once. The paper's reranker added +3.2 points on top of a much stronger model; on easy problems with a weak model, the gap here can be bigger.

4. Stage 2 (optional, RUN_LONG): GRPO on a tiny model

GRPO samples a group of answers per prompt (here 8), scores them, and pushes up the ones above the group average. We use the paper's reward weights (1.0 correct + 0.2 format) and its β = 0 (no KL penalty, no reference model). The task is easy on purpose (two-number sums) so a 135M model can visibly learn in a few minutes. The T4 has no bf16, so we train in fp32. TRL API checked against the current GRPOTrainer docs.

ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 8
SMALL = "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct"
grng = random.Random(7)
def sum_prompt():
    a, b = grng.randint(10, 99), grng.randint(10, 99)
    return {"prompt": [{"role": "user", "content": f"What is {a} + {b}? Reply with only the number inside <answer></answer>."}],
            "answer": str(a + b)}

def _text(c):
    return c[0]["content"] if isinstance(c, list) else str(c)

def correctness_reward(completions, answer, **kwargs):
    out = []
    for c, a in zip(completions, answer):
        m = re.search(r"<answer>\s*(-?\d+)\s*</answer>", _text(c))
        out.append(1.0 if m and m.group(1) == a else 0.0)
    return out

def format_reward(completions, **kwargs):
    return [0.2 if re.search(r"<answer>.*?</answer>", _text(c), re.S) else 0.0 for c in completions]

print(correctness_reward([[{"content": "<answer>42</answer>"}]], ["42"]), format_reward(["<answer>x</answer>"]))

GRPO_LOG = {}
if RUN_LONG:
    import torch
    from datasets import Dataset
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
    from trl import GRPOConfig, GRPOTrainer

    train_ds = Dataset.from_list([sum_prompt() for _ in range(400)])
    eval_rows = [sum_prompt() for _ in range(40)]
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(SMALL); tok.padding_side = "left"

    def mean_reward(model):
        model.eval(); total = 0.0
        for r in eval_rows:
            text = tok.apply_chat_template(r["prompt"], tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)
            ids = tok(text, return_tensors="pt").to(model.device)
            with torch.no_grad():
                out = model.generate(**ids, max_new_tokens=32, do_sample=False, pad_token_id=tok.eos_token_id)
            comp = tok.decode(out[0][ids["input_ids"].shape[1]:], skip_special_tokens=True)
            total += correctness_reward([comp], [r["answer"]])[0] + format_reward([comp])[0]
        return total / len(eval_rows)

    base = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(SMALL, dtype=torch.float32).to("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")   # the config says bf16; a T4 needs fp32
    GRPO_LOG["before"] = mean_reward(base)
    args = GRPOConfig(output_dir="/tmp/ap3_grpo", per_device_train_batch_size=16, num_generations=8,
                      max_completion_length=48, learning_rate=1e-5, beta=0.0, max_steps=80, logging_steps=10,
                      fp16=False, bf16=False, gradient_checkpointing=False, report_to="none", save_strategy="no")
    trainer = GRPOTrainer(model=base, reward_funcs=[correctness_reward, format_reward], args=args,
                          train_dataset=train_ds, processing_class=tok)
    trainer.train()
    GRPO_LOG["after"] = mean_reward(trainer.model)
    GRPO_LOG["history"] = [{k: h[k] for k in h if k in ("step", "reward", "loss", "completions/mean_length")} for h in trainer.state.log_history]
    for h in GRPO_LOG["history"]:
        print(h)
    print(f"mean reward (max 1.2): before {GRPO_LOG['before']:.2f} → after {GRPO_LOG['after']:.2f}")
else:
    print("RUN_LONG is False: skipping GRPO training.")
ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 9
check("distillation kept only correct rationales", all(extract_int(r["completion"][0]["content"]) == dict(TRAIN)[r["prompt"][0]["content"].split("Problem: ")[1]] for r in sft))
check("the teacher got at least 3 of 10 problems right", len(sft) >= 3)
check("code step-checker catches a wrong step", step_scores("Step 1: 2 + 2 = 5") == [0.0] and step_scores("Step 1: 2 + 2 = 4") == [1.0])
check("some reranker gets at least 2 of 6 test problems", max(ACC.values()) >= 2 / 6)
if RUN_LONG:
    check("GRPO raised the mean reward", GRPO_LOG["after"] > GRPO_LOG["before"])

Your turn

  1. Fewer samples. Rerank using only the first 2 of the 4 candidates. How much of the gain is left? (No new calls.)
  2. Min vs. mean. Score each solution by the average step score instead of the weakest step. Which picks better here, and why might min be safer?
  3. Close the loop (RUN_LONG). Fine-tune SmolLM2-135M-Instruct on ap3_distill.jsonl with TRL's SFTTrainer, then ask it one test problem. With 10 examples it won't get good at math; the point is to see the full pipeline run.
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 10
# 1. N = 2. Notice the oracle drops too: reranking can only pick from what was sampled.
two = evaluate([c[:2] for c in CANDS], [j[:2] for j in JUDGE])
for k in ACC:
    print(f"{k:18s} N=4 {ACC[k]:.0%}   N=2 {two[k]:.0%}")
ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 11
# 2. Mean aggregation. Notice: mean lets a long solution with one wrong step outscore a short correct one;
#    min treats any wrong step as fatal, which matches "one wrong step ruins the answer".
mean_acc = evaluate(CANDS, JUDGE, agg=lambda s: sum(s) / len(s))
print("code step-checker  min:", f"{ACC['code step-checker']:.0%}", "  mean:", f"{mean_acc['code step-checker']:.0%}")
ap3_math_reasoning.ipynb · cell 12
# 3. SFT on the distilled data (RUN_LONG only). prompt/completion conversational rows are supported by SFTTrainer.
if RUN_LONG and sft:
    from datasets import Dataset
    from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer
    sft_trainer = SFTTrainer(model=SMALL, train_dataset=Dataset.from_list(sft),
                             args=SFTConfig(output_dir="/tmp/ap3_sft", max_steps=30, per_device_train_batch_size=2,
                                            learning_rate=2e-5, logging_steps=5, fp16=False, bf16=False,
                                            report_to="none", save_strategy="no"))
    sft_trainer.train()
    m, t = sft_trainer.model, sft_trainer.processing_class
    text = t.apply_chat_template([{"role": "user", "content": STEP_PROMPT.format(q=TEST[0][0])}], tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)
    ids = t(text, return_tensors="pt").to(m.device)
    print(t.decode(m.generate(**ids, max_new_tokens=150, do_sample=False)[0][ids["input_ids"].shape[1]:], skip_special_tokens=True))
    print("gold:", TEST[0][1])
else:
    print("Set RUN_LONG = True to run the SFT step.")
Past project · AP4

Towards Effective Meta-Agents: Designing Agents that Design Agents (paper: Inefficiencies of Meta Agents for Agent Design)

Open in Colab

CS329A Winter 2025 · Batu El, Mert Yuksekgonul, James Zou

Paper · Poster

Problem
Meta-agents such as ADAS write new agents in a sample-evaluate-iterate loop. Do they learn from past designs, are the designs diverse enough to combine, and is the design cost ever worth it?
Method
Archive starts with 7 hand-written agents (Chain-of-Thought, Majority Voting, Refinement from Feedback, LLM Debate, Quality-Diversity, Routing, Step-back). A gpt-4o meta-agent writes agent code run on gpt-3.5, for 30 iterations, with three context strategies: cumulative (all past agents, as in ADAS), parallel (only the initial library), evolutionary (top 7 so far). MGSM, DROP, MMLU (128 train / 200 test) and GPQA (32 / 160), 3 runs. Diversity via cosine similarity of per-question score vectors and coverage; economics via total cost C0 + n*Cj.
Results
Best agent's train score, averaged over datasets: cumulative 55.0, parallel 60.0, evolutionary 60.3 (cumulative did not beat parallel). Evolutionary up to +10 points over cumulative on MGSM (41.4 vs. 56.5 train). Test score of the best-on-train agent, average: initial library 49.0, cumulative 52.2, parallel 55.9, evolutionary 55.2. Coverage average: cumulative 94.2%, parallel 95.9%, evolutionary 94.4%; evolutionary agents were the least diverse. Designed agents cost more per query than the initial ones (cumulative > evolutionary > parallel > initial). The designed agent becomes cheaper per correct answer only after about 15,000 queries, and only on MMLU and DROP with parallel context; elsewhere never.
Lessons
Before building a self-improving agent designer, do the cost arithmetic: design cost plus higher per-query cost often never pays back. Showing the meta-agent everything it has tried can be worse than showing nothing; curate the context (best parents). Designed agents tend to be similar, so 'pick the best per query' gains are limited unless you push for diversity. Meta-evaluation is noisy; use several runs.
Our rebuild
Six kinds of checkable puzzles (letter counts, reversing, sorting, weekdays, word problems, decimal comparison). Designs are data (system prompt + one of four fixed procedures), so no model-written code runs. llama3.1:8b is the meta-agent, llama3.2:3b runs agents. Starting library of 3, evolutionary context for 3 iterations, archive with score vectors, train/test comparison, cosine similarity and coverage, a break-even calculation with C0 + n*Cj, and in the solutions a parallel-context run (RUN_LONG), a per-kind router and a pricing what-if.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in ap4_meta_agents.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

The original project

Listed on the course page as Towards Effective Meta-Agents: Designing Agents that Design Agents. The linked paper is titled Inefficiencies of Meta Agents for Agent Design, by Batu El, Mert Yuksekgonul and James Zou (Stanford); the poster is titled Exploring Two Open Questions in Meta-Agent Design. Stanford CS329A, Winter 2025. Paper · Poster

Problem. Meta-agents such as ADAS (Hu et al. 2024, Automated Design of Agentic Systems) follow a sample–evaluate–iterate loop. Is that loop efficient?

Method. An agent is code that takes a question and calls a model. The archive starts with 7 hand-written agents: Chain-of-Thought, Majority Voting, Refinement from Feedback, LLM Debate, Quality-Diversity, Routing, and Step-back. Each round, the meta-agent (gpt-4o) writes a new agent conditioned on a chosen part of the archive; agents run on gpt-3.5. Three ways to choose that context: - Cumulative: show all previous agents (what ADAS does). - Parallel: show only the 7 starting agents, so every sample is independent. - Evolutionary: show the 7 best agents so far (the "parents").

Setup. MGSM, DROP, MMLU (128 train / 200 test each) and GPQA (32 train / 160 test), 30 iterations, 3 runs.

Key results (as reported). - Best agent's train score, averaged over datasets: cumulative 55.0, parallel 60.0, evolutionary 60.3. "Cumulative context curation does not outperform parallel", that is, showing all past designs was worse than showing none. Evolutionary gave up to +10 points over cumulative on MGSM (41.4 → 56.5 train). - Test score of the best-on-train agent, average: initial library 49.0, cumulative 52.2, parallel 55.9, evolutionary 55.2. - Diversity: designed agents get many of the same questions right. Evolutionary agents are the most similar; parallel gives the most coverage (questions solved by at least one agent): 95.9% on average vs. 94.2 cumulative, 94.4 evolutionary. - Cost: designed agents cost more per query than the starting agents (cumulative > evolutionary > parallel > initial). Counting the design cost, the designed agent is cheaper per correct answer only after about 15,000 queries, and only on MMLU and DROP with parallel context. On the other datasets it never breaks even.

Limitations they state. Only this one class of meta-agent; accuracy/F1 only; cost analysis fits tasks with strong verifiers; cosine similarity favors high-scoring agents; only 3 runs because each run is expensive.

Our mini version. Designs are data (a system prompt plus one of four fixed procedures), not free-form code, so nothing the model writes is ever executed. llama3.1:8b plays the meta-agent and llama3.2:3b runs the agents, mirroring their strong-designer / cheap-runner split. We use evolutionary context, measure diversity and coverage, and compute the break-even point with the paper's formula: total cost = C0 + n·Cj.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 2
import json, math, random, re
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from typing import Literal
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.checks import check

RUN_LONG = False                     # True: also run the "parallel" context strategy for comparison (~40 more calls)
DESIGNER, RUNNER = MODEL, "llama3.2:3b"          # MODEL is llama3.1:8b on a GPU runtime
T = 3                                # meta-agent iterations (the paper used 30)

1. A small, mixed task set with exact checkers

Six kinds of questions where small models stumble in different ways: counting letters, reversing words, sorting, weekday arithmetic, word problems, and comparing decimals. Mixed kinds give designs a chance to specialize.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 3
WORDS = ["strawberry", "banana", "committee", "bookkeeper", "mississippi", "parallel", "assessment", "balloon", "occurrence"]
DAYS = ["Monday", "Tuesday", "Wednesday", "Thursday", "Friday", "Saturday", "Sunday"]
KINDS = ["count", "reverse", "sort", "weekday", "math", "compare"]

def make_task(rng, kind):
    if kind == "count":
        w = rng.choice(WORDS); ch = rng.choice(sorted(set(w)))
        return dict(kind=kind, q=f"How many times does the letter '{ch}' appear in the word '{w}'?", a=str(w.count(ch)))
    if kind == "reverse":
        w = rng.choice(WORDS)
        return dict(kind=kind, q=f"Write the word '{w}' backwards (its letters in reverse order).", a=w[::-1])
    if kind == "sort":
        nums = rng.sample(range(1, 99), 6)
        return dict(kind=kind, q="Sort these numbers from smallest to largest: " + ", ".join(map(str, nums)), a=",".join(map(str, sorted(nums))))
    if kind == "weekday":
        s, k = rng.randrange(7), rng.randint(9, 60)
        return dict(kind=kind, q=f"Today is {DAYS[s]}. What day of the week will it be in {k} days?", a=DAYS[(s + k) % 7].lower())
    if kind == "math":
        a, b = rng.randint(6, 24), rng.randint(3, 12); c = rng.randint(5, a * b - 1)
        return dict(kind=kind, q=f"A crate holds {a} bottles. A shop gets {b} crates and sells {c} bottles. How many bottles are left?", a=str(a * b - c))
    x = rng.randint(1, 9); p, q = f"{x}.{rng.randint(10, 99)}", f"{x}.{rng.randint(1, 9)}"
    return dict(kind=kind, q=f"Which number is larger: {p} or {q}? Answer with the number.", a=max(p, q, key=float))

rng = random.Random(42)
TRAIN = [make_task(rng, k) for k in KINDS]
TEST = [make_task(rng, k) for k in KINDS[:4]]      # 4 test tasks keeps the notebook within ~80 model calls

def final_answer(text):
    m = re.findall(r"ANSWER:\s*(.+)", text or "", re.I)
    if m:
        return m[-1].strip()
    lines = (text or "").strip().splitlines()
    return lines[-1] if lines else ""

def correct(task, text):
    got = final_answer(text or "")
    if task["kind"] == "sort":
        return re.findall(r"\d+", got) == task["a"].split(",")
    if task["kind"] in ("reverse", "weekday"):
        return re.sub(r"[^a-z]", "", got.lower()) == task["a"]
    nums = re.findall(r"-?\d+(?:\.\d+)?", got)
    return bool(nums) and abs(float(nums[0]) - float(task["a"])) < 1e-9

for t in TRAIN:
    print(f"{t['kind']:8s} {t['a']:>20s}  ← {t['q']}")
print(correct(TRAIN[0], f"so... ANSWER: {TRAIN[0]['a']}"), correct(TRAIN[2], "ANSWER: 1, 2"))

2. What an "agent design" is here, and the interpreter that runs it

A design is a name, a one-line idea, a system prompt, and a procedure from a fixed menu. The interpreter counts calls and characters, so we can do cost accounting later (tokens ≈ characters / 4).

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 4
class Design(BaseModel):
    name: str
    idea: str = Field(description="one or two sentences: why this design should work")
    system_prompt: str = Field(description="instructions for the agent, under 120 words")
    procedure: Literal["direct", "think_step_by_step", "solve_then_check", "plan_then_solve"]

FMT = "\nEnd with one line: ANSWER: <your final answer>"

def run_agent(d: Design, q: str):
    """Returns (text, calls, chars)."""
    sp = d.system_prompt[:1200]
    chars, calls = 0, 0
    def call(prompt):
        nonlocal chars, calls
        try:
            out = ask(prompt, system=sp, model_name=RUNNER, temperature=0, max_tokens=600)
        except Exception as e:
            out = ""
        chars += len(sp) + len(prompt) + len(out); calls += 1
        return out
    if d.procedure == "direct":
        out = call(q + FMT)
    elif d.procedure == "think_step_by_step":
        out = call(q + "\nThink step by step." + FMT)
    elif d.procedure == "solve_then_check":
        first = call(q + "\nThink step by step." + FMT)
        out = call(f"Question: {q}\nA first attempt:\n{first[-1500:]}\n\nCheck this attempt carefully, step by step. "
                   "If it is wrong, fix it." + FMT)
    else:  # plan_then_solve
        plan = call(f"Question: {q}\nWrite a short numbered plan for solving it. Do not solve it yet.")
        out = call(f"Question: {q}\nPlan:\n{plan[-1200:]}\nNow follow the plan." + FMT)
    return out, calls, chars

def evaluate(d: Design, tasks):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        runs = list(ex.map(lambda t: run_agent(d, t["q"]), tasks))
    vec = [1.0 if correct(t, out) else 0.0 for t, (out, _, _) in zip(tasks, runs)]
    return {"vec": vec, "score": sum(vec) / len(vec), "calls": sum(r[1] for r in runs), "chars": sum(r[2] for r in runs),
            "fails": [(t["q"], final_answer(out)[:60], t["a"]) for t, (out, _, _), v in zip(tasks, runs, vec) if not v]}

3. The starting library: three hand-written agents

Like the paper's initial library (Chain-of-Thought, self-refine, and so on), but only three, to save calls.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 5
LIBRARY = [
    Design(name="Direct", idea="Just answer.", system_prompt="You are a careful assistant.", procedure="direct"),
    Design(name="Chain-of-Thought", idea="Reason before answering (Wei et al. 2022).",
           system_prompt="You are a careful assistant. Reason step by step before you answer.", procedure="think_step_by_step"),
    Design(name="Self-Refine", idea="Answer, then check and fix the answer (Madaan et al. 2023).",
           system_prompt="You are a careful assistant who double-checks work.", procedure="solve_then_check"),
]
ARCHIVE = []
for d in LIBRARY:
    r = evaluate(d, TRAIN)
    ARCHIVE.append({"design": d, "origin": "initial", **r})
    print(f"{d.name:18s} train {r['score']:.0%}  calls {r['calls']}")

4. The meta-agent loop: sample → evaluate → add to archive

select_context is the paper's φ. Evolutionary shows the best 3 designs so far with their scores and a few of their mistakes. Parallel shows only the starting library. Cumulative shows everything.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 6
def select_context(archive, phi):
    if phi == "parallel":
        return [a for a in archive if a["origin"] == "initial"]
    if phi == "cumulative":
        return list(archive)
    return sorted(archive, key=lambda a: (a["score"], -a["chars"]), reverse=True)[:3]      # evolutionary: top-k parents

def describe(a):
    d = a["design"]
    fails = "; ".join(f"Q: {q[:70]} | gave: {g} | correct: {c}" for q, g, c in a["fails"][:3]) or "none"
    return (f"- name: {d.name}\n  idea: {d.idea}\n  procedure: {d.procedure}\n  system_prompt: {d.system_prompt}\n"
            f"  train score: {a['score']:.0%}\n  mistakes: {fails}")

META = ("You design AI agents. An agent is a system prompt plus one procedure:\n"
        "- direct: one call, answer immediately\n- think_step_by_step: one call with step-by-step reasoning\n"
        "- solve_then_check: two calls, solve then verify and fix\n- plan_then_solve: two calls, write a plan then follow it\n\n"
        "The agents answer short puzzles: counting letters, reversing words, sorting numbers, weekday arithmetic, "
        "word problems, comparing decimals.\n\nHere are existing agents and how they did:\n{ctx}\n\n"
        "Design ONE new agent that will score higher. Learn from the mistakes. Give concrete, task-specific tactics "
        "in the system prompt (for example: spell the word letter by letter). Use a new name.")

def meta_search(archive, phi, T):
    archive = list(archive)
    for t in range(T):
        ctx = "\n".join(describe(a) for a in select_context(archive, phi))
        try:
            d = structured(META.format(ctx=ctx), Design, model_name=DESIGNER, temperature=0.8, max_tokens=700)
        except Exception as e:
            print("meta-agent output was invalid:", e); continue
        r = evaluate(d, TRAIN)
        archive.append({"design": d, "origin": f"{phi} #{t + 1}", "meta_chars": len(ctx) + len(META) + len(d.model_dump_json()), **r})
        print(f"[{phi} {t + 1}] {d.name[:30]:30s} {d.procedure:18s} train {r['score']:.0%}")
    return archive

ARCHIVE = meta_search(ARCHIVE, "evolutionary", T)

5. Question 1: did search beat the starting library?

Pick the best design on the training set, then run it once on the held-out test set, next to the best starting agent.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 7
best_init = max([a for a in ARCHIVE if a["origin"] == "initial"], key=lambda a: a["score"])
designed = [a for a in ARCHIVE if a["origin"] != "initial"]
best_new = max(designed, key=lambda a: a["score"]) if designed else best_init
test_init = evaluate(best_init["design"], TEST)
test_new = evaluate(best_new["design"], TEST) if best_new is not best_init else test_init
print(f"best starting agent : {best_init['design'].name:30s} train {best_init['score']:.0%}  test {test_init['score']:.0%}")
print(f"best designed agent : {best_new['design'].name:30s} train {best_new['score']:.0%}  test {test_new['score']:.0%}")
print("\nwinning design:\n", best_new["design"].model_dump_json(indent=2))

6. Question 2: are the agents different enough to combine?

Each agent's row of right/wrong answers is its "embedding". Cosine similarity near 1 means two agents solve the same questions. Coverage is the share of questions that at least one agent solves: the ceiling for a perfect router.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 8
def cosine(u, v):
    nu, nv = math.sqrt(sum(x * x for x in u)), math.sqrt(sum(x * x for x in v))
    return sum(x * y for x, y in zip(u, v)) / (nu * nv) if nu and nv else 0.0

vecs = [a["vec"] for a in ARCHIVE if any(a["vec"])]
pairs = [cosine(vecs[i], vecs[j]) for i in range(len(vecs)) for j in range(i + 1, len(vecs))]
coverage = sum(any(a["vec"][i] for a in ARCHIVE) for i in range(len(TRAIN))) / len(TRAIN)
print("agent  " + "  ".join(t["kind"][:5] for t in TRAIN))
for a in ARCHIVE:
    print(f"{a['design'].name[:22]:22s} " + "  ".join("  ✓  " if x else "  ·  " for x in a["vec"]))
print(f"mean pairwise similarity {sum(pairs) / max(len(pairs), 1):.2f}   coverage {coverage:.0%}   best single agent {max(a['score'] for a in ARCHIVE):.0%}")

7. Question 3: does the design cost ever pay off?

Cost in tokens (≈ characters / 4). C0 = everything spent to find the new agent: meta-agent prompts plus evaluating every designed agent on the training set. Cj = cost per query when deployed. After n queries: cost per correct answer = (C0 + n·Cj) / (n·accuracy). The starting agent has C0 = 0. The break-even n solves C0/(n·p_new) + C_new/p_new = C_init/p_init.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 9
tok = lambda chars: chars / 4
C0 = sum(tok(a["chars"] + a.get("meta_chars", 0)) for a in designed)
c_init, c_new = tok(test_init["chars"]) / len(TEST), tok(test_new["chars"]) / len(TEST)
p_init, p_new = max(test_init["score"], 1e-9), max(test_new["score"], 1e-9)
gap = c_init / p_init - c_new / p_new
breakeven = C0 / (p_new * gap) if gap > 0 and best_new is not best_init else float("inf")
print(f"design cost C0 ≈ {C0:,.0f} tokens")
print(f"per query: starting agent {c_init:,.0f} tokens at {p_init:.0%}; designed agent {c_new:,.0f} tokens at {p_new:.0%}")
print("break-even after", "never (the designed agent is not cheaper per correct answer)" if math.isinf(breakeven) else f"{breakeven:,.0f} queries")

What to notice. On a handful of tasks, a lucky design can look great on train and slip on test: that's the paper's warning about noisy meta-evaluation, and why they averaged 3 runs. Two-call designs often win on accuracy but double the per-query cost, so the break-even point can be "never". Before you build a meta-agent, do this arithmetic.

ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 10
check("archive holds the 3 starting agents plus designed ones", len(ARCHIVE) >= 3 + 1)
check("the best designed agent is no worse than the best starting agent minus one task", best_new["score"] >= best_init["score"] - 1 / len(TRAIN))
check("the best starting agent solves at least 2 of 6 training tasks", best_init["score"] >= 2 / 6)

Your turn

  1. Parallel vs. evolutionary. Set RUN_LONG = True and run meta_search with phi="parallel". The paper found parallel about as good as evolutionary and more diverse. What do you see? (Three iterations is very noisy.)
  2. A router from the archive. Each task has a kind. For each kind, pick the agent that did best on that kind in training. What is this router's training score, compared with the best single agent and with coverage? (No new calls.)
  3. Your own prices. Redo the break-even with real numbers: a designer token costs 10× a runner token. Does that change the answer?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 11
# 1. Parallel context (RUN_LONG only, ~3 meta calls + up to 36 agent calls).
if RUN_LONG:
    par = meta_search([a for a in ARCHIVE if a["origin"] == "initial"], "parallel", T)
    par_new = [a for a in par if a["origin"] != "initial"]
    pv = [a["vec"] for a in par_new if any(a["vec"])]
    pp = [cosine(pv[i], pv[j]) for i in range(len(pv)) for j in range(i + 1, len(pv))]
    print("parallel: best train", max(a["score"] for a in par_new), " mean similarity", round(sum(pp) / max(len(pp), 1), 2))
else:
    print("Set RUN_LONG = True to compare with parallel context.")
ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 12
# 2. A per-kind router. Notice: on training data the router can match coverage, but with one task per kind it is
#    "choosing on the test set". A real router needs its own held-out data, which is why agent selection is hard.
router = {}                                          # kind -> index of the chosen agent in ARCHIVE
for i, t in enumerate(TRAIN):
    router[t["kind"]] = max(range(len(ARCHIVE)), key=lambda j: (ARCHIVE[j]["vec"][i], -ARCHIVE[j]["chars"]))
router_score = sum(ARCHIVE[router[t["kind"]]]["vec"][i] for i, t in enumerate(TRAIN)) / len(TRAIN)
print({k: ARCHIVE[j]["design"].name for k, j in router.items()})
print(f"router {router_score:.0%} · best single {max(a['score'] for a in ARCHIVE):.0%} · coverage {coverage:.0%}")
ap4_meta_agents.ipynb · cell 13
# 3. Designer tokens cost 10x. Only the meta-agent part of C0 gets more expensive.
meta_part = sum(tok(a.get("meta_chars", 0)) for a in designed)
C0_priced = C0 + 9 * meta_part
be = C0_priced / (p_new * gap) if gap > 0 and best_new is not best_init else float("inf")
print("break-even with 10x designer price:", "never" if math.isinf(be) else f"{be:,.0f} queries")
Past project · AP5

ARCHON Augmented: Planning and Web-Enhanced Components

Open in Colab

CS329A Winter 2025 · Megan Mou, Sherry Xie, Emily Zhang, Andrew Park

Paper · Poster

Problem
Archon stacks inference-time techniques (generate, fuse, critique, rank, verify, unit tests) but has only seven building blocks, no tool use or web search, and its strong results relied on about 70B models.
Method
Three new blocks: a Planner at the front (rephrase + step-by-step plan passed to later blocks), an Expander after each generator (adds context so critics and rankers can judge), and a Web Search Tool Call (queries from the plan, top 10 Google results per query). Ablations on a closed-source configuration (GPT-4o generate/rank, Qwen2-72B critic/fuser) and an open-source ensemble, on a 500-question subset of Humanity's Last Exam (453 scored) and AlpacaEval (length-controlled win rate).
Results
HLE (closed / open): ARCHON 3.3% / 3.3%; + Planner 3.3% / 2.6%; + Planner + Expander 4.2% (19/453) / 3.8% (17/453); + Planner + Tool Call + Expander 3.3% / 2.9%. Zero-shot GPT-4o 3.1%. AlpacaEval LC (closed / mixed): ARCHON 67.73% / 57.01%; + Planner 69.62% / 67.06%; + Planner + Expander 66.01% / 68.30%; + Planner + Tool Call + Expander 73.40% / 73.92%. Web search hurt on HLE because results were off-topic or cluttered.
Lessons
A planner at the front of a pipeline is cheap and helped weaker open models catch up on instruction following. Search helps only if retrieval is precise; noisy pages can make answers worse. The value of each block depends on the task, so ablate each one on your own data. Small differences on a hard benchmark (a few questions out of 453) are noise.
Our rebuild
Questions about made-up towns (so search is required) over 16 local 'web pages', including look-alike decoy pages and clutter, searched with a 15-line BM25. Compares a single call, single call + search (RAG), planner + search + 1 generator, and the full pipeline: planner (plan + queries) -> BM25 search -> 3 generators (8B, 8B, 3B) -> critic + ranker -> fuser -> verifier with fallback. Reports accuracy and calls per question; solutions ablate the fuser/verifier for free, score each generator, and test a noisier web.

The code, cell by cell

Everything below is exactly what runs in ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb, in the same order. The notebook starts with the shared setup cell (shown in Module 00), then these cells.

The original project

ARCHON Augmented: Planning and Web-Enhanced Components by Megan Mou, Sherry Xie, Emily Zhang and Andrew Park (Stanford CS329A, Winter 2025; the poster is titled Multi-Component ARCHON). Paper · Poster · code: github.com/49emily/cs329a-archon

Background. Archon (Saad-Falcon et al. 2024, Archon: An Architecture Search Framework for Inference-Time Techniques) has seven building blocks: Generator, Fuser, Critic, Ranker, Verifier, Unit Test Generator, Unit Test Evaluator. It searches for the best way to stack them. Its strong results relied on models of about 70B parameters, and it had no tool use.

Method. Three new blocks: - Planner (first): rephrases the request and writes a step-by-step plan that is passed to every later block. - Expander (after each generator): adds context and detail to each candidate so critics and rankers can judge it. - Web Search Tool Call (after the planner): writes q search queries from the plan, calls the Google Search API, and passes the top 10 results per query to the generators.

Setup. A closed-source configuration (GPT-4o generating and ranking, Qwen2-72B as critic and fuser) and an open-source one (an ensemble including Qwen2-72B, WizardLM-2 8x22B, Llama-3 70B, Mixtral 8x22B and others). Benchmarks: a 500-question subset of Humanity's Last Exam (HLE; 453 scored) and AlpacaEval (length-controlled win rate).

Key results (as reported).

Architecture HLE closed HLE open AlpacaEval LC closed AlpacaEval LC open/mixed
ARCHON 3.3% (15/453) 3.3% (15/453) 67.73% 57.01%
Planner + ARCHON 3.3% (15/453) 2.6% (12/453) 69.62% 67.06%
Planner + Expander + ARCHON 4.2% (19/453) 3.8% (17/453) 66.01% 68.30%
Planner + Tool Call + Expander + ARCHON 3.3% (15/453) 2.9% (13/453) 73.40% 73.92%

Zero-shot GPT-4o scored 3.1% on HLE. On AlpacaEval the new blocks, especially the Planner, lifted the open-source setup from more than 10 points behind to slightly ahead of closed-source. On HLE, web search hurt: results were often off-topic or full of page clutter, which confused the models.

Limitations they state. Reasoning stays the bottleneck; the Expander's value depends on the benchmark; the planner is not yet query-adaptive. (Our note: on HLE the differences are a few questions out of 453, so they are within noise.)

Our mini version. Questions about made-up towns that no model can know, so search is required. The "web" is 16 local pages, including look-alike decoy pages, searched with BM25 (a classic keyword ranking). We skip the Expander to save calls. Generators mix llama3.1:8b and llama3.2:3b, a tiny "mixed-source" ensemble.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 2
import math, random, re
from collections import Counter
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from typing import List
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
from lal.llm import ask, structured
from lal.checks import check

1. A small made-up web

Eight towns, each with a founding year, a population, a river and a bridge. Three decoy pages describe "New ..." towns with different numbers, and five pages are pure clutter. All generated with a seed.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 3
rng = random.Random(5)
NAMES = ["Velmora", "Ostrand", "Brenhollow", "Quillmarsh", "Taversk", "Lunegate", "Corrowind", "Ashby Vale"]
RIVERS = ["Senn", "Ilver", "Draw", "Morrow", "Kesh", "Tull", "Avin", "Pellam"]
BRIDGES = ["Kestrel Bridge", "Lantern Bridge", "Old Mill Bridge", "Heron Bridge", "Copper Bridge", "Saint Ide Bridge", "Gull Bridge", "Iron Fox Bridge"]
TOWNS = {}
for n, r, b in zip(NAMES, RIVERS, BRIDGES):
    y = rng.randint(1100, 1800)
    TOWNS[n] = dict(founded=y, pop=rng.randint(3000, 95000), river=r, bridge=b, bridge_year=y + rng.randint(30, 300))

FILLER = ["The weekly market sells cheese, wool and honey.", "Tourists often visit the old clock tower.",
          "The local football club plays in the regional league.", "Summers are mild and winters are wet.",
          "A folk festival takes place every autumn.", "The town hall was renovated recently."]

def town_page(n, t):
    return (f"{n} is a town on the {t['river']} river. It was founded in {t['founded']}. Today it has a population of "
            f"{t['pop']:,}. The {t['bridge']} in {n} was built in {t['bridge_year']}. " + " ".join(rng.sample(FILLER, 3)))

DOCS = [{"title": n, "text": town_page(n, t)} for n, t in TOWNS.items()]
for n in rng.sample(NAMES, 3):                                    # decoys: similar names, different facts
    fake = dict(TOWNS[n], founded=TOWNS[n]["founded"] + 400, pop=TOWNS[n]["pop"] // 3 + 1000)
    DOCS.append({"title": f"New {n}", "text": f"New {n} is a newer settlement, not to be confused with {n}. It was founded in "
                 f"{fake['founded']} and has a population of {fake['pop']:,}. " + " ".join(rng.sample(FILLER, 2))})
for i in range(5):
    DOCS.append({"title": f"Travel blog {i + 1}", "text": " ".join(rng.sample(FILLER, 4)) + f" We loved our trip to {rng.choice(NAMES)}!"})
print(len(DOCS), "pages. Example:\n", DOCS[0]["text"])

Six questions. Most need two pages, and some need arithmetic on top.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 4
A, B, C, D = rng.sample(NAMES, 4)
QS = [
    (f"How many years passed between the founding of {A} and the building of the {TOWNS[B]['bridge']}?",
     abs(TOWNS[B]["bridge_year"] - TOWNS[A]["founded"])),
    (f"What is the combined population of {A} and {C}?", TOWNS[A]["pop"] + TOWNS[C]["pop"]),
    (f"Which town has more people, {B} or {D}?", max((B, D), key=lambda n: TOWNS[n]["pop"])),
    (f"On which river is the town that has the {TOWNS[C]['bridge']}?", TOWNS[C]["river"]),
    (f"How many more people live in {max((C, D), key=lambda n: TOWNS[n]['pop'])} than in {min((C, D), key=lambda n: TOWNS[n]['pop'])}?",
     abs(TOWNS[C]["pop"] - TOWNS[D]["pop"])),
    (f"Which was founded first, {A} or {D}, and by how many years? Answer with the number of years.",
     abs(TOWNS[A]["founded"] - TOWNS[D]["founded"])),
]
for q, a in QS:
    print(a, "←", q)

def answer_of(text):
    m = re.findall(r"ANSWER:\s*(.+)", text or "", re.I)
    return m[-1].strip() if m else ((text or "").strip().splitlines() or [""])[-1]

def is_right(got, gold):
    got = (got or "").replace(",", "")
    if isinstance(gold, int):
        nums = re.findall(r"-?\d+", got)
        return bool(nums) and (int(nums[0]) == gold or int(nums[-1]) == gold)
    pool = NAMES if gold in NAMES else RIVERS            # the FIRST name mentioned is the answer ("Velmora (52,000 vs Ostrand's 30,000)")
    hits = sorted((got.lower().find(n.lower()), n) for n in pool if n.lower() in got.lower())
    return bool(hits) and hits[0][1] == gold

2. The search tool: BM25 in 15 lines

BM25 scores a page by how often it contains the query words, with rare words counting more and long pages counting less (Robertson & Zaragoza 2009). It is what many search engines started from.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 5
def tokens(s):
    return re.findall(r"[a-z0-9]+", s.lower())

class BM25:
    def __init__(self, docs, k1=1.5, b=0.75):
        self.docs, self.k1, self.b = docs, k1, b
        self.tf = [Counter(tokens(d["title"] + " " + d["text"])) for d in docs]
        self.len = [sum(t.values()) for t in self.tf]
        self.avg = sum(self.len) / len(self.len)
        df = Counter(w for t in self.tf for w in t)
        self.idf = {w: math.log(1 + (len(docs) - n + 0.5) / (n + 0.5)) for w, n in df.items()}

    def search(self, query, k=3):
        q = tokens(query)
        def score(i):
            return sum(self.idf.get(w, 0) * self.tf[i][w] * (self.k1 + 1) /
                       (self.tf[i][w] + self.k1 * (1 - self.b + self.b * self.len[i] / self.avg)) for w in q)
        return [self.docs[i] for i in sorted(range(len(self.docs)), key=score, reverse=True)[:k]]

WEB = BM25(DOCS)
print([d["title"] for d in WEB.search(f"{A} founded")])

def as_context(pages):
    return "\n\n".join(f"[{d['title']}] {d['text']}" for d in pages)

3. Two simple baselines

  • Single call, no search: the model has to guess. Expect near 0, which proves the facts are really unknown.
  • Single call + search on the raw question: classic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG).
ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 6
FMT = "\nEnd with one line: ANSWER: <short answer>"

def single(q):
    return {"answer": answer_of(ask(q + FMT, max_tokens=300)), "calls": 1}

def single_rag(q):
    ctx = as_context(WEB.search(q, k=3))
    return {"answer": answer_of(ask(f"Web results:\n{ctx}\n\nQuestion: {q}\nUse only the web results." + FMT, max_tokens=400)), "calls": 1}

4. The augmented Archon pipeline

question → Planner (plan + search queries) → Web search (BM25) → 3 Generators (8B, 8B, 3B)
         → Critic + Ranker → Fuser → Verifier → answer          (7 model calls)

If the verifier says the fused answer is not supported by the pages, we fall back to the top-ranked candidate.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 7
class PlanOut(BaseModel):
    rephrased: str
    steps: List[str] = Field(max_length=5)
    queries: List[str] = Field(description="1 to 3 short search queries", max_length=3)

class Critique(BaseModel):
    notes: List[str] = Field(description="one short critique per candidate, in order")
    ranking: List[int] = Field(description="candidate numbers, best first")

class Verdict(BaseModel):
    supported: bool
    reason: str

def plan_and_search(q):
    try:
        p = structured(f"Question: {q}\nRephrase the question, write a short plan, and write 1-3 web search queries "
                       "that will find every fact the plan needs.", PlanOut, max_tokens=400)
    except Exception:
        p = PlanOut(rephrased=q, steps=["answer the question"], queries=[q])
    pages, seen = [], set()
    for query in (p.queries or [q])[:3]:
        for d in WEB.search(query, k=3):
            if d["title"] not in seen:
                seen.add(d["title"]); pages.append(d)
    return p, pages[:6]

def generate(q, plan, pages, model, temperature=0.7):
    prompt = (f"Web results:\n{as_context(pages)}\n\nQuestion: {q}\nPlan:\n" + "\n".join(f"- {s}" for s in plan.steps) +
              "\nFollow the plan. Use only the web results. Watch out for pages about similarly named places." + FMT)
    try:
        return ask(prompt, model_name=model, temperature=temperature, max_tokens=500)
    except Exception:
        return ""

GENERATORS = [MODEL, MODEL, "llama3.2:3b"]          # MODEL is llama3.1:8b on a GPU runtime

def archon(q):
    plan, pages = plan_and_search(q)
    cands = [generate(q, plan, pages, m) for m in GENERATORS]
    listing = "\n\n".join(f"=== Candidate {i + 1} ===\n{c[-1200:]}" for i, c in enumerate(cands))
    try:
        crit = structured(f"Web results:\n{as_context(pages)}\n\nQuestion: {q}\n\n{listing}\n\nCritique each candidate "
                          "(facts used, arithmetic) and rank them, best first.", Critique, max_tokens=500)
        order = [i - 1 for i in crit.ranking if 1 <= i <= len(cands)]
        order += [i for i in range(len(cands)) if i not in order]
        notes = crit.notes
    except Exception:
        order, notes = list(range(len(cands))), []
    top = order[:2]
    try:
        fused = ask(f"Web results:\n{as_context(pages)}\n\nQuestion: {q}\n\nBest candidates:\n" +
                    "\n\n".join(cands[i][-1200:] for i in top) + "\n\nCritiques:\n" + "\n".join(notes[:3]) +
                    "\n\nWrite one final, correct answer. Fix any mistakes." + FMT, max_tokens=500)
    except Exception:
        fused = cands[order[0]]
    final = answer_of(fused)
    try:
        v = structured(f"Web results:\n{as_context(pages)}\n\nQuestion: {q}\nProposed answer: {final}\n"
                       "Is this answer fully supported by the web results, with correct arithmetic?", Verdict, max_tokens=200)
        ok = v.supported
    except Exception:
        ok = True
    used = final if ok else answer_of(cands[order[0]])
    return {"answer": used, "calls": 7, "plan": plan, "pages": [d["title"] for d in pages],
            "cands": [answer_of(c) for c in cands], "order": order, "fused": final, "verified": ok}

def plan_search_single(q):
    """Ablation: planner + search, then one generator. Isolates the value of the generate/critique/fuse layers."""
    plan, pages = plan_and_search(q)
    return {"answer": answer_of(generate(q, plan, pages, MODEL, temperature=0)), "calls": 2}

5. Run everything

At most 6 + 6 + 12 + 42 = 66 model calls.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 8
def run(method):
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(2) as ex:
        return list(ex.map(method, [q for q, _ in QS]))

RES = {"single call": run(single), "single + search": run(single_rag),
       "planner + search + 1 gen": run(plan_search_single), "Archon augmented": run(archon)}
print(f"{'method':26s} accuracy  calls/question")
ACC = {}
for name, rs in RES.items():
    ACC[name] = sum(is_right(r["answer"], gold) for r, (_, gold) in zip(rs, QS)) / len(QS)
    print(f"{name:26s} {ACC[name]:6.0%}    {sum(r['calls'] for r in rs) / len(rs):.0f}")

for r, (q, gold) in zip(RES["Archon augmented"], QS):
    print(f"\nQ: {q}\n  gold {gold} | final {r['answer']} | candidates {r['cands']} | rank {r['order']} | verified {r['verified']}")
    print("  queries:", r["plan"].queries, "→ pages:", r["pages"])

What to notice. Search is what makes these questions answerable at all; the gap between the first two rows shows it. Planner-written queries usually find both pages a two-hop question needs, which a single raw-question search can miss. Whether the full stack beats "planner + search + 1 generator" is the interesting question: the extra 5 calls only pay off if the candidates disagree and the critic can tell which one is right. Look at the decoy pages in the retrieved lists: if a "New ..." page sneaks in, that is the HLE failure in miniature.

ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 9
check("no-search baseline is near zero (facts are really unknown)", ACC["single call"] <= 2 / 6)
check("search helps: single + search beats single call", ACC["single + search"] > ACC["single call"])
check("Archon pipeline answers at least 3 of 6", ACC["Archon augmented"] >= 3 / 6)
check("BM25 finds the right town page first", WEB.search(f"{A} founded population", k=1)[0]["title"] == A)

Your turn

  1. Is the fuser + verifier worth 2 calls? Using the saved results, score the pipeline as if it stopped after ranking (answer = top-ranked candidate). No new calls.
  2. Which generator is best? Score each generator's candidates on its own (8B #1, 8B #2, 3B). Does the ensemble beat its best member? No new calls.
  3. A noisier web. Add 10 more decoy pages ("Old Velmora", "Velmora Station", ...) with wrong numbers, rebuild the index, and rerun Archon on 2 questions. Does search start to hurt, as it did on HLE?
Show worked solutions (try the exercises first)
ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 10
# 1. Stop after the ranker. Notice: if this matches the full pipeline, the fuser and verifier are cost without value
#    here; that is exactly the kind of ablation the paper ran for each new block.
rank_only = sum(is_right(r["cands"][r["order"][0]], gold) for r, (_, gold) in zip(RES["Archon augmented"], QS)) / len(QS)
print(f"rank only {rank_only:.0%}   full pipeline {ACC['Archon augmented']:.0%}")
ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 11
# 2. Each generator alone, and the best-of-3 oracle. The ensemble can only beat its best member if the
#    ranker picks well; the oracle shows the ceiling.
for i, m in enumerate(GENERATORS):
    acc_i = sum(is_right(r["cands"][i], gold) for r, (_, gold) in zip(RES["Archon augmented"], QS)) / len(QS)
    print(f"generator {i + 1} ({m}): {acc_i:.0%}")
oracle = sum(any(is_right(c, gold) for c in r["cands"]) for r, (_, gold) in zip(RES["Archon augmented"], QS)) / len(QS)
print(f"any candidate right (oracle): {oracle:.0%}")
ap5_archon_augmented.ipynb · cell 12
# 3. A noisier web (14 calls). Notice whether the planner's queries now return decoys first.
noise = []
for n in NAMES[:5]:
    for prefix in ("Old", "Upper"):
        t = TOWNS[n]
        noise.append({"title": f"{prefix} {n}", "text": f"{prefix} {n} is near {n}. It was founded in {t['founded'] - 50} and has a "
                      f"population of {t['pop'] + 777:,}. The {t['bridge']} replica in {prefix} {n} was built in {t['bridge_year'] + 9}."})
clean_web = WEB
WEB = BM25(DOCS + noise)
noisy = [archon(q) for q, _ in QS[:2]]
WEB = clean_web
for r, (q, gold) in zip(noisy, QS[:2]):
    print(f"gold {gold} | noisy-web answer {r['answer']} | pages {r['pages']}")
Reference

Glossary

Agent
An LLM in a loop that chooses its own next step and uses tools.
Workflow / compound AI system
Code fixes the steps; LLMs fill in parts.
Harness
All code around the model: loop, tools, prompts, limits, logs.
Constrained decoding
The server forces output to match a schema, token by token.
Context rot
Answers get worse as the context grows, starting early.
Observation masking
Hiding old tool outputs to keep the context small.
Compaction
Summarizing older history when nearing the context limit.
RAG
Retrieval-augmented generation: find relevant passages, then answer from them.
BM25
Classic keyword search scoring. Good for exact strings.
RRF
Reciprocal rank fusion: merging two ranked lists by 1/(60+rank).
Reranker
A slower, more accurate model that reorders search results.
MCP
Model Context Protocol: a standard way to expose tools and data to models.
ReAct
Think, act, observe, repeat.
Reflexion
Write a lesson after a failure and retry with it.
CodeAct
The model acts by writing code instead of JSON tool calls.
DSPy
Framework where you declare inputs/outputs and an optimizer tunes the prompts.
GEPA
Prompt optimizer that reflects on failures in text to evolve better instructions.
SFT
Supervised fine-tuning: train on example input/output pairs.
On-policy distillation
The student writes; a teacher scores each token; the student learns from that.
GRPO
RL method that compares a group of answers to the same prompt and favors the better ones.
pass@k / pass^k
Any of k tries succeeds / all k tries succeed.
LLM-as-judge
Using a model to grade outputs. Needs bias controls and human calibration.
Prompt injection
Instructions hidden in content (a document, a web page) that try to steer the agent.
Flywheel
Usage creates data, data improves the system, a better system gets more usage.
Source: Stanford CS329Z "Engineering AI Agents", Fall 2026 (Diyi Yang, Michael Ryan, John Yang), lectures 1–11 and assigned readings, as of Nov 2, 2026. Lectures on evaluation, safety and coding agents had no slides yet; those modules draw on their assigned papers. Numbers are as reported in the slides and papers. Code is illustrative and should be tested before use.
